On the structure of big bang singularities in spatially homogenous solutions to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
Hans Ringström

Abstract




Chapter 1
Introduction

The subject of this article is the structure of big bang singularities in spatially homogeneous solutions to the
Einstein non-linear scalar
field equations
.
Given a
potential

V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
we are, in other words, interested in solving

\displaystyle G=

\displaystyle T,

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\phi=

\displaystyle V^{\prime}\circ\phi

(1.1b)

for a Lorentz manifold
(M,g)
and a function
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, where
\Box_{g}
is the wave operator associated with
g
,

\displaystyle G:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}-\tfrac{1}{2}Sg,

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle d\phi\otimes d\phi-\left[\tfrac{1}{2}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}+V\circ\phi\right]g.

Here
\mathrm{Ric}
and
S
denote the Ricci tensor and scalar curvature of
g
respectively. Moreover,
\phi
is referred to as the
scalar field
;

G
as the
Einstein tensor
;
and
T
as the
stress energy tensor
.
Solutions to (
1.1
), or systems obtained by including additional matter sources, have been studied by many authors. The reason is that they
can model both inflation and dark energy. However, the questions we consider in this article differ from the ones normally asked by physicists.
Our main goal in this article is to obtain a global picture of the dynamics in the spatially homogeneous
setting. Using the corresponding results, one can prove that there are large classes of spatially locally homogeneous solutions that are globally
non-linearly stable (in the absence of symmetries), both to the future and to the past. However, our main motivation stems from a desire to obtain
a more general understanding of the structure of big bang singularities, as we now explain.

The structure of cosmological singularities; the BKL proposal and initial data on the big bang.
One important proposal concerning the structure
of big bang singularities is due to Belinskiǐ, Khalatnikov and Lifschitz, BKL for short; see, e.g.,
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
. Very roughly speaking,
their main hypothesis is that big bang singularities are local, spacelike and oscillatory. In particular, they suggest that “spatial derivatives”
are asymptotically negligible, so that the evolution equations reduce to a family of ODE’s; that observers going into the singularity typically
lose the ability to communicate; and that the eigenvalues of the expansion normalised Weingarten maps of an appropriate foliation oscillate according
to a chaotic one-dimensional map. Recall here that the Weingarten map of a spacelike hypersurface is the second fundamental form with one index raised
by the induced metric. Moreover, the expansion normalised Weingarten map is the Weingarten map divided by the mean curvature. There are, however,
exceptions to this hypothesis. For example, particular types of matter, such as a stiff fluid or a scalar field, are expected to suppress the
oscillations so that the expansion normalised Weingarten map converges. The corresponding setting is referred to as convergent or quiescent. Non-linear
scalar fields belong to this category of matter models, and we are here therefore mainly interested in the quiescent setting. Several attempts to
formalise the ideas of BKL have been formulated in the literature; see, e.g.,
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
19
,
30
,
38
,
28
,
29
,
15
,
17
,
18
]
and
the references cited therein. In
[
19
,
30
]
, the authors develop the idea of a solution being
asymptotically velocity term dominated
near the singularity
(AVTDS); a solution is AVTDS if it asymptotes to a solution of the so-called
velocity term dominated
(VTD) system.
Roughly speaking, the VTD system is a truncated system of equations obtained by dropping some of the spatial derivatives. However, it is unfortunately
not geometric in nature. In
[
56
]
, a substitute in the form of a geometric
notion of initial data on the singularity is introduced; see Definition
1.17
for a slight generalisation in the present setting.
In the special case of a Gaussian foliation, a solution to the VTD system can be thought of as initial data on the singularity; see
[
56
]
for
a justification of this statement. However, the notion of initial data on the singularity is not tied to Gaussian foliations. In fact, beyond
covariance, this notion has several advantages: that a development induces data on the singularity can be interpreted as a covariant formulation
of the statement that it is AVTDS; if there are initial data on the singularity in a certain symmetry class, it is natural to conjecture that generic
solutions induce data on the singularity in this symmetry class, so that generic solutions are quiescent; if there are no initial data on the
singularity in a given symmetry class, generic solutions can be expected to be oscillatory in this symmetry class; and in quiescent settings, one
could ideally hope to parametrise solutions by data on the singularity. There are several caveats to these expectations. However, it is clear that
initial data on the singularity is a natural starting point for understanding the structure of singularities, and this is the perspective we take in
this article.

The map from initial data on the singularity to developments.

Given a notion of initial data on the singularity, the first question to ask is: do data uniquely determine corresponding developments? That the answer
is yes in the
3+1
-dimensional Einstein non-linear scalar field setting is verified in
[
23
]
, a result that generalises

[
5
,
33
,
22
]
. The results of
[
5
,
33
,
22
,
23
]
are all fundamental. On the other hand, they do not provide a global
picture of the dynamics. In fact, it can be argued
that they do not even give a local picture of the dynamics in the sense that it is not clear from these results that there is an open set of regular
initial data whose developments give rise to data on the singularity. The reason for this deficiency is that the results of, e.g.,

[
5
,
33
,
22
,
23
]
do not include any statements concerning the regularity of the map from data on the singularity to the corresponding
developments. If one were, e.g., able to prove that the map, say
F
, from the set of isometry classes of initial data on the singularity to the set of
isometry classes of developments is a local diffeomorphism, one could immediately conclude, e.g., that developments that induce data on the singularity
are stable and that there is an open set of regular initial data whose developments induce data on the singularity. If
F
is a local diffeomorphism, one
could also argue that exceptional subsets of initial data on the singularity correspond to exceptional
subsets of developments. Turning to the global properties of
F
, the ideal situation is when
F
is
a diffeomorphism. In that setting, isometry classes of initial data on the singularity do not only provide optimal asymptotic information, they
also parametrise the set of isometry classes of developments. However, even when
F
is not a global diffeomorphism, the image of
F
is a natural
reference and one can focus on the complement of the image of
F
. This complement might, e.g., be non-generic, and thereby irrelevant; it could include
an open set of solutions that do not have crushing singularities and are therefore irrelevant in the analysis of singularities etc. If one can verify that
the topology of the set of isometry classes of data on the singularity differs from the topology of the set of isometry classes of developments, it is
clear that
F
cannot be a diffeomorphism. This can be used to make deductions concerning the global dynamics of solutions; below we provide
examples of this.

Spikes.

In the spatially homogeneous setting, it is natural to conjecture generic developments to belong to the image of
F
. We prove several such results
in this article. However, in the spatially inhomogeneous setting, this is not to be expected. This is partly related to what is referred to as
“spikes” in the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-Gowdy symmetric setting. The reason for the terminology is that the metric is invariant under an effective action of

\mathbb{T}^{2}
in this setting, with the consequence that the metric components only depend on
(t,\vartheta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
(where the

t
-coordinate of a spacetime point
\xi
is the area of the orbit of
\xi
under the action of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
). Moreover, the limits, in the direction of
the singularity, of the eigenvalues of the expansion normalised Weingarten maps of the constant-
t
hypersurfaces are functions on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The
limits are typically smooth, except for a discrete set of elements of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
at which they make jumps. Since the limits are smooth when removing the
discrete set of points, and make finite jumps at the exceptional points, the discontinuities are called spikes. However, due to the symmetry reduction,
the spikes actually correspond to
2
-tori on the singularity (when representing the singularity as a
3
-torus). From that point of view, it is more
natural to think of the spikes as “scars”; two dimensional hypersurfaces on the singularity. In the
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-Gowdy symmetric setting, the
solutions with spikes can be built from developments arising from data on the singularity; see
[
45
]
. However, even more generally, it is natural
to expect scars to be subsets of the zero sets of the limits of structure coefficients associated with suitably normalised eigenvector fields of the
expansion
normalised Weingarten map. If one can demonstrate that the relevant limits are sufficiently regular and generically have the property that their
derivatives are non-zero when they vanish, one can hope to prove that the scars correspond to hypersurfaces more generally. Note, however, that any
such analysis is crucially dependent on understanding the properties of the map from regular initial data to asymptotic data. Note also
that if one ignores the spikes, generic
\mathbb{T}^{3}
-Gowdy symmetric vacuum solutions induce data on the remaining part of the singularity; see

[
57
]
. To summarise, even in the presence of spikes, it can be expected to be of crucial importance to understand the regularity properties
of the map from regular data to data on the singularity.

The attractor.
Concerning oscillatory solutions, there are only partial results in the spatially homogeneous setting, and no results in the
spatially inhomogeneous setting. However, there are some features of the analysis in the spatially homogeneous setting that may be relevant more
generally. For instance, some of the results in the spatially homogeneous setting are based on a detailed analysis of how solutions approach the
Kasner circle along a stable manifold and then diverge away from it along an unstable manifold. The first step of the analysis is thus to understand
what the stable and unstable manifolds are. In the spatially homogeneous setting, this is straightforward. However, in the spatially inhomogeneous
setting, it is more complicated. Nevertheless, it is quite conceivable that they can be obtained as sets of developments corresponding to initial
data on the singularity as in
[
22
]
,
[
33
]
or
[
23
]
. However, to understand the corresponding subset of regular initial data, we
need to understand the regularity of the map
F
. Similarly, but more ambitiously, one can hope to construct an attractor. Again, this can be expected
to require an understanding of the regularity of the map
F
.

Motivation, goals.
With the above observations in mind, it is clear that it is not only of interest to understand the structure of cosmological
singularities in the non-linear scalar field setting. It is of particular interest to do so from the perspective of initial data on the singularity.
Moreover, it is of central interest to understand the regularity of the map from initial data on the singularity to developments. The main purpose of
this article is to provide a global picture of the dynamics in the spatially homogenous setting, with particular focus on the regularity properties of
the map
F
. This yields a detailed analysis in a model case which can then be used as a reference when turning to situations with less symmetry.
Here we mainly focus on Bianchi class A solutions; i.e., the case that the relevant spacetimes arise from left invariant initial data on unimodular

3
-dimensional Lie groups. However, we also discuss the
k=-1
FLRW setting.

Concerning the Einstein scalar field case,
[
57
]
contains a demonstration of the fact that Bianchi class A solutions induce data on the
singularity unless they are vacuum solutions of type VIII or IX; see Table
1.1
for the relevant classification. Moreover, vacuum Bianchi
type VIII and IX solutions are either oscillatory or have a Cauchy horizon. On the other hand,
[
57
]
does not contain a discussion of the
regularity of the map
F
. Moreover, even though the scalar field setting is of interest, the non-linear scalar field setting is more important, since
it, in the physics literature, is used to model inflation and used as a mechanism to generate accelerated expansion in the late universe. Note, however,
that when physicists study inflation, they are not interested in the asymptotics in the direction in the singularity. Instead, they are interested in
a regime which is close to the singularity but distinct from it. Moreover, in the expanding direction, the interesting situation is the transition from
a matter dominated phase to a phase where the dark energy takes over. Since we are here interested in the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity
and not in an intermediate phase, our analysis is not directly relevant to the study of inflation, and since we do not include additional matter, our solutions do
not model the transition from a matter dominated phase to where the dark energy takes over. On the other hand, due to our analysis, we are able to prove
global non-linear stability of a large class of solutions which are good models of the early and late universe. Moreover, the analysis is a natural starting
point for including mattter.

As big bang formation has been analysed in the Einstein scalar field setting, one might expect analysing the past asymptotics to be
straightforward in the non-linear scalar field setting. However, the presence of the potential destroys a key monotonicity property. This makes the analysis
substantially more complicated.

Previous results.
Since the literature in the non-linear scalar field setting is vast, it is not possible to give an exhaustive list of
references. However,
[
21
,
31
,
32
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
35
]
are some examples of articles on this topic containing
mathematical results. See also the book
[
14
]
.
More recent results in the spatially flat FLRW setting with matter, using a dynamical systems perspective, are to be found in
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
.
We refer the interested reader to these articles as a starting point for entering the literature. The goal of many of the mathematical studies
is to model the accelerated expansion of the universe. A natural second step, once the future asymptotics are understood, is then to prove future
global non-linear stability of the corresponding solutions. Some examples of this are given by
[
27
,
52
,
53
,
58
,
55
,
24
,
39
]
. On the other
hand, to the best of our knowledge, the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity in the anisotropic setting have not been analysed in detail,
and this is the main purpose of the present article. Combining the detailed analysis with previous future global non-linear stability results and
a recent result concerning stable big bang formation then yields a large class of solutions that are past and future globally non-linearly stable.

Results, outline of the introduction.
In order to derive the regularity propeties of the map
F
, we first need to endow isometry classes of data
on the singularity with a smooth structure and similarly for isometry classes of developments. This requires the introduction of a substantial amount of
terminology. In Section
1.1
, we introduce the notion of regular Bianchi class A initial data. We also discuss corresponding
symmetry types; isotropic, locally rotationally symmetric etc. Fixing not only the Bianchi type, but also a symmetry type, turns out to be crucial
in order for it to be possible to endow the corresponding set of isometry classes of initial data with, say, fixed mean curvature with a natural
smooth structure. In Section
1.2
, we introduce a notion of data on the singularity in the Bianchi class A setting,
see Definition
1.17
. This notion is of course more restrictive than the one introduced in
[
56
]
, since it concerns a
spatially homogeneous setting. On the other hand, it is also more general in that we here do not require the eigenvalues of the limit of the expansion
normalised Weingarten map to be distinct. Moreover, we include data corresponding to Cauchy horizons as one possibility in
Definition
1.17
. Again, we introduce symmetry types in this setting and notation for the relevant sets of initial data on the
singularity. In Section
1.3
we turn to developments. In the setting of interest here, there are natural foliations of
the developments of Bianchi class A initial data. They are both Gaussian (the curves with constant space-coordinate parametrise unit timelike
geodesics orthogonal to the leaves of the foliation) and CMC (constant mean curvature). It is convenient to work with these developments. We
introduce them and the relevant terminology in Section
1.3
. We also prove existence of developments, given regular initial
data and deduce their basic properties. In Subsection
1.3.1
, we clarify what it means for a development to induce
data on the singularity; see Definition
1.38
. We also verify that the developments of isometric regular initial data induce
isometric data on the singularity (assuming that they induce data on the singularity). In Theorem
1.45
we prove that, under
suitable assumptions concerning the potential, data on the singularity give rise to unique developments. Moreover, we verify that isometric data on the
singularity give rise to isometeric developments. Before analysing the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity, it is important to keep in
mind that Bianchi type IX solutions are special in that
they include a large range of dynamics: solutions can induce data on the singularity; exhibit chaotic oscillations in the direction of the singularity;
have Cauchy horizons through which the solution can be extended in inequivalent ways; expand both to the future and the past (as in de Sitter space);
and have a static geometry (as in the Einstein static universe). If one is interested in studying cosmological singularities, the latter two
possibilities have to be excluded in some way. In Subsection
1.3.2
, we introduce conditions that serve this purpose.
In Subsection
1.3.3
, we are then in a position to introduce appropriate terminology for isometry classes of developments.

In Section
1.4
, we turn to the problem of endowing the set of isometry classes of developments with a smooth
structure. When doing so, we always focus on the simply connected setting; due to the form of the isometries induced on developments by isometries
of data, see Propositions
1.35
and
1.50
, one can always take quotients later. Next, a natural starting
point is to consider isometry classes of simply connected regular initial data of a fixed Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
(see Table
1.1
), fixed
symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}
(isotropic, locally rotationally symmetric etc.), fixed
mean curvature
\vartheta
and corresponding to a potential
V
. We denote this set by
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
(in practice, we also
exclude certain exceptional cases, such as initial data for Minkowski space). This set can be endowed with a smooth structure; see
Section
2.2
for details. If, for a given Bianchi type, all developments have the property that the mean curvature is strictly
monotonically decaying, then the natural map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
(the set of isometry classes of
developments corresponding to simply connected regular initial data of a fixed Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
, fixed symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}
and corresponding to a
potential
V
) is an injection, say
\iota_{\vartheta}
. Whatever smooth structure one wishes to endow
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with, it would be desirable
if this injection were a smooth diffeomorphism onto its image. In fact, the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
yields
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.
This means that one can endow
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with a smooth structure by first taking the disjoint union of the images of all the

\iota_{\vartheta}
and then taking the quotient of the union by the equivalence relation induced by the Einstein flow. Needless to say, for this to make
sense, the maps between subsets of isometry classes of regular initial data with distinct mean curvatures induced by the Einstein flow have to be
diffeomorphisms. Proving this statement does require an effort. Note also that, in favourable situations, there is one mean curvature, say
\vartheta_{0}
,
that is attained by all developments. In that case,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
can be used to parametrise
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.
However, for general potentials, there is no such
\vartheta_{0}
, and the more complicated construction mentioned above becomes necessary. There are additional
complications in the Bianchi type IX and isotropic Bianchi type I cases. In particular, the above discussion does not quite apply; we refer the reader to
Section
1.4
for the details.

Once we have introduced a smooth structure on the isometry classes of developments and isometry classes of data on the singularity, we can discuss the
regularity of the map from isometry classes of developments (that induce data on the singularity) to isometry classes of data on the singularity. In fact,
in Section
1.5
, we prove that this map is a diffeomorphism for several choices of Bianchi and symmetry type. Up to this point,
one could naively hope to generalise the results to much more general classes of spacetimes (with less or even no symmetry). However, it would be optimistic
to hope to obtain a global picture of the dynamics in general. In the Bianchi class A setting, we discuss the global dynamics in Section
1.6
.
To begin with, we note that there is a dichotomy in the direction of the singularity. Either the solution is
vacuum dominated
in the sense that

\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to zero (where
t
denotes the Gaussian time coordinate, i.e. it measures proper time to the singularity, and
\theta
denotes
the mean curvature of the constant-
t
hypersurfaces) or
matter dominated
in the sense that
\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to a non-zero limit in the
direction of the
singularity. The first result concerning the asymptotics is then contained in Theorem
1.85
. The conclusion is roughly
speaking the following: Ignoring isotropic Bianchi type I solutions for the moment, the remaining solutions induce data on the singularity unless they
are vacuum dominated, of Bianchi type VIII or IX and generic (in the sense that they are neither isotropic nor locally rotationally symmetric). Moreover,
the developments that do not induce data on the singularity are oscillatory. As a corollary, we can, e.g., conclude that for many choices of Bianchi
and symmetry type, all developments induce data on the singularity, so that the map from isometry classes of developments to isometry classes of data on
the singularity is a global diffeomorphism. In the remaining cases, it is sufficient to remove the developments with oscillatory singularities in order
to obtain a diffeomorphism.

The isotropic Bianchi type I setting is different and requires a separate treatment. One issue is the fact that there are isotropic Bianchi type I
solutions that do not have crushing singularities. However, even excluding those, there is an additional complication. In order to explain it, note first
that in the isotropic Bianchi type I setting, we impose stricter conditions on the potential, so that there is a range of mean curvatures that is attained
by the CMC foliations of all the developments that have a crushing singularity. This means that there is a
\vartheta_{0}
such that the isometry classes of
simply connected isotropic Bianchi type I developments with a crusing singularity and corresponding to a potential
V
, say

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
, can actually be identified with
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
. Naively, one would expect the Einstein flow
to define a diffeomorphism from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

to the corresponding set of isometry classes of initial data on the singularity, say
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
. However, there is a problem: these two sets
typically do not even have the same topology. To begin with
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is diffeomorphic to two copies of
\mathbb{R}
. If the potential is bounded,
the same is true of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
. In that case, one can therefore hope that all developments induce data on the singularity
and that the natural map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is a diffeomorphism. This is exactly what happens.
On the other hand, if the potential is bounded in one direction and unbounded in the other,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
is diffeomorphic to

\mathbb{R}
. There must thus be an exceptional solution that does not induce data on the singularity. Under suitable conditions on the potential, that is
exactly what happens. Finally, if the potential is unbounded in both directions, then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
is diffeomorphic to

\mathbb{S}^{1}
. There must thus be two exceptional solutions that do not induce data on the singularity. Under suitable conditions on the potential, that is
exactly what happens. In this sense, we obtain conclusions concerning the dynamics by merely studying the topologies of the set of isometry classes
of data on the singularity and the set of isometry classes of developments.

In Section
1.8
, we turn to the spatially homogeneous and isotropic negative spatial scalar curvature setting; i.e.,
the
k=-1
FLRW setting. The motivation for doing this is that we wish to cover all the FLRW settings. We begin by defining the relevant notion of
regular initial data. In this case there is, in analogy with the isotropic Bianchi type I setting, a notion of trivial regular initial data. These are
the data that give rise to the Milne model or analogous solutions with a positive cosmological constant. In
Proposition
1.109
, we prove that for every
\phi_{\infty}
, there is a solution such that
\phi(t)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}

and such that the geometry asymptotes to a vacuum solution with cosmological constant
\Lambda=V(\phi_{\infty})
(here we assume
V\geq 0
). It is important
to note that these solutions do not induce data on the singularity. In fact,
\bar{S}/\theta^{2}
does not converge to zero for these solutions, where

\bar{S}
denotes the scalar curvature of the leaves of spatial homogeneity. In Definitions
1.110
and

1.111
, we then introduce the notions of data on the singularity and development of regular initial data. In
Definition
1.113
we clarify what it means for developments to induce data on the singularity and in
Proposition
1.115
, we verify that, given data on the singularity, there are unique developments inducing the
prescribed initial data. Next, in Proposition
1.117
, we verify that developments either induce data on the
singularity or have Milne like asymptotics (or the analogous asymptotics with a positive cosmological constant). Finally, in
Proposition
1.118
, we verify that the latter case is Lebesgue and Baire non-generic.

In Section
1.9
, we then turn to the problem of deriving future asymptotics. In the case of an exponential potential,
this has already been done; see
[
53
]
and references cited therein. Under suitable assumptions on the potential, in particular that it has a
strictly positive lower bound,
we derive detailed asymptotics for generic Bianchi class A solutions in Proposition
1.119
. However, we exclude Bianchi
type IX solutions, since such solutions can both expand and contract in the future direction, and isolating the boundary between the two possibilities
is a subtle issue we do not sort out in this article. Moreover, we prove that the corresponding spatially locally homogeneous and spatially closed
solutions are future globally non-linearly stable in Corollary
1.120
. In Proposition
1.124
,
we carry out an analogous analysis in the
k=-1
FLRW setting.

In Section
1.10
we give a motivation of why we focus on Bianchi class A solutions. Finally, in Section
1.11
,
we give an outline of the article. Section
1.12
contains the acknowledgements.

1.1
Regular initial data

In the present article, we mainly restrict our attention to solutions arising from the following type of initial data.

Definition 1.1
.

Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
,
with potential
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, consist of the
following: a connected
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group
G
; a left invariant metric
\bar{g}
on
G
; a left invariant symmetric covariant

2
-tensor field
\bar{k}
on
G
; and two constants
\bar{\phi}_{0}
and
\bar{\phi}_{1}
satisfying the
constraint equations

\displaystyle\bar{S}-|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k})^{2}=

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}+2V(\bar{\phi}_{0}),

(1.2a)

\displaystyle d\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}=

\displaystyle 0

(1.2b)

(these equations are also referred to as the
Hamiltonian
and
momentum constraint equations
respectively).
Here
\bar{S}
denotes the scalar curvature associated with
\bar{g}
. The data are said to be
trivial

if
\bar{g}
is flat,
3\bar{k}=(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k})\bar{g}
,
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
and
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
.

Remark 1.2
.

In order to define the notion of unimodularity, let
G
be a Lie group and
\mathfrak{g}
the associated Lie algebra. Given
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, define

\mathrm{ad}_{X}:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathrm{ad}_{X}(Y)=[X,Y]
. Let
\eta_{G}\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be defined by
\eta_{G}(X)=\mathrm{tr}\ \mathrm{ad}_{X}
.
Then
G
is
unimodular

if
\eta_{G}=0
and
non-unimodular

if
\eta_{G}\neq 0
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group,
\mathfrak{g}
be the corresponding Lie algebra and
\{e_{i}\}
be a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
. Define
\gamma^{k}_{ij}

by the requirement that
[e_{i},e_{j}]=\gamma^{k}_{ij}e_{k}
. The condition of unimodularity implies that there is a unique symmetric matrix
\bar{n}
with
components
\bar{n}^{ij}
such that
\gamma_{ij}^{k}=\epsilon_{ijl}\bar{n}^{lk}
, where
\epsilon_{123}=1
and
\epsilon_{ijk}
is antisymmetric in all of its indices; see
[
34
]

for original references and, e.g.,
[
54
, Lemma 19.3, p. 206]
for a textbook presentation. It is convenient to introduce the following
terminology.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
G
be a connected
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group and
\{e_{i}\}
be a basis of the corresponding Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
\bar{n}
, defined
above, is called the
commutator matrix

associated with
\{e_{i}\}
. If, in addition,
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})

are Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations and
\{e_{i}\}
is orthonormal with respect to
\bar{g}
, then the
matrix
K
with components
\bar{k}(e_{i},e_{j})
is called the
Weingarten matrix

associated with
\{e_{i}\}
.

Remark 1.4
.

Under the assumptions of the definition, in particular, the assumption that
\{e_{i}\}
is orthonormal with respect to
\bar{g}
, (
1.2
)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle-\mathrm{tr}\bar{n}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}\bar{n})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}K^{2}+(\mathrm{tr}K)^{2}=

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}+2V(\bar{\phi}_{0}),

(1.3a)

\displaystyle K\bar{n}-\bar{n}K=

\displaystyle 0.

(1.3b)

This statement follows from
[
54
, Lemma 19.13, p. 210]
.

Changing basis, if necessary, it can be ensured that
\bar{n}
is diagonal and has diagonal elements
\bar{n}_{i}
of exactly one of the forms listed in
Table
1.1
. Moreover, this table yields a classification of the Lie algebras of
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie groups. We refer
the interested reader to
[
54
, Lemma 19.8, p. 208]
for a justification of these statements. For a discussion of corresponding simply
connected Lie groups, see the proof of Lemma
A.4
below.

Table 1.1:
Bianchi class A.

Type

\tilde{G}

+

0

\mathrm{I}_{0}

-

\mathrm{Sol}

Definition 1.5
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group. Then, using the notation introduced prior to the statement of the definition,
G
is said to
be of
Bianchi type

\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II},\mathrm{VI}_{0},\mathrm{VII}_{0},\allowbreak\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
if there is a frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
the diagonal elements of
\bar{n}
have the corresponding signs indicated in Table
1.1
.

Beyond the left invariance inherent in Definition
1.1
, some initial data have additional symmetries.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
be Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations. If the
Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}
is a multiple of
\bar{g}
and if
\bar{k}
is a multiple of
\bar{g}
, then the initial data are said to be
isotropic
.

Remark 1.7
.

Isotropic Bianchi type VII
0
initial data are of Bianchi type I; see Lemma
A.6
below. For this reason, we do not consider
such data in what follows.

Next, we introduce the notion of local rotational symmetry.

Definition 1.8
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
be Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations. Then the
data are said to be
locally rotationally symmetric

or
LRS

if they are not isotropic and if there is a family of Lie algebra isomorphisms

\Psi_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such that

\Psi_{t}e_{1}=e_{1},\ \ \ \Psi_{t}e_{2}=\cos(t)e_{2}+\sin(t)e_{3},\ \ \ \Psi_{t}e_{3}=-\sin(t)e_{2}+\cos(t)e_{3},

(1.4)

and such that for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the relation
\bar{k}(\Psi_{t}X,\Psi_{t}Y)=\bar{k}(X,Y)
holds.

Remark 1.9
.

In the simply connected setting, the
\Psi_{t}
arise from a family of Lie group isomorphisms, preserving the initial data; cf.

[
36
, Theorem 20.19, p. 530]
.

Remark 1.10
.

LRS Bianchi type VII
0
initial data are of Bianchi type I; see Lemma
A.6
below. For this reason, we do not consider
such data in what follows.

Local rotational symmetry appears in Bianchi types I, II, VII
0
, VIII and IX. However, there is a related symmetry in the case of Bianchi type
VI
0
.

Definition 1.11
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
be Bianchi type VI
0
initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations.
Then the data are said to be
permutation symmetric

if there is a Lie algebra isomorphism
\Psi
and an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to

\bar{g}
such that

\Psi e_{1}=e_{1},\ \ \ \Psi e_{2}=e_{3},\ \ \ \Psi e_{3}=e_{2}

(1.5)

and such that for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{g}
, the relation
\bar{k}(\Psi X,\Psi Y)=\bar{k}(X,Y)
holds.

It is convenient to introduce notation for the different types of initial data

Definition 1.12
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\mathcal{B}[V]

denotes the set of all Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations with
potential
V
which are non-trivial and are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type VII
0
. The corresponding
subset of simply connected initial data is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}[V]
.
If
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
is such that the Lie group of
\mathfrak{I}
is of Bianchi
type
\mathfrak{T}
, then the initial data
\mathfrak{I}
are said to be of
Bianchi type

\mathfrak{T}
.
The set of
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
of Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
is denoted

\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
.
The set
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]

is defined similarly. In all of these definitions, the argument
[V]
is omitted in case the potential
V

is clear from the context.

Remark 1.13
.

Consider trivial initial data
\mathfrak{I}
; see Definition
1.1
. Then (
1.2a
) implies that

V(\bar{\phi}_{0})=(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k})^{2}/3\geq 0
. If
\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}=0
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are initial data for a quotient of Minkowski space.
If
\pm\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}>0
, then
\mathfrak{I}
give rise to a development
(M_{\mathrm{dS}},g_{\mathrm{dS}})
, where
M_{\mathrm{dS}}=G_{0}\times\mathbb{R}
,
G_{0}
is
a Bianchi type I Lie group, and

g_{\mathrm{dS}}=-dt\otimes dt+e^{\pm 2Ht}\bar{g}_{0}.

(1.6)

Here
\bar{g}_{0}
is a flat metric on
G_{0}
and
H=[V(\bar{\phi}_{0})/3]^{1/2}
. In particular,
(M_{\mathrm{dS}},g_{\mathrm{dS}})
is a quotient of a part of de Sitter space.
Moreover, it is not a particularly natural model for the asymptotics in the contracting direction (it is, e.g., unstable in the contracting
direction). To summarise: Minkowski space is not interesting in a cosmological setting, and
(M_{\mathrm{dS}},g_{\mathrm{dS}})
is an unnatural model when analysing
the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity. Moreover, due to their additional symmetry, the corresponding initial data sets cause problems
in the regularity analysis of the set of isometry classes of initial data. For these reasons, we exclude trivial initial data. On the other, it should
be mentioned that
(M_{\mathrm{dS}},g_{\mathrm{dS}})
is important in the study of inflation, which takes place in intermediate regimes distinct from the singularity;
cf., e.g.,
[
4
]
.

In order for the sets of isometry classes of initial data to be smooth manifolds, it is necessary to focus on one symmetry class at a time. It is
therefore important to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.14
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
. The sets of isotropic, permutation symmetric and LRS elements of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]

are denoted
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
,

\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{per}}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]

and
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{LRS}}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]

respectively. The set of elements of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
which are
neither isotropic, permutation symmetric nor LRS
is denoted
\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{gen}}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
.
For
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

denotes the set of simply connected elements
of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Moreover,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

denotes the set of isometry classes of elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Remark 1.15
.

When we write
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
etc., we take it for granted that the Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
is such that the symmetry
type
\mathfrak{s}
makes sense; e.g., if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
, then
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
.

Remark 1.16
.

If
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the sets
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
can be parametrised by smooth manifolds; see
Lemma
2.15
and Remark
2.16
. In what follows we therefore assume these sets
to have been endowed with the corresponding smooth structures.

1.2
Data on the singularity

Next, we introduce a geometric notion of initial data on a singularity analogous to but more general than that given in

[
57
, Definition 10, p. 1510]
.

Definition 1.17
.

Let
G
be a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group,
\mathscr{H}
be a left invariant Riemannian metric on
G
,
\mathscr{K}
be a left invariant

(1,1)
-tensor field on
G
and
(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
are

quiescent Bianchi class A initial data on the singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations

if

1.

\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}=1
and
\mathscr{K}
is symmetric with respect to
\mathscr{H}
.

2.

\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}=1
and
\mathrm{div}_{\mathscr{H}}\mathscr{K}=0
.

3.

In case all the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are
<1
and there is one eigenvalue, say
p_{A}
, satisfying
p_{A}\leq 0
, then the vector subspace of

\mathfrak{g}
, say
\mathfrak{h}
, perpendicular to the eigenspace of
p_{A}
is a subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}
.

4.

If
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
, there is an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that

\mathscr{K}e_{1}=e_{1}
and such that if
\Psi_{t}
is defined by (
1.4
), then
\Psi_{t}
is a Lie algebra isomorphism
for all
t
.

Remark 1.18
.

The eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are constants due to the left invariance of
\mathscr{K}
.

Remark 1.19
.

If all the eigenvalues, say
p_{A}
, are
<1
and there is one eigenvalue, say
p_{1}
, satisfying
p_{1}\leq 0
, then, due to the requirement that
p_{A}<1

and the fact that the
p_{A}
sum up to
1
, it follows that
p_{2},p_{3}>0
, so that the eigenspace corresponding to
p_{1}
is
1
-dimensional and
\mathfrak{h}
is

2
-dimensional.

Definition 1.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},\mathscr{H}_{\alpha},\mathscr{K}_{\alpha},\Phi_{\alpha,1},\Phi_{\alpha,0})
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, be quiescent Bianchi class A initial data on the singularity for the
Einstein non-linear scalar field equations. Then
\chi:G_{0}\rightarrow G_{1}
is said to be an
isometry

from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
if
\chi

is a diffeomorphism such that

\chi^{*}\mathscr{H}_{1}=\mathscr{H}_{0},\ \ \ \chi^{*}\mathscr{K}_{1}=\mathscr{K}_{0},\ \ \ \chi^{*}\Phi_{1,0}=\Phi_{0,0},\ \ \ \chi^{*}\Phi_{1,1}=\Phi_{0,1}.

Here
\chi^{*}\mathscr{K}_{1}:=(\chi^{-1})_{*}\circ\mathscr{K}_{1}\circ\chi_{*}
.

It is of interest to introduce terminology for symmetry types of data on the singularity.

Definition 1.21
.

Let
(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
be quiescent Bianchi class A initial data on the singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations.
If
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
and
\mathscr{H}
has constant curvature, then the initial data on the singularity are said to be
isotropic
.
If the initial
data on the singularity are not isotropic and if there is a family of Lie algebra isomorphisms
\Psi_{t}
,

t\in\mathbb{R}
, and an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that (
1.4
) and
\mathscr{K}\Psi_{t}=\Psi_{t}\mathscr{K}

both hold for all
t
, then the initial data on the singularity are said to be
locally rotationally symmetric

or
LRS
.
If
G
is of Bianchi type VI
0
and if there is a Lie algebra isomorphism
\Psi
and an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with
respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that (
1.5
) and
\mathscr{K}\Psi=\Psi\mathscr{K}
hold, then the initial data on the singularity are said to
have a
permutation symmetry
.

Remark 1.22
.

That all the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
coincide is equivalent to
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
, since
\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}=1
.

Remark 1.23
.

If
\mathscr{K}e_{1}=e_{1}
(where
e_{1}
is a unit vector), then the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are
1
,
0
and
0
. This means that
\mathscr{K}v=0
for

v\perp e_{1}
. If
e_{A}
,
A=2,3
, are orthonormal vectors perpendicular to
e_{1}
, this means that
\Psi_{t}
defined by (
1.4
) is
a family of isometries such that
\Psi_{t}\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}\Psi_{t}
for all
t
. Assuming, additionally, (as we do in case
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
)
that
\Psi_{t}
is a Lie algebra isomorphism for all
t
, this means that the data on the singularity are LRS. By Theorem
1.45

below, it follows that the development is LRS.

Remark 1.24
.

Isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
initial data on the singularity are of Bianchi type I; see Lemma
A.7
below. For
this reason, we do not consider such data in what follows.

Definition 1.25
.

The set of quiescent Bianchi class A initial data on the singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations which are neither of
isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type VII
0
is denoted
\mathcal{S}
.
The corresponding set of simply connected initial data on the singularity is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}
.
Given a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
, the elements of
\mathcal{S}
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}
of Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
are denoted
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}

and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}

respectively.
The sets of isotropic, permutation symmetric and LRS elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
are denoted
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
,

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{per}}_{\mathfrak{T}}

and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{LRS}}_{\mathfrak{T}}

respectively. Finally, the set of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
which are neither isotropic, permutation symmetric nor LRS
is denoted
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{gen}}_{\mathfrak{T}}
.
For
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}

denotes the set of simply connected elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Finally,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}

denotes the set of isometry classes of elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Remark 1.26
.

Remark
1.15
is equally relevant here.

Remark 1.27
.

The sets
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
can be parametrised by smooth manifolds; see Remark
2.39
.
In what follows we therefore assume these sets to have been endowed with the corresponding smooth structures.

1.3
Developments

Due to general results, we know that there is a unique maximal globally hyperbolic development associated with data as
in Definition
1.1
; see, e.g.,
[
55
, Lemma 23.2, p. 398]
and
[
55
, Corollary 23.44, p. 418]
. However, for the
purposes of the present discussion, it is convenient to work with a specific representation.

Definition 1.28
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{h},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}[V]
. A
Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development of

\mathfrak{I}
is a triple
(M,g,\phi)
, where
M:=G\times J
,
J
is an open interval,
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J)
,

g=-dt\otimes dt+\bar{g},

(1.7)

and
\bar{g}
denotes a family of left invariant Riemannian metrics on
G
. Finally,
(M,g,\phi)
is required to satisfy the Einstein non-linear
scalar field equations, and for some
t_{0}\in J
, the initial data induced on
G_{t_{0}}:=G\times\{t_{0}\}
by
(M,g,\phi)
and pulled back by

\iota_{0}
are required to equal
\mathfrak{I}
, where
\iota_{0}(p):=(p,t_{0})
.

In case the left endpoint of
J
is
t_{-}
(
t_{-}
will always be assumed to equal
0
if
t_{-}>-\infty
) and
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as

t\downarrow t_{-}
(where
\theta(t)
denotes the mean curvature of
G_{t}
with respect to
g
), then the development is said to have a

crushing singularity
.

If
(M,g,\phi)
is a Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development of isotropic/LRS/permutation symmetric initial data, then
(M,g,\phi)
is
said to be an
isotropic/LRS/permutation symmetric development
.

Remark 1.29
.

In (
1.7
)
t
, by standard abuse of notation, denotes the time coordinate (mapping
(\bar{x},s)\in M
to
s
) as well as the corresponding
element of
J
.

Remark 1.30
.

We assign a Bianchi type to a Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development according to the Bianchi type of
G
; see
Definition
1.5
.

Given regular initial data, there is, up to time translation, a unique corresponding maximal development.

Proposition 1.31
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
. Then there is a unique (up to time translation) Bianchi class
A non-linear scalar field development of
\mathfrak{I}
, say
(M,g,\phi)
with
M=G\times J
and
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
, which is maximal in the sense that
either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|+|\phi|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
, where
\theta
denotes the mean curvature of the constant-
t

hypersurfaces. Moreover, this development is globally hyperbolic. Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi type
consistent with
\mathfrak{s}
. If
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and
t\in J
, then the initial data induced on
G_{t}
by
(M,g,\phi)
also belong to

\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Remark 1.32
.

In case
V\geq 0
, the development is maximal in the sense that either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
.

Remark 1.33
.

In most of this article, we are interested in developments with
t_{-}>-\infty
. In this case, we fix the time translation ambiguity by
demanding that
t_{-}=0
.

Proof.

See Subsection
3.2
below.
∎

Definition 1.34
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
. Then the unique development obtained in Proposition
1.31
is
denoted
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})

The following observation will be of interest.

Proposition 1.35
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
,
\alpha=0,1
. Assume
\chi:G_{0}\rightarrow G_{1}
to be an isometry from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
. Fix the time translation
ambiguity in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})
by demanding that the intial data
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}
be induced at the
t_{0}
hypersurface, for some fixed
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry from
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
to
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
(in particular, the existence interval is the same for the two
developments).

Remark 1.36
.

Under the assumptions of the proposition,
\chi
gives rise to an isometry between the initial data sets induced on
\{t\}\times G_{0}
and

\{t\}\times G_{1}
for any
t
in the existence interval.

Remark 1.37
.

If
\Gamma
is a free and properly discontinuous subgroup of the isometry group of an initial data set, then the proposition implies that

\Gamma\times\mathrm{Id}
is a free and properly discontinuous subgroup of the isometry group of the corresponding development. Taking the
quotient yields a spatially locally homogeneous development of the initial data induced on the quotient of the initial manifold with
\Gamma
.

Proof.

The proof can be found just above the statement of Lemma
A.11
.
∎

1.3.1
Developments inducing data on the singularity

In order to relate developments to data on the singularity, we first define what it means for a development to induce data on the singularity.

Definition 1.38
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
and
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
. Assume
(M,g,\phi)
to have a crushing singularity. Using the terminology
of Definition
1.28
, let
\bar{g}
and
K
be the metrics and Weingarten maps induced on
G_{t}
,
t\in J
, and define the

expansion normalised Weingarten map

by
\mathcal{K}:=K/\theta
. If there are
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
,

\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\rightarrow\Phi_{1}
and
\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta\rightarrow\Phi_{0}
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
; and such that the following
limit holds for all
v,w\in\mathfrak{g}
:

\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}v,\theta^{\mathcal{K}}w)=\mathscr{H}(v,w),

(1.8)

then the development
(M,g,\phi)
is said to
induce
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity
.

Remark 1.39
.

One way to define the Weingarten map is as the second fundamental form with one index raised by the induced metric.

Remark 1.40
.

When we say that
\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
, we consider
\mathcal{K}
to be a family of tensor fields, indexed by
t
, on the fixed manifold
G
, and
we mean that this family converges pointwise with respect to a fixed reference metric on
G
.

Remark 1.41
.

Under the assumptions of the definition,
t_{-}>-\infty
, so that we can assume
t_{-}=0
; see the proof of Theorem
1.45

in Section
9.1
.

Remark 1.42
.

In (
1.8
), it is understood that

\theta^{\mathcal{K}}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{l=0}^{\infty}\tfrac{1}{l!}(\ln\theta)^{l}\mathcal{K}^{l}.

Lemma 1.43
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
1.35
, if
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
induces
\mathfrak{I}_{1,\infty}
on the singularity,
then
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
also induces initial data, say
\mathfrak{I}_{0,\infty}
, on the singularity. Moreover,
\chi
is an isometry from

\mathfrak{I}_{0,\infty}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{1,\infty}
; cf. Definition
1.20
.

Proof.

Due to the conclusions of Proposition
1.35
,
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry from
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
to
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
.
Denote the metric and scalar field of
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})
by
g_{\alpha}
and
\phi_{\alpha}
respectively. Denote the mean curvature of
G_{\alpha}\times\{t\}
by

\theta_{\alpha}(t)
. Since
\theta_{\alpha}
is independent of the spatial coordinate, and since
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry, we have to have

\theta_{1}=\theta_{0}
. Next, since the scalar fields are independent of the spatial coordinate, the form of the isometry implies that

\phi_{0}=\phi_{1}
. Since
\theta_{1}=\theta_{0}
and
\phi_{1}=\phi_{0}
, it follows that the scalar fields induce the same data on the singularity.
It remains to consider the limits for the geometry. Let
\mathcal{K}_{\alpha}
denote the expansion normalised Weingarten map of the constant-
t
hypersurfaces
in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})
. Then

\mathscr{K}_{0}=\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\mathcal{K}_{0}=\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}(\chi^{-1})_{*}\circ\mathcal{K}_{1}\circ\chi_{*}=(\chi^{-1})_{*}\circ\mathscr{K}_{1}\circ\chi_{*}.

This yields the desired statement for the limit of the expansion normalised Weingarten map. Finally

\begin{split}\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\bar{g}_{0}(\theta_{0}^{\mathcal{K}_{0}}v,\theta_{0}^{\mathcal{K}_{0}}w)=&\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\bar{g}_{0}\big{(}(\chi^{-1})_{*}\theta_{1}^{\mathcal{K}_{1}}\chi_{*}v,(\chi^{-1})_{*}\theta_{1}^{\mathcal{K}_{1}}\chi_{*}w\big{)}\\
=&\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\bar{g}_{1}\big{(}\theta_{1}^{\mathcal{K}_{1}}\chi_{*}v,\theta_{1}^{\mathcal{K}_{1}}\chi_{*}w\big{)}=\mathscr{H}_{1}(\chi_{*}v,\chi_{*}w)=\chi^{*}\mathscr{H}_{1}(v,w).\end{split}

Thus
\mathscr{H}_{0}=\chi^{*}\mathscr{H}_{1}
. The lemma follows.
∎

In order to obtain results, we typically need to impose conditions on the potential. They can often be phrased in terms of the following terminology.

Definition 1.44
.

Let
\alpha_{V}\in[0,\infty)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the set of
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that there is a constant
c_{k}<\infty
with the property that

\textstyle{\sum}_{l=0}^{k}|V^{(l)}(s)|\leq c_{k}e^{\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|s|}

(1.9)

for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
is denoted
\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{k}
.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}:=\cap_{l=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{l}
.

The next result states that there is a unique development corresponding to data on the singularity. An analogous result in the Einstein scalar
field setting can be found in
[
57
, Theorem 16, p. 1513]
. A much more general result, in the
absence of symmetries
, is
contained in
[
23
, Theorem 1.9]
and
[
23
, Theorem 1.19]
; see also
[
5
,
33
,
22
]
. However, the assumptions in

[
5
,
33
,
22
,
23
]
are such that the following theorem is not a special case of the results of
[
5
,
33
,
22
,
23
]
.

Theorem 1.45
.

Let
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
; see Definition
1.44
. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
and

\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}:=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Then there is a unique
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
,
arising from
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
, inducing
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and if
J
is the existence interval of
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
, the left endpoint of
J
can be assumed to equal
0
.
Finally, if
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
satisfy all the conditions of Definition
1.17
but the last two;
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
;
and the final condition of Definition
1.17
is violated, then there is no Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development
inducing
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity.

Remark 1.46
.

It is only necessary to impose the condition
V\geq 0
in case
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
; i.e., if the last condition of
Definition
1.17
is satisfied.

Remark 1.47
.

If
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are isotropic, the metric of the development takes the form

g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\mathscr{H},

(1.10)

where
a
is a smooth and strictly positive function on the existence interval.
If
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are LRS, there is an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
, consisting of eigenvectors of

\mathscr{K}
(with eigenvalue
p_{i}
corresponding to
e_{i}
) such that the associated commutator matrix is diagonal with diagonal components
o_{i}
; the

o_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
;
p_{2}=p_{3}
;
o_{2}=o_{3}
; and either
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
or
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
. Moreover, if
\{\xi^{i}\}
is
the frame dual to
\{e_{i}\}
, then the metric takes the form

g=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\xi^{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(t)[\xi^{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+\xi^{3}\otimes\xi^{3}],

(1.11)

where the
a_{i}
are smooth and strictly positive functions on the existence interval.
Moreover, this statement holds irrespective of the choice of frame
\{e_{i}\}
with the above properties. In case
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are permutation
symmetric, a similar statement holds. The only difference concerning the frame is that
o_{2}=-o_{3}
and that
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
is automatically satisfied.
The conclusion concerning the form of the metric is the same.

Proof.

See Section
9.1
for a proof of both the theorem and of Remark
1.47
.
∎

Combining this result with Lemma
1.43
and Remark
A.9
yields the following
observation.

Lemma 1.48
.

Let
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
.
Assume that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces data
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity. Then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Moreover, if
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
is isometric with
\mathfrak{I}
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
;
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
induces data
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
on the
singularity;
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
; and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
are isometric. In particular, there is a well defined map
from elements of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
whose developments induce data on the singularity to
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Remark 1.49
.

If one is prepared to exclude initial data on the singularity satisfying the last condition of Definition
1.17
, then
the requirement
V\geq 0
can be omitted.

Proof.

The assumption that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces data
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
means that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}
for some symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
; see
Definition
1.38
. Then, due to Theorem
1.45
, there is a unique
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
, arising from
some
\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}_{1}}[V]
, inducing
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity. In particular,
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
. However, due to
Proposition
1.31
, the symmetry type is preserved by the evolution. This means that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}=\mathfrak{s}
. The first statement
of the lemma follows. The second statement is an immediate consequence of the first statement, Lemma
1.43
and
Remark
A.9
.
∎

In analogy with Proposition
1.35
, the following statement holds.

Proposition 1.50
.

Let
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
; see Definition
1.44
. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
and

\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,\alpha}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
. If

\chi
is an isometry from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
, see Definition
1.20
, then
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an
isometry of the corresponding developments obtained in Theorem
1.45
.

Remark 1.51
.

Remark
1.46
is equally relevant here.

Remark 1.52
.

As a consequence of the statement, isometry classes of data on the singularity induce isometry classes of developments.

Proof.

The proof is to be found at the end of Appendix
A
.
∎

The asymptotics in Theorem
1.45
are the best one can hope for, in the sense that they uniquely determine
the corresponding development. They therefore set the standard when deriving asymptotics in what follows.

1.3.2
The Bianchi type IX setting

The Bianchi type IX setting is complicated by the fact that the set of developments include de Sitter space, which expands both to the future and
to the past; non-vacuum solutions to the Einstein scalar field equations which start with a big bang and end with a big crunch, both of which are
quiescent; Bianchi type IX vacuum solutions with a big bang and a big crunch, both of which are oscillatory; solutions with a static geometry
(by balancing the matter content with a cosmological constant and the spatial scalar curvature; cf. the Einstein static universe);
solutions that have a quiescent big bang singularity but then expand in a de Sitter like fashion etc. Here, we are interested in solutions with a
crushing big bang singularity. This means that we have to exclude some sets of initial data. One way of doing so is to begin by noting that
(
1.2a
) can be written

\theta^{2}-\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}-3V(\bar{\phi}_{0})=\tfrac{3}{2}|\sigma|_{\bar{g}}^{2}-\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{S},

(1.12)

where
\sigma
is the trace free part of
\bar{k}
. Excluding isotropic Bianchi type I as well as Bianchi type IX initial data, the right hand side
of (
1.12
) is strictly positive. However, in the case of isotropic Bianchi type I, the right hand side is identically zero.
Moreover, in the case of Bianchi type IX, the right hand side can have any sign. However, it is worth noting that, e.g., in the case of de Sitter
space, the right hand side is strictly negative on all hypersurfaces of spatial homogeneity. Moreover, in the case of the Einstein static universe,
the right hand side is strictly negative and constant. One way to exclude these developments is thus to insist on initial data such that both the
mean curvature and the right hand side of (
1.12
) are strictly positive at some point in time (if these conditions are satisfied at
one point in time, then, due to Lemma
3.4
, they are satisfied for the entire past). While this is a useful criterion, it
does exclude some solutions one would like to include; e.g. isotropic solutions with a quiescent big bang singularity. A different way to proceed
is to demand that the initial data be such that the development has the following properties:
\theta>0
in a neighbourhood of
t_{-}
(this condition
excludes both de Sitter space and the Einstein static universe); and for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a
T
in the existence interval such that

X(t)/\theta^{2}(t)>-\epsilon
and
|V\circ\phi(t)|/\theta^{2}(t)<\epsilon
for all
t\leq T
, where
X
denotes the right hand side of (
1.12
). Since
we are interested in crushing singularities,
the assumption that
\theta(t)>0
in a neighbourhood of the singularity is quite reasonable. Even in the oscillatory Bianchi type IX vacuum setting, the
positive part of
\bar{S}/\theta^{2}
converges to zero. The lower bound on
X/\theta^{2}
is therefore quite natural. Finally, we are interested in situations
such that the contribution of the potential is asymptotically negligible in the direction of the crushing singularity. For that reason, the assumption
that
V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
converges to zero is reasonable. In the end, assuming suitable conditions on the potential, the first condition (i.e., that

X
and
\theta
be positive at one point in time) implies the second. However, proving that this is the case is non-trivial, and we therefore
introduce both conditions here.

Before stating the formal definitions, it is convenient to introduce the following notation, given
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
:

\mathfrak{X}(\theta,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1}):=\theta^{2}-\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}-3V(\bar{\phi}_{0}).

(1.13)

Definition 1.53
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}[V]
. Let
J
and
\theta
be the existence interval and mean curvature,
respectively, of the corresponding development, see Proposition
1.31
. If there is a
t_{0}\in J
such that
\theta(t)>0

for all
t\leq t_{0}
; and if for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a
T\in J
such that
X(t)/\theta^{2}(t)>-\epsilon
and
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta^{2}(t)<\epsilon
for all

t\leq T
, where
X(t):=\mathfrak{X}[\theta(t),\phi(t),\phi_{t}(t)]
, see (
1.13
), then
\mathfrak{I}
are said to be
almost positive

initial data, written
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
.
Next,
\mathfrak{I}
are said to be
positive

initial data if there is a
t_{0}\in J
such that
X(t_{0})>0
and
\theta(t_{0})>0
, written
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]
.
Finally
\mathfrak{I}
are said to be
pseudo positive

if they are either positive or almost positive, written
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
.
For
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
,

\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]:=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}[V]\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V],\ \ \ {}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]:=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}[V]\cap{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V].

Finally,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

denotes the set of isometry classes of elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Remark 1.54
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
, so that

\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
; see Corollary
4.12
.

Due to Lemmas
3.4
and
3.6
, if
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
, then there is a
t_{1}\in J
such
that
\theta(t)>0
and
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for
t\leq t_{1}
. Developments corresponding to initial data in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
therefore arise from the
following class of initial data.

Definition 1.55
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
,
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Let
J
and

\theta
be the existence interval and mean curvature, respectively, of the corresponding development, see Proposition
1.31
.
Let, moreover,
t_{0}
be as in Definition
1.28
. If
\theta(t_{0})>0
and
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
, then

\mathfrak{I}
are said to be
non-degenerate
,
written
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]
.
The corresponding set of simply connected initial data sets is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]
,
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]

the isometry classes thereof.

The reason we introduce the notion of non-degenerate initial data is that it plays an important role in the definition of a smooth structure on
the set of isometry classes of developments in the Bianchi IX setting.

1.3.3
Isometry classes of developments

Next, it is convenient to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.56
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
. Then the set of isometry
classes of developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
arising from
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with strictly positive mean curvature
is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.
If
V\geq 0
and
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
, the set of isometry classes of developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
for

\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.
The set of isometry classes of developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
for
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
with a
crushing singularity is denoted
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
.

1.4
Parametrising isometry classes of developments

We introduce notation for isometry classes of developments in Definition
1.56
. In order to discuss the regularity of the map from isometry
classes of developments to isometry classes of data on the singularity, we next note that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
can be given the structure of a
smooth manifold. The relevant smooth structure arises from the natural smooth structures on sets of isometry classes of initial data with fixed mean
curvature. We therefore first introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.57
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
,
\vartheta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
. Then

\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
,

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

denote the subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with mean curvature
\vartheta_{0}

respectively. Similarly, if
\vartheta_{0}>0
and
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
,

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

denote the subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]
and

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V]
with mean curvature
\vartheta_{0}
respectively. In what follows it is also
convenient to introduce the notation
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

for the subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
, respectively, with
\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0
.

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

are the corresponding subsets with mean curvature
\vartheta_{0}
.

Remark 1.58
.

The sets
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
can be parametrised by smooth manifolds;
see Remark
2.20
. If
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
and
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, then the sets

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
can be parametrised by smooth manifolds, see Remark
2.20
and
Lemma
8.20
. From now on, we tacitly assume that the above sets have been endowed with the corresponding smooth structures.

In case
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
,
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, developments of initial data
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
are such
that
\theta_{t}<0
; cf. Lemma
3.4
. Moreover, an element of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
uniquely
determines an isometry class of developments; cf. Proposition
1.35
. These basic observations can be improved to yield the
following conclusion.

Lemma 1.59
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\vartheta\in I_{V}:=([3\inf_{s}V(s)]^{1/2},\infty)
and fix a
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})
such that

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Then the map taking

\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
to
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces an injective map

\iota_{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V].

(1.14)

Moreover,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
can be endowed with a topology and a smooth structure such that the following holds: the image of

\iota_{\vartheta}
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
for all
\vartheta\in I_{V}
;
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image
for all
\vartheta\in I_{V}
; and the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
equals
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
8.2
.
∎

In the case of Bianchi type IX, the following holds.

Lemma 1.60
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
;
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
; and
0<\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the map taking

\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
to
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces an injective map

\iota_{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V].

(1.15)

Moreover,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
can be endowed with a topology and a smooth structure such that the following holds: the image of

\iota_{\vartheta}
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
for all
\vartheta>0
;
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image
for all
\vartheta>0
; and the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
equals
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Remark 1.61
.

In case
\mathfrak{t}=+
, it is not necessary to assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, it is sufficient to assume that
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
8.2
.
∎

The case of isotropic Bianchi type I solutions is slightly more complicated. This is due to the existence of solutions whose metrics asymptote to
metrics of the form (
1.6
). Depending on the potential, there could in principle be infinitely many distinct solution with asymptotics of
this type. None of the corresponding solutions have a crushing singularity. On the other hand, in order for this to happen, the potential has to
be quite exotic;
V^{\prime}
has to have infinitely many distinct zeros with infinitely many distinct values of
V
. In the case of isotropic Bianchi type I
solutions, we, for these reasons, consider a more restricted class of potentials.

Definition 1.62
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. If
V(s)
converges to a finite number as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
, denote the limit by
v_{\infty,\pm}
. If
V(s)
does
not converge to a finite number as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
, let
v_{\infty,\pm}:=0
. Define

v_{\max}(V):=\sup(\{v_{\infty,+},v_{\infty,-}\}\cup\{V(s_{0})\,|\,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R},\,V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0\}).

Let
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}

denote the set of
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
V(s)\geq 0
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
;
V^{\prime}
is bounded on every interval on which
V

is bounded;
V^{\prime}(s)
tends to a limit (finite or infinite) as
s\rightarrow\infty
and as
s\rightarrow-\infty
; and
v_{\max}(V)<\infty
.

Remark 1.63
.

The set
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
includes, e.g., the following three classes of potentials: non-negative polynomials; non-negative smooth functions such that

V>0
outside a compact set and such that
V^{\prime}/V
converges to a non-zero limit as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
; bounded non-negative smooth functions such
that
V^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow 0
as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
.

Under these additional assumptions on the potential, it is sufficient to use one fixed mean curvature to parametrise isometry classes of developments.
In fact, the following holds.

Lemma 1.64
.

Assume that the conditions of Lemma
1.59
hold. Assume, in addition, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and that

\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Then
\iota_{\vartheta}
introduced in (
1.14
) is surjective. In particular,

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
are diffeomorphic.

Proof.

The surjectivity is an immediate consequence of Lemmas
1.59
,
3.4
and

3.8
.
∎

Finally, we can state the result analogous to Lemmas
1.59
and
1.60
in the case of isotropic
Bianchi type I solutions.

Lemma 1.65
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and
\vartheta\in I_{V}:=([3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2},\infty)
. Then the map taking
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)

to
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces an injective map

\iota_{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V].

(1.16)

Moreover,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
can be endowed with a topology and a smooth structure such that the following holds: the image of

\iota_{\vartheta}
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
for all
