\vartheta\in I_{V}
;
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a diffeomorphism onto its
image for all
\vartheta\in I_{V}
; and the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
equals
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
.

Remark 1.66
.

In contrast to Lemmas
1.59
and
1.60
, we here restrict our attention to initial data with

\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0
; see Definition
1.57
. The reason for this is the following. The topology and smooth structure on

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
are obtained by taking a quotient of the disjoint union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
. For this construction
to lead to a smooth manifold, we need to verify that the map relating the different images (generated by the Einstein flow) is a local diffeomorphism.
To this end, it is convenient to know that
\theta_{t}\neq 0
at the constant-
t
hypersurfaces to be identified. This is the main purpose served
by the non degeneracy condition that
\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
8.2
.
∎

Unfortunately, we do not obtain a result analogous to Lemma
1.64
in the case of isotropic Bianchi type I
solutions. Nevertheless, the following holds.

Lemma 1.67
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and
\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
is homeomorphic to

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
.

Proof.

The proof can be found in Section
8.2
.
∎

Remark 1.68
.

Due to Lemma
2.25
and Remark
2.20
it follows that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
has
one of three possible topologies:
two disjoint copies of
\mathbb{R}
;
\mathbb{R}
; and
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. On the other hand, due to Section
2.3
, it is clear that

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is diffeomorphic to two copies of
\mathbb{R}
. In general, there can therefore not be a diffeomorphism from

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
. In particular, this mere topological observation provides information
concerning the dynamics.

1.5
The map from isometry classes of developments to isometry classes
of initial data on the singularity

Before turning to the regularity of the map from developments to data on the singularity, it is convenient to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.69
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and fix a
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})
such that
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
.
Let
\vartheta\in I_{V}:=([3\inf_{s}V(s)]^{1/2},\infty)
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)

denote the isometry classes of the elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
whose developments induce data on the singularity.
Due to Lemma
1.48
, there is then a well defined map

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}.

(1.17)

With this terminology, the following holds.

Proposition 1.70
.

Assume that the conditions of Definition
1.69
are satisfied and that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and the map (
1.17
) is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Moreover,
using the notation introduced in Lemma
1.59
, if
\vartheta_{i}\in I_{V}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, and

\iota_{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})

(1.18)

for some
x_{i}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
.
Next, define

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]:=\bigcup_{\vartheta\in I_{V}}\iota_{\vartheta}\big{(}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)\big{)}

and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}

by

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}(\iota_{\vartheta}(x))=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}(x).

Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
is a diffeomorphism.

Remark 1.71
.

The statement that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
is a diffeomorphism means, in particular, that the range of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
is
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Note also that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is the set of all isometry classes of simply connected developments of Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}

and symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}
(corresponding to initial data with strictly positive mean curvature), corresponding to a potential
V
, that give
rise to data on the singularity.

Remark 1.72
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the proposition,
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and
\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}

is a diffeomorphim. This follows by combining the proposition with Lemma
1.64
.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Section
9.2
.
∎

In the case of Bianchi type IX, we have to impose additional conditions; cf. Lemma
1.60
.

Definition 1.73
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
;
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
;
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
; and

0<\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)

denote the isometry classes of
the elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
whose developments induce data on the singularity. Due to
Lemma
1.48
, there is then a well defined map

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}.

(1.19)

Proposition 1.74
.

Assume that the conditions of Definition
1.73
are satisfied and that, for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
,
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
.
Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)

and the map (
1.19
) is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Moreover, using the notation introduced in
Lemma
1.60
, if
\vartheta_{i}>0
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, and

\iota_{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})

(1.20)

for some
x_{i}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, then

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. Next, define

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]:=\bigcup_{\vartheta>0}\iota_{\vartheta}\big{(}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)\big{)}

and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
by

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}\big{(}\iota_{\vartheta}(x)\big{)}=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}(x).

Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}
is a diffeomorphism
onto its image (which is a diffeomorphism in case
\mathfrak{t}\in\{\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
).

Remark 1.75
.

An observation similar to Remark
1.71
holds in this case as well.

Remark 1.76
.

In case
\mathfrak{t}=+
, the condition on
\alpha_{V}
can be relaxed to
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Section
9.2
.
∎

Finally, in the isotropic Bianchi type I setting, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.77
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and let
\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Let
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)

denote the isometry classes of
the elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
whose developments induce data on the singularity. Due to
Lemma
1.48
, there is then a well defined map

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}.

(1.21)

Proposition 1.78
.

Assume that the conditions of Definition
1.77
are satisfied and that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
.
Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)

and the map (
1.21
) is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Moreover, using the notation introduced in
Lemma
1.65
, if
\vartheta_{i}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\vartheta_{1}<\vartheta_{2}
and

\iota_{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})

(1.22)

for some
x_{i}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{i})
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, then

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. Next, define

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]:=\bigcup_{\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}}\iota_{\vartheta}\big{(}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)\big{)}

and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
by

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}\big{(}\iota_{\vartheta}(x)\big{)}=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}(x).

Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
is a diffeomorphism.

Remark 1.79
.

An observation similar to Remark
1.71
holds in this case as well.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Section
9.2
.
∎

1.6
Asymptotics in the direction of the singularity, the general case

Our next goal is to derive the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity. In particular, we want to prove that solutions induce data on
the singularity in the sense of Definition
1.17
. However, this does not always occur. One exception is, e.g., the case of
vacuum Bianchi type VIII and IX solutions, where, by vacuum, we mean that the potential and the scalar field vanish identically. In this case,
the asymptotics are oscillatory, if one excludes LRS solutions; see

[
49
, Theorem 5, p. 727]
. On the other hand, in the case of the Einstein scalar field equations, there is a clear dichotomy: either the
solution is vacuum, or, if there is a non-trivial matter content, the matter dominates and gives rise to convergent behaviour; see

[
50
, Theorem 19.1, p. 478]
(keeping in mind that scalar field matter is equivalent to an orthogonal stiff perfect fluid).
The reason for this dichotomy is that in the Einstein scalar field case, there is a monotonicity ensuring that the expansion normalised energy
density increases in the direction of the singularity. Introducing a non-zero potential, this monotonicity is destroyed. This causes significant
complications, and these complications are, in part, the cause of the length of this article.

In the presence of a potential, there is still a dichotomy between matter and vacuum dominated asymptotic behaviour in the direction of the
singularity. However, the dichotomy is not characterised by initial data, but rather by the asymptotics. Moreover, in order to obtain the
dichotomy, we need to impose conditions on the potential. In what follows, we therefore assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
; see
Definition
1.44
. The restrictions on
\alpha_{V}
depend on the Bianchi type:
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in the case of anisotropic Bianchi type I
and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Note that we here, as always, exclude isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
.

Proposition 1.80
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type,
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
be non-negative. Assume that
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in
the case of anisotropic Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and that
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, assume
that
\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}\geq 0
; that
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
; and that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]
in case
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
. Let
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
.
Then the associated existence interval is of the form
(0,t_{+})
and
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
. Moreover, there are two
possibilities. Either there is a
t_{0}>0
and a
C\in\mathbb{R}
such that
|\theta(t)\phi_{t}(t)|\leq C
for all
t\leq t_{0}
; or
\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)

converges to a non-zero limit as
t\downarrow 0
.

Remark 1.81
.

It is natural to conjecture that the result holds for all
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. However, the assumption
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
is needed for our method of
proof to work for the higher Bianchi types.

Proof.

The statement follows from Lemma
3.4
and Theorem
4.13
, keeping the Wainwright-Hsu formulation of
Section
3.3
in mind.
∎

In addition, if
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
, then either Proposition
1.80
applies or the following conclusion holds.

Proposition 1.82
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
be such that
\mathfrak{I}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]
. Let
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
. Then

|\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)|
converges to
(2/3)^{1/2}
as
t\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

By assumption,
X(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0
and
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow 0
. The conclusion follows.
∎

With the above observations in mind, it is natural to introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.83
.

A Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development as in Proposition
1.80
or Proposition
1.82
is said to
be
matter dominated

if
\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)
converges to a non-zero limit as
t\downarrow 0
, and is said to be
vacuum dominated

otherwise.

Remark 1.84
.

It is natural to ask if only vacuum solution are vacuum dominated. However, this is not the case. In fact,
Theorem
1.45
applies with
\Phi_{1}=0
in the case of Bianchi types I, II, VI
0
, VII
0
and LRS Bianchi types
VIII and IX. The corresponding solutions are clearly not matter dominated. Moreover, in the case that the potential is such that there are no
constant solutions to the non-linear scalar field equation, and in the case that
\Phi_{0}
is such that
V^{\prime}(\Phi_{0})\neq 0
, we can clearly
conclude that the scalar field is non-constant, so that the solution is not a vacuum solution. In the case of Bianchi type VIII and IX solutions
which are neither isotropic nor LRS, all the solutions for which
\phi
is constant are vacuum dominated, but proving the existence of vacuum
dominated solutions for which
\phi
is non-constant is more difficult.

Next, we state the conclusions concerning the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity.

Theorem 1.85
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type,
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
be non-negative, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in case
of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Assume
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
and let

\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with
\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}\geq 0
. In case

\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
assume, in addition, that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Then the development
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces initial data on the singularity
unless it is vacuum dominated,
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
. Finally, if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
,
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
and

\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
is vacuum dominated, then the expansion normalised Weingarten map
\mathcal{K}
does not converge. In fact, the
\alpha
-limit set of the
eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}
contains two distinct points on the Kasner circle and the line connecting them. Moreover,
\bar{S}/\theta^{2}
does not converge
to zero.

Remark 1.86
.

Remark
1.81
is equally relevant in this case.

Remark 1.87
.

In the statement of the theorem, the
Kasner plane
, say
P_{K}
, is represented by the set of
q\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
satisfying
q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}=1
and
the
Kasner circle
is represented by the
q\in P_{K}
such that
q_{1}^{2}+q_{2}^{2}+q_{3}^{2}=1
. Moreover, by the
\alpha
-limit set of the eigenvalues
of
\mathcal{K}
, we mean the set of
q\in P_{K}
such that there is a sequence
t_{k}\downarrow 0
(where
t=0
represents the singularity; i.e.,

\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
) with the property that
[p_{1}(t_{k}),p_{2}(t_{k}),p_{3}(t_{k})]
converges to
q
. Here
p_{i}(t)
,
i=1,2,3
,
are the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{K}(t)
. However, we do not impose a specific order on the
p_{i}
’s, so that the
\alpha
-limit set is invariant under permutations.

Proof.

The statements concerning convergence follow from Propositions
6.1
,
6.2
,

6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
. The statement concerning oscillations follows
from Proposition
5.13
.
∎

Combining this observation with Proposition
1.70
yields the following conclusion.

Corollary 1.88
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type,
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
be non-negative, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in case
of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Assume that
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
.
Then, if
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{gen})
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]={}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and
the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
introduced in the statement of Proposition
1.70
defines a diffeomorphism from the set of all
isometry classes of developments of simply connected initial data to the set of all isometry classes of simply connected initial data on the
singularity. Moreover,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{VIII}}^{\mathrm{gen}}[V]
coincides with the isometry classes of matter dominated developments.

Remark 1.89
.

Assuming
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{gen})
, it is clear that the subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
induced by the vacuum dominated
developments is a smooth
codimension one submanifold; this is an immediate consequence of the existence of the diffeomorphism and the structure of the set of isometry classes
of simply connected initial data on the singularity; see Section
2.3
. Moreover, in case
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{gen})
,
the set of isometry classes of matter dominated developments is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{VIII}}^{\mathrm{gen}}[V]
.

Remark 1.90
.

Assuming either
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{gen})
or that we are in the matter dominated case with
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{gen})
, the following
holds. The subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
inducing data on the singularity with
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
is a smooth codimension
2

submanifold if
\mathfrak{s}\notin\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS}\}
and a smooth codimension
1
submanifold if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
. Moreover, the subset inducing data such that
two of the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
coincide and differ from the third is a smooth codimension
1
submanifold if
\mathfrak{s}\notin\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS}\}
. Again,
the argument is similar to the one presented in the case of Remark
1.89
.

Remark 1.91
.

Consider initial data on the singularity with the property that
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
. Assuming the initial data on the singularity
to correspond to a development, they have to be LRS; see Theorem
1.45
. Moreover, the subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
inducing isometry classes of such data on the singularity is a smooth codimension
1
submanifold.

Next, we turn to Bianchi type IX. Combining Proposition
1.74
with Theorem
1.85
yields the
following corollary.

Corollary 1.92
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
be non-negative, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Then, if
\mathfrak{s}\neq\mathrm{gen}
,

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]={}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
and the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{pp}}
introduced in the statement of
Proposition
1.74
defines a diffeomorphism from the set of all isometry classes of developments of initial
data in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
to the set of all isometry classes of simply connected initial data on the singularity. Moreover,

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathrm{gen}}[V]
coincides with the isometry classes of matter dominated developments.

Remark 1.93
.

Observations similar to Remarks
1.89
–
1.91
hold in this case as well.

1.7
Asymptotics in the direction of the singularity, isotropic and spatially flat setting

The results of the previous two sections illustrate that the Einstein flow defines a diffeomorphism between isometry classes of developments and isometry
classes of data on the singularity in many situations. However, the isotropic Bianchi type I setting is special for several reasons. To begin
with, there are the solutions described in Remark
1.13
. However, even if we exclude these solutions by restricting our attention
to developments with a crushing singularity, we have the following obstruction to the existence of a diffeomorphism: Due to
Lemma
1.67
and Remark
1.68
, there are potentials such that the set of isometry
classes of simply connected isotropic Bianchi type I developments with a crushing singularity have a different topology from the set of isometry classes
of simply connected isotropic Bianchi type I data on the singularity. For this simple reason, there must, for such potentials, be exceptional developments
that have a crushing singularity but do not induce initial data on the singularity; cf. Proposition
1.78
. This complicates the
analysis. Nevertheless, it is possible to draw conclusions, some of which depend on more detailed assumptions concerning the potential. The state
space of interest is
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
. We endow this space with a smooth structure in Remark
2.16
.
However, the corresponding parametrisation arises from a general perspective containing redundant information. It is therefore convenient
to first introduce the following simplified terminology.

Definition 1.94
.

Fix a
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and a
\vartheta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
and
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0})
are defined by

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}:=

\displaystyle\{(\theta_{0},\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\,|\,\theta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0}),\ (\phi_{1},V^{\prime}(\phi_{0}))\neq(0,0)\},

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0}):=

\displaystyle\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0}),\ (\phi_{1},V^{\prime}(\phi_{0}))\neq(0,0)\}.

Remark 1.95
.

The Hamiltonian constraint is
\theta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0})
in the isotropic Bianchi type I setting; see
(
1.2a
). The condition
(\phi_{1},V^{\prime}(\phi_{0}))\neq(0,0)
excludes trivial initial data.

Remark 1.96
.

The non-triviality condition ensures that
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
is a smooth
2
-dimensional manifold and that if
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0})
is
non-empty, then it is a smooth
1
-dimensional manifold. These manifolds can be identified (via a diffeomorphism) with

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
respectively; see Lemma
2.15
and
Remarks
2.20
and
2.26
.

Remark 1.97
.

Since the conditions
\phi_{1}=0
and
V^{\prime}(\phi_{0})=0
are preserved by the evolution, it is clear that the non-triviality condition is preserved
by the evolution. Thus a solution corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
remains in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

Assume now that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and that
\vartheta_{0}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Then, due to Lemma
1.67
and
Remark
1.96
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0})
are homeomorphic. Moreover,
due to Lemma
2.25
and Remark
2.26
, the following holds:

B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{R}\sqcup\mathbb{R}&\mathrm{if}\ V\ \mathrm{bounded},\\
\mathbb{R}&\mathrm{if}\ \{V(s)|s\leq 0\}\ \mathrm{bounded\ and}\ \{V(s)|s\geq 0\}\ \mathrm{unbounded},\\
\mathbb{R}&\mathrm{if}\ \{V(s)|s\leq 0\}\ \mathrm{unbounded\ and}\ \{V(s)|s\geq 0\}\ \mathrm{bounded},\\
\mathbb{S}^{1}&\mathrm{if}\ \{V(s)|s\leq 0\}\ \mathrm{unbounded\ and}\ \{V(s)|s\geq 0\}\ \mathrm{unbounded},\end{array}\right.

where
\cong
signifies the existence of a diffeomorphism. On the other hand, due to Section
2.3
, it is clear that

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is diffeomorphic to two copies of
\mathbb{R}
. Only in the case of bounded potentials can we thus expect a diffeomorphism from
the set of isometry classes of developments with a crushing singularity to the set of isometry classes of data on the singularity. In fact, this
is exactly what happens.

Theorem 1.98
.

Assume
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to be bounded and to be such that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]={}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

and the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
introduced in Proposition
1.78
is a diffeomorphism from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

to
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
.

Remark 1.99
.

In order to prove that isotropic Bianchi type I developments with a crushing singularity induce data on the singularity, it is sufficient to assume
that
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and that
V
is bounded. The additional requirements on
V
are there to ensure that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
is a diffeomorphism.

Proof.

The statement is an immediate consequence of Proposition
1.78
, Corollary
10.5
,
Lemma
10.1
and Remark
10.2
.
∎

Turning, next, to the case that
V
is bounded for
s\leq 0
and unbounded for
s\geq 0
(or vice versa), it is natural to first consider the
case of an exponential potential:
V(s)=V_{0}e^{\lambda s}
for some constants
V_{0},\lambda>0
. In this case, the topology of the set of isometry classes
of developments with a crushing singularity is
\mathbb{R}
. A natural way to modify this set in order to obtain two copies of
\mathbb{R}
(the topology of the set
of isometry classes of data on the singularity) is to remove one point. In other words, the naive conjecture would be that there should be one
exceptional isometry class of developments with a crushing singularity that does not induce initial data on the singularity, and that the remaining
isometry classes do induce data on the singularity. This is what happens.

Proposition 1.100
.

Let
0<V_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<\lambda<\sqrt{6}
. Define
V(s):=V_{0}e^{\lambda s}
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})

be the mean curvature and the scalar field of a development corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
is the maximal
existence interval. Then
t_{-}>-\infty
,
t_{+}=\infty
and
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. Moreover, either the solution induces
initial data on the singularity; or it does not induce initial data on the singularity and it is the unique solution satisfying

3\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)=-\lambda
for all
t\in J
.
In particular, removing the unique solution satisfying
3\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)=-\lambda
for all
t
from

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
yields
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
(which is diffeomorphic to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
via
the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
introduced in Proposition
1.78
).

Proof.

The statement follows from Proposition
1.78
, Lemma
3.4
,
Proposition
10.15
,
Lemma
10.1
, Remark
10.2
and the following argument. The unique solution
satisfying
3\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)=-\lambda
for all
t
has the property that
\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to
\Phi_{1}:=-\lambda/3
in the direction
of the singularity. If this solution induced data on the singularity, we would, on the other hand, have
\Phi_{1}^{2}=2/3
; this follows from
Definition
1.17
and the fact that
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
in the isotropic setting. Combining these observations yields

\lambda^{2}=6
, contradicting the fact that
\lambda\in(0,\sqrt{6})
.
∎

Even though this result is of interest, the relevant class of potentials is very restricted. It would be of interest to prove a similar result for
a more general class of potentials. We, next, do so in a setting in which the potential is unbounded in both directions. In this case, the topology
of the set of isometry classes of developments with a crushing singularity is
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. A natural way to modify this set in order to obtain two copies
of
\mathbb{R}
(the topology of the set of isometry classes of data on the singularity) is to remove two points. In other words, the naive conjecture would
be that there should be two exceptional isometry classes of developments with a crushing singularity that do not induce initial data on the singularity,
and that the remaining isometry classes do induce data on the singularity. This is what happens. More specifically, the following result holds.

Theorem 1.101
.

Assume
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and that there are constants
C_{V}
and
M
such that
V(s)>0
and

\left|\left(\ln V\right)^{\prime\prime}(s)\right|\leq C_{V}\langle s\rangle^{-2}

(1.23)

for all
|s|\geq M
. This means that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)
converges to limits as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
. Call the limits
\lambda_{\pm}
and assume
that
-\sqrt{6}<\lambda_{-}<0
and that
0<\lambda_{+}<\sqrt{6}
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be the
mean curvature and the scalar field of a development corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
is the maximal
existence interval. Assuming that
\theta
is unbounded, there are the following, mutually exclusive, cases:

(i)

The solution is such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{-}}\left[3\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)+(\ln V)^{\prime}[\phi(t)]\right]=0

(1.24)

holds and
\phi(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\rightarrow t_{-}
. Up to time translation, there is exactly one such solution, and its image is a smooth
submanifold of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

(ii)

The solution is such that (
1.24
) holds and
\phi(t)\rightarrow-\infty
as
t\rightarrow t_{-}
. Up to
time translation, there is exactly one such solution, and its image is a smooth submanifold of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

(iii)

The solution has a crushing singularity and induces data on the singularity.

Moreover, assuming, in addition,
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)

and removing the two unique solutions mentioned in (i) and (ii) from the set of isometry classes
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
yields

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
(which is diffeomorphic to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
via the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
introduced in
Proposition
1.78
).

Remark 1.102
.

Results related to Proposition
1.100
and Theorem
1.101
, using somewhat different assumptions,
are to be found in
[
21
]
.

Remark 1.103
.

There are also solutions that do not have crushing singularities. However, under suitable assumptions on the potential, such solutions are
non-generic; cf. Proposition
10.8
and Remark
10.9
.

Remark 1.104
.

If we replace the assumptions that
V(s)>0
and that (
1.23
) hold for
|s|\geq M
with the assumption that these estimates
hold only for
s\geq M
and the assumption that
V
is bounded for
s\leq 0
; and if we remove the assumption that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow\lambda_{-}
as

s\rightarrow-\infty
, then either
(i)
or
(iii)
have to hold.

Remark 1.105
.

If
\bar{V}(s)=V(-s)
satisfies the conditions of Remark
1.104
, then either
(ii)
or
(iii)
have
to hold.

Remark 1.106
.

If
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}\cap\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and the assumptions of Remark
1.104
hold, then, removing
the unique solution as in
(i)
from the set of isometry classes
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
yields
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

(which is diffeomorphic to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
via the map
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
introduced in Proposition
1.78
). There is an
analogous statement in case the assumptions of Remark
1.104
are replaced by the assumptions of
Remark
1.105
.

Proof.

The statement follows from Proposition
1.78
, Theorem
10.17
,
Lemma
10.1
, Remark
10.2
and the following argument. The unique solutions
satisfying (
1.24
) are such that
\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to
-\lambda_{\pm}/3
, which is incompatible with
Definition
1.17
due to the assumption that
0<\lambda_{\pm}^{2}<6
; cf. the proof of Proposition
1.100
.
The statements of Remarks
1.104
,
1.105
and

1.106
follow by, in addition, appealing to Remark
10.18
.
∎

1.8
Asymptotics in the direction of the singularity, the isotropic negative spatial
curvature setting

Finally, we state results in the direction of the singularity in the isotropic negative spatial curvature setting. We begin by defining the
relevant class of regular initial data.

Definition 1.107
.

Locally homogeneous and isotropic negative curvature initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
,
with potential
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, consist of the following: a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold
(\bar{M},\bar{g})
; a covariant

2
-tensor field
\bar{k}
on
\bar{M}
which is a non-negative constant multiple of
\bar{g}
; and two constants
\bar{\phi}_{0}
and
\bar{\phi}_{1}
satisfying:

\bar{S}-|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k})^{2}=\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}+2V(\bar{\phi}_{0}).

(1.25)

The data are said to be
trivial

if
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
and
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
. Let
\mathcal{N}[V]

denote the set of all locally homogeneous and isotropic negative curvature initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field
equations with potential
V
.

Remark 1.108
.

In case the initial data are trivial and
V\geq 0
, the corresponding maximal globally hyperbolic development is given by
(M,g,\phi)
, where

M:=\bar{M}\times(0,\infty)
,

g:=-dt\otimes dt+a_{\Lambda}^{2}(t)\bar{g}_{-}

(1.26)

and
\phi\equiv\bar{\phi}_{0}
. Here
\bar{g}_{-}
is the constant multiple of
\bar{g}
with scalar curvature
-6
,
\Lambda:=V(\bar{\phi}_{0})
and

a_{\Lambda}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}H^{-1}\sinh(Ht)&\Lambda>0,\\
t&\Lambda=0,\end{array}\right.

(1.27)

where
H:=(\Lambda/3)^{1/2}
. In other words, if
\Lambda=0
, the maximal globally hyperbolic development is the
Milne model
.
In case the initial data are trivial with
V(\bar{\phi}_{0})>0
, we obtain a vacuum solution to Einstein’s
equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda
. These statements are justified below the proof of Lemma
10.19
.

It is of interest to know if asymptotics of this form appear more generally in the direction of the singularity. This turns out to be the case.

Proposition 1.109
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Given
\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
, there are unique smooth functions
a:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
and
\phi:(0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that if
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
is a complete hyperbolic manifold with scalar curvature
-6
,
M:=\bar{M}\times(0,\infty)
and
g
is defined by

g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\bar{g}_{-},

(1.28)

then
(M,g,\phi)
is a solution to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations with the property that
\phi(t)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}
and

\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
, where
\theta
denotes the mean curvature.

In addition, the unique
(a,\phi)
is such that

|\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)|+|\phi(t)-\phi_{\infty}|\leq Ct^{2}

(1.29)

for all
t\leq 1
and some constant
C
. Moreover, the metric asymptotes to a solution to the Einstein
vacuum equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda:=V(\phi_{\infty})
in the direction of the singularity, see Remark
1.108
,
in the sense that there is a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle|a(t)-a_{\Lambda}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{5},

(1.30)

\displaystyle\left|\theta(t)-3\tfrac{\dot{a}_{\Lambda}(t)}{a_{\Lambda}(t)}\right|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{3}

(1.31)

for all
t\leq 1
.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Subsection
10.2.1
.
∎

It is of interest to contrast these solutions with the ones inducing data on the singularity. To this end, it is convenient to record the
relevant notion of data on the singularity in the current setting.

Definition 1.110
.

Let
(\bar{M},\mathscr{H})
be a complete
3
-dimensional hyperbolic manifold,
\mathscr{K}
be the
(1,1)
-tensor field
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
on
\bar{M}
and

(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then
(\bar{M},\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
are
locally homogeneous and isotropic negative curvature initial
data on the singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations

if
\Phi_{1}^{2}=2/3
. The set of such data is denoted
\mathcal{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{iso}}
.

Next, we define the notion of a development.

Definition 1.111
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{N}[V]
. A

spatially locally homogeneous and isotropic non-linear scalar field development of

\mathfrak{I}
is a triple
(M,g,\phi)
, where
M:=\bar{M}\times J
,
J
is an open interval,
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J)
,

g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\bar{g},

(1.32)

and
a:J\rightarrow(0,\infty)
is smooth. Finally,
(M,g,\phi)
is required to satisfy the Einstein non-linear
scalar field equations, and for some
t_{0}\in J
, the initial data induced on
\bar{M}_{t_{0}}:=\bar{M}\times\{t_{0}\}
by
(M,g,\phi)
and pulled back by

\iota_{0}
are required to equal
\mathfrak{I}
, where
\iota_{0}(p):=(p,t_{0})
.

In case the left endpoint of
J
is
t_{-}
(
t_{-}
will always be assumed to equal
0
if
t_{-}>-\infty
) and
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as

t\downarrow t_{-}
(where
\theta(t)
denotes the mean curvature of
\bar{M}_{t}
with respect to
g
), then the development is said to have a

crushing singularity
.

Remark 1.112
.

As noted in Section
10.2
, if
V
is non-negative and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{N}[V]
, there is a unique spatially locally homogeneous and
isotropic non-linear scalar field development of
\mathfrak{I}
with a crushing singularity and a
J
which can be assumed to equal
(0,\infty)
.
We denote this development by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
.

That developments induce data on the singularity means the following.

Definition 1.113
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{N}[V]
and
(M,g,\phi)
be a spatially locally homogeneous and isotropic non-linear scalar field development of

\mathfrak{I}
. Using the terminology of Definition
1.111
, assume the development to have a crushing singularity,
let
K
be the Weingarten maps induced on
\bar{M}_{t}
,
t\in J
, and define the
expansion normalised Weingarten map

by
\mathcal{K}:=K/\theta
. If
there are
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(\bar{M},\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{iso}}
such that
\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
,
\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\rightarrow\Phi_{1}
and

\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta\rightarrow\Phi_{0}
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
; and such that the following limit holds for all
v,w\in T\bar{M}
:

\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}v,\theta^{\mathcal{K}}w)=\mathscr{H}(v,w),

(1.33)

then the development
(M,g,\phi)
is said to
induce
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity
.

Remark 1.114
.

The solutions obtained in Proposition
1.109
do not induce data on the singularity in the sense of
Definition
1.113
, since
\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}
converges to zero for these solutions. In addition, and more importantly, the
expansion normalised spatial scalar curvature of these solutions,
\bar{S}/\theta^{2}
, converges to
-2/3
. This conclusion should be contrasted
with the BKL proposal, in which spatial derivative terms are expected to become negligible in the limit. More specifically, the expansion
normalised spatial scalar curuvature is expected to tend to zero (except for the bounces in the oscillatory setting). Another important feature of
singularities in the BKL proposal is that
they are spacelike, unlike the singularities in the solutions mentioned in Remark
1.108
. Finally,
rescaling the metrics induced on the leaves of the foliation by
\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}v,\theta^{\mathcal{K}}w)
does not result in a finite limit.
On the other hand, the solutions obtained in Proposition
1.109
do have the following properties:
\phi_{t}/\theta

converges (to zero);
\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta
converges to a limit (
\phi_{\infty}
);
\mathcal{K}
converges to a limit (
\mathrm{Id}/3
);
and
\theta^{2}\bar{g}
converges to a limit (
9\bar{g}_{-}
). In this sense, one can think of
(\bar{M},9\bar{g}_{-},\mathrm{Id}/3,\phi_{\infty},0)
as data on the singularity.
Moreover, Proposition
1.109
can be thought of as a result stating that there is a unique corresponding
development.

Given data on the singularity, it is of interest to know that there are corresponding developments.

Proposition 1.115
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{iso}}
; see Definition
1.110
. Then there is a unique
(up to time translation) development in the sense of Definition
1.111
which induces the data
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
on the singularity
in the sense of Definition
1.113
.

Remark 1.116
.

The existence interval of the unique development (cf. Lemma
10.19
), say
J
, can be assumed to take the form
J=(0,t_{+})
.
Moreover, there are constants
a_{\infty}\in(0,\infty)
and
C
and a
t_{0}\in J
such that

\displaystyle|\theta^{1/3}a-a_{\infty}|+|t\theta-1|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{\gamma_{V}},

\displaystyle|\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}-\bar{\Phi}_{1}|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{\gamma_{V}},

\displaystyle|\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta-\bar{\Phi}_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle C\langle\ln t\rangle t^{\gamma_{V}}

for
t\leq t_{0}
, where
\gamma_{V}:=\min\{4/3,2(1-\alpha_{V})\}
and
(\bar{\Phi}_{1},\bar{\Phi}_{0})
are the data on the singularity for the scalar field.

Proof.

The proof of the proposition and the remark is to be found in Subsection
10.2.2
.
∎

At this stage, we know that the outcomes represented by Propositions
1.109
and
1.115
are
possible. However, it is also of interest to know if other asymptotics can occur. If we make slightly stronger assumptions concerning the potential,
we can conclude that these are the only possible outcomes.

Proposition 1.117
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{N}[V]
. Then there are unique
a\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))

and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J)
, where
J=(0,\infty)
, and a unique
t_{0}\in J
such that if
g
is given by
(
10.36
), where
\bar{g}_{-}
is the constant multiple of
\bar{g}
with scalar curvature
-6
, and

M:=\bar{M}\times J
, then
(M,g,\phi)
is a solution to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations such that the initial data induced
on
\bar{M}_{t_{0}}:=\bar{M}\times\{t_{0}\}
by
(M,g,\phi)
and pulled back by
\iota_{t_{0}}
equal
\mathfrak{I}
, where
\iota_{t_{0}}(p):=(p,t_{0})
.
Moreover, this solution has a crushing singularity at
t=0
.

Assuming, in addition to the above, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, the development either induces data on the singularity
in the sense of Definition
1.113
or it has asymptotics of the type obtained in
Proposition
1.109
.

Proof.

The statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma
10.19
and Proposition
10.20
.
∎

Fixing a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
, where the scalar curvature of
\bar{g}_{-}
is
-6
, the solutions with asymptotics as in
Proposition
1.109
are parametrised by one parameter, the asymptotic value of the scalar field. However, the solutions
inducing initial data on the singularity are, in addition to
\Phi_{1}\in\{\sqrt{2/3},-\sqrt{2/3}\}
, parametrised by two parameters:
\Phi_{0}
and the
constant multiplying
\bar{g}_{-}
in order to yield
\mathscr{H}
. From this point of view, one would naively expect generic solutions to induce data on the
singularity. In order to prove a corresponding statement, it is convenient to first parametrise the set of initial data introduced in
Definition
1.107
.
Fixing
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
as above, if
(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{N}[V]
are such that
\bar{g}=\alpha\bar{g}_{-}
and
\bar{k}=\alpha\beta\bar{g}_{-}
, the constraint
(
1.25
) reads

6\beta^{2}=\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{6}{\alpha^{2}}+2V(\bar{\phi}_{0}).

(1.34)

From this point of view, it is natural to introduce

\mathsf{N}_{-}[V]:=\{(\beta,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ 6\beta^{2}-\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}-2V(\bar{\phi}_{0})>0\}.

(1.35)

Given
(\beta,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{N}_{-}[V]
,
\alpha>0
is uniquely determined by (
1.34
). In this sense,
\mathsf{N}_{-}[V]
parametrises
initial data, given
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
. With this terminology, the following holds.

Proposition 1.118
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
be a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold with scalar curvature
-6
. Then the subset of
\mathsf{N}_{-}[V]

that gives rise to developments with the property that
\phi(t)
converges to a finite number is contained in a countable union of codimension one submanifolds.
In this sense, the outcome represented by Proposition
1.109
corresponds to a set of initial data which is both Baire and
Lebesgue non-generic.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Subsection
10.2.3
.
∎

1.9
Future asymptotics

Next, we turn to the future asymptotics. Here we, roughly speaking, consider two situations: either
V
is proportional to an exponential, or
V

has a positive lower bound. We begin by considering the latter case.

Proposition 1.119
.

Assume
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to have a strictly positive lower bound;
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
; and
V^{\prime}
to only
have simple zeroes. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and an
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
. Then there is a subset

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]\cap\{\theta>0\}
which is open, dense and has full measure. In addition, if

\xi\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
;
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is such that
[\mathfrak{I}]=\xi
; and
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
, then the following holds.
First,
M
and
g
are of the form
M=G\times J
and (
1.7
) respectively, where
G
is a Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
Lie group and
J=(t_{-},\infty)
.
Moreover, there is a left invariant Riemannian metric
\bar{g}_{\infty}
on
G
; an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
with the property that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
and
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
; and
constants
C>0
and
b>0
such that

\displaystyle|\phi(t)-s_{0}|+|\phi_{t}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{-bt},

(1.36a)

\displaystyle|\bar{K}(t)-H\mathrm{Id}|_{\bar{g}_{\infty}}+|e^{-Ht}\bar{g}(t)-\bar{g}_{\infty}|_{\bar{g}_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{-bt}

(1.36b)

for all
t\geq t_{0}
, where
H:=\sqrt{V(s_{0})/3}
,
t_{0}\in J
and
\bar{g}(t)
and
\bar{K}(t)
are the metric and Weingarten map induced on

M_{t}=G\times\{t\}
respectively.

Proof.

The statement follows from Propositions
11.1
and
11.3
below.
∎

Due to the results of
[
52
]
, taking appropriate quotients of the spacetimes in Proposition
1.119
leads to
solutions that are future globally non-linearly stable.

Corollary 1.120
.

Fix a solution to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations as constructed in Proposition
1.119
. Let
\Gamma

be a co-compact subgroup of the isometry group of the initial data which acts freely and properly discontinuously and let
\Sigma
be the
corresponding compact
quotient of the Lie group. Taking the corresponding quotient of the solution defines a solution on
\Sigma\times(t_{-},\infty)
, here referred to
as the background solution. Make a choice of Sobolev norms
\|\cdot\|_{H^{l}}

on tensor fields on
\Sigma
and fix a
t_{0}\in(t_{-},\infty)
. Let
(\Sigma,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
denote the initial data induced on

\Sigma\times\{t_{0}\}
by the background solution. Then there is an
\epsilon>0
such that if
(\Sigma,\bar{h},\bar{\kappa},\bar{\varphi}_{1},\bar{\varphi}_{0})
are initial
data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations with the same
V
as in the case of the background solution and

\|\bar{h}-\bar{g}\|_{H^{4}}+\|\bar{\kappa}-\bar{k}\|_{H^{3}}+\|\bar{\phi}_{0}-\bar{\varphi}_{0}\|_{H^{4}}+\|\bar{\phi}_{1}-\bar{\varphi}_{1}\|_{H^{3}}\leq\epsilon,

then the maximal globally hyperbolic development corresponding to
(\Sigma,\bar{h},\bar{\kappa},\bar{\varphi}_{1},\bar{\varphi}_{0})
is future causally geodesically
complete and there are expansions of the form given in the statement of
[
52
, Theorem 2, pp. 131–132]
to the future.

Remark 1.121
.

Due to
[
44
]
, every unimodular
3
-dimensional Lie group has a co-compact subgroup.

Remark 1.122
.

One particular consequence of the corollary is that the background solution is future causally geodesically complete (note that this is not
claimed in Proposition
1.119
).

Remark 1.123
.

Combining the corollary with the conclusions concerning the past asymptotics derived previously and the results of
[
42
]
yields past
and future global non-linear stability, curvature blow up in the direction of the singularity etc. for large classes of spatially locally
homogeneous solutions. We refer the interested reader to
[
42
]
for more detailed information.

Proof.

The proof of the statement is similar to, but slightly simpler than, the proof of
[
52
, Theorem 4, pp. 134-135]
. In fact, it is sufficient
to take the steps starting with the paragraph entitled stability at the bottom of
[
52
, p. 203]
until the end of the proof. We leave
the detailed verification to the reader.
∎

The second case to consider is that of an exponential potential:

V(\phi)=V_{0}e^{-\lambda\phi},

(1.37)

where
0<V_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda\in(0,\sqrt{2})
. The reason we require
\lambda\in(0,\sqrt{2})
is that we want the corresponding solutions
to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations to exhibit accelerated expansion. In fact, under these assumptions, the class of spatially
homogeneous solutions of interest in this paper do not only exhibit accelerated expansion, they also exhibit isotropisation. The spatially
homogeneous setting is considered, e.g., in
[
26
,
32
]
; see also
[
53
, Section 12]
. The future global non-linear
stability of suitable quotients of Bianchi class A solutions is considered in
[
27
,
53
]
. In fact, for spatially closed and spatially
locally homogeneous quotients of these spacetimes, there is a stability result analogous to Corollary
1.120
; see

[
53
, Theorem 3, p. 162]
. Moreover, we can, in analogy with Remark
1.123
, derive future and past global
non-linear stability results for large classes of spatially locally homogeneous solutions; see
[
42
]
for details.

Next, we consider the isotropic negative spatial curvature case.

Proposition 1.124
.

Assume
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to have a strictly positive lower bound;
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
; and
V^{\prime}
to only
have simple zeroes. Let
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
be a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold with scalar curvature
-6
. Then the subset of
\mathsf{N}_{-}[V]
,
see (
1.35
), that gives rise to developments with the property that
\phi(t)\rightarrow s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
with
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})<0
is
contained in a countable union of one dimensional submanifolds. Call the complement
\mathsf{G}_{-}^{+}[V]
. Then, given initial data in
\mathsf{G}_{-}^{+}[V]
,
the corresponding solution has the properties that there is an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi(t)\rightarrow s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
;
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
;
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
;

\phi_{t}(t)\rightarrow 0
; and
\theta(t)\rightarrow[3V(s_{0})]^{1/2}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover, the convergence is exponential.

Remark 1.125
.

Combining this result with the conclusions of Section
1.8
, and the results of
[
42
]
and
[
52
]

yields future and past global non-linear stability results for large classes of spatially closed solutions.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Section
11.2
.
∎

1.10
Motivation for the restriction to Bianchi class A

In this paper we mainly restrict our attention to Bianchi class A initial data. It is of interest to describe how this restriction fits into the
general context of spatially homogeneous solutions. Defining spatially homogeneous solutions to be the maximal globally hyperbolic
developments of homogeneous initial data, there are, in
3
dimensions, three possibilities: the initial data are left invariant on a
unimodular Lie group (Bianchi class A); the initial data are left invariant on a non-unimodular Lie group (Bianchi class B); or the initial
data induced on the universal covering space of the initial manifold are invariant under the isometry group of the standard
metric on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
(Kantowski-Sachs). Strictly speaking, the Bianchi classification is a classification of
3
-dimensional Lie algebras;
see
[
34
]
for a more detailed discussion, references and a historical overview. Since we wish to construct solutions for which we can later prove
future and past global non-linear stability, and since our result concerning stable big bang formation is based on the assumption of spatial
compactness, see
[
42
]
, we are here mainly interested in initial data that admit compact quotients. Compact quotients can arise
in two ways: either there is a co-compact subgroup, say
\Gamma
, of the Lie group
G
; or there is a co-compact subgroup
\Gamma
of the isometry group
of the initial data which is not a subgroup of
G
. We are mainly interested in the first case, since the second case means that the isometry
group of the initial data typically has to be substantially larger than
G
. This entails additional symmetry assumptions on the initial data (and
therefore more limited classes of models). If
G
is non-unimodular, there are no co-compact subgroups of
G
; see, e.g.,
[
6
, Section 3.3]
and
the argument presented on
[
25
, pp. 865-866]
. On the other hand, in case
G
is a unimodular
3
-dimensional Lie group, there are co-compact
subgroups
\Gamma
of
G
; see
[
44
]
.

1.11
Outline of the article

We begin our analysis in Chapter
2
by discussing how to parametrise isometry classes of regular initial data, initial
data on the singularity and developments by smooth manifolds. In Chapter
3
, we then turn to the construction
of Bianchi class A developments, given initial data. We begin by deriving the equations in the symmetry reduced setting. We then prove existence of
developments and prove some preliminary results concerning the asymptotics. In particular, we provide conditions ensuring that there is a crushing
singularity and discuss the integrability of the mean curvature. Using this information, we are then in a position to introduce expansion normalised
variables. This is the topic of Section
3.3
, where we introduce the Wainwright-Hsu formulation of the equations. These are the
equations we use throughout most of the article when analysing the asymptotics. In Chapter
4
, we then turn to the
problem of analysing the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity. We begin by characterising the solutions that converge to a special point.
We also make some basic observation concerning the lower Bianchi types. However, the main result of the chapter is Theorem
4.13

in which we demonstrate that there is a dichotomy between vacuum dominated and matter dominated solutions. We also derive detailed asymptotics
for the matter dominated solutions. We are then left with the problem of analysing the asymptotics in the vacuum dominated setting. This is the
subject of Chapter
5
. We prove that the anisotropic and non-LRS vacuum dominated Bianchi type VIII and IX solutions exhibit oscillations
and derive detailed asymptotics in all the other cases. Using this information, we are then in a position to prove that, with the exception of
the solutions with oscillatory singularities, all developments induce data on the singularity. This is the subject of
Chapter
6
. Next, we turn to the problem of specifying initial data on the singularity. In Chapter
7

we address this topic from the point of view of the Wainwright-Hsu formulation. Proving that the map from regular initial data to data on the
singularity has the desired regularity properties turns out to be quite technical. In Chapter
8
, we intiate the corresponding
discussion. To begin with, we discuss the regularity of the map from regular initial data to data on the singularity with respect to the
Wainwright-Hsu variables. However, there are many different perspectives. In particular, one can fix the mean curvature of the initial data or
consider general initial data. The former perspective gives rise to additional complications, since the time derivative of the mean curvature
along the Einstein flow need not always be non-zero. After introducing a substantial amount of notation, the first result is contained in
Lemma
8.13
. Essentially as a consequence of this lemma, we obtain regularity of a representation of the map from isometry classes
of initial data with fixed mean curvature to isometry classes of initial data on the singularity; see Lemma
8.16
.
In order to define a smooth structure on the set of isometry classes of developments, we next need to prove that the map, induced by the Einstein
flow, relating isometry classes of initial data with different mean curvature is a local diffeomorphism. This is the topic of
Section
8.2
. This section also contains proofs of Lemmas
1.59
,
1.60

and
1.65
; i.e., the results endowing isometry classes of developments with a smooth structure. In
Chapter
9
, we prove geometric existence and uniqueness results of developments corresponding to data on the
singularity. This chapter also contains a proof of Propositions
1.70
,
1.74
and

1.78
, proving, roughly speaking, that the Einstein flow generates a diffeomorphism between isometry classes of developments
that induce data on the singularity and isometry classes of initial data on the singularity.

In Chapter
10
, we turn to the
k=0
and
k=-1
FLRW solutions. The analysis in the isotropic Bianchi type I
setting turns out to be surprisingly technical. However, there is a simple explanation for this: not all solutions induce data on the singularity.
In other words, it is necessary to deal with exceptional cases. On the other hand, under rather strong assumptions on the potential, it is possible
to verify that there are exactly as many exceptional solutions as one would expect from a crude inspection of the topologies of the sets involved;
see the discussion in Section
1.7
for more details. The analysis in the
k=-1
FLRW setting is more straightforward, even
though there are exceptional cases in this situation as well. In Chapter
11
, we finally turn to future asymptotics.
In the appendix, we address the problem of parametrising solutions, regular data and data on the singularity.

1.12
Acknowledgements

The author would like to thank Claes Uggla for comments on an earlier version of the article and Artur Alho for suggesting additional references.
This research was funded by Vetenskapsrådet (the Swedish Research Council), dnr. 2017-03863 and 2022-03053.

Chapter 2
Parametrising solutions, data on the singularity and regular data

In this article, we are mainly interested in solutions with asymptotics as described in Theorem
1.45
. It is therefore
of interest to identify the set of regular initial data leading to such asymptotics. Moreover, we wish to determine the regularity of
the map taking regular initial data to data on the singularity. In order for these questions to make sense, we need to parametrise the set of
isometry classes of regular initial data, as well as the set of isometry classes of data on the singularity, by smooth manifolds.

2.1
Parametrising regular initial data

In order to explain how to parametrise regular initial data, let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}[V]
. Let, moreover,

\{e_{i}\}
be a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
which is orthonormal with respect to
\bar{g}
and let
\bar{n}
and
K
be the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated
with
\{e_{i}\}
; see Definition
1.3
. Recall that
\bar{n}
and
K
satisfy (
1.3
).
Due to (
1.3b
), there is a basis
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
for the Lie algebra which is orthonormal with respect to
\bar{g}
and such that

K
and
\bar{n}
are diagonal; see
[
54
, Corollary 19.14, p. 211]
. When convenient, we assume the diagonal elements of
\bar{n}

to have signs as indicated in Table
1.1
; this can be ensured by an argument similar to the proof of

[
54
, Lemma 19.8, p. 208]
. Given initial data
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, we thus obtain

(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, where
\bar{n}=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
and
K=\mathrm{diag}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
. The resulting

(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
in general depends on the choice of frame. For instance, in the case of Bianchi type I,
\bar{n}=0
and permuting the
diagonal elements of
K
corresponds to permuting the labels of the elements of the frame. Going in the opposite direction, fix

(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
satisfying (
1.3a
). Assume, moreover, that
\bar{n}
and
K
are diagonal and that the diagonal elements
of
\bar{n}
have signs as indicated in Table
1.1
corresponding to a
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group
G
. Then there is a
frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that the associated commutator matrix is
\bar{n}
. Given this frame, we define a metric
\bar{g}
by demanding that

\{e_{i}\}
be an orthonormal frame, and we define
\bar{k}
by demanding that the Weingarten matrix associated with
\{e_{i}\}
be
K
. Then

(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
are initial data such that the corresponding commutator and Weingarten matrices with respect to
\{e_{i}\}

are
\bar{n}
and
K
respectively. See Lemma
A.4
below for a justification of the above statements. Unfortunately, the resulting
initial data depend on the choice of frame. On the other hand, if
G
is simply connected, two initial data sets obtained in this way are isometric;
see Lemma
A.1
below. Next, note that the sum of the first two terms on the left hand side of (
1.3a
) is the scalar
curvature of
\bar{g}
, say
\bar{S}
; see
[
54
, (19.6), p. 209]
. Comparing this polynomial with Table
1.1
, it is clear that

\bar{S}\leq 0
unless
G
is of Bianchi type IX. Moreover,
\bar{S}
can only equal zero if the initial data are of Bianchi type I; isotropic or LRS Bianchi
type VII
0
; or Bianchi type IX. However, since we have excluded isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
initial data, see
Remark
1.10
,
\bar{S}=0
only occurs in the case of Bianchi types I and IX.

Maximal hypersurfaces.
In this article, we are not interested in trivial initial data; cf. Definitions
1.1
and

1.12
and Remark
1.13
. In the case of a non-negative potential
V
and initial data which are not of Bianchi type
IX, this means that we exclude maximal initial data, i.e. initial data with
\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}=0
. The reason for this is the following. Assume that

G
is not of Bianchi type IX, that
\mathrm{tr}K=0
and that
V
is
non-negative. Then it follows from (
1.3a
) that
\bar{S}=0
,
\mathrm{tr}K^{2}=0
,
\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}=0
and
V(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
. This means
that
K=0
,
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
,
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
(since
V\geq 0
and
V(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
) and that
G
is of Bianchi type I. In other words, the data are trivial.
Next, we define the sets which will form the basis for the parametrisation of isometry classes of regular initial data.

Definition 2.1
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathsf{B}

be the set of
(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in M_{3}(\mathbb{R})\times M_{3}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that (
1.3a
) holds;

\bar{n}=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
;
K=\mathrm{diag}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
; and, if
\bar{n}=0
and
K
is a multiple of the identity, then either
\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0

or
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})\neq 0
.
Next, let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that
\bar{n}=0
; let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{II}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that one of
the
\bar{n}_{i}
is non-zero and the other two vanish; let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that two of the
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero and have
opposite signs, and the remaining
\bar{n}_{i}
vanishes;
let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that two of the
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero and have
the same signs, and the remaining
\bar{n}_{i}
vanishes; let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VIII}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that all the
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero, but they
do not all have the same sign; and let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}
such that all the
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero, and all have the same sign.

Remark 2.2
.

Here
M_{3}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the set of real valued
3\times 3
-matrices.

Remark 2.3
.

Consider (
1.3a
). Restricting to the case that
K
and
\bar{n}
are diagonal, we can view the set defined by this equation as a subset of

\mathbb{R}^{8}
. Calculating the gradient (in
\mathbb{R}^{8}
) of the function obtained by subtracting the expression on the right hand side of (
1.3a
)
from the left hand side, it can be deduced that if the gradient vanishes, then
\bar{n}=0
,
K=0
,
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
and
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
. Such points do not
belong to
\mathsf{B}
. It follows that all the sets
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
are manifolds.

It is convenient to keep the following observation in mind.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
, where
\mathfrak{T}
is a Bianchi class A type. Then there is an
orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such that if
\bar{n}
and
K
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with

\{e_{i}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then
x:=(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

Remark 2.5
.

If
x
and
\mathfrak{I}
are related as in the statement of the lemma, we below say that
x
arises from
\mathfrak{I}
.

Proof.

That there is an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
of the desired form follows from the observations made at the beginning of the present section,
keeping in mind that trivial initial data are excluded from
\mathcal{B}[V]
and characterised by
\bar{n}=0
(since we have excluded isotropic and LRS Bianchi
type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
data),
K=\theta\mathrm{Id}/3
(for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
),
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
and
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
.
∎

Note that permuting the basis elements in
\{e_{i}\}
(cf. Lemma
2.4
) transforms
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
to another element of

\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. Similarly, changing
e_{i}
to
-e_{i}
for one
i\in\{1,2,3\}
transforms
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
to another element of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. These operations
can be summarised by the maps

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}:\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{B},\ \ \ \psi_{\sigma}^{-}:\mathsf{B}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}

for
\sigma\in S_{3}
, defined as follows:

\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1}):=(\pm\bar{n}_{\sigma(1)},\pm\bar{n}_{\sigma(2)},\pm\bar{n}_{\sigma(3)},k_{\sigma(1)},k_{\sigma(2)},k_{\sigma(3)},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1});

(2.1)

recall that
S_{3}
denotes the set of bijections from the set
\{1,2,3\}
to itself. Here we tacitly think of

\mathsf{B}
as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
. The map
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
(
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
) corresponds to permuting the basis vectors according to
\sigma
and then
flipping the sign of one of the basis vectors in case
\sigma
is an odd (even) permutation. The reason we exclude
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
from the domain of
definition of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
is that if we do not, then
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x)=\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)
for all
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
and
\sigma\in S_{3}
; i.e.,
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}

would then be redundant in the Bianchi type I setting, which causes unnecessary complications.

Let
\Gamma

denote the group consisting of the elements

\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
. It is natural to quotient
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
by this group. However, the result need not be a manifold. This is due to the fact that
non-trivial maps of the form
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
can have fixed points; in order to obtain a manifold, we would like the group action by which we quotient
to be free. In order to
identify the fixed points of non-trivial maps of the form
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
, note that there are three types of elements of
S_{3}
;
\sigma\in S_{3}

can have three fixed points (
\sigma=\mathrm{Id}
), one fixed point (there are
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that
\sigma
fixes
i
and swaps

j
and
k
; i.e.,
\sigma
is a transposition interchanging
j
and
k
) or no fixed points (in which case
\sigma
can be written as a product of
two transpositions).

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\Gamma^{\mathrm{ev}}

(
\Gamma^{\mathrm{odd}}
)
consist of the set of
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
such that
\sigma
is even (odd). Let
\Gamma^{\pm}

be the subset of

\Gamma
consisting of the maps
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
for
\sigma\in S_{3}
. Finally, let
\Gamma^{+,\mathrm{ev}}:=\Gamma^{+}\cap\Gamma^{\mathrm{ev}}

etc.

Remark 2.7
.

Clearly,
\Gamma^{\mathrm{ev}}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, but
\Gamma^{\mathrm{odd}}
is not. Moreover,
\Gamma^{+}
is a subgroup of
\Gamma
, but
\Gamma^{-}
is not.

Next, we determine the fixed points of maps of the form
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
. The map
\psi_{\mathrm{Id}}^{+}
is of course the identity. The map
\psi_{\mathrm{Id}}^{-}
does
not have any fixed points (since
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
does not belong to the domain of definition of
\psi_{\mathrm{Id}}^{-}
). Next, let us consider the case of even and
non-trivial
\sigma\in S_{3}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and assume
\sigma\in S_{3}
to have no fixed points (i.e.,
\sigma
is even and non-trivial). Then
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}

has no fixed points. Moreover, if
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
and
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
, then all the
\bar{n}_{i}
equal and
all the
k_{i}
equal, so that
\psi_{\lambda}^{+}(x)=x
for all
\lambda\in S_{3}
. In particular, if
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
, cf.
Remark
2.5
, and is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are isotropic. Finally, let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]

and assume that
x\in\mathsf{B}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}
, cf. Remark
2.5
. Then
\psi_{\lambda}^{+}(x)=x
for all
\lambda\in S_{3}
.

Proof.

By assumption, there are
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that

\sigma(i)=j
,
\sigma(j)=l
and
\sigma(l)=i
. If
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x)=x
, then
\bar{n}_{i}=-\bar{n}_{j}
,
\bar{n}_{j}=-\bar{n}_{l}
and
\bar{n}_{l}=-\bar{n}_{i}
. This means that

\bar{n}=0
, so that
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
; i.e.,
x
does not belong to the domain of definition of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
. Thus the map
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
does not have
any fixed points. If
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
, then
\bar{n}_{j}=\bar{n}_{i}
,
\bar{n}_{l}=\bar{n}_{j}
and
\bar{n}_{i}=\bar{n}_{l}
so that all the
\bar{n}_{m}
equal. Similarly, all the
k_{m}

equal. Next, let
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
and
\{e_{a}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such
that if
\bar{n}
and
K
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{a}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then
x:=(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
.
Assume, moreover, that
x
is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
(so that all the
\bar{n}_{m}
equal and all the
k_{m}
equal). Then
\bar{k}
is a multiple of

\bar{g}
. In order to prove that the Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}
is a multiple of
\bar{g}
, note that if
\bar{R}_{ab}:=\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(e_{a},e_{b})
, where

\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}
denotes the Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}
, then
\bar{R}_{ab}=0
for
a\neq b
. Moreover, denoting the diagonal components by
\bar{R}_{a}
,
then, if
\{a,b,c\}=\{1,2,3\}
,

\bar{R}_{a}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\bar{n}_{b}-\bar{n}_{c})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{n}_{a}^{2}

(2.2)

(no summation on any index). These statements are justified in
[
54
, Lemma 19.11, p. 209]
. Since all the
\bar{n}_{a}
equal, it follows from
(
2.2
) that all the
\bar{R}_{a}
equal. Thus the Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}
is a multiple of
\bar{g}
, and we conclude that

\mathfrak{I}
are isotropic. Finally, let
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
\{e_{a}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}
with
respect to
\bar{g}
such that if
\bar{n}
and
K
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{a}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then

x=(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. Then all the
k_{m}
equal; see Definition
1.6
. In order to prove that all the
\bar{n}_{m}

equal, note that (
2.2
) holds in this case as well. That all the
\bar{R}_{a}
equal follows from Definition
1.6
.
There are only two possibilities
for all the
\bar{R}_{a}
to equal: either all the
\bar{n}_{a}
equal, or two of the
\bar{n}_{a}
equal and the third vanishes. In the latter case,
\mathfrak{I}
are of
isotropic Bianchi type VII
0
. However, that possibility has been excluded in the definition of
\mathcal{B}[V]
; see Definition
1.12
. Thus all
the
\bar{n}_{a}
equal, and the last statement of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\sigma\in S_{3}
be a transposition and
x\in\mathsf{B}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
be a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
. Then
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
.
In fact, there are
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that if
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, then
\bar{n}_{i}=0
,
\bar{n}_{j}=-\bar{n}_{l}\neq 0

and
k_{j}=k_{l}
. In particular, if
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}[V]
, cf.
Remark
2.5
, and is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are permutation symmetric. Finally, let

\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}[V]
. Then there is an
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
, cf. Remark
2.5
, and a
transposition
\sigma
such that
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x)=x
.

Proof.

Let
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
be such that
\sigma
fixes
i
and swaps
j
and
l
. A fixed point
x
of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
is then such that

\bar{n}_{i}=-\bar{n}_{i}
,
\bar{n}_{j}=-\bar{n}_{l}
and
k_{j}=k_{l}
. Thus
\bar{n}_{i}=0
. Moreover, since
x\notin\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
,

\bar{n}_{j}
and
\bar{n}_{l}
have to be non-zero and have opposite signs. This means that if
x\in\mathsf{B}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
is a
fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
, then
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
. Moreover, if
x\in\mathsf{B}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
and is a fixed point of

\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are permutation symmetric initial data, see Definition
1.11
. Finally, let

\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}[V]
. We then wish to prove that there is an
x
as in the last statement of the lemma.
Let, to this end,
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
be an orthonormal basis of the type assumed to exist in
Definition
1.11
. Let
K
and
\bar{n}
be the corresponding Weingarten and commutator matrices. Due to the properties of

\Psi
, see Definition
1.11
,
\bar{n}^{11}=0
,
\bar{n}^{13}=-\bar{n}^{12}
,
\bar{n}^{23}=0
and
\bar{n}^{33}=-\bar{n}^{22}
. This means that there
are only two independent components of
\bar{n}
, namely
\bar{n}^{12}
and
\bar{n}^{22}
.
Similarly
\bar{k}_{12}=\bar{k}_{13}
and
\bar{k}_{22}=\bar{k}_{33}
. The eigenvalues
of
