\bar{n}
are
\lambda_{0}=0
,
\lambda_{+}
and
\lambda_{-}=-\lambda_{+}
. Since
\bar{n}\neq 0
, we can assume
\lambda_{+}
to satisfy
\lambda_{+}>0
.
Due to the symmetry of
\bar{n}
, there are orthonormal eigenvectors
v_{0}
,
v_{+}
and
v_{-}
corresponding to
\lambda_{0}
,
\lambda_{+}
and
\lambda_{-}

respectively. Due to the momentum constraint, the matrices
\bar{n}
and
K
have to commute; see (
1.3b
). This means that
v_{0}
and
v_{\pm}

are eigenvectors of
K
with eigenvalues
\kappa_{0}
and
\kappa_{\pm}
respectively. In order to verify that
\kappa_{+}=\kappa_{-}
, it is convenient to
first determine an eigenvector of
\bar{n}
corresponding to
\lambda_{0}
in terms of
\bar{n}^{12}
and
\bar{n}^{22}
(we leave the details to the reader). Then
one can verify that
K
is a multiple of the identity on the orthogonal space (in order to do so, it is useful to appeal to the already mentioned
relations between the coefficients as well as the relations arising from the fact that
K
and
\bar{n}
commute). To conclude, there is an
orthonormal frame
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such that if
\nu
and
\bar{K}
are the corresponding commutator and Weingarten matrices,
then
\nu=\mathrm{diag}(0,\nu_{0},-\nu_{0})
for a
0<\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
K=\mathrm{diag}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{2})
. The last statement of the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\sigma\in S_{3}
be a transposition and
x\in\mathsf{B}
be a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
. Then there are

\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that if
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, then
\bar{n}_{j}=\bar{n}_{l}
and
k_{j}=k_{l}
. In particular, if

x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
, cf. Remark
2.5
, is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
, but is not
a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are LRS. Finally, let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
. Then there is an

x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
, cf. Remark
2.5
, which is not a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
,
and a transposition
\sigma
such that
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
.

Remark 2.11
.

If
x
is as in the last statement of the lemma, then there is no transposition, say
\lambda
, different from
\sigma
such that

\psi_{\lambda}^{+}(x)=x
. In order to prove this statement, assume that there is such a
\lambda
. Then
\lambda\circ\sigma
is a non-trivial even
element of
S_{3}
under which
x
is invariant. Due to Lemma
2.8
, this means that
x
is invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
,
in contradiction with the assumptions.

Proof.

Let
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
be such that
\sigma
fixes
i
and swaps
j
and
l
. Then
\bar{n}_{j}=\bar{n}_{l}
and
k_{j}=k_{l}
. This means that if
x\in\mathsf{B}
arises
from
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
and is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{I}
are either isotropic or LRS, see Definition
1.8
. If
\mathfrak{I}
are
isotropic, then
x
is a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
; see Lemma
2.8
. Thus, if
x\in\mathsf{B}
arises from

\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
, cf. Remark
2.5
, is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
but is not a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
,
then
\mathfrak{I}
are LRS. Assume now that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
. Let
\{e_{i}\}
be an orthonormal
basis of the type assumed to exist in Definition
1.8
. Let
K
and
\bar{n}
be the corresponding Weingarten and commutator matrices. Then the
assumptions of Definition
1.8
imply that
K
and
\bar{n}
are diagonal. Moreover, if
k_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}
denote the diagonal components of
K

and
\bar{n}
respectively, then
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
and
k_{2}=k_{3}
. In particular, if
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, then there is a
transposition
\sigma
such that
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
. Finally, since
\mathfrak{I}
are not isotropic,
x
is not a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
.
∎

Definition 2.12
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type. Define
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
to be the subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
consisting of points
invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
. Define
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{per}}=\varnothing
and define, for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
,
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
to be the set of
p\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}

such that there is a map
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}\in\Gamma^{-,\mathrm{odd}}
with
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(p)=p
. Define

\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
to be the set of
p\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
such that there is a map
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}\in\Gamma^{+,\mathrm{odd}}
with

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(p)=p
. Define

\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}.

Define
\Gamma_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}:=\{\mathrm{Id}\}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathrm{iso}}:=\{\mathrm{Id},\psi^{-}_{\mathrm{Id}}\}
. Define
\Gamma_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}:=\Gamma^{\mathrm{ev}}
,

\Gamma_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}:=\Gamma^{+,\mathrm{ev}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}:=\Gamma^{\mathrm{ev}}
for
\mathfrak{T}\notin\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{VI}_{0}\}
. Finally, define

\Gamma_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\Gamma^{+}
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\Gamma
for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
.

Remark 2.13
.

Note that
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
;
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
;
and
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
is empty.

Due to the observations made prior to the statement of the definition we obtain the following conclusions.

Lemma 2.14
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
be such that
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are defined in Definition
2.12
and are non-empty. Then
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on

\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and the corresponding quotient is smooth and denoted by
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Proof.

By an argument similar to Remark
2.3
, the sets
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are smooth manifolds. Next, note that if
\sigma
has no fixed points
(i.e.,
\sigma
is a non-trivial even element of
S_{3}
), then
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
has no fixed points and
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
only fixes isotropic elements; see
Lemma
2.8
. If
\sigma
is odd, then
\sigma
is a transposition. Moreover, if
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x)=x
, then
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
,
and if
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
, then
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}\cup\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
; see Lemmas
2.9
and
2.10
. Combining these
observations with Definition
2.12
, it is clear that
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a finite group which acts on
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
without fixed points.
Thus
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, so that the quotient is smooth.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to parametrise
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
by smooth manifolds.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Then there is a bijection from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for the following combinations of

\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
: if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
; if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II},\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
; if

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{VI}_{0},\mathrm{per})
; and if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
and
\mathfrak{T}
is an arbitrary Bianchi class A type.

Remark 2.16
.

Combining the bijection obtained in the lemma with Lemma
2.14
yields a smooth structure on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

for the relevant combinations of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
. In what follows, we assume
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to have been endowed with this
smooth structure.

Remark 2.17
.

One immediate consequence of the proof is that if
\eta\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, with
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
as in the statement of the lemma, then
there are
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
such that
\eta
arises from
\mathfrak{I}
. Moreover, we can, with each
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

associate an
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
so that
x
arises from
\mathfrak{I}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})
be one of the combinations mentioned in the statement of the lemma and
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Then there is an

x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
; cf. Lemma
2.4
and Remark
2.5
. If
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
, then

x
is invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
due to Lemma
2.8
. This means that
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
; cf.
Definition
2.12
. Next, if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
, then we can assume
x
to belong to

\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
due to Lemma
2.9
and Definition
2.12
. If
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
, then we can
assume
x
to be such that it is not invariant under all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
, but such that there is a transposition
\sigma
such that

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
; see Lemma
2.10
. This means that
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
; see Definition
2.12
.
Finally, let
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
. We then wish to prove that
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Assume, to this end, that
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
for some

\mathfrak{r}\neq\mathrm{gen}
. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{iso}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
due to Lemma
2.8
, a contradiction. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{per}
, then

\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
due to Lemma
2.9
, a contradiction. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{LRS}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
due to
Lemma
2.10
, a contradiction. To conclude: we can, with each
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
associate an

x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, which, in its turn, gives rise to an element, say
[x]
, of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
; see Lemma
2.14
.
If
x_{i}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
i=1,2
, are both associated with
\mathfrak{I}
in this way, then
[x_{1}]=[x_{2}]
due to Lemma
A.8
.
In other words, there is a well defined map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover, again due to
Lemma
A.8
, this map descends to a map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Next, if two initial data
sets, say
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
, are mapped to the same element, then
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
are isometric; see Lemma
A.1
.
Thus the map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is well defined and injective. What remains is to prove surjectivity.

In order to prove surjectivity, let
[x]\in\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for some
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Due to Lemma
A.4
, there are non-trivial simply
connected regular initial data
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
and a basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, orthonormal with respect
to
\bar{g}
, such that if
\bar{n}
and
K
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then
x=(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
.
We need to prove that
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. All that remains to be demonstrated is that
\mathfrak{I}
are of symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}
and that
\mathfrak{I}

are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
; see Definition
1.12
. Assume, for the moment, that
\mathfrak{I}
are neither of isotropic
nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. Then we know that
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
. This means that
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}[V]
for some symmetry
type
\mathfrak{r}
. By the arguments presented above, this means that there is a basis
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, orthonormal with respect to
\bar{g}
, such that if

\bar{n}^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then
x^{\prime}=(\bar{n}^{\prime},K^{\prime},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
.
Combining these observations with Lemma
A.8
and the fact that
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are disjoint if

\mathfrak{s}\neq\mathfrak{r}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{s}
. In other words,
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. This proves surjectivity, with one caveat: we need to
exclude the possibility that
\mathfrak{I}
are isotropic or LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. Assume, to this end, that they are. Then the Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}

vanishes identically; see Lemma
A.6
. This means that
\bar{n}
has to be such that
\bar{n}_{i}=0
and
\bar{n}_{j}=\bar{n}_{l}
for some
i,j,l
such
that
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
; see (
2.2
). If
k_{j}=k_{l}
, then
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS}\}
, in
contradiction with the assumptions. Thus
k_{j}\neq k_{l}
. In particular, the initial data are not isotropic. If
k_{i}\notin\{k_{j},k_{l}\}
, the initial data
cannot be LRS either. Assume therefore, without loss of generality, that
k_{2}=k_{3}\neq k_{1}
, that
\bar{n}_{2}=0
and that
\bar{n}_{3}=\bar{n}_{1}
. In order for
\mathfrak{I}

to be LRS, there must be an orthonormal basis
\{e_{m}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
and a family
\Psi_{t}
of Lie algebra isomorphisms such that
the conditions of Definition
1.8
hold with
\{e_{i}\}

replaced by
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
. Let
K^{\prime}
and
\bar{n}^{\prime}
be the Weingarten and commutator matrices associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
. Then, due to the
the conditions of Definition
1.8
,
K^{\prime}
and
\bar{n}^{\prime}
are diagonal. Moreover, if
k_{i}^{\prime}
and
\bar{n}_{i}^{\prime}
denote the diagonal
components of
K^{\prime}
and
\bar{n}^{\prime}
respectively, then
\bar{n}_{2}^{\prime}=\bar{n}_{3}^{\prime}
and
k_{2}^{\prime}=k_{3}^{\prime}
. This means, in particular, that
k_{A}=k_{A}^{\prime}
for
A\in\{2,3\}
and
that
k_{1}=k_{1}^{\prime}
. Thus
e_{1}^{\prime}=\pm e_{1}
. Since the initial data are of Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
and
\bar{n}_{2}^{\prime}=\bar{n}_{3}^{\prime}
, we also have to have
\bar{n}_{2}^{\prime}\neq 0

and
\bar{n}_{1}^{\prime}=0
. Since the plane spanned by
e_{2}^{\prime}
and
e_{3}^{\prime}
has to coincide with the plane spanned by
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
(they are both the orthogonal
complement of
e_{1}^{\prime}=\pm e_{1}
), there is also a
t_{0}
such that
\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{2}^{\prime}=e_{2}
and
\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{3}^{\prime}=\pm e_{3}
. This means that

0=[e_{2}^{\prime},\pm e_{3}^{\prime}]=[\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{2}^{\prime},\pm\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{3}^{\prime}]=[e_{2},e_{3}]=\bar{n}_{1}e_{1}.

Thus
\bar{n}_{1}=0
. Since
\bar{n}_{2}=0
and
\bar{n}_{3}=\bar{n}_{1}
, we conclude that the initial data are of Bianchi type I, in contradiction with the assumptions.
To conclude,
\mathfrak{I}
are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. This completes the proof of surjectivity.
∎

2.2
Parametrising developments

We introduce the terminology
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
etc. in Definition
1.56
. In order to prove that these sets can be
parametrised by smooth manifolds, it is useful to first focus on sets of isometry classes of initial data with a fixed mean curvature.

Definition 2.18
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\vartheta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type
such that
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are defined in Definition
2.12
and non-empty. Using the notation introduced
in Lemma
2.14
, let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})

be the subsets of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
respectively with
\mathrm{tr}K=\vartheta_{0}
.
Moreover, let
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})

be the subset of
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
with
\mathrm{tr}K=\vartheta_{0}
and
\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0
.

In order to analyse the regularity of these sets, we begin by reformulating (
1.3a
). Let

k_{1}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta-\tfrac{2}{3}k_{+},\ \ \ k_{2}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta+\tfrac{1}{3}k_{+}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}k_{-},\ \ \ k_{3}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta+\tfrac{1}{3}k_{+}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}k_{-},

(2.3)

where
\theta=\mathrm{tr}K
. With this notation, (
1.3a
) can be written

\theta^{2}=k_{+}^{2}+k_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{3}{4}(\bar{n}_{1}^{2}+\bar{n}_{2}^{2}+\bar{n}_{3}^{2}-2\bar{n}_{1}\bar{n}_{2}-2\bar{n}_{2}\bar{n}_{3}-2\bar{n}_{3}\bar{n}_{1})+\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{\phi}_{1}^{2}+3V(\bar{\phi}_{0}).

(2.4)

Fixing the left hand side of (
2.4
) to be
\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for some
\vartheta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the only situation in which
the gradient of the right hand side vanishes is when
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
,
\bar{\phi}_{1}=0
,
V^{\prime}(\bar{\phi}_{0})=0
and
\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3V(\bar{\phi}_{0})
; i.e.,
if the initial data are trivial, see Definition
1.1
and Remark
1.13
. However, this possibility has
been excluded from
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, see Definition
2.1
. This observation leads to the following conclusion.

Lemma 2.19
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\vartheta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})
as in Definition
2.18
. Then
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
is a
smooth manifold.

Remark 2.20
.

The bijection whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
2.15
preserves the mean curvature. It therefore induces
a bijection from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
for the combinations of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
appearing
in Lemma
2.15
. Similarly, there is a bijection from
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
to

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0}):={}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\cap\{\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0\}.

(2.5)

Due to the lemma, these bijections define smooth structures on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
and

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
. In Section
8.2
, we explain how to combine these sets to define a smooth
structure on the set of isometry classes of developments.

Proof.

Due to the observations made prior to the statement of the lemma, the set of elements in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
with fixed mean curvature

\vartheta_{0}
, say
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
, is a smooth manifold. The elements of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
preserve the mean curvature
and therefore
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
. This means that the quotient, i.e.

\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
, is smooth.
∎

In the case of isotropic Bianchi type I, it is unfortunately not sufficient to focus on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
; we also have to
impose the condition that the corresponding developments have a crushing singularity. Moreover, in order to obtain a smooth structure for the
set of isometry classes of developments, we need the condition that there is a crushing singularity to be open. One way to ensure this is to demand
that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
, see Definition
1.62
. Imposing this condition also makes it possible to ensure that all solutions with a crushing
singularity attain one fixed mean curvature; i.e., it is possible to parametrise the set of elements in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with a crushing
singularity by
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
for a suitably chosen
\vartheta_{0}
. To justify these statements, we next make some observations
concerning potentials belonging to
\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
; see Definition
1.62
. If
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is bounded, then
v_{\max}(V)\geq\limsup_{s\rightarrow\infty}V(s)
.
Similarly, if
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
is bounded, then
v_{\max}(V)\geq\limsup_{s\rightarrow-\infty}V(s)
.

Proof.

Note that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}\Rightarrow\overline{V}\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
with
v_{\max}(\overline{V})=v_{\max}(V)
, where
\overline{V}(s):=V(-s)
. Thus
the second statement of the lemma follows by applying the first statement of the lemma to
\overline{V}
. We therefore only prove the
first statement. If
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}V(s)
exists, the desired conclusion follows from the definition. Assume that the limit does not
exist. Then
v_{-}<v_{+}
, where

v_{+}:=\limsup_{s\rightarrow\infty}V(s),\ \ \ v_{-}:=\liminf_{s\rightarrow\infty}V(s).

There are thus, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{n}\leq u_{n}
such that
s_{n},u_{n}\rightarrow\infty
,
V(s_{n})\geq v_{+}-1/n
,

V^{\prime}(s_{n})>0
and
V^{\prime}(u_{n})<0
. This means that there is a
w_{n}\in[s_{n},u_{n}]
such that
V^{\prime}(s)\geq 0
in
[s_{n},w_{n}]

and
V^{\prime}(w_{n})=0
. Since
V^{\prime}\geq 0
on
[s_{n},w_{n}]
, we have to have
V(w_{n})\geq v_{+}-1/n
. By the definition of
v_{\max}
, this means that

v_{\max}(V)\geq v_{+}-1/n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
v_{\max}(V)\geq v_{+}
. The lemma follows.
∎

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
; see Definition
1.62
. If
V
is bounded, then
v_{\max}(V)\geq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}V(s)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
and define

V_{+}:=\limsup_{s\rightarrow\infty}V(s),\ \ \ V_{-}:=\limsup_{s\rightarrow-\infty}V(s).

Then there are
s_{-}<s_{+}
such that
V(s)\leq V_{-}+\epsilon
for all
s\leq s_{-}
and
V(s)\leq V_{+}+\epsilon
for all
s\geq s_{+}
. If
V
attains its maximum on

[s_{-},s_{+}]
at an interior point, say
s_{0}
, then
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
, so that

v_{\max}(V)\geq V(s_{0})=\max_{s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]}V(s).

If
V
attains its maximum on
[s_{-},s_{+}]
on the boundary, then, due to Lemma
2.21
,

v_{\max}(V)+\epsilon\geq\max\{V_{+},V_{-}\}+\epsilon\geq\max_{s\in[s_{-},s_{+}]}V(s).

To conclude
v_{\max}(V)+\epsilon\geq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}V(s)
. Since this holds for all
\epsilon>0
, the desired conclusion follows.
∎

It is also going to be useful to know the following concerning potentials
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
; see Definition
1.62
. If
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is unbounded, there is an
s_{+}
such that
V^{\prime}(s)>0
for all

s\geq s_{+}
. If
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
is unbounded, there is an
s_{-}
such that
V^{\prime}(s)<0
for all
s\leq s_{-}
.

Remark 2.24
.

If
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is unbounded, it follows from the conclusions of the lemma that
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
s\rightarrow\infty
. There
are two possibilities. Either
V^{\prime}(s)>0
for all
s
. In that case
V(s)>v_{\max}(V)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Define, in this case,
s_{+}(V):=-\infty
. The
other possibility is that there is an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
. In that case, let
s_{a}:=\sup\{s\in\mathbb{R}:V^{\prime}(s)=0\}
. Due to the assumptions
and the conclusions of the lemma,
s_{a}\in\mathbb{R}
.
There are two possibilities. Either
V(s_{a})=v_{\max}(V)
, in which case we define
s_{+}(V):=s_{a}
; note that
s_{+}(V)
is then the smallest
s

such that
V(u)>v_{\max}(V)
for all
u>s
. The final possibility is that
V(s_{a})<v_{\max}(V)
. In that case there is a smallest
s
, larger than

s_{a}
, such that
V(s)=v_{\max}(V)
. Call this smallest
s

s_{b}
and define
s_{+}(V):=s_{b}
; note that
s_{+}(V)
is then the smallest
s

such that
V(u)>v_{\max}(V)
for all
u>s
. By the definition of
v_{\max}(V)
, it also follows that
V^{\prime}(s)>0
for all
s>s_{+}(V)
. One can
similarly define
s_{-}(V)
in case
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
is unbounded.

Proof.

For the same reason as in the proof of Lemma
2.21
, it is sufficient to prove the first statement.
Assume, in order to obtain a contradiction, that

\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is unbounded, but there is a sequence
s_{n}\rightarrow\infty
such that
V^{\prime}(s_{n})\leq 0
. If there is a subsequence

\{s_{n_{k}}\}
such that
V(s_{n_{k}})\rightarrow\infty
, we conclude that
v_{\max}(V)=\infty
, contradicting the fact that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
. Choosing a
subsequence, if necessary, we can thus assume that
V(s_{n})
converges to a finite limit, say
V_{+}
. By the assumptions, there is also a sequence

u_{n}\rightarrow\infty
such that
V(u_{n})\rightarrow\infty
. Combining the existence of these two sequences, an argument similar to the one presented
in the proof of Lemma
2.21
implies that there is a sequence
w_{n}\rightarrow\infty
such that
V^{\prime}(w_{n})=0
and

V(w_{n})\rightarrow\infty
. Thus
v_{\max}(V)=\infty
, contradicting the fact that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
.
∎

Fix a
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and a
\vartheta_{0}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Then, in the Bianchi class A setting, Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}\neq
IX initial data with
initial mean curvature
\vartheta_{0}
correspond to Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
non-linear scalar field developments with a crushing singularity; see
Lemma
3.8
. The condition that the solution have a crushing singularity of course excludes some isotropic Bianchi type I
solutions; see Remark
3.5
and Lemma
3.4
. Next, we turn to the topology of

\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
, which, for reasons explained in the introduction, is of particular interest.

Lemma 2.25
.

Fix a
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and a
\vartheta_{0}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. If
V
is bounded,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
is diffeomorphic to two
copies of
\mathbb{R}
. If one of
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
and
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is bounded and the other is unbounded, then

\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. If both are unbounded, then
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Remark 2.26
.

Due to Definition
2.1
, Definition
2.12
and Lemma
2.14
, it is clear that

\begin{split}\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}=&\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}/\Gamma^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}\\
=&\{(0,\theta_{0}\mathrm{Id}/3,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\,|\,\theta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0}),\ (\phi_{1},V^{\prime}(\phi_{0}))\neq(0,0)\}.\end{split}

This set can clearly be identified with
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}
. Similarly, we can identify
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
with

B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
.

Proof.

Due to Remark
2.26
, it is sufficient to determine the topology of
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
. To begin with,
it is useful to note that the condition of non-degeneracy is superfluous for our choice of
\vartheta_{0}
. Let, to this end,
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
be such
that

\vartheta_{0}^{2}=\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{1}^{2}+3V(\phi_{0}).

(2.6)

Then either
\phi_{1}\neq 0
or
\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3V(\phi_{0})
. In the latter case, we have to have
V^{\prime}(\phi_{0})\neq 0
, since

\vartheta_{0}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Thus, for our choice of
\vartheta_{0}
,

B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}(\vartheta_{0}):=\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0})\};

i.e., the condition of non-degeneracy is automatically satisfied.

In case
V
is bounded, then
\vartheta_{0}^{2}>3\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}V(s)
, cf. Corollary
2.22
, so that (
2.6
)
defines two disjoint manifolds, each diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
(the manifolds are distinguished by the sign of
\phi_{1}
).

Next, consider the case that
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
is unbounded but that
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is bounded. Due to Lemma
2.21

and the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
, we know that there is an
s_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
3V(s)<\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s\geq s_{1}
. Since, in addition,

V(u)\rightarrow\infty
as
u\rightarrow-\infty
, see Remark
2.24
, we conclude that

s_{0}:=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,3V(u)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}\ \forall\,u\geq s\}

is a real number. In particular,
3V(s_{0})=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
and
3V(s)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s\geq s_{0}
. Moreover, by the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
,

V^{\prime}(s_{0})\neq 0
. However, the possibility
V^{\prime}(s_{0})>0
is excluded by the definition of
s_{0}
. This means that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})<0
. Assume, in order to obtain
a contradiction, that there is an
s_{2}<s_{0}
such that
3V(s_{2})\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}
. Then
V
must attain a local maximum on
[s_{2},s_{0}]
at an
s_{3}\in(s_{2},s_{0})
.
Then
V^{\prime}(s_{3})=0
, and
3V(s_{3})>3V(s_{0})=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
, in contradiction with the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
. Thus
3V(s)>\vartheta_{0}^{2}

for all
s<s_{0}
. The set of

\phi_{0}
such that (
2.6
) has a solution is thus equal to
[s_{0},\infty)
. Assume, in order to obtain a contradiction,
that there is an
s_{+}>s_{0}
such that
3V(s_{+})=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
. Since
3V(s)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s\geq s_{0}
, this means that
s_{+}
is a local
maximum, so that
V^{\prime}(s_{+})=0
. However, this contradicts the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
. Thus
3V(s)<\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s>s_{0}
. We can thus
define the following two sets:

A_{\pm}:=\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0}),\ \pm\phi_{1}\geq 0\}.

By the above observations,

A_{\pm}=\left\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\phi_{0}\in[s_{0},\infty),\ \phi_{1}=\pm\left(2\vartheta_{0}^{2}/3-2V(\phi_{0})\right)^{1/2}\right\}.

Moreover, the sets
A_{\pm}
are each homeomorphic to
[s_{0},\infty)
, by projection to the
\phi_{0}
-axis, and they only intersect in
(s_{0},0)
. This means
that the union is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
. Since the union is
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
and
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
is a smooth

1
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we obtain the desired conclusion. The statement in case
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
is bounded and

\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
is unbounded follows by applying the same argument to
\overline{V}
introduced in the proof of Lemma
2.21
.

Next, assume that
\{V(s)\,|\,s\leq 0\}
and
\{V(s)\,|\,s\geq 0\}
are both unbounded. Then
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
due to
Remark
2.24
, a fact we use tacitly in what follows. Fix an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Choose
s_{a}<s_{0}<s_{b}
such that

V(s_{a})>V(s_{0})
and
V(s_{b})>V(s_{0})
. Then
V
has an interior minimum on
[s_{a},s_{b}]
attained at, say,
s_{1}
, so that
V^{\prime}(s_{1})=0
. Note that

\vartheta_{0}^{2}>3V(s_{1})
by the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
. Let

\displaystyle\sigma_{-}:=

\displaystyle\inf\{s\leq s_{1}\,|\,3V(u)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}\ \forall\,u\in[s,s_{1}]\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}:=

\displaystyle\sup\{s\geq s_{1}\,|\,3V(u)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}\ \forall\,u\in[s_{1},s]\}.

Then
\sigma_{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}
;
\sigma_{-}<s_{1}<\sigma_{+}
;
3V(s)\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}
on

[\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+}]
; and
3V(s)=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for
s\in\{\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+}\}
. Since
3V(\sigma_{-})=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
, we cannot have

V^{\prime}(\sigma_{-})=0
. Moreover, by the definition of
\sigma_{-}
, we cannot have
V^{\prime}(\sigma_{-})>0
. This means that
V^{\prime}(\sigma_{-})<0
. Similarly,

V^{\prime}(\sigma_{+})>0
. Assume that there is a
\sigma_{0}<\sigma_{-}
such that
3V(\sigma_{0})\leq\vartheta_{0}^{2}
. Then
V
must have a local maximum in

(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{-})
, say
\sigma_{1}
. Then
3V(\sigma_{1})\geq 3V(\sigma_{-})=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
and
V^{\prime}(\sigma_{1})=0
. However, this contradicts the
definition of
\vartheta_{0}
. Thus
3V(s)>\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s<\sigma_{-}
. Similarly,
3V(s)>\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s>\sigma_{+}
. Assume, in
order to reach a contradiction, that there is an
s\in(\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+})
such that
3V(s)=\vartheta_{0}^{2}
. Then
s
is a local maximum of
V

so that
V^{\prime}(s)=0
. This contradicts the definition of
\vartheta_{0}
. To conclude,
3V(s)<\vartheta_{0}^{2}
for all
s\in(\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+})
. Define

B_{\pm}:=\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\vartheta_{0}^{2}=3\phi_{1}^{2}/2+3V(\phi_{0}),\ \pm\phi_{1}\geq 0\}.

By the above observations,

B_{\pm}=\left\{(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,|\,\phi_{0}\in[\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+}],\ \phi_{1}=\pm\left(2\vartheta_{0}^{2}/3-2V(\phi_{0})\right)^{1/2}\right\}.

Moreover, the sets
B_{\pm}
are each homeomorphic to
[\sigma_{-},\sigma_{+}]
, by projection to the
\phi_{0}
-axis, and they only intersect in

(\sigma_{\pm},0)
. This means that the union is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Since the union is
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})
and
B_{\mathrm{I},+}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta_{0})

is a smooth
1
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

2.3
Parametrising data on the singularity

In analogy with
\mathsf{B}
, we next introduce
\mathsf{S}
as a first step in the parametrisation of isometry classes of initial data on the singularity.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
\mathsf{S}

be the set of
(o,P,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in M_{3}(\mathbb{R})\times M_{3}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\mathrm{tr}P=1,\ \ \mathrm{tr}P^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}=1;

(2.7)

o=\mathrm{diag}(o_{1},o_{2},o_{3})
;
P=\mathrm{diag}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
; if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
and
p_{i}=1
, then
o_{j}=o_{k}
; and if
p_{l}<1
for all
l
and

o_{i}\neq 0
for some
i
, then
p_{i}>0
. Next, let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that all the
o_{i}
vanish; let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{II}}
denote
the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that one of
the
o_{i}
is non-zero and the other two vanish; let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that two of the
o_{i}
are non-zero and have
opposite signs, and the remaining
o_{i}
vanishes; let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}

denote the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that two of the
o_{i}
are non-zero and have
the same signs, and the remaining
o_{i}
vanishes; let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VIII}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that all the
o_{i}
are non-zero, but they
do not all have the same sign; and let
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{IX}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{S}
such that all the
o_{i}
are non-zero, and all have the same sign.

Just as regular initial data give rise to elements of
\mathsf{B}
, see Lemma
2.4
, initial data on the singularity give rise to
elements of
\mathsf{S}
.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
; see Definition
1.25
. Then there is an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}

of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that if
o
is the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}\}
and
P
is the matrix with components

P_{ij}=\mathscr{H}(\mathscr{K}e_{i},e_{j})
, then
x:=(o,P,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}
.

Remark 2.29
.

If
x
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are related as in the statement of the lemma, we below say that
x
arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
. If
P
is related to
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}

and
\{e_{i}\}
as in the statement of the lemma, we below say that
P
is the Weingarten matrix associated with
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
and
\{e_{i}\}
.

Remark 2.30
.

The converse statement, that elements of
\mathsf{S}
arise from initial data on the singularity, can be found in Lemma
A.5
.

Proof.

Let
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
; let
o^{\prime}
be the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
; and let
P^{\prime}
be
the matrix with components
P_{ij}^{\prime}=\mathscr{H}(\mathscr{K}e_{i}^{\prime},e_{j}^{\prime})
. Then the condition
\mathrm{div}_{\mathscr{H}}\mathscr{K}=0
is equivalent to
o^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
commuting; see

[
54
, Lemma 19.13, p. 210]
. Since
o^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
are also symmetric, this means that
o^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
can be simultaneously diagonalised
by means of an orthogonal transformation. There is thus an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that if
o
is the
corresponding commutator matrix and
P
is the matrix with components
P_{ij}=\mathscr{H}(\mathscr{K}e_{i},e_{j})
, then
o
and
P
are diagonal. That

\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}=1
and
\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}=1
hold implies that (
2.7
) holds. Next, denote the diagonal components of
P
by

p_{i}
(note that the
p_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
). There are two cases to consider. Either all the
p_{i}<1
or there is one
p_{i}
which equals
1
.

Assume that one of the
p_{i}
, say
p_{1}
, equals
1
. Due to (
2.7
), it follows that
p_{2}=p_{3}=0
. In particular,

\mathscr{K}e_{A}=0
for
A\in\{2,3\}
. Next, due to Definition
1.17
, there is an orthonormal basis
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect
to
\mathscr{H}
such that
\mathscr{K}\bar{e}_{1}=\bar{e}_{1}
and such that if
\Psi_{t}
is defined by (
1.4
) (with
e_{i}
replaced by
\bar{e}_{i}
), then

\Psi_{t}
is a Lie algebra isomorphism for all
t
. Clearly,
\bar{e}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
, so that
\{e_{2},e_{3}\}
and
\{\bar{e}_{2},\bar{e}_{3}\}
span the same plane. Thus

\mathscr{K}\bar{e}_{A}=0
for
A\in\{2,3\}
, and if

\bar{P}
is the matrix with components
\bar{P}_{ij}=\mathscr{H}(\mathscr{K}\bar{e}_{i},\bar{e}_{j})
, then
\bar{P}=\mathrm{diag}(1,0,0)
. Next, if
\bar{o}
is the commutator matrix associated
with
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
, then the existence of the family
\Psi_{t}
of Lie algebra isomorphisms implies that
\bar{o}=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{o}_{1},\bar{o}_{2},\bar{o}_{2})
. Thus

(\bar{o},\bar{P},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}
.

Next, assume that all the
p_{l}<1
. Then, since
\mathrm{tr}P=1
, it follows that if
p_{i}\leq 0
and
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
, then
p_{j},p_{k}>0
. This means,
since the
p_{l}
are eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
, that the eigenspace of
\mathscr{K}
corresponding to
p_{i}
is perpendicular to the span of the eigenspaces
corresponding to
\{p_{j},p_{k}\}
, say
\mathfrak{h}
. Due to Definition
1.17
it follows that
\mathfrak{h}
is a subalgebra, which means that

[e_{j},e_{k}]\in\mathfrak{h}
. In particular,
[e_{j},e_{k}]\perp e_{i}
. On the other hand
[e_{j},e_{k}]=\pm o_{i}e_{i}
(no summation). This means that
o_{i}=0
. Thus

(o,P,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}
.
∎

Define
\sigma_{\pm}
so that

p_{1}=\tfrac{1}{3}-\tfrac{2}{3}\sigma_{+},\ \ \ p_{2}=\tfrac{1}{3}+\tfrac{1}{3}\sigma_{+}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\sigma_{-},\ \ \ p_{3}=\tfrac{1}{3}+\tfrac{1}{3}\sigma_{+}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\sigma_{-};

(2.8)

i.e., let
\sigma_{+}:=(1-3p_{1})/2
and
\sigma_{-}=\sqrt{3}(p_{2}-p_{3})/2
. Then the first equality in (
2.7
) is automatically
satisfied, and the second reads

\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{3}{2}\Phi_{1}^{2}=1.

(2.9)

In particular, (
2.7
) defines a smooth manifold. Next, note that the maps introduced in (
2.1
)
leave
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
invariant. We can therefore also think of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
as a map from
\mathsf{S}
to
\mathsf{S}
and
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
as a map from

\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
to
\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
. Strictly speaking, we should introduce different notation for these maps. However, for the sake of brevity,
we use the same notation and assume the domain of definition to be clear from the context. We also use the notation introduced in
Definition
2.6
. As before,
\psi_{\mathrm{Id}}^{+}
is the identity and
\psi_{\mathrm{Id}}^{-}
has no fixed points.

Lemma 2.31
.

Assume
\sigma\in S_{3}
to have no fixed points (i.e.,
\sigma
is even and non-trivial). Then
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
, considered as a map from

\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
to
\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
, has no fixed points. Moreover, if

x=(o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}

and
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
,
then all the
o_{i}
equal and all the
p_{i}
equal
1/3
, so that
\psi_{\lambda}^{+}(x)=x
for all
\lambda\in S_{3}
. In particular, if
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}

arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, cf. Remark
2.29
, and is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are
isotropic. Finally, let
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
and assume that
x\in\mathsf{S}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
, cf.
Remark
2.29
. Then
\psi_{\lambda}^{+}(x)=x
for all
\lambda\in S_{3}
.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma
2.8
. The details are left to the reader.
∎

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
\sigma\in S_{3}
be a transposition and
x\in\mathsf{S}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}
be a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
. Then
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
.
In fact, there are
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that if
x=(o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
, then
o_{i}=0
,
o_{j}=-o_{l}\neq 0

and
p_{j}=p_{l}
. In particular, if
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
, cf.
Remark
2.29
, and is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are permutation symmetric. Finally, let

\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
. Then there is an
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
, cf. Remark
2.29
,
and a transposition
\sigma
such that
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x)=x
.

Proof.

All the statements but the last one follow by arguments that are almost identical to the corresponding arguments in the proof
of Lemma
2.9
. In order to justify the last statement, let

\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
. We then wish to prove that there is an
x
as in the last statement of the lemma.
Let, to this end,
\Psi
be a Lie algebra isomorphism and
\{e_{i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that
(
1.5
) and
\mathscr{K}\Psi=\Psi\mathscr{K}
hold; cf. Definition
1.21
. Let
o
be the commutator matrix associated with

\{e_{i}\}
and let
P
be the Weingarten matrix associated with
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
and
\{e_{i}\}
. That
o
and
P
commute follows from

\mathrm{div}_{\mathscr{H}}\mathscr{K}=0
; cf. the proof of Lemma
2.28
. Given this information, the proof of the last statement of the
lemma is essentially identical to the
proof of the corresponding statement in Lemma
2.9
. Let us, however, note that
1
cannot be an eigenvalue of

\mathscr{K}
; this is not compatible with Bianchi type
\mathrm{VI}_{0}
due to the arguments presented in the proof of Lemma
2.28
.
Moreover, the final condition for membership in
\mathsf{S}
is fulfilled due to the last part of the proof of Lemma
2.28
.
∎

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
\sigma\in S_{3}
be a transposition and
x\in\mathsf{S}
be a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
. Then there are

\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that if
x=(o_{1},o_{2},o_{3},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
, then
o_{j}=o_{l}
and
p_{j}=p_{l}
. In particular, if

x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, cf. Remark
2.29
, is a fixed point of
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
, but is not
a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are LRS. Finally, let
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
. Then there is an

x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
, cf. Remark
2.29
, which is not a fixed point of all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
,
and a transposition
\sigma
such that
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
.

Remark 2.34
.

An observation analogous to Remark
2.11
holds in this case as well.

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof of Lemma
2.10
. The details are left to the reader.
∎

In analogy with Definition
2.12
, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 2.35
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and recall the notation introduced in Definition
2.6
. Define
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
to be the
subset of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
. Define
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{per}}=\varnothing
and define, for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
,
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
to
be the set of
p\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
such that there is a map
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}\in\Gamma^{-,\mathrm{odd}}
with
\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(p)=p
. Define
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
to be the
set of
p\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
such that there is a map
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}\in\Gamma^{+,\mathrm{odd}}
with
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(p)=p
. Define

\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}-\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}.

Remark 2.36
.

Note that
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
;
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
;
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
is empty.

In analogy with Lemma
2.14
, the following statement holds.

Lemma 2.37
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
be such that
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
see Definitions
2.35
and
2.12
, are defined and non-empty. Then
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a smooth manifold,

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and the corresponding quotient is denoted
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
, then the second last condition in the list of requirements for membership in
\mathsf{S}
is void and the last is automatically
satisfied. Moreover,
P=\mathrm{Id}/3
,
\Phi_{0}
is an arbitrary element of
\mathbb{R}
and
\Phi_{1}\in\{-(2/3)^{1/2},(2/3)^{1/2}\}
. If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{I}
,
then
o=0
and if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, then
o=\nu_{0}\mathrm{Id}
for some
0\neq\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
are smooth manifolds. Moreover, the
groups
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
act freely and properly discontinuosly on them.

Next, let
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
. Then
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
is the union of three sets, corresponding to which
o_{i}
vanishes, such
that the closure of each of these sets has empty intersection with the other two. It is sufficient to focus on the set corresponding to

o_{1}=0
. If
x
belongs to this set, then it is of the form

x=(0,o_{2},-o_{2},p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
with
o_{2}\neq 0
. This means that (
2.9
) takes the form
\sigma_{+}^{2}+3\Phi_{1}^{2}/2=1

(note that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are parametrised by (
2.8
) in which
\sigma_{-}
has been set to zero). There is no restriction
on
(o_{2},\Phi_{0})
other than
o_{2}\neq 0
. Note that
p_{i}
cannot equal
1
for any
i
, since the definition of
\mathsf{S}
would then require

o_{j}=o_{k}
, where
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
, which is incompatible with
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
. This means that the second last condition in the list of
requirements for membership in
\mathsf{S}
is void. The final condition takes the form
p_{2}>0
; i.e.,
\sigma_{+}>-1
. In particular,

\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
is a smooth manifold and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on this manifold.

Next, let
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
. In this setting, each
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
consists of three disjoint sets, corresponding to a choice of
i

such that if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
then the corresponding elements satisfy
p_{j}=p_{k}
and
o_{j}=o_{k}
; note that if
p_{j}=p_{k}
and
o_{j}=o_{k}
,
we cannot have both
p_{i}=p_{j}
and
o_{i}=o_{j}
since the corresponding elements are invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
. In fact, the closure of each
of these sets has empty intersection with the other two. It is thus sufficient to focus on the case that
i=1
. If
x
belongs to the
corresponding set, then
x
is of the form
x=(o_{1},o_{2},o_{2},p_{1},p_{2},p_{2},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
. Whether
o_{1}
or
o_{2}
are zero or non-zero, and
whether
o_{1}
has the same sign as
o_{2}
or the opposite sign (in case both are non-zero) depends on the symmetry type. However, the
corresponding conditions are immaterial to the question at hand: is
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
a smooth manifold? The condition that
x
be LRS
but not isotropic corresponds to the condition that
(o_{1}-o_{2},p_{1}-p_{2})\neq(0,0)
, an open condition. Next, (
2.9
) takes the
form
\sigma_{+}^{2}+3\Phi_{1}^{2}/2=1
and there is no restriction on
\Phi_{0}
. If there is a
p_{i}
which equals
1
, then it has to be
p_{1}

(and this corresponds to
\sigma_{+}=-1
). In this case, we automatically have
o_{2}=o_{3}
. In other words, the second last condition in the list of
requirements for membership in
\mathsf{S}
is automatically satisfied. The form of the final condition depends on the Bianchi type, but it in practice
corresponds to an open condition on
\sigma_{+}
. In particular,
\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
is a smooth manifold and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}

acts freely and properly discontinuously on this manifold.

Finally, assume that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
. The signs and vaninshing/non-vanishing of the
o_{i}
depend on the symmetry type and are immaterial to the question
at hand, as in the LRS setting. Removing isotropic, LRS and permutation symmetric elements corresponds to removing closed subsets from
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
; the
set of fixed points of a finite number of continuous maps is clearly closed. There is no restriction on
\Phi_{0}
. Clearly, (
2.9
) defines
a smooth manifold. Next, note that if
p_{i}=1
and
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
, then
p_{j}=p_{k}=0

due to (
2.7
) and
o_{j}=o_{k}
due to the definition of
\mathsf{S}
. This means that the corresponding elements in
\mathsf{S}
are LRS,
something we exclude here. This means that the second last condition in the list of requirements for membership in
\mathsf{S}
is void. The final condition in
the definition of
\mathsf{S}
corresponds to an open condition on
\sigma_{\pm}
depending on the Bianchi type. To conclude,
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}
is a smooth
manifold and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on this manifold.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to parametrise
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
by smooth manifolds.

Lemma 2.38
.

There is a bijection from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for the following combinations of

\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
: if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
; if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II},\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
; if

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{VI}_{0},\mathrm{per})
; and if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
and
\mathfrak{T}
is an arbitrary Bianchi class A type.

Remark 2.39
.

Combining the bijection obtained in the lemma with Lemma
2.37
yields a smooth structure on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}

for the relevant combinations of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}
. In what follows, we assume
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to have been endowed with this
smooth structure.

Proof.

Let
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})
be one of the combinations mentioned in the statement of the lemma and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Then there is an

x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
; cf. Lemma
2.28
and Remark
2.29
. If

\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
, then
x
is invariant under
\Gamma^{+}
due to Lemma
2.31
. This means that
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
; cf.
Definition
2.35
. Next, if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
, then we can assume
x
to belong to

\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
due to Lemma
2.32
and Definition
2.35
. If
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
, then we can
assume
x
to be such that it is not invariant under all the maps in
\Gamma^{+}
, but such that there is a transposition
\sigma
such that

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x)=x
; see Lemma
2.33
. This means that
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
; see Definition
2.35
.
Finally, let
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
. We then wish to prove that
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Assume, to this end, that
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
for some

\mathfrak{r}\neq\mathrm{gen}
. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{iso}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
due to Lemma
2.31
, a contradiction. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{per}
, then

\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{per}
due to Lemma
2.32
, a contradiction. If
\mathfrak{r}=\mathrm{LRS}
, then
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
due to
Lemma
2.33
, a contradiction. To conclude: we can, with each
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
associate an

x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, which, in its turn, gives rise to an element, say
[x]
, of
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
; see Lemma
2.37
.
If
x_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
i=1,2
, are both associated with
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
in this way, then
[x_{1}]=[x_{2}]
due to
Lemma
A.11
. In other words, there is a well defined map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover,
again due to Lemma
A.11
, this map descends to a map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Next, if two
initial data sets, say
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
, are mapped to the same element, then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
are isometric;
see Lemma
A.3
. Thus the map from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is well defined and injective. What remains
is to prove surjectivity.

In order to prove surjectivity, let
[x]\in\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for some
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Due to Lemma
A.5
, there are
simply connected initial data on the singularity
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
and a basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
, orthonormal with respect to

\mathscr{H}
, such that if
o
and
P
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
, then
x=(o,P,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
.
We need to prove that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. All that remains to be demonstrated is that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are of symmetry type
\mathfrak{s}
and
that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
; see Definition
1.25
. Assume, for the moment,
that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. Then we know that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. This means that

\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
for some symmetry type
\mathfrak{r}
. By the arguments presented above, this means that there is a basis
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
of

\mathfrak{g}
, orthonormal with respect to
\mathscr{H}
, such that if
o^{\prime}
and
P^{\prime}
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
and

\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
, then
x^{\prime}=(o^{\prime},P^{\prime},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
. Combining these observations with Lemma
A.11
and the
fact that
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{r}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are disjoint if

\mathfrak{s}\neq\mathfrak{r}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{r}=\mathfrak{s}
. In other words,
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. This proves surjectivity, with one caveat: we need to
exclude the possibility that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are isotropic or LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. Assume, to this end, that they are. Then the Ricci tensor of
\mathscr{H}

vanishes identically; see Lemma
A.7
. This means that
o
has to be such that
o_{i}=0
and
o_{j}=o_{l}
for some
i,j,l
such
that
\{i,j,l\}=\{1,2,3\}
; see (
2.2
). If
p_{j}=p_{l}
, then
x\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS}\}
, in
contradiction with the assumptions. Thus
p_{j}\neq p_{l}
. In particular, the initial data are not isotropic. If
p_{i}\notin\{p_{j},p_{l}\}
, the initial data
cannot be LRS either. Assume therefore, without loss of generality, that
p_{2}=p_{3}\neq p_{1}
, that
o_{2}=0
and that
o_{3}=o_{1}
. In order for
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}

to be LRS, there must be an orthonormal basis
\{e_{m}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
and a family
\Psi_{t}
of Lie algebra isomorphisms such that
the conditions of Definition
1.21
hold with
\{e_{i}\}
replaced by
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
. Let
P^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}
be the Weingarten and commutator matrices
associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
. Then, due to the the conditions of Definition
1.21
,
P^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}
are diagonal. Moreover, if

p_{i}^{\prime}
and
o_{i}^{\prime}
denote the diagonal components of
P^{\prime}
and
o^{\prime}
respectively, then
o_{2}^{\prime}=o_{3}^{\prime}
and
p_{2}^{\prime}=p_{3}^{\prime}
. This means, in particular, that

p_{A}=p_{A}^{\prime}
for
A\in\{2,3\}
and that
p_{1}=p_{1}^{\prime}
. Thus
e_{1}^{\prime}=\pm e_{1}
. Since the initial data on the singularity are of Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
and

o_{2}^{\prime}=o_{3}^{\prime}
, we also have to have
o_{2}^{\prime}\neq 0
and
o_{1}^{\prime}=0
. Since the plane spanned by
e_{2}^{\prime}
and
e_{3}^{\prime}
has to coincide with the plane spanned by

e_{2}
and
e_{3}
(they are both the orthogonal complement of
e_{1}^{\prime}=\pm e_{1}
), there is also a
t_{0}
such that
\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{2}^{\prime}=e_{2}
and

\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{3}^{\prime}=\pm e_{3}
. This means that

0=[e_{2}^{\prime},\pm e_{3}^{\prime}]=[\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{2}^{\prime},\pm\Psi_{t_{0}}e_{3}^{\prime}]=[e_{2},e_{3}]=o_{1}e_{1}.

Thus
o_{1}=0
. Since
o_{2}=0
and
o_{3}=o_{1}
, we conclude that the initial data are of Bianchi type I, in contradiction with the assumptions.
To conclude,
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. This completes the proof of surjectivity.
∎

Chapter 3
Constructing a development

Our next goal is to construct developments as in Definition
1.28
corresponding to regular initial data
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
;
i.e., to prove Proposition
1.31
. As a starting point, we derive the relevant equations.

3.1
Equations, Bianchi class A development

The construction of a development, given initial data, is based on solving (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) below. Next, we explain how these
equations arise by expressing the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations for a Bianchi class A development as in
Definition
1.28
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
G
be a connected
3
-dimensional unimodular Lie group,
I
be an open interval and
g
be a smooth metric on
M:=G\times I
of the form
(
1.7
), where
\bar{g}
is a smooth family of left invariant Riemannian metrics on
G
. Let
t_{0}\in I
and let
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}

be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}(t_{0})
. Let
e_{0}:=\partial_{t}
. Define
e_{i}
by the requirement that
e_{i}|_{t_{0}}=e_{i}^{\prime}
and

\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{i}=0
, where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
g
. Then
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is a smooth global orthonormal frame for
(M,g)
,
e_{i}\perp e_{0}

for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
e_{i}|_{t}\in\mathfrak{g}
for all
t\in I
. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:=g
,
\theta_{ij}:=\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{0},e_{j}\rangle
,

\theta:=\delta^{ij}\theta_{ij}
and
\sigma_{ij}:=\theta_{ij}-\theta\delta_{ij}/3
. Let
n^{ij}
be the components of the commutator matrix associated with

\{e_{i}\}
. Then
\theta_{ij}
and
n^{ij}
are independent of the spatial coordinate.

Assume that
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and that
\phi\in C^{\infty}(I)
. Then
(M,g,\phi)
satisfies the Einstein equations if and only if the following
equations are satisfied:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{mjl}n^{li}\theta_{ij}=

\displaystyle 0,

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma_{lm}=

\displaystyle-\theta\sigma_{lm}-s_{lm},

(3.1b)

\displaystyle\theta_{t}=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{3}{2}\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}-\tfrac{1}{2}n_{ij}n^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{4}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}-\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2},

(3.1c)

\displaystyle\theta^{2}=

\displaystyle\tfrac{3}{2}\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}+\tfrac{3}{2}n_{ij}n^{ij}-\tfrac{3}{4}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}+\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}+3V\circ\phi,

(3.1d)

where indices are raised and lowered with the Kronecker delta and

\displaystyle b_{lm}=

\displaystyle 2n_{m}^{\phantom{m}i}n_{il}-(\mathrm{tr}n)n_{lm},

(3.2a)

\displaystyle s_{lm}=

\displaystyle b_{lm}-\tfrac{1}{3}(\mathrm{tr}b)\delta_{lm}.

(3.2b)

Moreover, the Jacobi identities yield

\partial_{t}n_{ij}=2\sigma^{k}_{\phantom{k}(i}n_{j)k}-\tfrac{1}{3}\theta n_{ij},

(3.3)

where the parenthesis denotes symmetrization. Finally, the evolution equation for the scalar field can be written

\phi_{tt}=-\theta\phi_{t}-V^{\prime}\circ\phi.

(3.4)

Assume (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) to be satisfied. Due to (
3.1a
), it follows that the
e_{i}^{\prime}
can be chosen so that

n^{ij}(t_{0})=0
for
i\neq j
and
\theta_{ij}(t_{0})=0
for
i\neq j
.
Given that
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
has been chosen in this way, there are smooth functions
a_{i}:I\rightarrow(0,\infty)
such that

e_{i}=a_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
n^{ij}(t)=0
for
i\neq j
and
t\in I
; and
\theta_{ij}(t)=0
for
i\neq j
and
t\in I
.

Remark 3.2
.

In what follows, we always take for granted that
e_{i}^{\prime}
has been chosen so that
n^{ij}(t_{0})=0
for
i\neq j
and
\theta_{ij}(t_{0})=0
for
i\neq j
.
Denoting the diagonal components of
n(t_{0})
by
\bar{n}_{i}
, the matrix
n
with components
n^{ij}
satisfies
n=\mathrm{diag}(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
, where

n_{1}=\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}a_{3}}\bar{n}_{1},\ \ n_{2}=\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}a_{3}}\bar{n}_{2},\ \ n_{3}=\frac{a_{3}}{a_{1}a_{2}}\bar{n}_{3}.

(3.5)

Proof.

Define
e_{0}^{\prime}:=\partial_{t}
. Writing
e_{i}=e_{i}^{\alpha}e_{\alpha}^{\prime}
, the equations for the
e_{i}
can be written as system of linear ODE with smooth coefficients (depending
only on time) for
e_{i}^{\alpha}
. This guarantees the existence of the smooth vector fields
e_{i}
. Due to the equation, the form of the metric, the fact that

e_{0}
is a unit vector field and the fact that
e_{i}^{0}|_{t_{0}}=0
, it follows that
e_{i}^{0}=0
for all
t
. Since
e_{0}\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle=0
, it is also clear that

\{e_{i}\}
is an orthonormal frame for
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}(t)
. To conclude,
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is a smooth global orthonormal frame for
(M,g)
and

e_{i}|_{t}\in\mathfrak{g}
for all
t\in I
. Define
\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\delta}
by
\nabla_{e_{\beta}}e_{\delta}=\Gamma^{\alpha}_{\beta\delta}e_{\alpha}
. Due to the fact that
\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{i}=0
it
can then, using arguments similar to those presented in

[
54
, Subsection B.2.1, p. 279]
, be verified that
\Gamma^{0}_{00}=0
and
\Gamma_{0i}^{0}=\Gamma_{i0}^{0}=\Gamma^{i}_{00}=0
. Moreover,

\Gamma^{i}_{0l}=0
,
\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=\theta_{ij}
and
\Gamma^{i}_{j0}=\theta_{ij}
. Finally,
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}
are the connection
coefficients associated with the metric induced on the constant-
t
hypersurfaces. Again, due to the equation for the
e_{i}
, the conclusions of

[
54
, Subsection B.2.2, p. 280]
and
[
54
, Subsection B.2.3, pp. 280–281]
hold. This means that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(e_{0},e_{0})=

\displaystyle-\theta_{t}-\theta^{ij}\theta_{ij},

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(e_{0},e_{m})=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{mjl}n^{li}\theta_{ij},

(3.6b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}(e_{l},e_{m})=

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\theta_{lm}+\theta\theta_{lm}+\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(e_{l},e_{m}).

(3.6c)

On the other hand, due to
[
54
, Lemma 19.11, p. 209]
,

\overline{\mathrm{Ric}}(e_{l},e_{m})=2n_{m}^{\phantom{m}i}n_{il}-n^{ij}n_{ij}\delta_{lm}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}\delta_{lm}-(\mathrm{tr}n)n_{lm}.

(3.7)

In the present setting, the Einstein equations can be written

R_{\alpha\beta}-\nabla_{\alpha}\phi\nabla_{\beta}\phi-V\circ\phi\cdot g_{\alpha\beta}=0.

(3.8)

Since
\phi
is assumed to be independent of the spatial variables, it follows from (
3.6b
) that the
0m
-components
of (
3.8
) are equivalent to (
3.1a
). Due to (
3.6a
), the
00
-component of (
3.8
)
is equivalent to

\theta_{t}+\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{3}\theta^{2}+\phi_{t}^{2}-V\circ\phi=0.

(3.9)

Due to (
3.6c
) and (
3.7
), the
lm
components of (
3.8
) are given by

\partial_{t}\theta_{lm}+\theta\theta_{lm}+2n_{m}^{\phantom{m}i}n_{il}-n^{ij}n_{ij}\delta_{lm}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}\delta_{lm}-(\mathrm{tr}n)n_{lm}-V\circ\phi\cdot\delta_{lm}=0.

(3.10)

It is convenient to divide the equation into its trace free parts and its trace. The trace free part is given by (
3.1b
).
Taking the trace of (
3.10
) yields

\theta_{t}+\theta^{2}-n_{ij}n^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}-3V\circ\phi=0.

(3.11)

Subtracting a third times (
3.11
) from (
3.9
) yields

\tfrac{2}{3}\theta_{t}+\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{3}n_{ij}n^{ij}-\tfrac{1}{6}(\mathrm{tr}n)^{2}+\phi_{t}^{2}=0,

so that (
3.1c
) holds. Finally, subtracting (
3.11
) from (
3.9
) yields the Hamiltonian constraint
(
3.1d
). It remains to be demonstrated is that if (
3.1
) holds, then Einstein’s equations are satisfied. We
already know that the
0m
-components of (
3.8
) as well as the trace free part of the
lm
-equations are satisfied. That the

00
-equations and the trace of the
lm
-equations are satisfied then follows from (
3.1c
) and (
3.1d
).

Next, due to the arguments presented in
[
54
, Subsection B.3, pp. 281–282]
, the Jacobi identities imply that

\partial_{t}n^{ik}-\theta^{k}_{\phantom{k}l}n^{li}-\theta^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}n^{jk}+\theta n^{ki}=0,

(3.12)

so that (
3.3
) holds. We leave the proof of (
3.4
) to the reader.

In order to prove the final statement of the lemma, assume that
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
has been chosen so that
n^{ij}(t_{0})=0
for
i\neq j
and
\theta_{ij}(t_{0})=0

for
i\neq j
. Then it follows from (
3.1b
) and (
3.3
) that collecting the off-diagonal components of
\sigma_{ij}
and the
off-diagonal components of
n_{ij}
into one vector, say
x
, then
x
satisfies an equation of the form
x_{t}=Ax
. Since
x(t_{0})=0
, it follows
that
x(t)=0
for all
t\in I
. Next,

0=\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{i}=[e_{0},e_{i}]+\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{0}=e_{0}(e_{i}^{j})e_{j}^{\prime}+\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\theta_{ij}e_{j}^{k}e_{k}^{\prime}.

This means that

e_{0}(e_{i}^{k})=-\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\theta_{ij}e_{j}^{k}=-\theta_{i}e_{i}^{k}

(no summation in the last expression), where
\theta_{i}:=\theta_{ii}
(no summation). Since
e_{i}^{k}(t_{0})=\delta_{i}^{k}
, it follows that
e_{i}^{k}(t)=0
for all

i\neq k
and all
t\in I
. The final statement of the lemma follows.
∎

3.2
Existence of developments

Next, we prove Proposition
1.31
. However, it is convenient to first make the following technical observation.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
0\leq\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume
0\leq n_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2,3
, to be such that
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}\leq\alpha
. Then

p:=n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-2(n_{1}n_{2}+n_{2}n_{3}+n_{3}n_{1})\geq-10\alpha^{2/3}.

Proof.

We can, without loss of generality, assume that
n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq n_{3}
. If
n_{3}\leq\alpha^{1/3}
, then

p\geq-2n_{1}(n_{2}+n_{3})\geq-4\alpha^{2/3}.

If
n_{2}\leq n_{3}/4
, then

p\geq n_{3}^{2}-2n_{3}(n_{1}+n_{2})\geq n_{3}^{2}-n_{3}^{2}=0.

Assume now that
n_{2}\geq n_{3}/4
and that
n_{3}\geq\alpha^{1/3}
. Then

p\geq-2n_{1}n_{2}-2n_{1}n_{3}\geq-2\alpha^{2/3}-8\alpha^{2/3}.

The lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.31
.

Let
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})=\mathfrak{I}
. Due to Lemma
2.4
, there is an orthonormal basis
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with
respect to
\bar{g}
such that if
\bar{n}
and
K
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}
, then

\bar{n}
and
K
are diagonal. Moreover, due to (
1.3a
), (
3.1d
) is satisfied initially.
Next, let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and solve (
3.1b
), (
3.1c
), (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) with the following initial data:

\sigma_{ij}(t_{0})
are the components of the trace free part of
K
;
\theta(t_{0}):=\mathrm{tr}K
;
n(t_{0}):=\bar{n}
;
\phi(t_{0}):=\bar{\phi}_{0}
and

\phi_{t}(t_{0}):=\bar{\phi}_{1}
. Let
I
denote the maximal existence interval. Due to (
3.1b
), (
3.3
) and the fact that the off-diagonal
components of
\sigma_{ij}
and
n_{ij}
vanish initially, it follows that
n(t)
and
\sigma(t)
are diagonal for all
t
. At this stage, we can
define
a_{i}
by

\partial_{t}a_{i}=(\sigma_{ii}+\theta/3)a_{i},\ \ \ a_{i}(t_{0})=1

(3.13)

(no summation). Let
\phi
be the solution obtained above (by solving (
3.4
)),
M:=G\times I
,
e_{0}:=\partial_{t}
and
e_{i}:=a_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{\prime}

(no summation). Define a Lorentz metric on
M
by the condition that
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is an orthonormal frame. The proof of the statement that

(M,g,\phi)
is a globally hyperbolic development of
\mathfrak{I}
is very similar to the proof of
[
54
, Proposition 20.3, p. 215]
; the
reader is encouraged to generalise the corresponding argument. In order to prove the uniqueness statement, assume that there are two developments
with corresponding existence intervals
I_{i}
,
i=1,2
. By time translation, we can assume that the initial data are specified at
t=t_{0}
for
both developments. We can choose
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
as the initial frame (since the solutions induce the same initial data at
t_{0}
) for both developments.
For both developments, we can introduce a Fermi-Walker propagated frame as in the statement of Lemma
3.1
. The corresponding

\sigma_{ij}
’s etc. then satisfy the same equations with the same initial data. This means that the
\sigma_{ij}
, the
\theta
, the
n_{ij}
and
the
\phi
of the two solutions coincide. Moreover, both frames take the form
e_{i}:=a_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{\prime}
(no summation). Since the
a_{i}
have to satisfy
(
3.13
), it follows that the frames are the same. The only thing that remains to be verified is that the existence intervals are
the same. However, the criteria that either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|+|\phi|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
guarantee that the
existence intervals are maximal. On the other hand, we need to verify that maximal solutions to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
)
satisfy these criteria. Assume, to this end, that
t_{+}<\infty
and that
\phi
and
\theta
remain bounded to the future. Excluding Bianchi
type IX for the moment, (
3.1d
) then implies that
\phi_{t}
and the
\sigma_{ij}
remain bounded to the future. Combining this observation
with (
3.3
) leads to the conclusion that all the variables are bounded to the future. This means that the solution can be extended beyond

t_{+}
, contradicting the assumption that the existence interval is maximal. In the case of Bianchi type IX, assume, without loss of generality, that
the
n_{i}
are strictly positive and consider
f:=n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}
; here
n_{i}:=n_{ii}
(no summation). Due to (
3.3
),
f_{t}=-\theta f
. This means,
in particular, that
f
remains bounded to the future; say
f\leq C
. Due to Lemma
3.3
, this means that the sum of the second and
third terms on the right hand side of (
3.1d
) is bounded from below to the future. Moreover, combining this lower bound with
(
3.1d
) and the boundedness of
\theta
and
\phi
implies that
\sigma
and
\phi_{t}
are bounded to the future. Combining this bound with
(
3.3
) yields the conclusion that all the variables remain bounded to the future, contradicting
t_{+}<\infty
.
This contradiction finishes the argument in the Bianchi type IX setting. The argument in the opposite time direction is similar. This leads
to the desired dichotomy: either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|+|\phi|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
. In case
V\geq 0
, the above arguments lead
to the improved dichotomy that either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
, as stated in Remark
1.32
.

In order to prove the final statement, assume, to begin with, that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
. Due to Lemma
2.8
, we can
then assume that all the
\sigma_{ij}
vanish initially and all the
n_{i}
equal initially. Combining these observations with (
3.1
)–(
3.3
)
yields the conclusion that
\sigma_{ij}(t)=0
for all
t
and that
n_{i}(t)=n_{j}(t)
for all
t
and all
i,j
. Thus the solution remains isotropic.
Note also that (
3.13
) yields the conclusion that all the
a_{i}
equal. This means that the spacetime metric takes the form
(
1.7
) with
\bar{g}=a^{2}(t)\bar{h}
, where
\bar{h}
is the initial metric.

Assume
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
. Then there is an
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
; see
Remark
2.5
, Lemma
2.10
and Definition
2.12
. We can, without loss of generality, assume
that
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
is such that
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
,
k_{2}=k_{3}
and
(\bar{n}_{1},k_{1})\neq(\bar{n}_{2},k_{2})
. Next, (
3.1b
) and
(
3.3
) can be used to derive a homogeneous first order equation for
\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{33}
and
n_{22}-n_{33}
. Since these quantities vanish initially,
we conclude that they always vanish. If, in addition,
\sigma_{11}-\sigma_{22}
and
n_{11}-n_{22}
vanish at some later point, we similarly, by uniqueness, conclude
that they always vanish, contradicting the properties of the initial data. Thus we always have
(\sigma_{11},n_{11})\neq(\sigma_{22},n_{22})
for all
t
. These
observations lead to the conclusion that that the initial data induced by the development at the constant-
t
hypersurfaces are LRS. Next, since

\sigma_{22}=\sigma_{33}
, (
3.13
) implies that
a_{2}=a_{3}
. This means that the metric can be written

g=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\xi^{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(t)(\xi^{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+\xi^{3}\otimes\xi^{3}),

(3.14)

where
\{\xi^{i}\}
is the frame dual to the frame used for the initial data, say
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
.

Assume
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}[V]
. Then there is an
x\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
; see
Remark
2.5
, Lemma
2.9
and Definition
2.12
. We can, without loss of generality, assume
that
x=(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
is such that
\bar{n}_{1}=0
,
\bar{n}_{3}=-\bar{n}_{2}
and
k_{2}=k_{3}
; i.e.
\bar{n}_{11}=0
,
\bar{n}_{33}=-\bar{n}_{22}
and

\bar{\sigma}_{22}=\bar{\sigma}_{33}
. Since the equation for
n_{11}

is homogeneous, this means that
n_{11}=0
. Moreover, (
3.1b
) and (
3.3
) can be used to derive a homogeneous first order equation
for
\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{33}
and
n_{22}+n_{33}
. Since these quantities vanish initially, we conclude that they always vanish. This means that the
permutation symmetry carries over to the development. Moreover, since
\sigma_{22}=\sigma_{33}
, (
3.13
) implies that
a_{2}=a_{3}
. This
means that the metric can again be written as in (
3.14
).

Assume
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}[V]
. Then, by the previous arguments, it cannot induce initial data with a higher degree of symmetry at another time.
The final statement of the proposition follows.
∎

In what follows, it will be of interest to know if the mean curvature is integrable or not.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-negative,
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}[V]
. Let
J
and
\theta
be the existence interval
and mean curvature, respectively, of the corresponding development, see Proposition
1.31
. Assume that
\theta
is initially non-negative.
If
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, there are the following two possibilities:

•

\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and
\theta(t)>0
for all
t\in J
. Moreover, either
J=\mathbb{R}
or
J=(0,\infty)
(after a time translation, if
necessary). If
J=\mathbb{R}
, then
\theta\notin L^{1}(-\infty,0)
and
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,\infty)
. If
J=(0,\infty)
, then

\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
;
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
; and
\theta\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
.

•

\mathfrak{I}
is not isotropic;
\theta(t)>0
for all
t\in J
; up to a translation in
t
,
J=(0,\infty)
;
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty

as
t\downarrow 0
;
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all
t
;
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
; and
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
.

In addition,
\theta
converges to a non-negative real number as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
,
X(t):=\mathfrak{X}[\theta(t),\phi(t),\phi_{t}(t)]
, see (
1.13
), and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]
, then
J=(0,t_{+})
and there is a
t_{0}\in J

such that
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
;
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
;
X(t)>0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
; and
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,t_{0})
.

Remark 3.5
.

In order to illustrate that
J=\mathbb{R}
can occur, assume that there is an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
,
V(s_{0})>0
and
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})<0
. There is then an
isotropic Bianchi type I solution with
\theta(t)=[3V(s_{0})]^{1/2}
and
\phi(t)=s_{0}
. The corresponding spacetime is a vacuum solution with a
positive cosmological constant. Since it arises from trivial initial data, it is, however, excluded in the present setting. On the other hand, there are also
non-vacuum solutions converging to the corresponding equilibrium; see the proof of Proposition
11.1
below. Note, in particular, that
these solutions do not have crushing singularities, the setting we are interested in here. More generally,
the isotropic Bianchi type I solutions are non-generic; the complement of the corresponding set of initial data in Bianchi type I is open and dense
(as well as of full measure).

Proof.

Let us first consider the case that
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and that there is a
t_{1}
in the existence interval such that
\theta(t_{1})=0
. Then, due to the
discussion prior to Definition
2.1
, the initial data are trivial, a conclusion that contradicts
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
. Since

\theta\geq 0
initially, we conclude that
\theta(t)>0
for all
t\in J
if
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Next, recall that the scalar curvatures of the constant-
t

hypersurfaces are given by
