\bar{S}:=-n_{ij}n^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(n)^{2};

(3.15)

see, e.g.,
[
54
, (19.6), p. 209]
. Consider, moreover, the function

X:=\theta^{2}-\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}-3V\circ\phi,

(3.16)

used in
[
40
,
46
]
. Note that (
3.1d
) and (
3.15
) imply that

X=-\tfrac{3}{2}\bar{S}+\tfrac{3}{2}\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}.

(3.17)

Since
\bar{S}\leq 0
for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
,
X
is non-negative.

Assume now that there is a
t_{1}
such that
X(t_{1})=0
. Then
\sigma_{ij}(t_{1})=0
and
\bar{S}(t_{1})=0
. By arguments similar to those given prior to
Definition
2.1
, this means that the solution is isotropic and of Bianchi type I. If
X
ever vanishes,
X
thus vanishes
identically. Due to (
3.16
), this means that as long as
\theta
is bounded,
\phi_{t}
is bounded (so that
\phi
cannot blow up in finite time).
On the other hand,
\theta(t)>0
for all
t
, so that (
3.1c
) implies that
\theta
decays to a non-negative value to
the future. In particular, the solution cannot blow up in finite time to the future, so that
[t_{0},\infty)\subset J
. There are two possibilies
concerning the existence interval. Either the existence interval is
\mathbb{R}
, or it is
(0,\infty)
after a time translation, if necessary. If
\theta

converges to a strictly positive number to the future, then it is clear that
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
. If
\theta
converges to zero, note that
(
3.1c
) and (
3.1d
) imply that
\theta_{t}\geq-\theta^{2}
, so that

\theta(t)\geq\theta(1)\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{1}^{t}\theta(s)ds\big{)}.

For this to be consistent with
\theta
converging to zero, we have to have
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
. If the existence interval is
\mathbb{R}
, then,
since
\theta(t)\geq\theta(0)>0
for all
t\leq 0
, it is clear that
\theta\notin L^{1}(-\infty,0)
. Finally, consider the case that
the existence interval equals
(0,\infty)
. Then
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
, since
\theta
increases to the past, and since the
existence interval would be unbounded to the past in case
\theta
were bounded to the past; see Remark
1.32
. For a proof that

\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
in this case, see below. If
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
we, from now on, therefore restrict our attention to anisotropic solutions such that

X(t)>0
and
\theta(t)>0
for all
t
.

If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, we assume that
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]
. By Definition
1.53
, there is thus a
t_{0}\in J
such that
\theta(t_{0})>0

and
X(t_{0})>0
. If
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, fix an arbitrary
t_{0}\in J
. Then
X(t_{0})>0
and it can be computed that

X_{t}=\theta(\bar{S}-3\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij})=-\tfrac{2}{3}\theta X-2\theta\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij};

see
[
55
, (26.12), p. 452]
, where we have set the terms associated with the Vlasov matter to zero. In case
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, we know that
\theta>0

and
X>0
. In case
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, the fact that
\theta(t_{0})>0
and
X(t_{0})>0
implies that
X_{t}(t_{0})<0
, so that
X
increases to the past. Combining this
observation with (
3.16
) implies that
\theta
remains positive to the past. To summarise, if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, then
X(t)>0
and
\theta(t)>0
for

t\leq t_{0}
. This means that for all Bianchi types,

X_{t}\leq-\tfrac{2}{3}\theta X

(3.18)

for
t\leq t_{0}
. Letting

\Theta(t)=\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t}\theta(s)ds,

(
3.18
) implies that for
t\leq t_{0}

\tfrac{d}{dt}(e^{2\Theta/3}X)=e^{2\Theta/3}\left(\tfrac{2}{3}\theta X+X_{t}\right)\leq 0.

In particular, due to (
3.16
) and the fact that
V
is non-negative,

e^{2\Theta(t)/3}\theta^{2}(t)\geq e^{2\Theta(t)/3}X(t)\geq X(t_{0}),

for
t\leq t_{0}
, so that

\tfrac{1}{3}e^{\Theta(t)/3}\theta(t)\geq\tfrac{1}{3}X^{1/2}(t_{0}).

Integrating this inequality from
t_{a}
to
t_{b}
, where
t_{a}\leq t_{b}\leq t_{0}
, yields

e^{\Theta(t_{b})/3}-e^{\Theta(t_{a})/3}\geq\tfrac{1}{3}X^{1/2}(t_{0})(t_{b}-t_{a}),

(3.19)

so that

e^{\Theta(t_{b})/3}\geq\tfrac{1}{3}X^{1/2}(t_{0})(t_{b}-t_{a})+e^{\Theta(t_{a})/3}.

If the solution existed globally to the past, we could let
t_{a}\rightarrow-\infty
in this inequality, something which is clearly impossible.
By a time translation, if necessary, we can thus assume that
J=(0,t_{+})
. Combining this observation with Remark
1.32

yields the conclusion that
\theta(t)
is unbounded to the past. Next, note that (
3.1c
) and (
3.17
) imply that

\theta_{t}=-\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}-\tfrac{1}{3}X-\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}.

(3.20)

In other words,
\theta
increases monotonically to the past for
t\leq t_{0}
, since
X(t)>0
for
t\leq t_{0}
. Since
\theta
is unbounded to the past,
this means that
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
.

For the remainder of the analysis, we consider the cases
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
separately. Assume
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Since
\theta
is decaying
to the future and is bounded from below by
0
, we obtain global existence to the future by Remark
1.32
. Thus the existence
interval is
(0,\infty)
and
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
. Combining (
3.1c
) and (
3.1d
) yields

\theta_{t}=-\theta^{2}-\bar{S}+3V\circ\phi.

(3.21)

In particular,

\tfrac{d}{dt}(e^{\Theta}\theta)=e^{\Theta}(\theta^{2}+\theta_{t})=e^{\Theta}[-\bar{S}+3V\circ\phi]\geq 0,

since
\bar{S}\leq 0
when
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Thus
e^{\Theta}\theta
decreases to the past, so that it converges to a constant
\alpha_{0}\geq 0
. We must thus have

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\Theta(t)=-\infty,

so that
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
(this argument also applies if
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
and
J=(0,\infty)
). In order to prove that

\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
, note that (
3.9
) implies that

\theta_{t}=-\tfrac{1}{3}(1+q)\theta^{2},

(3.22)

where

q:=3\tfrac{\sigma_{ij}\sigma^{ij}}{\theta^{2}}+3\tfrac{\phi_{t}^{2}}{\theta^{2}}-3\tfrac{V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}.

(3.23)

Note that, due to (
3.1d
), all the terms on the right hand side of (
3.23
) are bounded. In fact,
q\in[-1,2]
,
so that
|q|\leq 2
. Combining this observation with (
3.22
) yields, for
t\geq t_{0}
,

\theta(t)=\theta(t_{0})\exp\big{(}-\tfrac{1}{3}\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t}[1+q(s)]\theta(s)ds\big{)}\geq\theta(t_{0})\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t}\theta(s)ds\big{)}.

(3.24)

We know
\theta
to be monotonically decaying. If it decays to a strictly positive number, then it is obvious that
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
.
If
\theta(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow 0
, then (
3.24
) implies that
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
.

Next, consider the case that
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
. All that remains to be demonstrated is that
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,t_{0})
. Assuming the opposite, it follows
that
f:=n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}
remains bounded to the past;
f_{t}=-\theta f
due to (
3.3
). Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
(n_{1}n_{2}n_{3})(t)\leq\alpha
for all

t\leq t_{0}
. Due to Lemma
3.3
,

n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-2(n_{1}n_{2}+n_{2}n_{3}+n_{3}n_{1})\geq-10\alpha^{2/3}

for all
t\leq t_{0}
. In other words,
\bar{S}
is bounded from above to the past. On the other hand,

\tfrac{d}{dt}(e^{2\Theta}\theta)=e^{2\Theta}(\theta^{2}-\bar{S}+3V\circ\phi),

(3.25)

where we appealed to (
3.21
). Since
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
and
\bar{S}
is bounded from above to the past,
there is a
t_{1}\in(0,t_{0})
such that the right hand side of (
3.25
) is
\geq 0
for
t\leq t_{1}
. This means that

e^{2\Theta}\theta
converges to a non-negative real number to the past. Since
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
, this means that

\Theta(t)\rightarrow-\infty
; i.e.,
\theta
is not integrable to the past.
∎

Beyond the case that both
X
and
\theta
are strictly positive, it is of interest to consider the following possibility.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
; see Definition
1.53
. Let
J
and
\theta
be the existence
interval and mean curvature, respectively, of the corresponding development, see Proposition
1.31
. Then, up to a time translation,
there are
t_{1}\in J
and
t_{+}>0
such that
\theta(t)>0
for
t\leq t_{1}
;
J=(0,t_{+})
;
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
;
\theta_{t}(t)<0

for all
t\leq t_{1}
; and
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,t_{1})
.

Proof.

By assumption, see Definition
1.53
, there is a
t_{0}\in J=(t_{-},t_{+})
such that
\theta(t)>0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. If there is a
t\leq t_{0}

such that
X(t)>0
, then the desired conclusions follow from Lemma
3.4
. We can therefore assume that
X(t)\leq 0
for all

t\leq t_{0}
. Combining this assumption with the assumptions of the lemma yields
X(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. Next, again by
the assumptions of the lemma,
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. On the other hand, (
3.16
) can be reformulated to

1=\frac{X}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\phi_{t}^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{3V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}},

(3.26)

so that
3\phi_{t}^{2}(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 2
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. Combining the above observations and assumptions with (
3.20
) yields a

T\in J
such that
\theta_{t}\leq-\theta^{2}/2
for all
t\leq T
. This means that
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past, so that
t_{-}=0
. All that
remains to be demonstrated is that
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,t_{1})
. However, this follows by the same argument as at the end of the proof of
Lemma
3.4
.
∎

In the case of Bianchi type IX with a vanishing potential, we obtain the following improvement.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
V=0
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}[V]
. Let
J
and
\theta
be the existence interval and mean curvature, respectively, of the corresponding
development, see Proposition
1.31
. Then
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
, where
t_{-},t_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, if
t_{0}\in(t_{-},t_{+})
,
then
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
;
\theta(t)\rightarrow-\infty
as
t\uparrow t_{+}
;
\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{-},t_{0})
;
and
\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{0},t_{+})
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that, in the spatially homogeneous setting, orthogonal stiff fluids are equivalent to a scalar field. In fact, letting

\rho=\phi_{t}^{2}/2
, where
\phi
is the scalar field, the stress energy tensor of the scalar field can be interpreted as the stress energy
tensor of an orthogonal stiff fluid; see, e.g.,
[
57
, Section 1.4]
. Given this correspondence, the statements are essentially immediate
consequences of results in
[
50
]
. In particular, the statement
concerning the existence interval follows from
[
50
, Lemma 21.8, p. 493]
. In order to deduce the statements concerning the mean
curvature, note that, according to
[
50
, Lemma 21.6, p. 492]
, there is a
t_{0}\in(t_{-},t_{+})
such that the mean curvature

\theta
is strictly positive on the interval
I_{-}:=(t_{-},t_{0})
and strictly negative on
I_{+}:=(t_{0},t_{+})
. Next, let the time coordinates

\tau_{\mp}
be defined on
I_{\mp}
(up to constants) by
d\tau_{\mp}/dt=\theta/3
, see
[
50
, (137), p. 487]
. Then the asymptotic regime

t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
corresponds to
\tau_{\pm}(t)\rightarrow-\infty
; see
[
50
, Lemma 22.5, p. 498]
. Note that this implies the
non-integrability statements of the proposition. Finally, since the mean curvature
\theta
satisfies
\partial_{\tau_{\pm}}\theta=-(1+q)\theta
, see

[
50
, (139), p. 487]
, where
q=2\Omega+2\Sigma_{+}^{2}+2\Sigma_{-}^{2}
,
\Omega=3\rho/\theta^{2}
and
\rho\geq 0

is the energy density of the scalar field, the statements concerning the divergence of the mean curvature follow.
∎

When relating solutions to asymptotic data, it is useful to know that the following refinement of Lemma
3.4
holds.

Lemma 3.8
.

Assume, in addition to the conditions of Lemma
3.4
, that
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
; see
Definition
1.62
. If
\bar{\theta}>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
, then, using the notation of Lemma
3.4
,

\{\theta(t)\,|\,t\in J\}
contains
([3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2},\infty)
. If
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
, then
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t
. If

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
, then
\theta_{t}\leq 0
and the zeros of
\theta_{t}
are isolated.

Proof.

If
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
, then
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t
due to Lemma
3.4
. If
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
, then

\theta_{t}=-3\phi_{t}^{2}/2\leq 0
; see (
3.1c
). In particular, if
\theta_{t}(t_{1})=0
, then
\phi_{t}(t_{1})=0
. If, in addition,
V^{\prime}[\phi(t_{1})]=0
,
then
\phi(t)=\phi(t_{1})
for all
t
; cf. (
3.4
). This means that the original initial data had to be trivial, contradicting the assumptions.
If
\phi_{t}(t_{1})=0
, we must thus have
V^{\prime}[\phi(t_{1})]\neq 0
, so that
\phi_{tt}(t_{1})\neq 0
due to (
3.4
). Thus
\phi_{t}
is non-zero in a
punctured neighbourhood of
t_{1}
, so that
\theta_{t}<0
in a punctured neighbourhood of
t_{1}
. What remains to be demonstrated is that the desired
statement concerning the range of the mean curvature holds.

Due to
[
55
, Corollary 26.4, p. 450]
, we know that there is a
0\leq V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\theta(t)\rightarrow(3V_{1})^{1/2}
and

\phi_{t}^{2}(t)+2V\circ\phi(t)\rightarrow 2V_{1}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. If
V_{1}=0
, the desired statement follows, assuming
\theta
diverges to

\infty
to the past. We can therefore assume that
V_{1}>0
. Since
\theta
is monotonically decreasing, see (
3.1c
),
\theta(t)\geq(3V_{1})^{1/2}

for all
t
. Due to the arguments presented in the proof of
[
55
, Corollary 26.7, p. 452]
, we also know that
\phi_{t}(t)\rightarrow 0
as

t\rightarrow\infty
and that
\phi(t)
converges to a limit (finite or infinite). Moreover,

V^{\prime}\circ\phi(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Assume that
\phi(t)
converges to a finite limit, say
s_{0}
. Then
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
and it is clear
that
\theta(t)
converges to
[3V(s_{0})]^{1/2}
. If we know that
\theta
diverges to
\infty
to the past, the desired statement follows in this case.
If
\phi
tends to an infinite limit, then either
V_{1}=v_{\infty,+}
or
V_{1}=v_{\infty,-}
, using the terminology of Definition
1.62
. Again, the
statement follows in this case, assuming
\theta
diverges to
\infty
to the past. If
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
,
\theta
diverges to
\infty

to the past due to Lemma
3.4
. It therefore only remains to prove divergence for
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
.

Assume, in order to obtain a contradiction, that
\theta
is bounded to the past. Since
\theta
is monotonically decreasing, this means that
\theta

converges to
\theta_{-}\in(0,\infty)
to the past. Moreover,
J=\mathbb{R}
due to Remark
1.32
. Due to (
3.1d
),
\phi_{t}

and
V\circ\phi
(and thereby
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
; see Definition
1.62
) remain bounded to the past. Due to (
3.4
), this
means that
\phi_{t}
and
\phi_{tt}
remain bounded to the past. Since
\phi_{t}^{2}
is integrable to the past, this means that
\phi_{t}
converges to zero to the
past. Combining this observation with (
3.1d
) and the fact that
\theta
converges, we conclude that
V\circ\phi(t)
converges to the past. By
an argument similar to
the proof of
[
55
, Corollary 26.7, p. 452]
, we conclude that
\phi
converges to a limit (finite or infinite). Due to the assumptions, this means that

V^{\prime}\circ\phi
converges to a limit. Moreover, this limit is a real number since
V\circ\phi
remains bounded to the past. Since
\theta
converges to a
finite value,
\phi_{t}
converges to zero and
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
converges, it follows from (
3.4
) that
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
has to converge to zero. If

\phi(t)
converges to a finite number, say
s_{0}
, we conclude that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
. This means that
\theta(t)\rightarrow[3V(s_{0})]^{1/2}
, contradicting the
assumptions (note that
\theta
is monotonically increasing to the past). Assume now that
\phi(t)\rightarrow\pm\infty
. Then
V\circ\phi(t)
converges
to
v_{\infty,\pm}
. This means that
\theta(t)\rightarrow(3v_{\infty,\pm})^{1/2}
, again contradicting the assumptions. To conclude, the assumption that

\theta
does not diverge to infinity to the past yields a contradiction.
∎

3.3
Wainwright-Hsu formulation

In order to analyse the asymptotics, it is convenient to expansion normalise the variables in analogy with the work of Wainwright and Hsu

[
59
]
. We begin by changing the time coordinate.

Lemma 3.9
.

Assume the conditions of Lemma
3.4
to be satisfied with
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and let
J
denote the existence interval of the
development. Let
\tau
be a function satisfying

\frac{d\tau}{dt}=\frac{\theta}{3}.

(3.27)

Then
\tau
is a smooth diffeomorphism from
J
to
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
3.4
, we know that
\theta
is strictly positive, that
\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{-},t_{0})
and that

\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{0},\infty)
, where
J=(t_{-},\infty)
and
t_{0}\in J
. The statement follows.
∎

In the Bianchi type IX setting, the following result holds.

Lemma 3.10
.

Assume that either the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are satisfied with
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
or that the conditions of
Lemma
3.6
are satisfied. Let
J=(0,t_{+})
denote the existence interval of the development. Then there is a
t_{0}\in J

such that
\theta(t)>0
for
t\leq t_{0}
. Define
\tau
by
\tau(t_{0})=0
and (
3.27
). Then
\tau
is a smooth diffeomorphism from
(0,t_{0}]
to

(-\infty,0]
.

Proof.

The conclusion follows from the definition of
\tau
and Lemmas
3.4
and
3.6
.
∎

In what follows, we assume that either the conditions of Lemma
3.9
or of Lemma
3.10
are satisfied, and we
restrict the time coordinate
\tau
accordingly. Moreover, we expansion normalise the variables as in
[
59
]
(see also

[
54
, Section 22.1, p. 233]
). In other words,

\Sigma_{ij}:=\frac{\sigma_{ij}}{\theta},\ \ N_{ij}:=\frac{n_{ij}}{\theta},\ \ \Sigma_{+}:=\frac{3}{2}(\Sigma_{22}+\Sigma_{33}),\ \ \Sigma_{-}:=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}(\Sigma_{22}-\Sigma_{33}).

(3.28)

Let
N_{i}
and
\Sigma_{i}

be the diagonal elements of
N_{ij}
and
\Sigma_{ij}
respectively. In the derivations to follow, it is convenient to keep
in mind that

3(\Sigma_{1}^{2}+\Sigma_{2}^{2}+\Sigma_{3}^{2})=3\Sigma_{ij}\Sigma^{ij}=2(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}).

(3.29)

Next, let

\displaystyle\phi_{0}:=

\displaystyle\phi,\ \ \ \phi_{1}:=\phi^{\prime},

(3.30a)

\displaystyle\Psi:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}+\frac{3V\circ\phi_{0}}{\theta^{2}},\ \ \ \Omega:=\frac{9}{2}\frac{V\circ\phi_{0}}{\theta^{2}},

(3.30b)

\displaystyle q:=

\displaystyle 2(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi-\Omega).

(3.30c)

In (
3.30a
), the prime denotes a derivative with respect to
\tau
. With notation as in (
3.30
), equation
(
3.9
) yields

\theta^{\prime}=-(1+q)\theta.

(3.31)

In terms of the expansion normalised variables, the equations (
3.3
) and (
3.1b
) read

\displaystyle N_{i}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(q+6\Sigma_{i})N_{i},

(3.32a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\Sigma_{i}-3S_{i}

(3.32b)

respectively, where

S_{i}:=B_{i}-\frac{1}{3}(B_{1}+B_{2}+B_{3})

(3.33)

and, if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
,

B_{i}:=N_{i}(N_{i}-N_{j}-N_{k});

cf. (
3.2b
). Since

6\Sigma_{1}=-4\Sigma_{+},\ \ \ 6\Sigma_{2}=2\Sigma_{+}+2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-},\ \ \ 6\Sigma_{3}=2\Sigma_{+}-2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-},

(3.34)

(
3.32
) yields

\displaystyle N_{i}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle f_{i}(q,\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})N_{i},

(3.35a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\pm}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\Sigma_{\pm}-3S_{\pm},

(3.35b)

where

\displaystyle f_{1}(q,\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-}):=

\displaystyle q-4\Sigma_{+},

(3.36a)

\displaystyle f_{2}(q,\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-}):=

\displaystyle q+2\Sigma_{+}+2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-},

(3.36b)

\displaystyle f_{3}(q,\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-}):=

\displaystyle q+2\Sigma_{+}-2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}

(3.36c)

and

\displaystyle S_{+}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}-N_{1}(2N_{1}-N_{2}-N_{3})],

(3.37a)

\displaystyle S_{-}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}(N_{3}-N_{2})(N_{1}-N_{2}-N_{3}).

(3.37b)

Turning to the evolution equations for
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
, see (
3.4
), they can be written

\displaystyle\phi_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\phi_{1},

(3.38a)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\phi_{1}-\frac{9V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}}{\theta^{2}}

(3.38b)

Finally, the Hamiltonian constraint can be written

\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi+\frac{3}{4}[N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2}-2(N_{1}N_{2}+N_{2}N_{3}+N_{1}N_{3})]=1.

(3.39)

This relation can be used to deduce that

q=2-\frac{3}{2}[N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2}-2(N_{1}N_{2}+N_{2}N_{3}+N_{1}N_{3})]-2\Omega.

(3.40)

It is important to note that if we let
N_{i}
,
\Sigma_{\pm}
,
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
and
\theta
be solutions to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.38
), where
q
is given by (
3.40
), and if we define
f
by

f:=\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi+\frac{3}{4}[N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2}-2(N_{1}N_{2}+N_{2}N_{3}+N_{1}N_{3})]-1,

then

f^{\prime}=-2(2-q)f.

(3.41)

It is also of interest to note that the evolution equation for
\Psi
can be written

\Psi^{\prime}=-2(2-q)\Psi+4\Omega.

(3.42)

For future reference, it is useful to make the following observation.

Remark 3.11
.

When analysing the asymptotics of solutions, it is convenient to work with the
\Sigma_{\pm}
as opposed to the
\Sigma_{i}
, since this choice reduces
the number of variables. However, the natural geometric assumptions and conclusions are phrased in terms of isometry classes of initial
data, isometry classes of data on the singularity and isometry classes of solutions. This necessitates taking quotients, as in, e.g.,
Lemma
2.14
. Moreover, the associated groups act naturally on solutions when expressed using the variables
\Sigma_{i}
.
For this reason, it is useful to keep in mind that (
3.29
) holds. In particular,
q
introduced in (
3.30c
) and the
Hamiltonian constraint (
3.39
) are invariant
under the maps
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
introduced in (
2.1
) (where the
\bar{n}_{i}
are replaced by the
N_{i}
, the
k_{i}
are replaced by
the
\Sigma_{i}
and the
\bar{\phi}_{i}
are replaced by the
\phi_{i}
). Note also that with this choice, (
3.32
) replaces
(
3.35
)–(
3.37
), so that
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
maps solutions to solutions.

We make the following observation concerning the existence interval of solutions to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.39
).

Lemma 3.12
.

Assume that
V
is a smooth non-negative function. Then the existence interval of solutions to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.39
) which are not of Bianchi type IX is
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Since we have excluded Bianchi type IX, the polynomial in the
N_{i}
appearing on the left hand side of (
3.39
) is non-negative.
Combining this observation with (
3.39
) yields the conclusion that
\Sigma_{+}
,
\Sigma_{-}
and
\Psi
are bounded. This means that

\phi_{1}
is bounded, so that
\phi_{0}
cannot blow up in finite time. From (
3.30
) and (
3.39
),
it follows that
-1\leq q\leq 2
. Since
-1\leq q\leq 2

and the
\Sigma_{\pm}
are bounded, (
3.35a
) implies that the
N_{i}
cannot blow up in finite time. Finally,
(
3.31
) implies that
\theta
can neither blow up nor go to zero in finite time. In short, the solution cannot blow up in finite
time, and we obtain global existence.
∎

Chapter 4
Asymptotic behaviour in the direction of the singularity

Our next goal is to analyse the asymptotic behaviour of solutions in the direction of the singularity. We begin by introducing appropriate notation
and characterising the solutions that converge to a special point.

4.1
Characterising convergence to a special point

We use the following terminology in the formulation of the results.

Definition 4.1
.

The unit circle in the
\Sigma_{+}\Sigma_{-}
-plane is referred to as the
Kasner circle
.
On the Kasner circle, the points
(-1,0)
,
(1/2,\sqrt{3}/2)

and
(1/2,-\sqrt{3}/2)
are referred to as the
special (or Taub) points
.
In terms of the variables
\Sigma_{i}
and
N_{i}
introduced in
connection with (
3.28
), the subspaces of locally rotationally symmetric (LRS) solutions are defined by
N_{i}=N_{j}
,

\Sigma_{i}=\Sigma_{j}
and
(N_{k},\Sigma_{k})\neq(N_{i},\Sigma_{i})
for some
i,j,k
satisfying
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
; i.e., there are three
such subspaces.

Remark 4.2
.

The definition of local rotational symmetry coincides with the one for initial data introduced in Definition
2.12
and
corresponds to the one introduced in Definition
1.8
; see Lemma
2.10
and the definition of the
expansion normalised variables.

Remark 4.3
.

Up to a rotation in the
\Sigma_{+}\Sigma_{-}
-plane and a suitable corresponding permutation of the
N_{i}
(or, alternatively, the application of a

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}\in\Gamma^{+,\mathrm{ev}}
when using the
\Sigma_{i}
,
N_{i}
variables), the LRS subspaces are given by the conditions that

\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
. Note also that the special points belong to the intersection of the LRS subspaces and the Kasner circle.

In the derivation of the asymptotics, it will be of interest to keep the following observation in mind. The main ideas of the proof go back to

[
49
, pp. 721–722]
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\beta_{V}}^{0}
for some
0<\beta_{V}\in\mathbb{R}
and assume that either the conditions of Lemma
3.9
or the conditions of
Lemma
3.10
are satisfied. Consider the corresponding solution to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.39
). Then the
existence interval includes
(-\infty,0]
. If, in addition,
[\Sigma_{+}(\tau),\Sigma_{-}(\tau)]\rightarrow(-1,0)
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, then the solution
is contained in the invariant set defined by
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
.

Remark 4.5
.

By the symmetries of the equations, there are similar statements in case
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
converges to one of the other special points. Moreover, in
case
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, the assumption that
\Sigma_{-}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
can be omitted.

Remark 4.6
.

Due to this result, if one of the eigenvalues of the expansion normalised Weingarten map converges to
1
and the other two converge to
0
in the
direction of the singularity, then the solution has an additional symmetry; it is LRS. This is the reason we impose the final condition in
Definition
1.17
.

Proof.

Due to the fact that the conditions of Lemma
3.9
or Lemma
3.10
are satisfied, the existence interval
includes
(-\infty,0]
. If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
and the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are satisfied, then
X(\tau)>0
for

\tau\leq\tau_{0}\leq 0
and some
\tau_{0}
. This means that
3V\circ\phi_{0}/\theta^{2}\leq 1
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, see (
3.16
), so that

\Psi-\Omega\geq-\tfrac{3}{2}\tfrac{V\circ\phi_{0}}{\theta^{2}}\geq-\tfrac{1}{2}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Thus
q
introduced in (
3.30c
) satisfies
q\geq 2\Sigma_{+}^{2}-1
. Since
\Sigma_{+}
tends to
-1
, this means that
q
is bounded
from below by
1
in the limit. Next, assume that the conditions of Lemma
3.6
are satisfied. Then
V\circ\phi_{0}/\theta^{2}

converges to zero in the direction of the singularity. This means that
\Omega\rightarrow 0
. Combining this observation with the definition of

q
and the fact that
\Sigma_{+}\rightarrow-1
, it follows that
q
is bounded from below by
2
in the limit. Combining these observations with
(
3.35a
) yields the conclusion that in the Bianchi type IX setting,
N_{1}
,
N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}
converge to zero exponentially in
the direction of the singularity. In particular, in the case of Bianchi type IX,
-N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})
converges to zero. On the other hand,
(
3.39
) can be written

\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi+\frac{3}{4}[N_{1}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}]-\frac{3}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})=1.

(4.1)

Due to the fact that the last term on the left hand side of (
4.1
) converges to zero and
\Sigma_{+}
converges to
-1
, it is
clear that
\Psi\rightarrow 0
(so that
\phi_{1}\rightarrow 0
and
\Omega\rightarrow 0
due to (
3.30b
) and the fact that
V
is
non-negative), and that the polynomial in the
N_{i}
appearing on the left hand side of (
3.39
) converges to zero. When

\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, we obtain the same conclusions, since the fourth term on the left hand side of (
3.39
) is non-negative. In fact,
in this case, we can also deduce that
\Sigma_{-}\rightarrow 0
from the assumption that
\Sigma_{+}\rightarrow-1
. Thus,
irrespective of whether
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
or
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, the above observations, combined with (
3.40
), yield the conclusion that
q

converges to
2
. Appealing to (
3.35a
), it furthermore follows that for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a constant
C_{\epsilon}
such that

|N_{1}(\tau)|+|(N_{1}N_{2})(\tau)|+|(N_{1}N_{3})(\tau)|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}

(4.2)

for
\tau\leq 0
. Similarly, due to (
3.31
),
1/\theta(\tau)\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(3-\epsilon)\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this estimate
with the fact that
\phi^{\prime}
converges to zero and
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\beta_{V}}^{0}
, it follows that
|\Omega(\tau)|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
.

Next, define

\mathfrak{f}:=\frac{4}{3}\Sigma_{-}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}.

(4.3)

Since the set defined by
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
is invariant,
\mathfrak{f}
is either identically zero or always strictly positive. It can also be computed
that

\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=-\frac{8}{3}(2-q)\Sigma_{-}^{2}+2(q+2\Sigma_{+})(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}+4\sqrt{3}N_{1}(N_{2}-N_{3})\Sigma_{-}.

(4.4)

On the other hand, by the above observations,
2-q
,
q+2\Sigma_{+}
and
N_{1}
converge to zero in the direction of the singularity. It follows that for
every
\epsilon>0
, there is a
T
such that
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}(\tau)\leq\epsilon\mathfrak{f}(\tau)
for all
\tau\leq T
. This means that

\mathfrak{f}(T)\leq\mathfrak{f}(\tau)\exp[\epsilon(T-\tau)]

(4.5)

for all
\tau\leq T
. Next, (
3.35b
) and (
3.40
) yield

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=-\frac{3}{2}(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}(\Sigma_{+}+1)+\frac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}(2-\Sigma_{+})-\frac{3}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})(1-2\Sigma_{+})-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}.

(4.6)

In case
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, then
\Sigma_{+}+1\geq 0
, and the first term is negative. In case
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
and
\Sigma_{+}+1\leq 0
, then
(
4.1
) implies that

-(\Sigma_{+}+1)(-\Sigma_{+}+1)+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi+\frac{3}{4}[N_{1}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}]=\frac{3}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3}).

This means that

|\Sigma_{+}+1|\leq\frac{3}{4}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})

when
\Sigma_{+}+1\leq 0
. Combining this observation with (
4.2
) yields the conclusion that
|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}
for
all
\tau\leq 0
, assuming
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1\leq 0
. Combining this observation with (
4.2
), (
4.6
) and the
fact that
|\Omega(\tau)|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
, it follows that, irrespective of whether
\Sigma_{+}+1\leq 0
or
\Sigma_{+}+1\geq 0
,

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Integrating this estimate from
-\infty
to
\tau
yields
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}
. Since the negative part of

\Sigma_{+}+1
satisfies a similar bound, it follows that

|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1|\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Next, note that the constraint (
4.1
) yields

\frac{3}{4}\mathfrak{f}\leq\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\frac{3}{4}(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}+\Psi+\frac{3}{4}N_{1}^{2}=\frac{3}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})+(1-\Sigma_{+})(1+\Sigma_{+})\leq C_{\epsilon}e^{(6-\epsilon)\tau}.

Combining this estimate with (
4.5
) yields the conclusion that

\mathfrak{f}(T)\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp[(6-\epsilon)\tau+\epsilon(T-\tau)]

for all
\tau\leq T
. Choosing
\epsilon
small enough and letting
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
yields the conclusion that the right hand side converges to
zero. Thus
\mathfrak{f}(T)=0
and the statement of the proposition follows.
∎

In what follows, it will also be convenient to have the following asymptotic characterisation of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
solutions.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Consider a solution to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.39
) with
N_{1}=0
and
N_{2}N_{3}>0
. Assume that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(N_{2}/N_{3},\Sigma_{+},\phi^{\prime})(\tau)=(1,\sigma_{+},\Phi_{1}),

(4.7)

where
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}/6=1
and
\sigma_{+}>-1
. Then the solution is contained in the invariant set defined by
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
;
i.e., the solution is either LRS or isotropic.

Proof.

Combining the assumptions of the proposition with (
3.39
) yields the conclusion that
\Sigma_{-}
,
\Omega
and
N_{2}-N_{3}
converge to zero as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Due to these conclusions and (
3.30c
), it follows that
q\rightarrow 2
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Combining these observations with the assumptions and the equations, it is clear that
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
converge to zero
exponentially. By the assumptions concerning
V
; the fact that
q
converges to
2
; the fact that
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{6}
and (
3.31
),
it is also clear that
\Omega
decays exponentially. This means that
q-2
converges to zero exponentially. Since
q-2
and the
N_{i}
converge to zero
exponentially, (
3.35b
) implies that
\Sigma_{+}-\sigma_{+}
and
\Sigma_{-}
decay exponentially. Returning to the equations with this information at hand yields

|N_{i}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2(1+\sigma_{+})\tau},\ \ \ |q(\tau)-2|+|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\epsilon\tau}

(4.8)

for
i=2,3
and
\tau\leq 0
, where
\epsilon:=\min\{2(1+\sigma_{+}),3(1-\alpha_{V})\}
. Next, note that

\left(\tfrac{N_{2}}{N_{3}}\right)^{\prime}=4\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}\tfrac{N_{2}}{N_{3}}.

Combining this observation with (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) yields
N_{2}(\tau)/N_{3}(\tau)-1=O(e^{2\epsilon\tau})
,
so that

[N_{2}(\tau)-N_{3}(\tau)]^{2}=N_{3}^{2}(\tau)[N_{2}(\tau)/N_{3}(\tau)-1]^{2}\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})\tau+4\epsilon\tau]

(4.9)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Next, let

h(\tau):=\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}[2-q(s)]ds.

Then

|(e^{h}\Sigma_{-})^{\prime}(\tau)|=3e^{h(\tau)}|S_{-}(\tau)|\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})\tau+2\epsilon\tau]

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Integrating this estimate yields the conclusion that

|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)|\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})\tau+2\epsilon\tau]

(4.10)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Defining
\mathfrak{f}
as in (
4.3
), the estimates (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) imply that

\mathfrak{f}(\tau)\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})\tau+4\epsilon\tau]

(4.11)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. On the other hand, (
4.4
) and the fact that
2-q\geq 0
imply that
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\leq 2(q+2\Sigma_{+})\mathfrak{f}
. Integrating
this estimate from
\tau
to some fixed
T\geq\tau
and using the above observations yields

\mathfrak{f}(T)\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})(T-\tau)]\mathfrak{f}(\tau)

for all
\tau\leq T
. Combining this estimate with (
4.11
) yields

\mathfrak{f}(T)\leq C\exp[4(1+\sigma_{+})T]e^{4\epsilon\tau}

for all
\tau\leq T
. Letting
\tau
tend to
-\infty
yields the conclusion that
\mathfrak{f}(T)=0
, so that the solution is contained in the invariant
set defined by
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
.
∎

4.2
Preliminary observations concerning the asymptotics

As a first step in the derivation of the asymptotics, we consider anisotropic Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II solutions.

Lemma 4.8
.

Consider an anisotropic development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume it to be either
Bianchi type I or non-LRS Bianchi type II. In case it is
of Bianchi type II, assume
N_{1}\neq 0
. Then
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
converges to a limit
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In case the
solution is of non-LRS Bianchi type I,
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
does not belong to any of the line segments connecting a special point to its
antipodal point. In the case of Bianchi type II,
\sigma_{-}\neq 0
and
\sigma_{+}\leq 1/2
. Moreover,
\Psi
converges to a limit
\Psi_{\infty}

and there is a constant
\theta_{\infty}>0
such that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)
converges to
\theta_{\infty}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In the case
of Bianchi type II,
N_{1}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.

Assume, in addition to the above, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then there are constants
\alpha>0
,
\theta_{\infty}>0
,
\Phi_{1}

and
\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)-\Phi_{1}|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{1}\tau-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(4.12a)

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)-\sigma_{-}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(4.12b)

\displaystyle|q(\tau)-2|+|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau}

(4.12c)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. In the case of Bianchi type II,
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
satisfies
\sigma_{+}<1/2
and
\sigma_{-}\neq 0
and there is,
in addition to the above constants, an
m_{1}
such that the following estimate also holds for all
\tau\leq 0
:

|\ln|N_{1}(\tau)|-(2-4\sigma_{+})\tau-m_{1}|\leq Ce^{\alpha\tau}.

(4.13)

In both cases,

\frac{1}{6}\Phi_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}=1.

(4.14)

Finally, in the case of Bianchi type I,
\alpha
can be chosen to equal
6-\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\Phi_{1}|
, and in the case of Bianchi type II,
it can be chosen to equal
\min\{4-8\sigma_{+},6-\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\Phi_{1}|\}
.

Proof.

In the case of Bianchi type I, the expressions
S_{\pm}
vanish and the expression in the
N_{i}
appearing in
(
3.39
) vanishes as well. Using the relation (
3.40
), the equations (
3.35b
)
thus take the form

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=-2\Omega\Sigma_{+},\ \ \ \Sigma_{-}^{\prime}=-2\Omega\Sigma_{-}.

(4.15)

Moreover, since we assume that the solution is not isotropic,
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})\neq(0,0)
. This means that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
moves away from the origin
radially as time decreases. Due to the bounds on
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
, this implies that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
converges to a limit, say
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Moreover, in the non-LRS setting, this limit cannot belong to any of the line
segments connecting a special point to its antipodal point. Combining this observation with the constraint yields the conclusion
that
\Psi
converges to a limit. Since one of
\Sigma_{\pm}
has to be non-vanishing, the equations (
4.15
) imply that
\Omega
has
to be integrable to the past. Next, note that

\theta(\tau)=\theta(\tau_{0})\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}=\theta(\tau_{0})\exp\big{(}-3(\tau-\tau_{0})+\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}2\Omega(s)ds\big{)}.

Since
\Omega
is integrable to the past, we conclude that there is a
\theta_{\infty}>0
such that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)
converges to

\theta_{\infty}
.

Turning to the Bianchi type II case, note that
\Sigma_{-}\neq 0
(since
\Sigma_{-}=0
would imply that the solution is LRS). Moreover,
S_{-}=0
, so that
combining (
3.35b
) and (
3.40
) yields

\Sigma_{-}^{\prime}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(3N_{1}^{2}+4\Omega)\Sigma_{-}.

This implies that both
N_{1}^{2}
and
\Omega
are integrable to the past. Moreover,
|\Sigma_{-}|
increases to the past, so that
\Sigma_{-}
, since it is
bounded, has to converge to a non-zero value
\sigma_{-}
. Due to these observations, both of the terms on the right hand side of the equation
for
\Sigma_{+}
, see (
3.35b
),
are integrable to the past, so that
\Sigma_{+}
has to converge to a limit, say
\sigma_{+}
. Next, since
N_{1}^{2}
is integrable and
N_{1}N_{1}^{\prime}
is
bounded, it follows that
N_{1}
has to converge to zero. Since
q-2
is integrable to the past, the desired conclusion concerning
\theta

follows by an argument similar to the one given in the case of Bianchi type I. Turning to limitations on
\sigma_{+}
, note that if

\sigma_{+}>1/2
, then (
3.35a
) and (
3.36a
), combined with the fact that
q-2
is integrable to the past, means that
N_{1}^{2}

diverges to infinity, contradicting the conclusion that
N_{1}
converges to
0
. Thus
\sigma_{+}\leq 1/2
. Finally, the convergence of
\Psi

follows from the constraint.

Assume now, in addition, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Combining the Hamiltonian constraint (
3.39
) with the
definition of
\Psi
, (
3.30b
), it is clear that
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{6}
, so that there is a constant
C_{\phi}\geq 0
with the property
that

|\phi(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}|\tau|+C_{\phi}

(4.16)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. This means that there is a constant
C
with the property that

\Omega(\tau)\leq Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Next, we wish to prove that
N_{1}
converges to zero exponentially. Note, to
this end, that

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=\tfrac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}(2-\Sigma_{+})-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}\geq-2|\Omega|\geq-Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(4.17)

for all
\tau\leq 0
, where we appealed to (
3.35b
), (
3.37a
) and (
3.40
). Integrating this estimate
from
-\infty
to
\tau
yields the conclusion that

\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq\sigma_{+}-Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. From the above, we know that
\sigma_{+}\leq 1/2
. We now wish to exclude the possibility that
\sigma_{+}=1/2
. Assume, therefore,
that
\sigma_{+}=1/2
. Then

q-4\Sigma_{+}\leq q-2+Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}.

However, we already know
q-2
to be integrable to the past. This means that there is a constant
C
such that

|N_{1}(\tau)|=|N_{1}(0)|\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}[q(s)-4\Sigma_{+}(s)]ds\big{)}\geq|N_{1}(0)|e^{-C}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. However, this is incompatible with the fact that
N_{1}
converges to zero. Thus
\sigma_{+}<1/2
. This implies that

N_{1}
converges to zero exponentially. Combining this fact with (
4.17
) yields the conclusion that
\Sigma_{+}
converges exponentially
to its limit. This means that
q-4\Sigma_{+}=2-4\sigma_{+}+\dots
, where the dots represent exponentially decaying terms. Thus
N_{1}=O[e^{(2-4\sigma_{+})\tau}]
.
Moreover, there is an
\alpha>0
such that
\Sigma_{\pm}-\sigma_{\pm}=O(e^{\alpha\tau})
and
q-2=O(e^{\alpha\tau})
. Next, note that
V^{\prime}\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
decays
to zero exponentially at the same rate as
\Omega
; this follows from
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
and an argument similar to the above. Considering
(
3.38b
), it is thus clear that
\phi_{1}
converges exponentially to a limit, say
\Phi_{1}
. This can be integrated to conclude that

\phi-\Phi_{1}\cdot\tau
converges exponentially to a limit. In fact, (
4.12a
) holds. The estimates (
4.12b
) and
(
4.12c
) follow from the equations and the improved bounds on
\Omega
and
N_{1}
. The estimate (
4.13
) follows by integrating
(
3.35a
), keeping (
3.36a
) and (
4.12
) in mind. The equality (
4.14
) follows by taking the
limit of (
3.39
). The final statement of the lemma concerning
\alpha
follows by repeating the above arguments with the improved estimate for

\phi
in mind.
∎

Next, we make a technical observation concerning Bianchi type VII
0
solutions.

Lemma 4.9
.

Consider a Bianchi type VII
0
development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that

N_{1}=0
. Then
(N_{2}N_{3})(\tau)
is bounded for
\tau\leq 0
.

Proof.

By assumption, the development is neither isotropic nor LRS. Thus
(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}
is always non-zero. Define

Z_{-1}:=\tfrac{\tfrac{4}{3}\Sigma_{-}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}}{N_{2}N_{3}};

see
[
59
, p. 1429]
and
[
12
, p. 63]
. Then
Z_{-1}
is strictly positive and it can be computed that

Z_{-1}^{\prime}=-\tfrac{16}{3}\tfrac{(\Sigma_{+}+1)\Sigma_{-}^{2}}{\tfrac{4}{3}\Sigma_{-}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}}Z_{-1}.

In particular,
Z_{-1}
increases to the past. This means that for
\tau\leq 0
,

(N_{2}N_{3})(\tau)\leq\tfrac{1}{Z_{-1}(0)}\left(\tfrac{4}{3}\Sigma_{-}^{2}+(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}\right)(\tau)\leq\tfrac{4}{3Z_{-1}(0)},

where we appealed to (
3.39
) in the last step. The lemma follows.
∎

The goal of the next lemma is to provide a condition ensuring that
\Omega
decays to zero exponentially in the direction of
the singularity.

Lemma 4.10
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
which is not an isotropic Bianchi type I development.
Then there is a constant
c_{\theta}>0
such that
\theta(\tau)\geq c_{\theta}e^{-\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Assuming, in addition, that

V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, it follows that
\Omega
decays exponentially. In fact, there is a constant
C_{\Omega}
such that,
for all
\tau\leq 0
,

\Omega(\tau)\leq C_{\Omega}e^{2(1-3\alpha_{V})\tau}.

(4.18)

Assume, in addition to the above, that the solution is of Bianchi type VI
0
or VII
0
with
N_{1}=0
. Then there are constants

\theta_{\infty}>0
and
C_{\Omega}
such that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)\rightarrow\theta_{\infty}
and

\Omega(\tau)\leq C_{\Omega}e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(4.19)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Finally, there is a
\sigma_{+}>-1
such that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\Sigma_{+}(\tau)=\sigma_{+},\ \ \ \lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}q(\tau)=2,\ \ \ \lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(N_{2}-N_{3})(\tau)=0.

(4.20)

Remark 4.11
.

In the case of Bianchi type VI
0
, the last equality in (
4.20
) implies that both
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
converge to zero.

Proof.

Due to (
3.18
),
X^{\prime}\leq-2X
. Thus
X(\tau)\geq X(0)e^{-2\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Since
X(0)>0
due to the assumptions,
see the proof of Lemma
3.4
, and since
\theta^{2}\geq X
, it is clear that there is a constant
c_{\theta}>0

such that
\theta(\tau)\geq c_{\theta}e^{-\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. If (
4.16
) holds, we conclude that
(
4.18
) holds, since
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. However, (
4.16
) holds if

\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, the fact that
X(t)>0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
implies that
|\phi^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{6}
for
\tau\leq 0
, so
that (
4.16
) holds for
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
as well.

Assume now that the solution is of Bianchi type VI
0
or VII
0
with
N_{1}=0
. Note that

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=-\tfrac{3}{2}(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}(\Sigma_{+}+1)-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}

(4.21)

due to (
3.35b
), (
3.37a
) and (
3.40
). Due to (
4.18
), the second term on the right hand side
converges to zero exponentially. Next, note that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)
cannot converge to
-1
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
due to
Proposition
4.4
.
This means that there is an
\eta>0
and a sequence
\tau_{k}\rightarrow-\infty
such that
1+\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{k})\geq\eta
. Consider (
4.21
).
The first term is non-positive and only causes
\Sigma_{+}
to increase towards the past. The second term converges to zero exponentially
(since
|\Sigma_{+}|\leq 1
). This means that for
k
large enough

\big{|}\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau_{k}}2\Omega(s)\Sigma_{+}(s)ds\big{|}\leq\tfrac{\eta}{2}.

In particular, for
k
large enough,
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1\geq\eta/2
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{k}
. Combining this observation with (
4.21
), we
conclude that
(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}
is integrable to the past. Combining the fact that
\Omega
and
(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}
are integrable to the past with
(
3.40
) yields the conclusion that
q-2
is integrable to the past. Thus
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)\rightarrow\theta_{\infty}
and
(
4.19
) hold by arguments similar to ones presented in the proof of Lemma
4.8
.

Combining (
4.21
) with the fact that
(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}
and
\Omega
are integrable to the past, we conclude that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)

converges to a limit
\sigma_{+}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. By the above,
\sigma_{+}>-1
. Since
(N_{2}-N_{3})^{2}
is integrable to the past
and has bounded derivative to the past (this follows from the constraint (
3.39
) and Lemma
4.9
), it follows
that
N_{2}-N_{3}
converges to zero. Since
\Omega
and
N_{2}-N_{3}
converge to zero, (
3.40
) yields the conclusion that
q
converges to
2
.
The lemma follows.
∎

It is of interest to note that this lemma has the following corollary.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}[V]\subset\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]
, so that

\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}[V]=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}[V]
; see Definition
1.53
.

Proof.

Since there is a
T
such that
X(t)>0
for all
t\leq T
due to Lemma
3.4
and the assumptions, all that remains
to be proven is that
\Omega
converges to zero. However, that
\Omega
converges to zero follows from Lemma
4.10
.
∎

4.3
The vacuum and matter dominated cases

Next, we demonstrate that there is a dichotomy: either the expansion normalised energy density decays exponentially towards the past, or it
converges to a non-zero contribution.

Theorem 4.13
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
which is not an isotropic Bianchi type I development. Assume

V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in case of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Then either there is a constant
C
such that

\Omega(\tau)+|\phi^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq C\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}

(4.22)

for all
\tau\leq 0
; or there are constants
\alpha_{\infty}>0
,
\theta_{\infty}>0
,
C_{\infty}
,
\sigma_{\pm}
,
\Phi_{1}\neq 0
and
\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)-\sigma_{-}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\infty}e^{\alpha_{\infty}\tau},

(4.23a)

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}(\tau)-\Phi_{1}|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{1}\tau-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\infty}e^{\alpha_{\infty}\tau},

(4.23b)

\displaystyle|N_{1}(\tau)|+|N_{2}(\tau)|+|N_{3}(\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\infty}e^{\alpha_{\infty}\tau},

(4.23c)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\infty}e^{\alpha_{\infty}\tau},

(4.23d)

\displaystyle\Omega(\tau)\leq

\displaystyle C_{\infty}e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(4.23e)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Moreover, in the latter case, if
N_{i}\neq 0
, then
f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})>0
.

Assume, on the contrary, that the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are not satisfied, but that the conditions of
Lemma
3.6
are. Assume, moreover, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then the second case described
above holds. In particular, (
4.23
) holds. Moreover,
\sigma_{\pm}=0
and
\Phi_{1}^{2}=6
.

Remark 4.14
.

Due to this result, Definition
1.83
is meaningful, and below we speak of matter and vacuum dominated developments.

Proof.

Assume, to begin with, that the conditions of the first part of the lemma are satisfied. In particular, the conditions of
Lemma
3.4
are then satisfied, so that the development is not of LRS Bianchi type VII
0
. Let

F(\tau)=\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[2-q(s)]ds\big{)}\Psi(\tau).

(4.24)

Then (
3.42
) yields

F^{\prime}(\tau)=4\Omega(\tau)\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[2-q(s)]ds\big{)}.

(4.25)

In particular,
F
is bounded from below by
0
and is decreasing to the past. There is thus a constant

F_{0}\geq 0
such that

\begin{split}F(\tau)-F_{0}=&\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}4\Omega(s)\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{s}^{0}2[2-q(u)]du\big{)}ds\\
=&18\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}\tfrac{V[\phi(s)]}{\theta^{2}(0)}\exp\big{(}-2\textstyle{\int}_{s}^{0}[1+q(u)]du\big{)}\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{s}^{0}2[2-q(u)]du\big{)}ds\\
=&\tfrac{18}{\theta^{2}(0)}\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}V[\phi(s)]e^{6s}ds.\end{split}

(4.26)

Since
V[\phi(\tau)]\leq Ce^{-6\alpha_{V}\tau}
for
\tau\leq 0
, cf. the beginning of the proof of Lemma
4.10
, (
4.26
) yields

|F(\tau)-F_{0}|\leq Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(4.27)

for
\tau\leq 0
, so that

\big{|}\Psi(\tau)-F_{0}\exp\big{(}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[2-q(s)]ds\big{)}\big{|}\leq C\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds-6\alpha_{V}\tau\big{)}.

Let us first consider the case that
F_{0}=0
. Then

\Psi(\tau)\leq C\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds-6\alpha_{V}\tau\big{)}.

(4.28)

In the case of anisotropic Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II,
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and
q
converges to

2
; see Lemma
4.8
. Combining these observations with (
4.28
) yields the conclusion
that
\Psi
converges to zero exponentially. In the remaining cases,
\theta(\tau)\geq c_{\theta}e^{-\tau}
, see Lemma
4.10
,
and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. This, again, yields the conclusion that
\Psi
converges to zero exponentially. This means that
\phi
converges as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, so that
V\circ\phi
is bounded to the past. Going through the above estimates with this information in mind yields the
conclusion that

\Psi(\tau)\leq C\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}

(4.29)

for
\tau\leq 0
. On the other hand, (
3.30b
) and (
3.31
) yield

\Psi=\tfrac{3}{2}\tfrac{\phi_{t}^{2}+2V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\geq\tfrac{3V_{\inf}}{\theta^{2}(0)}\exp\big{(}-2\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}[1+q(s)]ds\big{)},

where we use the fact that
V
has a non-negative infimum,
V_{\inf}
. In particular, it is thus
natural to introduce the quantity

P(\tau)=\exp\big{(}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}\Psi(\tau).

Appealing to (
3.42
) yields

P^{\prime}(\tau)=\exp\big{(}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}(-6\Psi+4\Omega)=-9\tfrac{\phi_{t}^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\exp\big{(}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\big{)}=-9\tfrac{\phi_{t}^{2}}{\theta^{2}(0)}.

(4.30)

In particular,
P
increases to the past (and, by the above, we know
P
to be bounded). Thus there
is a
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
P(\tau)\rightarrow P_{0}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Since

P=\tfrac{3}{2}\tfrac{\phi_{t}^{2}+2V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}(0)},

and since
\phi
converges to a limit, say
\phi_{0}
, it is clear that
\phi_{t}^{2}
has to converge to a limit. Keeping (
4.30
)
in mind, it is clear that
\phi_{t}
has to converge to zero. Consider (
3.4
). We know that
\phi
converges to a limit, say

\phi_{0}
. Let
C
be a constant such that
C\geq 2|V^{\prime}(\phi_{0})|+1
. Then there is a
t_{0}>0
such that
C>|V^{\prime}[\phi(t)]|+1/2
for

t\leq t_{0}
. Next, we wish to prove, by contradiction, that there is a
T\in(0,t_{0})
such that
|(\theta\phi_{t})(t)|\leq C
for all

t\leq T
. Assume, therefore, that there is a sequence
t_{k}\downarrow 0
such that
|(\theta\phi_{t})(t_{k})|>C
. Fix a
t>0
such that

\phi_{t}(t)\neq 0
. Then, due to (
3.4
), (
3.27
) and (
3.31
),

\partial_{t}(\theta\phi_{t})=-\left(\tfrac{4}{3}+\tfrac{2}{3}(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Psi)-\tfrac{2}{3}\Omega+\tfrac{V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{\theta\phi_{t}}\right)\theta^{2}\phi_{t}

at
t
. Fix
T
so that
\Omega(t)\leq 1/2
for
t\leq T
. Then, assuming
t\leq\min\{T,t_{0}\}
and
|(\theta\phi_{t})(t)|\geq C
,

\tfrac{2}{3}\Omega+\left|\tfrac{V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{\theta\phi_{t}}\right|\leq\tfrac{1}{3}+\tfrac{C-1/2}{C}=\tfrac{4}{3}-\tfrac{1}{2C}.

This means that if
t\leq\min\{T,t_{0}\}
and
|(\theta\phi_{t})(t)|\geq C
, then
|\theta\phi_{t}|
increases to the past. This
means that
|(\theta\phi_{t})(s)|\geq C
for all
s\in(0,t)
. Combining this conclusion with (
3.4
) and the fact that

|V^{\prime}[\phi(s)]|\leq C-1/2
for
s\in(0,t)
yields the conclusion that
|\phi_{t}|
is non-zero and increasing to the past. This means
that it converges to a non-zero value, contradicting previous conclusions. Thus
|(\theta\phi_{t})(t)|<C
for all
t\leq\min\{T,t_{0}\}
.
This means that

|\phi^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq C\exp\left(-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[1+q(s)]ds\right)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this estimate with (
4.29
) yields (
4.22
).

Next, assume
F_{0}>0
. Combining (
4.24
) and (
4.27
) then yields

\Psi(\tau)=\exp\left(\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[2-q(s)]ds\right)\left[F_{0}+O(e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau})\right].

(4.31)

If
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, then
\Psi\leq 1
due to the constraint. If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
, then
X(t)>0
for
t\leq t_{0}
, see
Lemma
3.4
. Due to (
3.16
) and (
3.30b
), this means that
\Psi\leq 1
in case

\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
. Thus
\Psi(\tau)\leq 1
for all
\tau\leq 0
. If
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, then the first factor on the right hand side of
(
4.31
) is monotonically increasing to the past; note that
2-q\geq 0
if
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
due to (
3.40
).
Combining this observation with (
4.31
) and the fact that
\Psi\leq 1
implies that
\Psi
converges to a strictly
positive number, and that

\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{0}2|2-q(s)|ds<\infty.

(4.32)

Next, let
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
and define

\displaystyle Z_{N}:=

\displaystyle(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})^{1/3}\exp\big{(}-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}[2-q(s)]ds\big{)},

\displaystyle Z:=

\displaystyle\frac{(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})^{1/3}}{\Psi^{1/2}}=Z_{N}\big{[}F_{0}+O(e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau})\big{]}^{-1/2}.

Then
Z_{N}^{\prime}=2Z_{N}
, so that
Z_{N}(\tau)=e^{2\tau}Z_{N}(0)
. This means that

(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})^{1/3}\leq e^{2\tau}Z_{N}(0)\big{[}F_{0}+O(e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau})\big{]}^{-1/2}\leq Ce^{2\tau}

(4.33)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this estimate with Lemma
3.3
and (
3.40
) yields

2-q\geq-Ce^{4\tau}

(4.34)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this estimate with (
4.31
) and the fact that
\Psi\leq 1
yields the conclusion that
(
4.32
) holds when
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
.

Introduce
\omega=\Psi^{1/2}
. Then

\omega^{\prime}=-\left[(2-q)-\tfrac{2\Omega}{\omega^{2}}\right]\omega.

Assuming
N_{2}N_{3}\neq 0
and defining
g=|N_{2}N_{3}|/\omega
then yields

g^{\prime}=\left[2(\Sigma_{+}+1)^{2}+2\Sigma_{-}^{2}+2\Psi-2\Omega-\tfrac{2\Omega}{\omega^{2}}\right]g.

Since
\Omega
and
\Omega/\omega^{2}
decay to zero exponentially and
\Psi
converges to a positive number, it is clear that
g
converges to
zero exponentially as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Since
\omega
is bounded, this implies that
N_{2}N_{3}\rightarrow 0
exponentially as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. The arguments concerning
N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}
are similar; these quantities both converge to zero exponentially as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Note also that, due to (
3.40
),

2-q=2\Omega+\tfrac{3}{2}[N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2}-2(N_{1}N_{2}+N_{2}N_{3}+N_{1}N_{3})].

(4.35)

Since
2-q,\Omega,N_{1}N_{2},N_{1}N_{3},N_{2}N_{3}\in L^{1}((-\infty,0])
, this equality implies that
N_{i}^{2}\in L^{1}((-\infty,0])
. Moreover, due to
(
3.39
) and the fact that
N_{1}N_{2},N_{1}N_{3},N_{2}N_{3}
all converge to zero,
\Psi
,
\Sigma_{\pm}
and the
N_{i}
are bounded to the past
(so that
N_{i}^{\prime}
is bounded to the past). Thus
N_{i}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, so that
q(\tau)\rightarrow 2
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. By the above, all quadratic polynomials in the
N_{i}
’s are integrable to the past. Combining this observation with the
fact that
2-q
is integrable to the past and the equations (
3.35b
) yields the conclusion that
\Sigma_{+}
and
\Sigma_{-}

converge to the past. Thus there are constants
\sigma_{\pm}
,
\psi
such that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\Sigma_{+}(\tau)=\sigma_{+},\ \ \ \lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\Sigma_{-}(\tau)=\sigma_{-},\ \ \ \lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\Psi(\tau)=\psi.

Moreover,
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}+\psi=1
. Since the
N_{i}
converge to zero we know that if
N_{i}\neq 0
, then
f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\geq 0
.
The question is then if equality can hold. Assume, therefore, without loss of generality, that
\sigma_{+}=1/2
and that
N_{1}\neq 0
. Then
N_{2}
,

N_{3}
,
N_{1}N_{2}
,
N_{1}N_{3}
and
\Omega
converge to zero exponentially (since
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}=1-\psi<1
), so that, due to (
3.37a
)
and (
3.40
),

2-q=\tfrac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}+\dots,\ \ \ S_{+}=-N_{1}^{2}+\dots,

where the dots signify exponentially decaying terms. Combining this with (
3.35b
) yields

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=\tfrac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}(2-\Sigma_{+})+\dots,

where the dots signify exponentially decreasing terms. Since there, for every
\epsilon>0
, is a constant
c_{\epsilon}>0
such that

N_{1}^{2}(\tau)\geq c_{\epsilon}e^{\epsilon\tau}
for
\tau\leq 0
, we conclude that
\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}
is strictly positive in the limit. This means that there is a

T\leq 0
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)>1/2
for
\tau\leq T
. Moreover, due to (
3.40
),
q(\tau)
can be assumed to be strictly less
than
2
for
\tau\leq T
. Combining these observations, we conclude that
q(\tau)-4\Sigma_{+}(\tau)<0
for
\tau\leq T
. This is inconsistent
with the fact that
N_{1}
converges to zero. We conclude that if
N_{1}\neq 0
, then
\sigma_{+}<1/2
; i.e.,
f_{1}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})>0
.
By applying the symmetries, we obtain the analogous conclusions concerning the other limiting cases. Given the above information, the
asymptotics (
4.23
) can finally be derived. We leave the details to the reader. The first part of the
theorem follows.

Assume now that the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are not satisfied, but that the conditions of
Lemma
3.6
are. Due to (
3.26
) and the assumptions,
\phi_{\tau}^{2}
then converges to
6
.
Next, due to (
3.20
), there is, for every
\epsilon>0
, a
T\in J
such that
\theta_{t}\leq-(1-\epsilon)\theta^{2}
for all
t\leq T
,
so that

\theta^{\prime}\leq-3(1-\epsilon)\theta

for
t\leq T
. For every
\epsilon>0
, there is thus a
\tau_{\epsilon}
such that

\theta(\tau)\geq\exp[-3(1-\epsilon)\tau],\ \ \ |\phi(\tau)|\leq-\sqrt{6}(1+\epsilon)\tau

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{\epsilon}
. Due to the assumptions, this means that
\Omega
converges to zero exponentially. Combining this observation with
(
4.24
), (
4.25
) and the fact that
\Psi\geq 1
, it follows that

|F^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{\eta\tau}F(\tau)

for some
\eta>0
and
\tau\leq\tau_{\epsilon}
. In particular,
F
converges to a non-zero number to the past. Moreover, since

V\circ\phi(\tau)\leq Ce^{-6\alpha_{V}(1+\epsilon)\tau}
in the present setting, (
4.27
) is replaced by

|F(\tau)-F_{0}|\leq Ce^{6[1-(1+\epsilon)\alpha_{V}]\tau}

(4.36)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{\epsilon}
. This means that (
4.31
) is replaced by

\Psi(\tau)=\exp\left(\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}2[2-q(s)]ds\right)\left[F_{0}+O(e^{6[1-(1+\epsilon)\alpha_{V}]\tau})\right].

(4.37)

Combining this observation with the fact that
\Psi
converges to
1
and an argument similar to the proof of (
4.33
)
and (
4.34
) yields the conclusion that the polynomial on the left hand side of (
3.39
) is
bounded from below by
-Ce^{4\tau}
. Since
\Psi
converges to
1
, this means that
\Sigma_{\pm}\rightarrow 0
. Returning to
(
3.30c
) with this information at hand yields the conclusion that
q\rightarrow 2
. Combining this information with
(
3.32a
) yields the conclusion that all the
N_{i}
are decaying exponentially. Due to (
3.40
), it follows that

2-q
decays exponentially. At this stage, it is straightforward to deduce the remaining conclusions. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Chapter 5
The vacuum dominated setting

Due to Theorem
4.13
, there is a dichotomy: either
\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to a non-zero limit, or it converges to zero.
This leads to the notion of vacuum and matter dominated developments; see Definition
1.83
. In the matter dominated
case, the conclusions of Theorem
4.13
can be used to deduce detailed asymptotics. However, it is less clear what happens in the
vacuum dominated setting. The purpose of the present chapter is to derive the asymptotics in the vacuum dominated setting for anisotropic Bianchi types
I, II, VI
0
and VII
0
as well as for the isotropic and LRS Bianchi types VIII and IX. In the anisotropic and non-LRS Bianchi type VIII and IX
settings, we prove that the asymptotics are oscillatory.

5.1
Vacuum dominated Bianchi types I, II, VI
0
and VII
0

Next, let us return to the case that (
4.22
) holds. We first restrict our attention to Bianchi types I and II.

Corollary 5.1
.

Consider an anisotropic development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume it to be of Bianchi type I or
II with
N_{1}\neq 0
, and such that (
4.22
) holds. Assume, moreover, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in
case of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Then, in the case of Bianchi type I, there is a point

(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
on the Kasner circle (which is non-special if the solution is not LRS) and constants
\theta_{\infty}>0
and
\Phi_{0}

such that

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.1a)

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)-\sigma_{-}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.1b)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau}

(5.1c)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. In the the case of Bianchi type II, there is a point
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
on the Kasner circle with

\sigma_{+}<1/2
(which is non-special if the solution is not LRS) and constants
\theta_{\infty}>0
,
m_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.2a)

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)-\sigma_{-}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(5.2b)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(5.2c)

\displaystyle|\ln|N_{1}(\tau)|-f_{1}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\tau-m_{1}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau}

(5.2d)

for all
\tau\leq 0
, where
\alpha:=\min\{6,2f_{1}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\}
.

Proof.

Combining the results of Lemma
4.8
with (
4.22
), it
is clear that in the case of Bianchi type I,
q-2=O(e^{6\tau})
and
\phi^{\prime}=O(e^{6\tau})
. Combining these observations with (
3.35b
) and
(
3.31
) yields (
5.1
). Since
\Psi(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, it is clear that
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})

belongs to the Kasner circle. Due to the conclusions of Lemma
4.8
it is also clear that
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
can only be
a special point when the solution is LRS.

Similarly, in the case of non-LRS Bianchi type II, Lemma
4.8
implies that
q-2
decays exponentially. Combining this observation
with (
4.22
) yields the conclusion that
\phi^{\prime}=O(e^{6\tau})
. Integrating this estimate yields (
5.2a
).
Combining these observations with (
4.13
) yields the conclusion that
q-2=O(e^{\alpha\tau})
, where
\alpha=\min\{6,4(1-2\sigma_{+})\}
, and
that
N_{1}^{2}=O(e^{4(1-2\sigma_{+})\tau})
. Combining these observations with the equations yields (
5.2b
)–(
5.2d
). The
remaining conclusions of the corollary in the non-LRS Bianchi type II setting follow from the fact that
\Psi
converges to zero and the conclusions
of Lemma
4.8
.

Finally, consider the LRS Bianchi type II setting. Then

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=\tfrac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}(2-\Sigma_{+})-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}.

Since
\Omega
converges to zero exponentially, see (
4.18
), and
|\Sigma_{+}|\leq 1
, it is clear that
N_{1}^{2}
is integrable to the past
(so that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)
converges to a limit as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
).
This means that
q-2
is integrable to the past. Combining this observation with (
4.22
) yields (
5.2a
).
Since
N_{1}^{2}
is integrable and its derivative is bounded, we conclude that
N_{1}
converges to zero. Since, additionally,
\Psi
converges to zero,
due to (
4.22
), and
\Sigma_{-}=0
, the constraint (
3.39
) implies that
\Sigma_{+}
converges to either
1
or
-1
. However,
it cannot converge to
1
, since this would imply that
|N_{1}|
tends to infinity to the past. Combining these observations with the equations yields
the remaining conclusions.
∎

Next, let us turn to Bianchi types VI
0
and VII
0
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume it to be of Bianchi type
VI
0
or VII
0
with
N_{1}=0
, and such that (
4.22
) holds. Assume, in addition, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}

for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
.
Then there is a point
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
on the Kasner circle with
\sigma_{+}>1/2
and constants
\theta_{\infty}>0
,
m_{2}
,
m_{3}
and

\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.3a)

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)-\sigma_{+}|+|\Sigma_{-}(\tau)-\sigma_{-}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(5.3b)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau},

(5.3c)

\displaystyle|\ln|N_{i}(\tau)|-f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\tau-m_{i}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\alpha\tau}

(5.3d)

for all
\tau\leq 0
and
i=2,3
, where
\alpha:=\min\{6,2f_{2}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}),2f_{3}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\}
.

Proof.

Note that the conclusions of Lemma
4.10
hold in the present setting.
Combining (
3.39
) with (
4.20
) and (
4.22
), it follows that
\Sigma_{-}^{2}
converges to

1-\sigma_{+}^{2}
. Since
\Sigma_{-}
is continuous, this implies that
\Sigma_{-}
converges to
\sigma_{-}
, where
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
belongs to the
Kasner circle. In order for this to be consistent with the fact that
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
are bounded to the past (in the case of Bianchi type
VI
0
this is an immediate consequence of the constraint, and in the case of Bianchi type VII
0
, it follows from
Lemma
4.9
and the constraint), we have to have
1+\sigma_{+}+\sqrt{3}\sigma_{-}\geq 0
and

1+\sigma_{+}-\sqrt{3}\sigma_{-}\geq 0
. Since
\sigma_{+}>-1
, this implies that
\sigma_{+}\geq 1/2
. However, assuming that
\sigma_{+}=1/2
,
Proposition
4.4
and Remark
4.5
yield the conclusion that either
N_{2}=0
or
N_{3}=0
, contradicting the
assumptions. This means that
\sigma_{+}>1/2
. The remaining conclusions of the corollary follow by revisiting the equations using the above
information.
∎

5.2
Vacuum dominated isotropic and LRS Bianchi types VIII and IX

Next, we consider vacuum dominated LRS Bianchi type VIII solutions.

Corollary 5.3
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
where
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Assume
it to be of LRS Bianchi type VIII with
N_{1}<0
,
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
, and such that (
4.22
) holds. Then there are constants

\theta_{\infty}>0
,
m_{1}
,
m_{2}=m_{3}
and
\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.4a)

\displaystyle|\ln|N_{i}(\tau)|-f_{i}(2,-1,0)\tau-m_{i}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.4b)

\displaystyle|q(\tau)-2|+|\Omega(\tau)|+|\Psi(\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.4c)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.4d)

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau}

(5.4e)

for all
\tau\leq 0
and
i=1,2,3
.

Proof.

Combining Lemma
4.10
and (
4.22
), it follows that
\Psi
and
\Omega
decay as
e^{2\tau}
. Next, note that
(
3.35b
), (
3.37a
), (
3.40
) and the fact that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
yield

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=\tfrac{3}{2}N_{1}^{2}(2-\Sigma_{+})-6N_{1}N_{2}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-\Sigma_{+}\right)-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}.

(5.5)

On the other hand,
|\Sigma_{+}|\leq 1
due to (
3.39
), so that
|2\Omega(\tau)\Sigma_{+}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
and some constant

C>0
. Assume, for a moment, that there is a
\tau_{1}\leq 0
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})\leq 1/2-Ce^{2\tau_{1}}
. We then wish to prove that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq 1/2

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Assume, in order to obtain a contradiction, that
\tau_{2}\leq\tau_{1}
is such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2})=1/2
and
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)<1/2
for

\tau_{2}<\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Then the first two terms on the right hand side of (
5.5
) are non-negative on
[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
. Thus

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}(\tau)\geq-Ce^{2\tau}
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
. Integrating this estimate yields

\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2})\leq\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})+\tfrac{C}{2}e^{2\tau_{1}}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{C}{2}e^{2\tau_{1}}<\tfrac{1}{2},

a contradiction. In other words, there are two possibilities. Either there is a
\tau_{1}
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq 1/2-Ce^{2\tau}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}

or there is a
\tau_{1}
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq s_{+}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and some
s_{+}<1/2
. Let us consider the first case. Then

q-4\Sigma_{+}=2\Sigma_{+}^{2}-4\Sigma_{+}+2\Psi-2\Omega.

Note that for
\Sigma_{+}\in[1/2,1]
,
2\Sigma_{+}^{2}-4\Sigma_{+}\leq-3/2
. This means that
q-4\Sigma_{+}
is, in the limit as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, bounded from above by

-3/2
. This means that
|N_{1}|
tends to infinity exponentially. On the other hand,
|N_{1}|\leq 2/\sqrt{3}
due to (
3.39
). This contradiction
leads to the conclusion that there is a
\tau_{1}
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq s_{+}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and some
s_{+}<1/2
. Combining this observation
with (
5.5
) leads to the conclusion that
N_{1}^{2}
and
N_{1}N_{2}
are integrable to the past; note that
\Sigma_{+}
is bounded, the last term on
the right hand side of (
5.5
) is integrable,
2-\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq 2-s_{+}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and
1/2-\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq 1/2-s_{+}
for
all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Since
N_{1}
,
N_{1}^{\prime}
and
(N_{1}N_{2})^{\prime}
are bounded to the past, we conclude that
N_{1}
and
N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero. Combining this observation
with (
3.39
) yields the conclusion that
\Sigma_{+}
converges to
-1
, so that
q
converges to
2
. This means that
N_{1}
and
N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero
exponentially, so that
q-2
converges to zero exponentially. This means that
|\phi^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{6\tau}
, so that (
5.4e
) follows.
Note also that
\Sigma_{+}
converges exponentially to
-1
. Thus
|N_{1}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{6\tau}
and
|(N_{1}N_{2})(\tau)|\leq Ce^{6\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Moreover,

\Omega(\tau)\leq Ce^{6\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. This means that
|q(\tau)-2|\leq Ce^{6\tau}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. Thus (
5.4c
)
and thereby (
5.4d
) hold. Combining the above also yields (
5.4a
). Combining the above with the equations
for the
N_{i}
yields the remaining conclusions.
∎

In the vacuum dominated isotropic or LRS Bianchi type IX setting, the following holds.

Corollary 5.4
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
where
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Assume it to be
of either isotropic or LRS Bianchi type IX with
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
, and such that (
4.22
) holds. Then there are constants

\theta_{\infty}>0
,
m_{1}
,
m_{2}=m_{3}
and
\Phi_{0}
such that

\displaystyle|\Sigma_{+}(\tau)+1|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.6a)

\displaystyle|\ln N_{i}(\tau)-f_{i}(2,-1,0)\tau-m_{i}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.6b)

\displaystyle|q(\tau)-2|+|\Omega(\tau)|+|\Psi(\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.6c)

\displaystyle|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau},

(5.6d)

\displaystyle|\phi_{1}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{6\tau}

(5.6e)

for all
\tau\leq 0
and
i=1,2,3
.

Proof.

Due to (
3.30c
), (
3.35b
) and (
3.39
),

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=(1-\Sigma_{+}^{2})(4-2\Sigma_{+})-\Psi(4-2\Sigma_{+})-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}+9N_{1}N_{2}.

(5.7)

Note that there is a constant
C
such that
\Psi+\Omega\leq Ce^{2\tau}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
; this follows from (
3.30c
),
(
4.22
) and the Lemma
4.10
. Given
0<\epsilon<1
, there is a
c_{\epsilon}>0
such that if

\Sigma_{+}\in[-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
, then the first term on the right hand side of (
5.7
) is bounded from below by
c_{\epsilon}
. Let
T\leq\tau_{0}
be such
that the sum of the absolute values of the second and third terms on the right hand side of (
5.7
) is bounded from above by
c_{\epsilon}/2

for
\tau\leq T
, assuming
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\in[-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
. This means that if
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\in[-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
and
\tau\leq T
, then
\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}(\tau)\geq c_{\epsilon}/2
.
This means that
\Sigma_{+}
decreases to the past until it leaves
[-1+\epsilon,1-\epsilon]
. Moreover, it cannot return to this interval at a later time. To conclude,
given
\epsilon>0
, there are two possibilities. Either there is a time
T\leq\tau_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)<-1+\epsilon
for all
\tau\leq T
or a time

T_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)>1-\epsilon
for all
\tau\leq T_{1}
. In other words, either
\Sigma_{+}
converges to
-1
or it converges to
1
. This
means that
q
converges to
2
. Assume that
\Sigma_{+}
converge to
1
. Then
N_{1}
tends to infinity exponentially and
N_{2}
tends to zero exponentially
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. This means that the polynomial on the left hand side of (
3.39
) tends to infinity, contradicting
(
3.39
). The only possibility that remains is that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\rightarrow-1
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. This means that
N_{1}
and

N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero exponentially. Since
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
\Psi
converges to zero exponentially, this means that
\Sigma_{+}+1
decays exponentially,
see (
3.39
),
so that
q-2
decays exponentially. Combining this observation with (
4.22
), it is clear that
\Omega=O(e^{6\tau})
and

\phi^{\prime}=O(e^{6\tau})
. Moreover,
N_{1}^{2}=O(e^{12\tau})
and
N_{1}N_{2}=O(e^{6\tau})
. Returning to (
3.39
) and (
3.40
) with this
information at hand yields (
5.6a
) and (
5.6c
). Given this information, the remaining conclusions of
the lemma can be deduced.
∎

5.3
The generic Bianchi type VIII and IX settings

Our next goal is to prove that if (
4.22
) holds in the case of a Bianchi type VIII or IX solution which is neither isotropic
nor LRS, then the solution is oscillatory. To prove this, it is natural to return to the arguments presented in
[
49
]
. Due to
the presence of the non-linear scalar field, complications arise. Nevertheless, suitable modifications of the arguments presented in
[
49
]
yield
the desired conclusion.

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume it to either be of Bianchi type VIII,
with
N_{1}<0
,
N_{2}>0
and
N_{3}>0
; or of Bianchi type IX, with
N_{i}>0
for all
i
. Assume, finally, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
and that (
4.22
) holds. Then the set of
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})(\tau)
for
\tau\leq 0
is bounded.
Next, assume that
\|N(\tau)\|\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.
Then, up to a permutation of the variables in the case of Bianchi type IX,
N_{2}(\tau)
,
N_{3}(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
and
N_{1}(\tau)
,

N_{1}(\tau)N_{2}(\tau)
,
N_{1}(\tau)N_{3}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.

Remark 5.6
.

We here use the notation
\|N\|:=(N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2})^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Note that
(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})^{\prime}=3q(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})
. Since
\Omega
is integrable to the past, see Lemma
4.10
, this means that
|N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}|

is bounded to the past; say
|N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}|(\tau)\leq C
for all
\tau\leq 0
and some
C\geq 1
. Given this observation, the arguments presented in

[
49
, Lemmas 6, 7 and 15]
yield the desired conclusion.
∎

Next, we wish to generalise
[
49
, Lemma 16, p. 724]
to the present setting.

Lemma 5.7
.

Consider a Bianchi type IX development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
with
N_{i}>0
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
,
where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. If
\tau_{1}\leq 0
,

-1+\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{1}}<\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})<\tfrac{1}{3},

where
C_{\Omega}
is the constant appearing in (
4.18
), and
9N_{1}<N_{2}+N_{3}
in
[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
, then

\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})-\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{1}}\leq\Sigma_{+}(\tau)

(5.8)

for all
\tau\in[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
.

Proof.

Combining (
3.35b
) and (
3.40
) yields
q-2\leq 3N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})
and

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=(q-2)(\Sigma_{+}+1)+\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}(N_{1}-N_{2}-N_{3})+2\Omega.

(5.9)

This yields the conclusion that for
-1<\Sigma_{+}<1/3
,

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}<\tfrac{1}{2}(9N_{1}-N_{2}-N_{3})N_{1}+2\Omega.

(5.10)

Assume now that that there is a
\tau\in[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)
is strictly less than the left hand side of
(
5.8
). Then there is a subinterval
[\tau_{b},\tau_{a}]
of
[\tau_{2},\tau_{1}]
such that
-1<\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})
for

\tau\in[\tau_{b},\tau_{a}]
,
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{a})=\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{1})
and
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{b})
is strictly less than the left hand side of (
5.8
). Integrating
(
5.10
), keeping (
4.18
) and the above observations in mind yields

\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{1}}<\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{a})-\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{b})<\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{b}}^{\tau_{a}}2\Omega(s)ds\leq\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{a}},

which is a contradiction.
∎

Next, we generalise
[
49
, Lemma 17, p. 724]
to the present setting.

Lemma 5.8
.

Consider a Bianchi type VIII development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
with
N_{1}<0
,
N_{2}>0
and
N_{3}>0
. Assume
that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. Then there is an
\epsilon_{0}>0
with the following
properties. Fix an
\epsilon

such that
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
. Then there are constants
A>0
and
T\leq 0
(depending only on
\epsilon
and the solution) such that if

[\tau_{1},\tau_{0}]
