is an interval with
\tau_{0}\leq T
and
N_{2}(\tau)
,
N_{3}(\tau)\geq A
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{0}]
,
and if
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{0})\geq-1+2\epsilon
, then
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq-1+\epsilon
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{0}]
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that combining (
3.35b
) and (
3.40
) yields

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=-(2-q)(\Sigma_{+}+1)+\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})+2\Omega.

(5.11)

Assume now, in order to arrive at a contradiction, that there is a
\tau_{a}\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{0}]
such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{a})=-1+\epsilon
.
This
\tau_{a}
can, without loss of generality, be assumed to be the first
\tau
before
\tau_{0}
at which
\Sigma_{+}
attains the value

-1+\epsilon
. Then there is a
\tau_{b}\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{0}]
with
\tau_{b}\geq\tau_{a}
such that
-1+\epsilon\leq\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq-1+2\epsilon
for all

\tau\in[\tau_{a},\tau_{b}]
. Moreover,
\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{b})=-1+2\epsilon
. Integrating (
5.11
) with this information in mind yields

\epsilon\leq\tfrac{9}{2}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{a}}^{\tau_{b}}N_{1}^{2}(\tau)d\tau-\tfrac{9}{2}\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{a}}^{\tau_{b}}[N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})](\tau)d\tau+2\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{a}}^{\tau_{b}}\Omega(\tau)d\tau.

(5.12)

Fixing
T
close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending only on
\epsilon
and the solution, it is clear that the last term on the right hand
side of (
5.12
) can be assumed to be smaller than
\epsilon/6
; this is an immediate consequence of Lemma
4.10
.
Assuming
\epsilon_{0}
to be small enough, we can assume

q-4\Sigma_{+}
,
q-\Sigma_{+}+\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}
and
q-\Sigma_{+}-\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}
to be bounded from below by
2
on
[\tau_{a},\tau_{b}]
. This means that
N_{1}^{2}
,

N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}
are arbitrarily small at
\tau_{b}
(by choosing
A
large enough and using the fact that
|N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}|(\tau)\leq C

for
\tau\leq 0
) and exponentially decaying backwards in time (at a fixed rate). Choosing
\epsilon_{0}
small enough and
A
large enough (depending
only on
\epsilon
and the solution), we conclude that the sum of the first two terms on the right hand side of (
5.12
) are bounded
from above by
\epsilon/3
. This yields the inequality
\epsilon\leq\epsilon/2
, which is a contradiction. The lemma follows.
∎

Our next goal is to prove the existence of an
\alpha
-limit point, as in
[
49
]
. There is one problem in doing so: in order to obtain a closed
autonomous system, we here, as opposed to the vacuum setting studied in
[
49
]
, need to include
\theta
, but
\theta
diverges to
\infty
in
the direction of the singularity. There is a simple remedy to this problem; we simply introduce the variable
u=1/\theta
and replace all occurrences
of
1/\theta
with
u
. Moreover, we replace (
3.31
) by
u^{\prime}=(1+q)u
. We then obtain an autonomous system of equations for
u
,
\phi_{0}
,

\phi_{1}
,
\Sigma_{+}
,
\Sigma_{-}
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
and
N_{3}
satisfying the constraint (
3.39
). In what follows, we refer to this system as the

modified Wainwright-Hsu equations
.
Note also that setting
u=0
and
\phi_{1}=0
leads to the vacuum version of the original Wainwright-Hsu equations; i.e., exactly the equations studied
in
[
49
]
. Next, note that if (
4.22
) and the conditions of Lemma
4.10
hold, then
\Omega
and
\phi^{\prime}

decay as
e^{2\tau}
, so that there is a
\phi_{\infty}
with the property that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(u,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})(\tau)=(0,\phi_{\infty},0).

(5.13)

That there is an
\alpha
-limit point in the case of Bianchi type IX follows by an argument similar to the proof of
[
49
, Proposition 2, p. 724–725]
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Consider a Bianchi type IX development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
with
N_{i}>0
. Assume
that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. Then the corresponding solution to the modified
Wainwright-Hsu equations has an
\alpha
-limit point.

Proof.

Under the assumptions of the proposition, there is a
\phi_{\infty}
such that (
5.13
) holds. In the present setting, it is
therefore sufficient to prove that there is a sequence
\tau_{k}\rightarrow-\infty
such that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-},N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})(\tau_{k})
converges.
Assume that there is no limit point of the desired type. Then, since
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
remains bounded to the past,
\|N\|
has to converge to
\infty
.
Due to Lemma
5.5
, we can assume that
N_{1}
,
N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}
converge to zero and that
N_{2}
,
N_{3}
converge
to infinity. Due to (
3.35a
),
(N_{2}N_{3})^{\prime}=2(q+2\Sigma_{+})N_{2}N_{3}
. Since
N_{2}N_{3}\rightarrow\infty
, this means that

\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{\tau}(q+2\Sigma_{+})ds\rightarrow\infty

(5.14)

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Combining this observation with (
3.30c
) and the fact that
\Omega
is integrable to the past, it is clear
that

\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}\Sigma_{+}ds\rightarrow-\infty

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Due to Lemma
5.7
, it follows that there is a
T\leq 0
such that

\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

for all
\tau\leq T
. Next, assume that there is a
\tau_{2}\leq T
such that

-1+\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{2}}<\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2}).

Then, due to Lemma
5.7
,

-1<\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2})-\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau_{2}}\leq\Sigma_{+}(\tau)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{2}
. For future reference, we denote the expression in the middle by
\alpha
. Note, moreover, that since
\Sigma_{+}>-1
,
q-2\leq q+2\Sigma_{+}
.
Introduce
\mathcal{A}:=\{\tau:q+2\Sigma_{+}<0\}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}:=\mathcal{A}\cap[\tau,\tau_{2}]
. Due to (
5.10
) and the fact that the first
term on the right hand side of (
5.10
) is negative,

\begin{split}\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2})-\Sigma_{+}(\tau)=&\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{2}}\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}ds\leq\textstyle{\int}_{\mathcal{A}_{\tau}}\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}ds+\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{2}}2\Omega ds\\
\leq&\textstyle{\int}_{\mathcal{A}_{\tau}}(q-2)(\Sigma_{+}+1)ds+\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{2}}4\Omega ds,\end{split}

where we appealed to (
5.9
) in the last step. Since
q-2<q+2\Sigma_{+}<0
and
1+\Sigma_{+}\geq 1+\alpha>0
in
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
, it follows that

\Sigma_{+}(\tau_{2})-\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq(1+\alpha)\textstyle{\int}_{\mathcal{A}_{\tau}}(q+2\Sigma_{+})ds+\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{2}}4\Omega ds.

However, due to (
5.14
), the first term on the right hand side tends to
-\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
and the second term
on the right hand side is bounded due to (
4.18
). This implies that
\Sigma_{+}\rightarrow\infty
, a contradiction. Next, assume that

\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq-1+\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

for all
\tau\leq T
. Since
N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}
converge to zero, (
3.39
) then implies that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})\rightarrow(-1,0)
.
Combining this observation with Proposition
4.4
yields the conclusion that
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
; i.e., that the solution is
LRS. Then, due to Corollary
5.4
, all the
N_{i}
converge, contradicting the assumptions.
To conclude, the assumption that
\|N\|\rightarrow\infty
leads to a contradiction. Since
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
remains bounded to the past, we conclude
that the desired limit point exists.
∎

Next, we prove the existence of an
\alpha
-limit point in the Bianchi VIII setting by carrying out an argument similar to the proof of
[
49
, Proposition 3, p. 725]
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider a Bianchi type VIII development with
N_{1}<0
,
N_{2}>0
and
N_{3}>0
, satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
.
Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. Then the corresponding solution to the modified
Wainwright-Hsu equations has an
\alpha
-limit point.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
5.9
, it is sufficient to demonstrate that there is a sequence
\tau_{k}\rightarrow-\infty
such that

(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-},N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})(\tau_{k})
converges. Due to the constraint (
3.39
),
\Sigma_{+}
,
\Sigma_{-}
and
N_{1}
are bounded for all
\tau
.
If there is no
\alpha
-limit point, we can therefore, appealing to Lemma
5.5
, assume that
N_{1}
,
N_{1}N_{2}
and
N_{1}N_{3}

converge to zero and that
N_{2}
,
N_{3}
converge to infinity. In particular,
(N_{2}N_{3})(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Combining
this observation with (
3.30c
), (
3.35a
) and Lemma
4.10
yields the conclusion that

\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{0}(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Sigma_{+})(s)ds\rightarrow-\infty

(5.15)

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.
Let us first consider the case that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\rightarrow-1
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Then Proposition
4.4
implies
that
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
, so that Corollary
5.3
yields a contradiction to the assumption.
If
\Sigma_{+}
does not converge to
-1
, we can appeal to Lemma
5.8
to conclude that there is an
\epsilon>0
and a
T\leq 0

such that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq-1+\epsilon/2
for all
\tau\leq T
. Combining this observation with (
5.11
) and (
3.30c
) yields,
assuming
\tau\leq T
,

\begin{split}\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}=&2(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}-1)(\Sigma_{+}+1)+\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})+2\Psi(\Sigma_{+}+1)-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}\\
\leq&\epsilon(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Sigma_{+}-\Sigma_{+}-1)+\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})+2\Psi(\Sigma_{+}+1)-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}\\
\leq&\epsilon(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Sigma_{+})-\tfrac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}+\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{9}{2}N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})+2\Psi(\Sigma_{+}+1)-2\Omega\Sigma_{+}.\end{split}

Since the last four terms converge to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
(where we used the conclusions of Lemma
5.5
),
it follows that there is a
T_{1}
such that

\Sigma_{+}^{\prime}\leq\epsilon(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Sigma_{+})

for all
\tau\leq T_{1}
. Integrating this inequality from
\tau\leq T_{1}
to
T_{1}
yields

\Sigma_{+}(T_{1})-\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\leq\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{T_{1}}\epsilon(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\Sigma_{+})(s)ds\rightarrow-\infty,

where we appealed (
5.15
) in the last step. Since
\Sigma_{+}
is bounded, this conclusion leads to a contradiction. The proposition
follows.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Consider a Bianchi type VIII or IX development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. Then
(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})(\tau)
tends to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.

Proof.

Note that
(N_{1}N_{2}N_{3})^{\prime}=3qN_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
. Since
\Omega
decays exponentially, there are two possibilities. Either
q
is integrable to the past, and

N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
converges to a non-zero number, or
q
is non-integrable, and
N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
converges to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. What remains
is thus to exclude the first possibility. Due to Propositions
5.9
and
5.10
, there is an
\alpha
-limit point, say

x_{*}
, of the solution to the modified Wainwright-Hsu equations. Assuming
N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
to converge to a non-zero number,
x_{*}
is of Bianchi type

\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
. Moreover, due to (
5.13
),
x_{*}
are effectively initial data for the vacuum version
of the Wainwright-Hsu equations studied in
[
49
]
. Moreover, applying the flow of the modified Wainwright-Hsu equations to
x_{*}
yields a
Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}
vacuum solution, the closure of whose range is contained in the the
\alpha
-limit set of the original solution. However, since

N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
is strictly monotonic along the vacuum flow (see the proof of
[
49
, Lemma 13, p. 722]
for a justification), we get a contradiction to the fact that the product

N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}
converges for the original solution. This means that
q
is not integrable to the past, and the desired conclusion follows.
∎

Next, we generalise
[
49
, Theorem 3, p. 726]
to the present setting.

Proposition 5.12
.

Consider a Bianchi type
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume it to be non-LRS
and with two
N_{i}
positive and one negative in case
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VIII}
and with all
N_{i}
positive in case
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
,
where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds. Then the corresponding solution to the modified Wainwright-Hsu equations has an
\alpha
-limit
point, say
x_{*}=(0,\phi_{\infty},0,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-},n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})
, such that
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
is not a special point on the Kasner circle and such that

n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0
.

Proof.

Let
Y_{*}=(0,\phi_{\infty},0,s_{+},s_{-},\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
be an
\alpha
-limit point of the solution to the modified Wainwright-Hsu equations; see
(
5.13
) and Propositions
5.9
and
5.10
. Due to (
5.13
), we, in what follows,
focus on
y_{*}=(s_{+},s_{-},\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
. Note that

\bar{n}_{1}\bar{n}_{2}\bar{n}_{3}=0
due to Lemma
5.11
. If
(s_{+},s_{-})
is not a special point, we are thus done. Assume therefore,
without loss of generality, that
(s_{+},s_{-})=(-1,0)
(note that, in the present proposition, we do not assume
N_{1}<0
when
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VIII}
). Since the
solution is not LRS, we cannot have
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})\rightarrow(-1,0)
; see Proposition
4.4
. Let
\{\tau_{k}\}
be a time sequence with

\tau_{k}\rightarrow-\infty
such that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-},N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})(\tau_{k})\rightarrow y_{*}
. Then there is an
0<\epsilon<1/1000
and a
sequence
\{s_{k}\}
with
s_{k}\leq\tau_{k}
such that
(\Sigma_{+}(s_{k}),\Sigma_{-}(s_{k}))
converges to the boundary of the ball of radius
\epsilon
and center
(-1,0)
.
Moreover,
s_{k}
can be assumed to
be the first time
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
reaches the boundary after
\tau_{k}
. If there is a subsequence of
N(s_{k})
which is bounded (where

N=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3})
), we can extract a convergent subsequence of the desired type. We can therefore assume that
\|N(s_{k})\|
tends to infinity.
As in the proof of
[
49
, Lemma 15]
, we can assume that two
N_{i}(s_{k})
tend to infinity. On the other hand,
it is, due to (
3.35a
) and (
3.36a
), clear that
|N_{1}|
decays from
\tau_{k}
to
s_{k}
(note that
q-4\Sigma_{+}\approx 6
in the ball
of radius
\epsilon
and center
(-1,0)
). This means that
N_{1}<0
if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VIII}
. We can thus assume
N_{1}<0
and
N_{2},N_{3}>0
in the case of
Bianchi type VIII. Moreover,
N_{2}(s_{k}),N_{3}(s_{k})\rightarrow\infty
,
(N_{1}N_{2})(s_{k})\rightarrow 0
,
(N_{1}N_{3})(s_{k})\rightarrow 0
, irrespective of
Bianchi type.

Bianchi type VIII.
Next, let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be the constant appearing in the statement of Lemma
5.8
and let
0<\eta<\epsilon_{0}

be such that
\Sigma_{+}(s_{k})\geq-1+2\eta
. Then there are corresponding constants
A
and
T
as in the statement of Lemma
5.8
.
We can, without loss of generality, assume
A\geq 1
. We can also, by removing elements from the sequence if necessary, assume that
s_{k}\leq T

for all
k
and that
N_{3}(s_{k})\geq 100A
for all
k
. By choosing
A
appropriately, we can also assume that there is a sequence

v_{k}\leq s_{k}
such that
N_{3}(v_{k})=10A
and
N_{3}(\tau)\geq 10A
in
[v_{k},s_{k}]
; the reason for this is that we know that there is
an
\alpha
-limit point. Moreover, for
k
large enough, we can assume that there are sequences
u_{k}
and
t_{k}
with

v_{k}\leq u_{k}\leq t_{k}\leq s_{k}
such that
N_{3}(t_{k})=10^{20}A
,
N_{3}(u_{k})=10^{10}A
and
10^{10}A\leq N_{3}(\tau)\leq 10^{20}A
for all

\tau\in[u_{k},t_{k}]
. Since
|N_{2}-N_{3}|\leq 2
due to the constraint, this means that
|N_{2}/N_{3}-1|\leq 2\cdot 10^{-10}A^{-1}
on

[u_{k},t_{k}]
. Next, since
q+2\Sigma_{+}-2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}\leq 6
(due to the constraint and the fact that
q\leq 2
),

10\leq\ln\tfrac{N_{3}(t_{k})}{N_{3}(u_{k})}=\textstyle{\int}_{u_{k}}^{t_{k}}(q+2\Sigma_{+}-2\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-})(\tau)d\tau\leq 6(t_{k}-u_{k}).

Thus
t_{k}-u_{k}\geq 1
. Finally, assume that
|\Sigma_{-}|\geq 1/10
in
[u_{k},t_{k}]
. Then

\big{|}\textstyle{\int}_{u_{k}}^{t_{k}}4\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}(\tau)d\tau\big{|}\geq\tfrac{2\sqrt{3}}{5}.

(5.16)

On the other hand

\tfrac{N_{2}(t_{k})}{N_{3}(t_{k})}=\exp\left(\textstyle{\int}_{u_{k}}^{t_{k}}4\sqrt{3}\Sigma_{-}(\tau)d\tau\right)\tfrac{N_{2}(u_{k})}{N_{3}(u_{k})}.

(5.17)

However, (
5.16
) and (
5.17
) are not consistent with the fact that
|N_{2}/N_{3}-1|\leq 2\cdot 10^{-10}A^{-1}
on

[u_{k},t_{k}]
. To conclude, there must thus be a sequence
r_{k}\in[u_{k},t_{k}]
such that
|\Sigma_{-}(r_{k})|\leq 1/10
,
\Sigma_{+}(r_{k})\geq-1+\eta

and
|N_{i}(r_{k})|\leq 10^{20}A+2
. Taking an appropriate subsequence of
r_{k}
yields the desired
\alpha
-limit point (note that the statement
that
n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}=0
follows from Lemma
5.11
).

Bianchi type IX.
In this case there is, due to the constraint and the fact that
[N_{1}(N_{2}+N_{3})](s_{k})\rightarrow 0
, an
\eta>0
such that

-1+\eta+\tfrac{C_{\Omega}}{1-3\alpha_{V}}e^{2(1-\alpha_{V})s_{k}}<\Sigma_{+}(s_{k})\leq 0

for
k
large enough. Moreover,
9N_{1}(s_{k})<N_{2}(s_{k})+N_{3}(s_{k})
for
k
large enough. If the latter inequality holds for all
\tau\leq s_{k}
, then
Lemma
5.7
implies that
\Sigma_{+}(\tau)\geq-1+\eta
for all
\tau\leq s_{k}
, contradicting the assumption that there
is a sequence tending to
-\infty
along which
\Sigma_{+}
converges to
-1
. Given this observation the rest of the proof can essentially be taken
verbatim from the proof of
[
49
, Theorem 3, p. 726]
.
∎

Corollary 5.13
.

Consider a Bianchi type VIII or IX development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that it is not LRS. Assume that

V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and that (
4.22
) holds.
Then the
\alpha
-limit set of the solution contains the closure of a Bianchi type II vacuum orbit. In particular, it contains at least two points
on the Kasner circle (with the
N_{i}
all vanishing), at least one of which is non-special.

Proof.

Due to the proof of
[
49
, Lemma 14, p. 723]
, we know that there is a non-special
\alpha
-limit point on the Kasner circle. By an argument which is
essentially identical to the proof of
[
50
, Proposition 6.1, p. 421]
, it follows that the
\alpha
-limit set of the solution contains the
closure of a Bianchi type II vacuum orbit.
∎

Chapter 6
From asymptotics to data on the singularity

In the last two chapters, we derive the asymptotic behaviour of solutions in terms of the Wainwright Hsu variables, both in the matter and in the vacuum
dominated settings. Here we use this information to conclude that non-oscillatory solutions induce data on the singularity.

6.1
The matter dominated case

We begin by considering the matter dominated case.

Proposition 6.1
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
which is not an isotropic Bianchi type I development. Assume
that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in case of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. Assume that
the development is matter dominated, i.e. that
\phi_{t}/\theta
converges to a non-zero limit in the direction of the singularity; see
Definition
1.83
. Then the development induces data on the singularity; see Definition
1.38
.

Proof.

In what follows, we make the assumptions and use the notation introduced in the proof of Proposition
1.31
. Note, to begin with, that
the
e_{i}^{\prime}
are eigenvector fields of the expansion normalised Weingarten map of the leaves of the foliation. The corresponding eigenvalues are

\ell_{i}:=\Sigma_{i}+1/3
. Next, note that (
3.13
) holds. Recalling (
3.27
), this means that

\tfrac{da_{i}}{d\tau}=3\ell_{i}a_{i}

(6.1)

and
a_{i}(0)=1
, where we have changed to
\tau
-time and we fix the translation ambiguity in
\tau
by demanding that
\tau(t_{0})=0
. Thus

\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}=\theta(0)^{\ell_{i}(\tau)}\exp\left(-\ell_{i}(\tau)\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{\tau}[1+q(s)]ds+\int_{0}^{\tau}3\ell_{i}(s)ds\right).

Due to (
3.40
) and (
4.23
), it is clear that
q-2
decays exponentially and that the difference between

\ell_{i}
and its limit decays exponentially. This means that
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
converges to a strictly positive limit. In other words, there
is a Riemannian metric
\mathscr{H}
such that (
1.8
) holds. Since the
\ell_{i}
converge, it is also clear that the expansion normalised
Weingarten map converges to a limit, say
\mathscr{K}
. Next, due to (
4.23
), it is clear that
\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}
converges
to a limit, say
\Phi_{1}
and that
\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta
converges to a limit, say
\Phi_{0}
.

That
\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}=1
is an immediate consequence of the fact that
\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{K}
. Since
\mathscr{K}
and
\mathscr{H}
are both diagonal with respect to

\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
, it is clear that
\mathscr{K}
is symmetric with respect to
\mathscr{H}
. Since the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
is diagonal
and the
e_{i}^{\prime}
are eigenvectors of
\mathscr{K}
, it is clear that
\mathrm{div}_{\mathscr{H}}\mathscr{K}=0
. That
\mathrm{tr}\mathscr{K}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}=1
follows by taking the limit of
(
3.39
), keeping (
4.23
) in mind. Finally, since
\Phi_{1}\neq 0
, it is clear that
1
cannot be an
eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
. On the other hand, due to Theorem
4.13
, if
N_{i}\neq 0
, then
f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})>0
. This means that
condition 3 of Definition
1.17
is fulfilled.

It remains to be verified that the data on the singularity are neither of isotropic nor of LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
; cf.
Definition
1.25
. Assume, to this end, that they are. This means, in particular, that the original regular initial data
\mathfrak{I}

are of Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. Moreover, there is an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
and a family
\Psi_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, of Lie
algebra isomorphisms such that (
1.4
) and
\mathscr{K}\Psi_{t}=\Psi_{t}\mathscr{K}
both hold for all
t
. In the LRS setting, this is true by definition. In
the isotropic setting,
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
, so that
\mathscr{K}
commutes with any Lie algebra isomorphism. As noted in the proof of
Lemma
2.8
, there is only one way for
\mathscr{H}
to have constant curvature; given an orthonormal frame
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}

with respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that the corresponding commutator matrix, say
\bar{n}
, is diagonal with
\bar{n}=\mathrm{diag}(0,\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
, then
\bar{n}_{2}
and
\bar{n}_{3}

have to equal. This means that, defining
\Psi_{t}
by (
1.4
) with
e_{i}
replaced by
\bar{e}_{i}
,
\Psi_{t}
is a family of Lie algebra
isomorphisms and
\mathscr{K}\Psi_{t}=\Psi_{t}\mathscr{K}
holds for all
t
.

Combining the existence of the frame
\{e_{i}\}
and the isomorphisms
\Psi_{t}
with the fact that
\mathscr{K}
is symmetric with respect to
\mathscr{H}
, it follows that the

e_{i}
are eigenvector fields of
\mathscr{K}
. Moreover, if
\mathscr{K}e_{i}=p_{i}e_{i}
(no summation), then
p_{2}=p_{3}
. Next, let
\nu
be the commutator matrix associated
with
\{e_{i}\}
. Then
\nu=\mathrm{diag}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{2})
due to the fact that the
\Psi_{t}
are isomorphisms. Since the Lie group is of Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
, it
follows that
\nu_{1}=0
. Concerning the original
development, we can assume that
N_{1}=0
. In other words, if
n^{\prime}
is the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
, then
n^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(0,n_{2}^{\prime},n_{3}^{\prime})
. Next,
let
\alpha_{i}
denote the limit of
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
and
\bar{e}_{i}:=\alpha_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{\prime}
. Then
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
is an orthonormal frame of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
. Let

\bar{n}
denote the commutator matrix associated with
\{\bar{e}_{i}\}
. Then
\bar{n}=\mathrm{diag}(0,\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
. Let
A
be a matrix such that
\bar{e}_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}e_{j}
.
Then, due to the transformation law between commutator matrices associated with orthonormal bases, see
[
54
, (19.3), p. 207]
,

\nu=(\det A)^{-1}A^{t}\bar{n}A.

Note that
A
is orthogonal, so that
\det A=\pm 1
. On the other hand, exchanging
e_{1}
by
-e_{1}
, if necessary, we can ensure that
\det A=1
. This
means that
A\nu=\bar{n}A
. From this, it can be deduced that
\nu_{2}=\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
and that
\bar{e}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
. Interchanging
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
with
-e_{1}
and
-e_{3}
,
respectively, and interchanging
\Psi_{t}
with
\Psi_{-t}
, if necessary, we can assume that
\bar{e}_{1}=e_{1}
. Moreover,
\bar{e}_{2}
and
\bar{e}_{3}
are obtained by applying
a rotation to
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
. We can therefore, without loss of generality, assume that
\bar{e}_{i}=e_{i}
. Next, note that if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
, then

N_{k}=\tfrac{1}{\theta}\tfrac{a_{k}}{a_{i}a_{j}}n_{k}^{\prime}\ \ \ \mathrm{and}\ \ \ \bar{n}_{k}=\tfrac{\alpha_{k}}{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}n_{k}^{\prime}.

Since
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
, it follows that

\tfrac{N_{2}}{N_{3}}=\tfrac{a_{2}^{2}}{a_{3}^{2}}\tfrac{n_{2}^{\prime}}{n_{3}^{\prime}}=\tfrac{a_{2}^{2}}{a_{3}^{2}}\tfrac{\alpha_{2}^{-2}}{\alpha_{3}^{-2}}\tfrac{\bar{n}_{2}}{\bar{n}_{3}}=\tfrac{a_{2}^{2}}{a_{3}^{2}}\tfrac{\alpha_{2}^{-2}}{\alpha_{3}^{-2}}.

Since
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
converges to
\alpha_{i}
, we conclude that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\left(\tfrac{N_{2}}{N_{3}}\right)(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\left(\theta^{2(\ell_{3}-\ell_{2})}\tfrac{\theta^{2\ell_{2}}a_{2}^{2}}{\theta^{2\ell_{3}}a_{3}^{2}}\tfrac{\alpha_{2}^{-2}}{\alpha_{3}^{-2}}\right)(\tau)=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\theta^{2(\ell_{3}-\ell_{2})}(\tau).

(6.2)

Next, note that
\ell_{i}
is the eigenvalue of
\mathcal{K}
corresponding to the eigenvector
e_{i}^{\prime}
. Since
e_{i}^{\prime}
is parallel to
\bar{e}_{i}=e_{i}
;
\mathscr{K}e_{i}=p_{i}e_{i}

(no summation); and
\mathcal{K}
converges to
\mathscr{K}
, it is clear that
\ell_{i}
converges to
p_{i}
. By the above, we, in addition, know that
\ell_{i}

converges exponentially to its limit and that
p_{2}=p_{3}
. This means that
\ell_{2}-\ell_{3}
converges to zero exponentially. On the other hand,
\ln\theta

does not grow faster than linearly. Combining these observations with (
6.2
) yields the conclusion that
N_{2}/N_{3}
converges
to
1
in the direction of the singularity. Since
\Sigma_{-}
converges to zero (note that
2\Sigma_{-}=\sqrt{3}(\ell_{2}-\ell_{3})
);
\Sigma_{+}
converges to a limit, say

\sigma_{+}
;
\phi^{\prime}
converges to a non-zero
limit, say
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
;
\Omega
converges to zero; and all the
N_{i}
converge to zero, appealing to (
3.39
) yields the conclusion that

\sigma_{+}\in(-1,1)
and that
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\bar{\Phi}_{1}^{2}/6=1
. Due to the above observations, the conditions of Proposition
4.7
are
satisfied. This means that the original solution is contained in the invariant set given by
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
. This means that
a_{2}=a_{3}
,
\ell_{2}=\ell_{3}

and
N_{2}=N_{3}
, so that the original initial data are of either isotropic or LRS Bianchi type
\mathrm{VII}_{0}
. However, this contradicts the assumption
that the original initial data belong to
\mathcal{B}[V]
. The proposition follows.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

Assume that the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are not satisfied, but that the conditions of Lemma
3.6

are. Assume, moreover, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then the development induces data on the singularity.

Proof.

Due to the conclusions of Theorem
4.13
, the statement follows by an argument which is essentially identical to the proof of
Proposition
6.1
.
∎

6.2
The vacuum dominated case

Proposition 6.3
.

Consider an anisotropic development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
in case of Bianchi type I and non-LRS Bianchi type II; and
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
otherwise. If the development is vacuum dominated,
see Definition
1.83
, and of Bianchi type I or II, then it induces data on the singularity.

Proof.

That the development is vacuum dominated means that (
4.22
) holds. This means that the conclusions of
Corollary
5.1
hold, so that the desired conclusion follows by an argument which is essentially identical to the proof of
Proposition
6.1
, with one exception: in this case,
1
can be an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
. On the other hand, if

1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
, the limit point on the Kasner circle is special. Without loss of generality, we can assume
that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})(\tau)\rightarrow(-1,0)
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Due to Proposition
4.4
, we conclude that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
that
N_{2}=N_{3}
for the entire solution, so that
N_{2}=N_{3}=0
. This means that
\ell_{2}=\ell_{3}
, using the notation of the proof of
Proposition
6.1
, so that
a_{2}=a_{3}
and
\mathscr{H}(e_{2}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})=\mathscr{H}(e_{3}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime})
. Rescaling the frame
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}

appropriately yields a frame
\{e_{i}\}
which is orthonormal with respect to
\mathscr{H}
; such that
\mathscr{K}e_{1}=e_{1}
; such that
\mathscr{K}e_{A}=0
for

A\in\{2,3\}
; and such that the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}\}
is
\mathrm{diag}(n,0,0)
. In other words, the frame
\{e_{i}\}
is
of the form required in condition 4 of Definition
1.17
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Consider an anisotropic development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. If the development is vacuum dominated, see Definition
1.83
, and of Bianchi type VI
0
or
VII
0
, then it induces data on the singularity.

Proof.

Due to Corollary
5.2
, the desired conclusion follow by an argument similar to the above. Note, however, that
1
cannot be
an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
in this case.
∎

Finally, the following conclusions hold concerning vacuum dominated LRS Bianchi type VIII and IX developments.

Proposition 6.5
.

Consider a development satisfying the conditions of Lemma
3.4
. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. If the development is vacuum dominated, see Definition
1.83
, and of LRS Bianchi type VIII
or of isotropic or LRS Bianchi type IX, then it induces data on the singularity.

Proof.

In this case, the desired conclusion follows from Corollaries
5.3
and
5.4
and arguments
similar to the above.
∎

Chapter 7
Specifying data on the singularity

Our next goal is to specify data on the singularity. In the end, we wish to prove Theorem
1.45
. However, as a first
step, we here specify data on the singularity for solutions to (
3.31
) and (
3.35
)–(
3.39
). The corresponding
geometric results
are to be found in Chapter
9
. Note that similar results are derived in the non-degenerate Einstein stiff fluid
setting in
[
57
, Subsections 2.4–2.5]
. However, we here chose different variables, shortening the arguments in
[
57
]
. On
the other hand, we here also deal with the case that the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
coincide. This causes substantial additional complications in
comparison with
[
57
]
. We begin by proving an abstract existence and uniqueness result.

7.1
Abstract setting

Fix
1\leq k\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\geq\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\mathcal{X}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times(-\infty,\tau_{0}],\mathbb{R}^{k})
. Consider

x^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathcal{X}[x(\tau),\tau].

(7.1)

Fix an
\epsilon>0
and let
X_{\tau_{0}}
be the space of continuous functions
x:(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that

\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}(e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|)\leq 1
. For
x_{a},x_{b}\in X_{\tau_{0}}
define

\begin{split}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{b}):=&\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}(e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x_{a}(\tau)-x_{b}(\tau)|).\end{split}

(7.2)

Note that
(X_{\tau_{0}},d_{\tau_{0}})
is a complete metric space. Assume that there is a constant
C_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for every

\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
,

|\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau]|\leq C_{\mathcal{X}}e^{3\epsilon\tau/2},\ \ \ |\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau]-\mathcal{X}[x_{b}(\tau),\tau]|\leq C_{\mathcal{X}}e^{2\epsilon\tau}d_{\tau_{1}}(x_{a},x_{b})

(7.3)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
and all
x_{a},x_{b}\in X_{\tau_{1}}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Given the above assumptions, there is a uniquely determined unbounded interval
I_{\max}\subset(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
and a unique solution

x\in C^{1}(I_{\max},\mathbb{R}^{k})
to (
7.1
) such that
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
and
such that
I_{\max}
is the maximal existence interval of the solution
x
to (
7.1
). Moreover, this unique solution
is smooth.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
,
x\in X_{\tau_{1}}
and define
\Phi[x]
by

\Phi[x](\tau):=\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}\mathcal{X}[x(s),s]ds

for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Clearly,
\Phi[x]:(-\infty,\tau_{1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
is well defined and continuous; see (
7.3
).
Moreover,

|\Phi[x](\tau)|\leq Ce^{3\epsilon\tau/2}

(7.4)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, where
C
only depends on
C_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\epsilon
. For
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending
only on
\epsilon
and
C_{\mathcal{X}}
, it is thus clear that
\Phi[x]\in X_{\tau_{1}}
for all
x\in X_{\tau_{1}}
. Next, due to the second inequality in
(
7.3
),

d_{\tau_{1}}(\Phi[x_{a}],\Phi[x_{b}])\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{\tau_{1}}(x_{a},x_{b})

for all
x_{a},x_{b}\in X_{\tau_{1}}
, assuming
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
to be close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending only on
\epsilon
and
C_{\mathcal{X}}
.
By the Banach fixed point theorem, we conclude that
\Phi
has a unique fixed point in
X_{\tau_{1}}
for
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
close enough to

-\infty
. By the definition of
\Phi
, the properties of
\mathcal{X}
and an iterative argument, it is clear that this fixed point, say
y
, is
smooth and solves (
7.1
). Let
x
be the maximal solution to (
7.1
) with initial data

y(\tau_{2})
at
\tau_{2}\leq\tau_{1}
and let
I_{\max}
be the corresponding existence interval. Then
x(\tau)=y(\tau)
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
.
Moreover, since
y
is a fixed point of
\Phi
, (
7.4
) implies that
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.

Assume now
x_{i}\in C^{1}(I_{\max,i},\mathbb{R}^{k})
,
i=1,2
, to be two solutions satisfying the conditions of the lemma. Then, for

\tau_{1}\in I_{\max,1}\cap I_{\max,2}
close enough to
-\infty
, it is clear that
x_{i}\in X_{\tau_{1}}
,
i=1,2
. Since
x_{i}
,
i=1,2
, both satisfy
(
7.1
) and converge to
0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, it follows from the first inequality in (
7.3
)
that
\Phi[x_{i}]=x_{i}
, if we consider the
x_{i}
to be elements of
X_{\tau_{1}}
. However, for
\tau_{1}
close enough to
-\infty
, we know that
fixed points of
\Phi
are unique. This means that
x_{1}(\tau)=x_{2}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Since solutions to (
7.1
) are
uniquely determined by initial data, it is clear that
x_{1}=x_{2}
and
I_{\max,1}=I_{\max,2}
. The lemma follows.
∎

7.2
Data on the singularity and adapted variables

Our next goal is to specify data on the singularity for solutions to (
3.31
) and (
3.35
)–(
3.39
), keeping
Remark
3.11
in mind. Before doing so, note that if

\mu_{k}
are functions satisfying

\mu_{k}^{\prime}=q+6\Sigma_{k},

(7.5)

then
N_{k}
can be written
N_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}}
(no summation), where
\epsilon_{k}
equals
-1
,
0
or
1
, assuming the initial data for the

\mu_{i}
are chosen appropriately. We specify the following type of data on the singularity.

Definition 7.2
.

Let
\mathsf{V}
be the set of

(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0})\in\{-1,0,1\}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{9}

such that
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3}=0
and

p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{9}\Phi_{1}^{2}=1,

(7.6)

where
p_{i}=\sigma_{i}+1/3
, and such that one of the following two conditions hold: (i) If
\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
, then
p_{i}>0
.
(ii) There are
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that
p_{i}=1
,
m_{j}=m_{k}
,
\epsilon_{j}=\epsilon_{k}
and all the
\epsilon_{l}
are non-zero.

Define, for
\lambda\in S_{3}
,

\begin{split}&\eta_{\lambda}^{\pm}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0})\\
:=&(\pm\epsilon_{\lambda(1)},\pm\epsilon_{\lambda(2)},\pm\epsilon_{\lambda(3)},m_{\lambda(1)},m_{\lambda(2)},m_{\lambda(3)},\sigma_{\lambda(1)},\sigma_{\lambda(2)},\sigma_{\lambda(3)},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0}).\end{split}

Note that
\eta_{\lambda}^{\pm}
maps
\mathsf{V}
to
\mathsf{V}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
V\in\mathfrak{P}^{2}_{\alpha_{V}}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
and

\Pi:=(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{3},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{1},\Phi_{0})\in\mathsf{V}.

(7.7)

Then there is a unique solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.38
), where
q
is defined by
(
3.40
), such that

N_{k}\neq 0
if and only if
\epsilon_{k}\neq 0
, such that
N_{k}
, if non-zero, has the same sign as
\epsilon_{k}
, and such that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}[\nu_{1}(\tau),\nu_{2}(\tau),\nu_{3}(\tau),s_{1}(\tau),s_{2}(\tau),s_{3}(\tau),\psi_{0}(\tau),\psi_{1}(\tau),\varkappa(\tau)]=0,

(7.8)

where

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\mu_{i}(\tau)-6(\Sigma_{i}+1/3)\tau-m_{i},

(7.9a)

\displaystyle s_{i}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}(\tau)-\sigma_{i},

(7.9b)

\displaystyle\varkappa(\tau):=

\displaystyle\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\varkappa_{\infty},

(7.9c)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(\tau)-\phi_{1}\tau-\Phi_{0}.

(7.9d)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(\tau):=

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(\tau)-\Phi_{1},

(7.9e)

Here, if
\epsilon_{k}\neq 0
,
\mu_{k}
is chosen so that
N_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}}
(no summation). In particular,
\mu_{k}
then satisfies
(
7.5
). If
\epsilon_{k}=0
,
\mu_{k}
is defined to be the solution to (
7.5
) with the property that

\mu_{k}-6p_{k}\tau\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, where
p_{k}=\sigma_{k}+1/3
. Finally, the solution also satisfies the Hamiltonian
constraint (
3.39
) and the relation (
3.30c
).

If
(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3},\theta,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
is the solution corresponding to
\Pi\in\mathsf{V}
and
\lambda\in S_{3}
, then the solution
corresponding to
\eta_{\lambda}^{\pm}(\Pi)
is given by

(\pm N_{\lambda(1)},\pm N_{\lambda(2)},\pm N_{\lambda(3)},\Sigma_{\lambda(1)},\Sigma_{\lambda(2)},\Sigma_{\lambda(3)},\theta,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
.

Remark 7.4
.

In order for the statement of the proposition to be meaningful, we need to prove that if
\epsilon_{k}=0
, then there is a unique solution
\mu_{k}
to
(
7.5
) (where
q
and
\Sigma_{k}
are the functions associated with the solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.38
);
cf. Remark
3.11
) such that
\mu_{k}-6p_{k}\tau\rightarrow 0
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Note, to this end, that if all the
p_{i}
satisfy
p_{i}<1
, then the remaining assumptions (excluding the ones involving

\nu_{k}
for
k
such that
\epsilon_{k}=0
) imply that the
N_{i}
converge to zero exponentially. If there is an
i
such that
p_{i}=1
, we can
assume that
p_{1}=1
, so that Lemma
4.4
applies and implies that
N_{2}=N_{3}
and that
\Sigma_{-}=0
. This means that
N_{1}
and

N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero exponentially. Moreover, due to the asymptotics of
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
and
\theta
; the fact that

|\Phi_{1}|\leq\sqrt{6}
, see (
7.6
); and the assumptions
concerning
V
, it is clear that
\Omega
decays to zero exponentially. Combining these observations with (
3.40
), it is clear that

q-2
converges to zero exponentially. Combining the above arguments with the equations for
\Sigma_{\pm}
, it follows that the
\Sigma_{\pm}

converge exponentially to their limits. Returning to (
7.5
) with this information in mind, the desired conclusion follows.

Remark 7.5
.

Assume that
p_{1}=1
. Then if
\epsilon_{2}\neq\epsilon_{3}
there is no corresponding solution. Moreover, if
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{3}\neq 0

and
m_{2}\neq m_{3}
, there is no corresponding solution.

Proof.

In the following arguments, it is convenient to work with
\Sigma_{\pm}
instead of
\Sigma_{i}
; cf. Remark
3.11
. We
therefore introduce
s_{\pm}=\Sigma_{\pm}-\sigma_{\pm}
, where the relation between
\Sigma_{\pm}
(
\sigma_{\pm}
) and
\Sigma_{i}
(
\sigma_{i}
) is given by
(
3.34
). With this notation, and notation as in (
7.9
),

\displaystyle\nu_{k}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle q-2+(2-q)f_{k}(0,\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})\tau+3f_{k}(0,S_{+},S_{-})\tau,

(7.10a)

\displaystyle s_{\pm}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\Sigma_{\pm}-3S_{\pm},

(7.10b)

\displaystyle\varkappa^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2-q,

(7.10c)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle[(2-q)\phi_{1}+9\theta^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}]\tau,

(7.10d)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\phi_{1}-9\theta^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}.

(7.10e)

Introducing
x:=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},s_{+},s_{-},\psi_{0},\psi_{1},\varkappa)
, the right hand side of (
7.10
)
defines a time dependent vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, say
\mathcal{X}
, depending on the parameters
\Pi
. With this notation,
(
7.10
) reads

x^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathcal{X}[x(\tau),\tau;\Pi];

(7.11)

note that since
N_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}}
and (
7.9
) holds, the right hand side of (
7.10
) can be expressed
in terms of
x
,
\tau
and
\Pi
(we here tacitly assume
q
to be defined by (
3.40
)). Next, let
\tau_{0}\leq 0
;
\eta_{i}:=6
if

\epsilon_{i}=0
;
\eta_{i}:=6p_{i}
if
\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
, where

p_{i}:=f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})/6=\sigma_{i}+1/3;

(7.12)

and
\eta_{\phi}:=3(1-\alpha_{V})
. If all the
p_{i}<1
, let

\epsilon:=\min\{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3},\eta_{\phi}\}.

(7.13)

If there is a
p_{i}=1
, assume
p_{1}=1
, so that
\sigma_{+}=-1
,
\sigma_{-}=0
and
\Phi_{1}=0
. Let, moreover,
\epsilon:=\min\{3,\eta_{\phi}\}
. Note that

\epsilon>0
due to the assumptions. Define
X_{\tau_{0}}
and
d_{\tau_{0}}
as in (
7.2
) and the adjacent text, with
k=8
. Here and
below, we use the notation

x_{a}=(\nu_{1,a},\nu_{2,a},\nu_{3,a},s_{+,a},s_{-,a},\psi_{0,a},\psi_{1,a},\varkappa_{a})

(7.14)

and similarly for
x_{b}
. Next, we wish to verify that (
7.3
) is satisfied. Assume, to this end, that
x_{a},x_{b}\in X_{\tau_{1}}

for some
\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{0}
and note that

\displaystyle N_{i,a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle n_{i}\exp[6p_{i}\tau+\nu_{i,a}(\tau)+f_{i}(0,s_{+,a}(\tau),s_{-,a}(\tau))\tau],

(7.15a)

\displaystyle\theta_{a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\theta_{\infty}\exp[-3\tau+\varkappa_{a}(\tau)],

(7.15b)

\displaystyle\phi_{1,a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\psi_{1,a}(\tau)+\Phi_{1},

(7.15c)

\displaystyle\phi_{0,a}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\psi_{0,a}(\tau)+[\psi_{1,a}(\tau)+\Phi_{1}]\tau+\Phi_{0},

(7.15d)

where
n_{i}:=\epsilon_{i}e^{m_{i}}
and
\theta_{\infty}:=e^{\varkappa_{\infty}}
. In case
\sigma_{+}=-1
, we assume
N_{2,a}=N_{3,a}
and
\Sigma_{-,a}=0
, since this
condition is necessary in case
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})\rightarrow(-1,0)
; see Proposition
4.4
.
Replacing
\phi_{0}
and
\theta
in the second equality in (
3.30b
) with
\phi_{0,a}
and
\theta_{a}
respectively yields

\Omega_{a}
. Replacing
N_{i}
and
\Omega
in (
3.40
) with
N_{i,a}
and
\Omega_{a}
respectively yields
q_{a}
. Moreover,
replacing the
N_{i}
appearing in the formulae for
S_{\pm}
with
N_{i,a}
yields
S_{\pm,a}
. Thus

|S_{+,a}(\tau)|+|S_{-,a}(\tau)|\leq C(n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2})e^{2\epsilon\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, where
C
is a numerical constant. Moreover, there is a constant
C
, depending only on
\epsilon
, such that

\Omega_{a}(\tau)+\theta_{a}(\tau)^{-2}|V^{\prime}[\phi_{0,a}(\tau)]|\leq Cc_{1}\theta_{\infty}^{-2}e^{\sqrt{6}|\Phi_{0}|}e^{2\eta_{\phi}\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, where we used the fact that
|\Phi_{1}|\leq\sqrt{6}
and
c_{1}
is the constant appearing in
(
1.9
). Introducing

K_{\infty}:=n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}+c_{2}\theta_{\infty}^{-2}e^{\sqrt{6}|\Phi_{0}|},

(7.16)

we conclude that there is a constant
C
, depending only on
\epsilon
, such that

|S_{+,a}(\tau)|+|S_{-,a}(\tau)|+|q_{a}(\tau)-2|+\theta_{a}(\tau)^{-2}|V^{\prime}[\phi_{0,a}(\tau)]|\leq CK_{\infty}e^{2\epsilon\tau}

(7.17)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
. Combining (
7.17
) with the fact that
|\Sigma_{\pm,a}(\tau)|\leq 2
and

|\phi_{1,a}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}+1
for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, it follows that

|\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]|\leq CK_{\infty}\langle\tau\rangle e^{2\epsilon\tau}

(7.18)

for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, where
C
only depends on
\epsilon
. Thus the first estimate in (
7.3
) holds.

Next, we prove the second estimate in (
7.3
). Note, to this end, that (assuming one of
i,j
equals
1
in case

\sigma_{+}=-1
)

\begin{split}N_{i,a}N_{j,a}-N_{i,b}N_{j,b}=&n_{i}n_{j}e^{2\epsilon_{ij}\tau}\exp[\nu_{i,b}+\nu_{j,b}+f_{i}(0,s_{+,b},s_{-,b})\tau+f_{j}(0,s_{+,b},s_{-,b})\tau]\\
&(\exp[\delta\nu_{i}+\delta\nu_{j}+f_{i}(0,\delta s_{+},\delta s_{-})\tau+f_{j}(0,\delta s_{+},\delta s_{-})\tau]-1),\end{split}

where
\epsilon_{ij}\geq\epsilon
,
\delta\nu_{i}:=\nu_{i,a}-\nu_{i,b}
and
\delta s_{\pm}:=s_{\pm,a}-s_{\pm,b}
. Due to the definition of
X_{\tau_{1}}
and the
fact that
\tau_{1}\leq 0
, it follows that
|\nu_{i,a}|\leq 1
,
|s_{+,b}(\tau)|\leq e^{\epsilon\tau}
etc., so that

\begin{split}|N_{i,a}N_{j,a}-N_{i,b}N_{j,b}|\leq&C|n_{i}n_{j}|\langle\tau\rangle e^{2\epsilon_{ij}\tau}(|\delta\nu_{i}|+|\delta\nu_{j}|+|\delta s_{+}|+|\delta s_{-}|)\\
\leq&C|n_{i}n_{j}|\langle\tau\rangle e^{(2\epsilon_{ij}+\epsilon)\tau}d_{\tau_{1}}(x_{a},x_{b})\end{split}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
, where
C
only depends on
\epsilon
. Next,

\left|\theta_{a}^{-2}V\circ\phi_{0,a}-\theta_{b}^{-2}V\circ\phi_{0,b}\right|\leq\theta_{a}^{-2}|V\circ\phi_{0,a}-V\circ\phi_{0,b}|+|V\circ\phi_{0,b}|\cdot|\theta_{a}^{-2}-\theta_{b}^{-2}|.

(7.19)

Keeping in mind that

V(\xi)-V(\zeta)=\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}V^{\prime}[s\xi+(1-s)\zeta]ds\cdot(\xi-\zeta)

(7.20)

as well as the definitions of
\phi_{0,a}
,
\phi_{0,b}
,
\theta_{a}
and
\theta_{b}
, the estimates for
V
etc., it can be verified that

\begin{split}\left|\theta_{a}^{-2}V\circ\phi_{0,a}-\theta_{b}^{-2}V\circ\phi_{0,b}\right|\leq&CK_{\infty}e^{2\epsilon\tau}[|\delta\psi_{0}|+\langle\tau\rangle|\delta\psi_{1}|+|\delta\varkappa|]\\
\leq&CK_{\infty}\langle\tau\rangle e^{3\epsilon\tau}d_{\tau_{1}}(x_{a},x_{b})\end{split}

(7.21)

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C
only depends on
\epsilon
;
\delta\psi_{i}:=\psi_{i,a}-\psi_{i,b}
; and
\delta\varkappa:=\varkappa_{a}-\varkappa_{b}
. There
is a similar estimate with
V
replaced by
V^{\prime}
. However, this estimate necessitates control of two derivatives of
V
. It is here we use the fact that

V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{2}
and the fact that
c_{2}
appears in the definition of
K_{\infty}
, see (
7.16
), and not
c_{1}
. By arguments of this
type, it can be verified that

|\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]-\mathcal{X}[x_{b}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]|\leq CK_{\infty}\langle\tau\rangle^{2}e^{3\epsilon\tau}d_{\tau_{1}}(x_{a},x_{b}).

Thus the second estimate in (
7.3
) holds.

Existence and uniqueness.
That there is a unique solution
x\in C^{1}(I_{\max},\mathbb{R}^{k})
to (
7.11
) now follows from
Lemma
7.1
. However, the uniqueness is in the class of solutions satisfying
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Note that
x
satisfies (
7.10
). Define
N_{i}
,
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
and
\theta
in analogy
with (
7.15
) and
\Sigma_{\pm}
by
\Sigma_{\pm}:=s_{\pm}+\sigma_{\pm}
. Finally, the
S_{\pm}
are defined by
(
3.37
) and
q
is defined by (
3.40
). It can then be verified that (
3.31
)–(
3.38
)
are satisfied. Finally, note that, due to
the fact that we replaced
q
by (
3.40
) in the equations, the identity (
3.41
) holds. Since
2-q
is integrable, the
fact that the limit of
f
is zero implies that
f
vanishes identically. Thus (
3.39
) holds. Combining (
3.39
)
and (
3.40
) yields (
3.30c
). Finally
x(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, so that (
7.8
)
holds. In order to prove uniqueness for solutions
x
such that
x(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, we need to prove that
convergence to zero implies that
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Assume that there is a solution to
(
3.31
)–(
3.38
) with the properties stated in the proposition. Then
x(\tau)\rightarrow 0

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
due to (
7.8
), where
x:(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{8}
is well defined for some choice of

\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,
x
is smooth. Due to the assumptions, all the
N_{i}
converge to zero exponentially (except if
\sigma_{+}=-1
,
in which case
N_{1}
and
N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero exponentially). The same is true of

\Omega
and
\theta^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}
. Due to the definition of
q
, it follows that
q-2
converges to zero exponentially. Using these
observations, it can be demonstrated (by an argument similar to the proof of (
7.18
)) that
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0

as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Existence and uniqueness in the general setting follow.

Symmetry.
The final statement of the proposition follows by uniqueness and the symmetries of the equations.

In order to prove the statement in Remark
7.5
, assume that there is a solution corresponding to data on
the singularity with
\sigma_{+}=-1
and
\sigma_{-}=0
. Then, due to Proposition
4.4
,
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
. This is clearly
incompatible with
\epsilon_{2}\neq\epsilon_{3}
. Assume now, in addition, that
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{3}\neq 0
. Then
\mu_{2}=\mu_{3}
(since
N_{2}=N_{3}
). On the other hand,

\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}
; since
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
S_{-}=0
, it follows that
\nu_{2}^{\prime}=\nu_{3}^{\prime}
, so that
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}
since
\nu_{2}
and
\nu_{3}
both converge to zero
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. From (
7.9a
) and the fact that
\Sigma_{-}=0
, it then follows that
m_{2}=m_{3}
.
∎

Chapter 8
The map taking regular initial data to data on the singularity

For many Bianchi class A types, initial data give rise to data on the singularity, see Chapter
6
. In the present
chapter, we take the first steps in the analysis of the properties of the corresponding map.

8.1
Smoothness of the map from regular initial data to the
leading order asymptotics

In the end, we are interested in analysing the map taking isometry classes of regular initial data (or isometry classes of developments) to isometry
classes of initial data on the singularity; cf. Lemma
1.48
. In particular, we wish to prove that the corresponding map is
defined on an open subset and smooth. In practice, this involves analysing the behaviour of solutions to (
3.31
) and
(
3.35
)–(
3.39
). However, in what follows, it is convenient to use the
variables
\Sigma_{i}
as opposed to
\Sigma_{\pm}
; see Remark
3.11
. We begin by introducing some terminology concerning the
background solutions we perturb in the results below.

Definition 8.1
.

Consider a solution
(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3},\theta,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
), cf.
Remark
3.11
, where

V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. It is said to be an
admissible convergent solution
if the following conditions are satisfied.
First, the existence interval includes
(-\infty,T]
for some
T\leq 0
and there are constants
\sigma_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, and
\Phi_{1}
such that

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}[\Sigma_{1}(\tau),\Sigma_{2}(\tau),\Sigma_{3}(\tau),\phi_{1}(\tau)]=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\Phi_{1}).

(8.1)

Next, there is a constant
C_{\theta}
such that
\ln\theta(\tau)\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}
for all
\tau\leq T
. Finally, one of the following conditions
are satisfied:

(i)

If
N_{i}\neq 0
, then
\sigma_{i}+1/3>0
. Moreover, there are constants
C_{\infty}
and
\alpha_{\infty}>0
such that

|N_{i}(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{\alpha_{\infty}\tau}
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and all
\tau\leq T
.

(ii)

The solution is of LRS Bianchi type VIII or IX. Moreover,
\Phi_{1}=0
and there are
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
such that
\sigma_{i}=2/3

and
\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{k}=-1/3
.

Remark 8.2
.

If the conditions of one of the propositions in Chapter
6
are satisfied, then the corresponding solution to
(
3.31
)–(
3.39
) is an admissible convergent solution. We leave the verification of this
statement to the reader.

Before addressing the differentiability of the map from initial data to asymptotic data, it is convenient to change variables. Note, to this end, that
if
\mu_{k}
are functions satisfying (
7.5
), then
N_{k}
can be written
N_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}}
(no summation), where
\epsilon_{k}

equals
-1
,
0
or
1
, assuming the initial data for the
\mu_{k}
are chosen appropriately. Introduce the variables

\nu_{k}:=\mu_{k}-6(\Sigma_{k}+1/3)\tau

(8.2)

(the notation used in the present chapter differs from that used in Section
7.2
). Define, moreover,

\varkappa:=\ln\theta+3\tau,\ \ \ \psi_{0}:=\phi_{0}-\phi_{1}\tau

(in what follows, we are only interested in asymptotic regimes in which
\theta>0
). Then

\displaystyle\nu_{k}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle q-2+6(2-q)\Sigma_{k}\tau+18S_{k}\tau,

(8.3a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{k}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\Sigma_{k}-3S_{k},

(8.3b)

\displaystyle\varkappa^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2-q,

(8.3c)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle[(2-q)\phi_{1}+9\theta^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}]\tau,

(8.3d)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-q)\phi_{1}-9\theta^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}

(8.3e)

for
k=1,2,3
, where it is understood that
\phi_{0}=\psi_{0}+\phi_{1}\tau
, that
q
is given by (
3.40
), that
\theta=e^{-3\tau+\varkappa}
,
that
\Omega
is defined by (
3.30
) and that the
S_{i}
are given by (
3.33
). Moreover, it is understood that

N_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}}
(no summation), where the
\mu_{k}
are defined in terms of the
\nu_{k}
and
\Sigma_{k}
via (
8.2
).
For these equations, the constraint (
3.39
) takes the form

\begin{split}1=&\frac{3}{2}(\Sigma_{1}^{2}+\Sigma_{2}^{2}+\Sigma_{3}^{2})+\frac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}+3e^{6\tau-2\varkappa}V(\psi_{0}+\phi_{1}\tau)\\
&+\frac{3}{4}[\epsilon_{1}^{2}e^{2\mu_{1}}+\epsilon_{2}^{2}e^{2\mu_{2}}+\epsilon_{3}^{2}e^{2\mu_{3}}-2(\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}e^{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}}+\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{3}e^{\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{3}e^{\mu_{1}+\mu_{3}})].\end{split}

(8.4)

Given initial data at
\tau_{0}
satisfying (
8.4
) with
\tau
replaced by
\tau_{0}
, the system (
8.3
) is
equivalent to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) in the case of Bianchi types VIII and IX. For the other Bianchi types, there is a
redundancy: if
\epsilon_{k}=0
, there is no need to keep track of
\nu_{k}
since it decouples from the rest of the equations. On the other hand, if
V
is
non-negative (the case we are most interested in), and the Bianchi type is different from IX, then the existence interval of solutions to
(
3.31
)–(
3.39
) is
\mathbb{R}
, see Lemma
3.12
. If
\epsilon_{k}=0
, we can therefore define
\nu_{k}
by
(
8.3a
) and the condition that
\nu_{k}(0)=0
. Here we are interested in solutions such
that the right hand sides in (
8.3
) all converge to zero exponentially. This means that we, with the initial data, can associate
asymptotic data, which are the limits of the
\nu_{i}
etc. In order to, later on, obtain conclusions concerning the map from isometry classes of
initial data to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations to isometry classes of initial data on the singularity, it is convenient to
specify initial data for
N_{i}
instead of for
\nu_{i}
. If the initial datum at
\tau=0
for
N_{i}
is
n_{i}
, then we define

\nu_{i}(0):=\ln|n_{i}|
in case
n_{i}\neq 0
and
\nu_{i}(0):=0
otherwise. In order to be more specific, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 8.3
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\mathsf{P}\subset\mathbb{R}^{9}
denotes the set of

(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{9}

such that
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3}=0
and

\begin{split}1=&\frac{3}{2}(\sigma_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{3}^{2})+\frac{1}{6}\bar{\psi}_{1}^{2}+3e^{-2\varkappa_{0}}V(\bar{\psi}_{0})+\frac{3}{4}[n_{1}^{2}+n_{2}^{2}+n_{3}^{2}-2(n_{1}n_{2}+n_{2}n_{3}+n_{3}n_{1})],\end{split}

and such that if
\sigma_{i}=n_{i}=0
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, then either
\bar{\psi}_{1}\neq 0
or
V^{\prime}(\bar{\psi}_{0})\neq 0
.
Next, let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that all the
n_{i}
vanish; let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{II}}
denote
the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that one of the
n_{i}
is non-zero and the other two vanish; let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that two
of the
n_{i}
are non-zero and have opposite signs, and the remaining
n_{i}
vanishes; let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{VII}_{0}}

denote the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that two
of the
n_{i}
are non-zero and have the same signs, and the remaining
n_{i}
vanishes; let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{VIII}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that all
the
n_{i}
are non-zero, but they do not all have the same sign; and let
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{P}
such that all the
n_{i}
are
non-zero, and all have the same sign.

Remark 8.4
.

Note that
\mathsf{P}
is a smooth manifold.

In analogy with (
2.1
), we introduce

\begin{split}&\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1})\\
:=&(\pm n_{\lambda(1)},\pm n_{\lambda(2)},\pm n_{\lambda(3)},\sigma_{\lambda(1)},\sigma_{\lambda(2)},\sigma_{\lambda(3)},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1})\end{split}

(8.5)

for
\lambda\in S_{3}
. Note that
\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
maps
\mathsf{P}
to
\mathsf{P}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
to
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
for a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
.
However, with this definition,
\xi_{\lambda}^{+}(p)=\xi_{\lambda}^{-}(p)
if
p\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I}}
. As in the case of
\psi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
, we therefore impose
the following restriction on the domains of definition:

\xi_{\lambda}^{+}:\mathsf{P}\rightarrow\mathsf{P},\ \ \ \xi_{\lambda}^{-}:\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I}}\rightarrow\mathsf{P}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I}}.

In order to relate
\mathsf{P}
with the set
\mathsf{B}
introduced in Definition
2.1
, it is convenient to define the map

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}:\mathsf{P}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
:

\begin{split}&\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1})\\
:=&(e^{\varkappa_{0}}n_{1},e^{\varkappa_{0}}n_{2},e^{\varkappa_{0}}n_{3},(\sigma_{1}+1/3)e^{\varkappa_{0}},(\sigma_{2}+1/3)e^{\varkappa_{0}},(\sigma_{3}+1/3)e^{\varkappa_{0}},\bar{\psi}_{0},e^{\varkappa_{0}}\bar{\psi}_{1}/3).\end{split}

(8.6)

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type. Then
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
defines a diffeomorphism from
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
to
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}\cap\{\mathrm{tr}K>0\}
.
Finally, if
\lambda\in S_{3}
, then

\psi_{\lambda}^{\pm}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}\circ\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}.

(8.7)

Proof.

We leave the proof to the reader.
∎

In analogy with Definition
2.6
, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\Lambda
denote the group consisting of the elements
\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
. Let
\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}
(
\Lambda^{\mathrm{odd}}
)
consist of the set of
\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
such that
\lambda
is even (odd). Let
\Lambda^{\pm}

be the subset of

\Lambda
consisting of the maps
\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
for
\lambda\in S_{3}
. Finally, let
\Lambda^{+,\mathrm{ev}}:=\Lambda^{+}\cap\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}

etc.

In analogy with Definition
2.12
, we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 8.7
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type. Define
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
to be the subset of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
consisting of points invariant
under
\Lambda^{+}
.
Define
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{per}}=\varnothing
and define, for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
to be the set of
p\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
such that there is a map

\xi_{\lambda}^{-}\in\Lambda^{-,\mathrm{odd}}
with
\xi_{\lambda}^{-}(p)=p
. Define

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
to be the set of
p\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
such that there is a map
\xi_{\lambda}^{+}\in\Lambda^{+,\mathrm{odd}}
with
\xi_{\lambda}^{+}(p)=p
.
Define

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}-\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}.

Define
\Lambda_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}:=\{\mathrm{Id}\}
and
\Lambda_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathrm{iso}}:=\{\mathrm{Id},\xi^{-}_{\mathrm{Id}}\}
. Define
\Lambda_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}:=\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}
,

\Lambda_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}:=\Lambda^{+,\mathrm{ev}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}:=\Lambda^{\mathrm{ev}}
for
\mathfrak{T}\notin\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{VI}_{0}\}
. Finally, define

\Lambda_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\Lambda^{+}
and
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=\Lambda
for
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
.

Remark 8.8
.

Note that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
;
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{per}}
is empty unless
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
;
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
is empty.

In analogy with Lemma
2.14
, the following statement holds.

Lemma 8.9
.

Fix
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
be such that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are defined in Definition
8.7
and non-empty. Then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly discontinuously on

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and the corresponding quotient is denoted
\mathsf{N}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Proof.

Since
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}\circ\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}[\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\cap\{\mathrm{tr}K>0\}]
, the statement is an
immediate consequence of Lemma
2.14
.
∎

In some situations it is of interest to fix
\varkappa
; note that under the diffeomorphism between
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, see
Lemma
8.5
, fixing
\varkappa

corresponds to fixing the initial mean curvature. We therefore, for a fixed
\varkappa_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, introduce the notation
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\varkappa_{0})

and

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})

for the subsets of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
with the initial datum for
\varkappa
equalling
\varkappa_{0}
. Since we have eliminated trivial
initial data, these sets are manifolds.

In the case of Bianchi type IX, we need to introduce terminology analogous to the one introduced in Definitions
1.53
and

1.55
.
We use the notation
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathrm{nd}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})

to denote the subsets of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}(\varkappa_{0})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
respectively, such that the
corresponding solutions to (
8.3
) have the following properties:
\varkappa^{\prime}(\tau)-3<0
for all
\tau\leq 0
; and there is a

\tau_{0}\leq 0
such that

\mathfrak{X}[e^{-3\tau_{0}+\varkappa(\tau_{0})},\psi_{0}(\tau_{0})+\phi_{1}(\tau_{0})\tau_{0},e^{-3\tau_{0}+\varkappa(\tau_{0})}\phi_{1}(\tau_{0})/3]>0.

Note here that initial data in
\mathsf{P}
correspond to data at
\tau=0
for (
8.3
). Next, we use the notation
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathrm{nd}}
,

\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})

to denote the subsets of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}
,

\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}(\varkappa_{0})
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
respectively, such that the corresponding solutions to
(
8.3
) have the following properties:
\varkappa^{\prime}(\tau)-3<0
for all
\tau\leq 0
;

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\big{(}e^{6\tau-2\varkappa(\tau)}\mathfrak{X}[e^{-3\tau+\varkappa(\tau)},\psi_{0}(\tau)+\phi_{1}(\tau)\tau,e^{-3\tau+\varkappa(\tau)}\phi_{1}(\tau)/3]\big{)}_{-}=0,

where, for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{-}=x
if
x\leq 0
and
x_{-}=0
if
x>0
; and

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\big{[}e^{6\tau-2\varkappa(\tau)}V(\psi_{0}(\tau)+\phi_{1}(\tau)\tau)\big{]}=0.

Finally,

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathrm{nd}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathrm{nd}}\cup\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathrm{nd}},\ \ \ \mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0}):=\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})\cup\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}\cup\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}},\ \ \ \mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0}):=\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})\cup\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0}).

Next, we introduce terminology for the asymptotic information.

Definition 8.10
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{s}
be a corresponding symmetry type. Let
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}

denote the subset of

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}
whose elements give rise to admissible convergent solutions in the sense of Definition
8.1
, and let

\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}:=\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}\cap\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Define
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}

to be the set of elements

(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{9}

with the following properties. First, all the
\bar{n}_{i}
vanish if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{I}
; exactly one
\bar{n}_{i}
is non-zero if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{II}
; two
\bar{n}_{i}
are
non-zero and have opposite signs and one
\bar{n}_{i}
vanishes if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VI}_{0}
; two
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero and have the same sign and one
\bar{n}_{i}

vanishes if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VII}_{0}
; all three
\bar{n}_{i}
are non-zero, but they do not all have the same sign if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VIII}
; and all three
\bar{n}_{i}

are non-zero and have the same sign if
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
. Second,
\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}+\sigma_{3}=0
. Third,

p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}+p_{3}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{9}\Phi_{1}^{2}=1,

(8.8)

where
p_{i}:=\sigma_{i}+1/3
. Fourth, if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
and
p_{i}=1
, then
\bar{n}_{j}=\bar{n}_{k}
. Fifth, if
p_{l}<1
for all
l
and
\bar{n}_{i}\neq 0

for some
i
, then
p_{i}>0
.

Clearly,
\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}
introduced in (
8.5
) leaves
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}
invariant so that we can define
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}

in analogy with Definition
8.7
. Moreover, the following analogy of Lemma
8.9
holds.

Lemma 8.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a Bianchi class A type and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
be such that
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
are
defined in analogy with or as in Definition
8.7
, respectively, and non-empty. Then
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
acts freely and properly
discontinuously on
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and the corresponding quotient is denoted
\mathsf{W}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Next, define

\begin{split}&\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\\
:=&(e^{2p_{1}\varkappa_{\infty}}\bar{n}_{1},e^{2p_{2}\varkappa_{\infty}}\bar{n}_{2},e^{2p_{3}\varkappa_{\infty}}\bar{n}_{3},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\varkappa_{\infty}/3,\Phi_{1}/3),\end{split}

(8.9)

where
p_{i}=\sigma_{i}+1/3
. Then
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}
maps
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}
to
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, see Definition
2.27
. Moreover,

\psi_{\lambda}^{\pm}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}\circ\xi_{\lambda}^{\pm}.

(8.10)

The reason for introducing
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}
is that we, in the end, define
\bar{n}_{k}
as the limit of
\epsilon_{k}e^{\nu_{k}}
, and then the
e^{2p_{k}\varkappa_{\infty}}\bar{n}_{k}

are the
o_{k}
appearing in Definition
2.27
.

In some situations it is of interest to fix
\varkappa
initially. This leads to the following terminology.

Definition 8.12
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\varkappa_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Define
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0}):=\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\cap\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
.
Similarly,
for
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
, define
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}:=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\cap\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}

and

\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0}):=\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\cap\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\varkappa_{0})
.

Next, we address the regularity of the map from regular initial data to asymptotic data.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
. Then

\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is an open subset of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Fix

\xi=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1})\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}.

Let
\epsilon_{i}=1
if
n_{i}>0
,
\epsilon_{i}=-1
if
n_{i}<0
and
\epsilon_{i}=0
if
n_{i}=0
. If
n_{i}\neq 0
, let
\nu_{i}(0)=\ln|n_{i}|
. If
n_{i}=0
, let
\nu_{i}(0)=0
.
Finally, let
\Sigma_{i}(0)=\sigma_{i}
;
\varkappa(0)=\varkappa_{0}
;
\psi_{0}(0)=\bar{\psi}_{0}
; and
\phi_{1}(0)=\bar{\psi}_{1}
. Let
x
be the corresponding solution
to (
8.3
) and

(\nu_{1,\infty},\nu_{1,\infty},\nu_{1,\infty},\sigma_{1,\infty},\sigma_{2,\infty},\sigma_{3,\infty},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})=\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}x(\tau).

(8.11)

Define

\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\xi):=(\epsilon_{1}e^{\nu_{1,\infty}},\epsilon_{2}e^{\nu_{2,\infty}},\epsilon_{3}e^{\nu_{3,\infty}},\sigma_{1,\infty},\sigma_{2,\infty},\sigma_{3,\infty},\varkappa_{\infty},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}).

(8.12)

Then
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is defined on
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and takes its values in
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover,
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is smooth and at each point

\xi\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T},*}^{\mathfrak{s}}|_{\xi}
is surjective. In particular,
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a local diffeomorphism.

Next, let
\varkappa_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
and assume that the
q
corresponding to
\xi_{0}
is
>-1
. Then there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
in

\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
, say
U
, so that
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, restricted to
U
and composed
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}
, see (
8.9
),
is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

Remark 8.14
.

If one is only interested in proving that
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is
C^{m}
, it is sufficient to demand that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{m+1}
.

Proof.

Fix an admissible convergent solution, say
S:=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3},\theta,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
. In the arguments to follow,
it is convenient to replace the
\Sigma_{i}
by
\Sigma_{\pm}
, see (
3.34
), and we do so from now on. Moreover, in what follows,
if condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, we assume that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
. Consider (
3.39
).
If condition (i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, then the assumptions imply that the polynomial in the
N_{i}
on the left hand side
of (
3.39
) can be estimated by
Ce^{2\alpha_{\infty}\tau}
for all
\tau\leq T
, where
C
only depends on
C_{\infty}
. This means that there is
a constant
C
such that
|\phi_{1}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}+Ce^{2\alpha_{\infty}\tau}
for all
\tau\leq T
, where
C
only depends on
C_{\infty}
. Combining this
observation with the fact that
\ln\theta(\tau)\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}
, it is clear that there are constants
C_{\Phi}
and
C
such that

|\phi_{0}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}|\tau|+C_{\Phi},\ \ \ \Omega(\tau)\leq Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(8.13)

for all
\tau\leq T
, where
C
only depends on
c_{0}
,
C_{\theta}
and
C_{\Phi}
, and
c_{0}
is the constant appearing in (
1.9
).
If condition (ii) of Definition
8.1

is satisfied, then
\Phi_{1}=0
. Thus (
8.13
) holds for all
\tau\leq T
. Next, note that if condition
(i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, then combining (
8.13
) with the fact that the polynomial in the
N_{i}
on the
left hand side of (
3.39
) decays exponentially yields the conclusion that (
8.8
) holds (or, equivalently,
that
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}+\Phi_{1}^{2}/6=1
, where
\sigma_{\pm}
are the limits of
\Sigma_{\pm}
). If condition (ii) of
Definition
8.1
is satisfied, then this equality holds automatically. Combining this observation with (
3.30c
) yields the
conclusion that
q\rightarrow 2
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, so that for any
\beta<6
, there is a
C_{\beta}
such that
|N_{1}(\tau)|\leq C_{\beta}e^{\beta\tau}

and
|(N_{1}N_{2})(\tau)|\leq C_{\beta}e^{\beta\tau}
for all
\tau\leq T
(assuming (ii) holds). To summarise, regardless of whether condition (i) or (ii) holds,

q-2
,
\Sigma_{+}-\sigma_{+}
and
\Sigma_{-}-\sigma_{-}
converge to zero exponentially. In particular, if condition (ii) holds, there is a constant

C_{\infty}
such that
|N_{1}(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{6\tau}
and
|(N_{1}N_{2})(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{6\tau}
for all
\tau\leq T
.

Fix an
\epsilon>0
and a
\tau_{0}\leq T
. Next, write (
8.3
) schematically as
x^{\prime}(\tau)=\mathcal{X}[x(\tau),\tau]
. Let
x
denote the solution to
(
8.3
) corresponding to the solution
S
and let
\bar{x}
denote another solution to (
8.3
) arising from initial
data in
\mathsf{P}
(so that the constraint (
3.39
) is satisfied due to (
3.41
)). Assume the
\epsilon_{i}
’s
which are implicit in the definition of the right hand side of (
8.3
) to be the same for
x
and
\bar{x}
. Assume, moreover,
\bar{x}
to
correspond to an LRS solution with
\bar{N}_{2}=\bar{N}_{3}
and
\bar{\Sigma}_{-}=0
in case
S
satisfies condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
. We also use the
notation

x=(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-},\varkappa,\psi_{0},\phi_{1}),\ \ \ \bar{x}=(\bar{\nu}_{1},\bar{\nu}_{2},\bar{\nu}_{3},\bar{\Sigma}_{+},\bar{\Sigma}_{-},\bar{\varkappa},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1}).

Assume that
|\bar{x}(\tau_{0})-x(\tau_{0})|<\epsilon
and let

\mathcal{A}:=\{\tau\leq\tau_{0}:|\bar{x}(s)-x(s)|<2\epsilon\ \forall\ s\in[\tau,\tau_{0}]\}.

Clearly,
\mathcal{A}
is an open, connected and non-empty subset of
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
. Thus
\mathcal{A}=(\tau_{-},\tau_{0}]
for some
\tau_{-}\in[-\infty,\tau_{0})
.
Assume
\tau_{-}>-\infty
and estimate, for
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
,

|\bar{N}_{i}|=|N_{i}|e^{\bar{\nu}_{i}-\nu_{i}}e^{f_{i}(0,\bar{\Sigma}_{+}-\Sigma_{+},\bar{\Sigma}_{-}-\Sigma_{-})\tau}\leq e^{-8\epsilon\tau+2\epsilon}|N_{i}|.

If condition (i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied and
N_{i}\neq 0
, it follows that
|\bar{N}_{i}(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{(\alpha_{\infty}-8\epsilon)\tau+2\epsilon}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, if condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, then

|\bar{N}_{1}(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{(6-8\epsilon)\tau+2\epsilon},\ \ \ |(\bar{N}_{1}\bar{N}_{2})(\tau)|\leq C_{\infty}e^{(6-16\epsilon)\tau+4\epsilon}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Assuming
\epsilon>0
to be small enough, we can thus assume that there is an
\eta>0
, which is numerical if condition (ii) holds
and which depends only on
\alpha_{\infty}
in case condition (i) holds, and a constant
C
, depending only on
C_{\infty}
such that

|\bar{S}_{\pm}(\tau)|+|2-\bar{q}(\tau)-\bar{\Omega}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\eta\tau}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
; see (
3.40
). Next, note that

\ln\bar{\theta}(\tau)\geq\ln\theta(\tau)-2\epsilon\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}-2\epsilon

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Thus

\tfrac{V\circ\bar{\phi}_{0}(t)}{\bar{\theta}^{2}(\tau)}+\tfrac{|V^{\prime}\circ\bar{\phi}_{0}(t)|}{\bar{\theta}^{2}(\tau)}\leq Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}e^{\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\bar{\phi}_{0}(\tau)-\phi_{0}(\tau)|}\leq Ce^{[6(1-\alpha_{V})-2\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}\epsilon]\tau}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
, where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
and
C_{\Phi}
. Assuming
\epsilon>0
to be small enough (depending only on
\alpha_{V}
and,
in case condition (i) holds,
\eta_{\infty}
), we conclude that there is an
\eta>0
with the same dependence such that

|\bar{S}_{\pm}(\tau)|+|2-\bar{q}(\tau)|+\bar{\Omega}(\tau)+\tfrac{|V^{\prime}\circ\bar{\phi}_{0}(t)|}{\bar{\theta}^{2}(\tau)}\leq Ce^{2\eta\tau}

(8.14)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
, where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
,
C_{\infty}
and
C_{\Phi}
. Next, note that due to these estimates,
(
3.39
) and (
3.40
), it follows that there is a constant
C
, depending only on
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
,
C_{\infty}
and
C_{\Phi}
,
such that

|\bar{\Sigma}_{+}(\tau)|+|\bar{\Sigma}_{-}(\tau)|+|\bar{\phi}_{1}(\tau)|\leq C

(8.15)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Combining (
8.3
), (
8.14
) and (
8.15
), it is clear that

|\bar{x}^{\prime}(\tau)|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle e^{2\eta\tau}
for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
, so that
|\bar{x}(\tau)-\bar{x}(\tau_{0})|\leq C\langle\tau_{0}\rangle e^{2\eta\tau_{0}}
for all

\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Note that this estimate holds for
x
as well. This means that

|\bar{x}(\tau)-x(\tau)|\leq\epsilon+C_{X}\langle\tau_{0}\rangle e^{2\eta\tau_{0}}

(8.16)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}
. Here
C_{X}
only depends on
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
,
C_{\infty}
,
C_{\Phi}
,
\alpha_{V}
and, in case condition (i) holds,
\eta_{\infty}
.
Moreover, this estimate is based on the assumption that
\epsilon
is small enough, the bound depending only on
\alpha_{V}
and, in case condition (i) holds,

\eta_{\infty}
. Assuming
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending only on
\epsilon
,
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
,
C_{\infty}
,
C_{\Phi}
,
\alpha_{V}
and, in
case condition (i) holds,
\eta_{\infty}
, it is clear that it can be ensured that the right hand side of (
8.16
) is

\leq 3\epsilon/2
. To summarise the above argument, we first fix a small enough
\epsilon>0
, depending only on
\alpha_{V}
and, in case condition (i) holds,

\eta_{\infty}
. Given this
\epsilon>0
, we fix a
\tau_{0}
close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending only on
\epsilon
,
c_{1}
,
C_{\theta}
,
C_{\infty}
,
C_{\Phi}
,

\alpha_{V}
and, in case condition (i) holds,
\eta_{\infty}
. Assume that
|\bar{x}(\tau_{0})-x(\tau_{0})|<\epsilon
and define
\mathcal{A}
as above. Then
\mathcal{A}
is of the form

\mathcal{A}=(\tau_{-},\tau_{0}]
for some
\tau_{-}<\tau_{0}
. Moreover, the above argument yields the conclusion that if
\tau_{-}>-\infty
, then

|\bar{x}(\tau)-x(\tau)|\leq 3\epsilon/2
on
\mathcal{A}
, so that there is a
\tau_{1}<\tau_{-}
such that
\tau_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
, in contradiction of the definition of
\tau_{-}
.
Thus
\tau_{-}=-\infty
.

By the above argument, we conclude that if condition (i) is satisfied, then there is an open neighbourhood of
x(\tau_{0})
(in the constraint
hypersurface represented by (
8.4
), with
\tau
replaced by
\tau_{0}
, and the same
\epsilon_{i}
’s as in the case of
x
)
such that the solutions to (
8.3
) arising from initial data in this neighbourhood give rise to admissible convergent solutions
