satisfying condition (i). Moreover, if condition (ii) is satisfied, then there is an open neighbourhood of
x(\tau_{0})
in the
set of LRS Bianchi type VIII or IX initial data such that the corresponding solutions satisfy either condition (i) or (ii). In order to translate
this observation to a statement concerning
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, note that, given
p\in\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, there is an open neighbourhood of
p
in

\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, say
U
, such that the
\epsilon_{i}
’s of the points in
U
coincide with the
\epsilon_{i}
’s of
p
. This means that the map from
U
to initial
data for (
8.3
) is a smooth map; cf. the statement of the lemma. This proves that
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is an open subset of
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Next, let us prove that the image of
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
under
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a subset of
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. That the Bianchi type is preserved is an
immediate consequence of the definition of
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. That the limit of the sum of the
\Sigma_{i}
is zero is an immediate consequence of the
fact that the sum of the
\Sigma_{i}
is zero. We have already verified that (
8.8
) holds. If

\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
and
p_{i}=1
, then we can assume that
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
, so that
\sigma_{1}=2/3
and
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{3}=-1/3
. This means that

\sigma_{+}=-1
and
\sigma_{-}=0
. Proposition
4.4
then implies that
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
. This means that
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
, as
required by Definition
8.10
. Next, assume, in order to obtain a contradiction, that all the
p_{l}<1
, that
\bar{n}_{1}\neq 0
and
that
p_{1}\leq 0
. This means that
\sigma_{+}\geq 1/2
so that
q-4\Sigma_{+}
converges to a limit which is
\leq 0
. However, this is not consistent with

N_{1}
converging to zero exponentially at a fixed rate.
Finally, that the image of
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is at least as symmetric as the initial data is
an immediate consequence of the definition. However, the image could potentially be more symmetric. That this is not possible follows from the
fact that symmetries of the asymptotic data imply the corresponding symmetries of the solution; see Proposition
7.3
.

Let
\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. By the above, there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
, say
U\subset\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, such that
U\subset\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Given
\xi\in U
, we denote by
x(\cdot;\xi)
the solution
x
to (
8.3
) corresponding to initial data
x(0)
obtained from

\xi
as described in the statement of the lemma. When we write
\Sigma_{k}
etc. in what follows, we view
\Sigma_{k}
etc. as being defined on

(-\infty,0]\times U
. In the arguments to follow, we also prefer to phrase the equations in terms of
\Sigma_{\pm}
as opposed to
\Sigma_{i}
;
cf. Remark
3.11
. If condition (i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, the assumptions and the above
arguments imply that, assuming
U
to be small enough and
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to
-\infty
, there are constants
\eta>0
,
C
and
C_{k}

such that if
N_{i}\neq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle e^{\mu_{i}}\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{\eta\tau},

(8.17a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\phi_{1}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C,

(8.17b)

\displaystyle e^{-2\varkappa+6\tau}|V^{(k)}\circ\phi_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{k}e^{\eta\tau}

(8.17c)

on
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\times U
. In case condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied,
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
and

\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
. This means that we can assume
\Sigma_{-}=0
and
N_{2}=N_{3}
on
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\times U
. Moreover, we can assume
(
8.17b
) and (
8.17c
) to still hold. However, (
8.17a
) is replaced by

|N_{1}|+|N_{1}N_{2}|\leq Ce^{\eta\tau}

(8.18)

on
(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\times U
. In order to analyse the regularity in the initial data, let
\{E_{i}\}
denote a frame of vector
fields on
U
and let
E_{\mathbf{I}}=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}
if
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
, where
i_{j}\in\{1,2,3\}
for
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Due to (
3.40
), (
8.17
) and (
8.18
), the right hand sides of (
8.3
)
have uniform exponential decay, irrespective of whether condition (i) or condition (ii) holds. More
specifically, the right hand sides are, in absolute value, bounded by
C\langle\tau\rangle e^{\eta\tau}
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
.
Applying
E_{i}
to (
8.3
) yields a linear system of equations for
E_{i}\nu_{j}
,
E_{i}\Sigma_{\pm}
etc. However, each
expression
E_{i}\nu_{j}
etc. on the right hand side is multiplied by a uniformly exponentially decaying factor. Defining an appropriate energy
(summing the squares of
E_{i}\nu_{j}
,
E_{i}\Sigma_{\pm}
etc.) then yields the conclusion that the energy is bounded to the past. This argument
can then be iterated to conclude that all the derivatives
E_{\mathbf{I}}\nu_{i}
etc. are uniformly bounded to the past. Combining these
observations with (
8.3
) then yields the conclusion that all the
E_{\mathbf{I}}\nu_{i}
etc. converge uniformly and
exponentially. This means that the limits of
\nu_{i}
etc. are all smooth functions on
U
. In particular, the map
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is smooth.

Next, we wish to prove that the differential of
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is always surjective. Recall, to this end, the constraint
(
8.4
). In what follows, we consider the evolution from initial data at
\tau_{0}
to asymptotic data. We therefore
focus on
V_{0}:=\{x(\tau_{0};\eta):\eta\in U\}
. Note that we can assume
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to
-\infty
that

\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}

(8.19)

in
V_{0}
. Fix a
\zeta\in V_{0}
and let
\partial_{\nu_{i}}
,
i=1,2,3
, be vector fields on
V_{0}
corresponding to differentiation in the
\nu_{i}
directions (so
that if we view
\Sigma_{+}
etc. as coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{8}
, then
\partial_{\nu_{i}}\Sigma_{+}=0
etc.). In general, the vector fields
\partial_{\nu_{i}}
are not tangential to
the hypersurface defined by (
8.4
). In order to obtain a vector field which is tangential, let

X_{r}:=\Sigma_{+}\partial_{\Sigma_{+}}+\Sigma_{-}\partial_{\Sigma_{-}}+\phi_{1}\partial_{\phi_{1}}

(8.20)

in case
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
. If
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per}\}
, we can assume
\Sigma_{-}=0
and then
X_{r}
should be replaced by
\Sigma_{+}\partial_{\Sigma_{+}}+\phi_{1}\partial_{\phi_{1}}
.
If
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
,
\Sigma_{+}=\Sigma_{-}=0
and
X_{r}
should be replaced by
\phi_{1}\partial_{\phi_{1}}
. In what follows, we assume
X_{r}
to take the form (
8.20
)
and leave it to the reader to carry out the corresponding modifications in case
\mathfrak{s}\neq\mathrm{gen}
. Note that

X_{r}\left(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}\right)=2\left(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}\right)\geq 1,

where we appealed to (
8.19
). For some function
a_{i}
, apply
Y_{i}=a_{i}X_{r}+\partial_{\nu_{i}}
to the right hand
side of (
8.4
) with
\tau
replaced by
\tau_{0}
, say
F
. This yields

Y_{i}(F)=2a_{i}\left(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}\right)+a_{i}R_{1}+R_{2},

where
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
and

\displaystyle R_{1}:=

\displaystyle 3\phi_{1}\tau_{0}e^{6\tau_{0}-2\varkappa}V^{\prime}(\psi_{0}+\phi_{1}\tau_{0})+9\Sigma_{1}\tau_{0}\epsilon_{1}^{2}e^{2\mu_{1}}+9\Sigma_{2}\tau_{0}\epsilon_{2}^{2}e^{2\mu_{2}}+9\Sigma_{3}\tau_{0}\epsilon_{3}^{2}e^{2\mu_{3}}

\displaystyle-9(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2})\tau_{0}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}e^{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}}-9(\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3})\tau_{0}\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{3}e^{\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}}-9(\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{3})\tau_{0}\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{3}e^{\mu_{1}+\mu_{3}},

\displaystyle R_{2}:=

\displaystyle\tfrac{3}{2}(\epsilon_{i}^{2}e^{2\mu_{i}}-\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j}e^{\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}}-\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{i}+\mu_{k}}).

The goal is to choose
a_{i}
so that
Y_{i}(F)=0
. However, assuming
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to
-\infty
, we can assume that

|R_{1}|\leq 1/2
. This means that we can define
a_{i}
by

a_{i}:=-\frac{R_{2}}{2\left(\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}\right)+R_{1}}.

Then
Y_{i}
is tangential to the constraint hypersurface at
\tau=\tau_{0}
, but typically not the constraint hypersurfaces at other times;
note that
Y_{i}
is independent of
\tau
. Moreover,
|a_{i}|\leq Ce^{\eta\tau_{0}}
, where we appealed to (
8.17a
) and
(
8.18
). From now on, we consider
Y_{i}
to be a vector field on
V_{0}
and we define

x:(-\infty,\tau_{0}]\times V_{0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{8}
by the condition that
x(\cdot;\zeta)
is the solution to
(
8.3
) with
x(\tau_{0};\zeta)=\zeta
. Next, we consider the evolution equations for
Y_{i}x
. Applying
Y_{i}
to
(
8.3
), it can be estimated that

|(Y_{i}x)^{\prime}|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau}|Y_{i}x|

(8.21)

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Since
|(Y_{i}x)(\tau_{0};\zeta)|
can be assumed to be arbitrarily close to
1
by assuming
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to

-\infty
, (
8.21
) implies that
|(Y_{i}x)(\tau;\zeta)|\leq 2
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, assuming
\tau_{0}
to be close enough to

-\infty
. Inserting this information into (
8.21
) implies that

\big{|}\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}(Y_{i}x)(\tau;\zeta)-(Y_{i}x)(\tau_{0};\zeta)\big{|}\leq C\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}}.

However, up to an error of the order of magnitude
O(e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
,
(Y_{i}x)(\tau_{0};\zeta)
is the unit vector in the direction of
\nu_{i}
. This
means that if
\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
, then
\partial_{\bar{n}_{i}}+O(\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
is in the image of
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
(where we use the standard
Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{8}
to measure the size of vector fields); here and below, we denote the coordinates on the target
\mathbb{R}^{8}
by
\bar{n}_{i}
,

\sigma_{\pm}
,
\varkappa_{\infty}
,
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
, where
\bar{n}_{i}=0
in case
\epsilon_{i}=0
. By a similar argument,

\partial_{\varkappa_{\infty}}+O(\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
and
\partial_{\Phi_{0}}+O(\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
are in the image of
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
.
Note that this proves surjectivity in the case that
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
.

Next, assume
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
and consider the vector fields

W:=\Sigma_{-}\partial_{\Sigma_{+}}-\Sigma_{+}\partial_{\Sigma_{-}},\ \ \ P_{\pm}:=\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}\partial_{\Sigma_{\pm}}-\Sigma_{\pm}\partial_{\phi_{1}}.

Unfortunately,
W(F)
and
P_{\pm}(F)
are in general non-zero. However, this can be corrected by adding a small multiple of
X_{r}
, as before.
Arguments similar to the above then yield the conclusion that
w+O(\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
and
p_{\pm}+O(\langle\tau_{0}\rangle^{2}e^{\eta\tau_{0}})
are in
the image of
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
, where

w:=\sigma_{-}\partial_{\sigma_{+}}-\sigma_{+}\partial_{\sigma_{-}},\ \ \ p_{\pm}:=\tfrac{1}{6}\Phi_{1}\partial_{\sigma_{\pm}}-\sigma_{\pm}\partial_{\Phi_{1}}.

Note also that if
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})=0
, then
|\Phi_{1}|=\sqrt{6}
and
p_{\pm}
span a two dimensional subset of the corresponding tangent space
of
\mathsf{Q}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
(the same is true if
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})
is close to zero). If
(\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})\neq 0
, then
w
and

\sigma_{+}p_{+}+\sigma_{-}p_{-}=\tfrac{1}{6}\Phi_{1}(\sigma_{+}\partial_{\sigma_{+}}+\sigma_{-}\partial_{\sigma_{-}})-(\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2})\partial_{\Phi_{1}}

are orthogonal and both non-zero. For the above reasons, the map
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
is surjective at each point in
U
of
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
. In case

\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
, we already know
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
to be surjective. In case
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{per},\mathrm{LRS}\}
, a similar argument yields the same conclusion;
it is sufficient to focus
P_{+}
. In particular,
\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a local diffeomorphism.

Next, let
\varkappa_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
and assume that
q(0;\xi_{0})>-1
. Due to the above arguments, there is an open neighbourhood,
say
U
, of
\xi_{0}

in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
such that
q(0;\xi)>-1
for
\xi\in U
and
q
converges uniformly to
2
in
U
. This means that there is a
T_{0}\leq 0
such that

q(\tau;\xi)\geq 1
for all
\tau\leq T_{0}
and
\xi\in U
. In particular,
\theta^{\prime}(\tau;\xi)\leq-2\theta(\tau;\xi)<0
for all
\tau\leq T_{0}

and
\xi\in U
. Assume that
\tau_{0}\leq T_{0}
and that
\theta(\tau_{0};\xi_{0})=\theta_{0}
. Then, due to the implicit function theorem, there is a
neighbourhood
W
in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
of
\xi_{0}
and a smooth map
T:W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\theta(T(\xi);\xi)=\theta_{0}
. Next,
note that we can think of
\xi
as initial data for (
3.31
)–(
3.39
);
N_{i}(0):=n_{i}
,

\Sigma_{i}(0):=\sigma_{i}
,
\phi_{0}(0):=\bar{\psi}_{0}
,
\phi_{1}(0):=\bar{\psi}_{1}
,
\theta(0):=e^{\varkappa_{0}}
. We denote the corresponding solution by

X(\tau;\xi)
. Since the flow associated with (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) is smooth, we obtain a smooth map
\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}
defined by

\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}(\xi):=X[T(\xi);\xi]
for
\xi\in W
. Taking the logaritm of the mean curvature, we can interpret
\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}(\xi)
as an element of

\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\theta_{0})
. By reversing the flow and restricting the domain, if necessary, it is clear that
\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}
has a smooth inverse (recall
that
\theta^{\prime}(0;\xi_{0})<0
), so that it is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Next, note that we can assume estimates such as
(
8.17
) and (
8.18
) to hold uniformly on
W
to the past of
T_{0}
. Assume, from now on, that

\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}
is a diffeomorphism from
W\subset\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
to
W_{2}\subset\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\theta_{0})
. Let
\zeta\in W_{2}
and

\xi:=\Xi_{\mathrm{loc}}^{-1}(\zeta)
. We would like to start from
\zeta
and evolve backwards in time. However, this leads to a problem: If we wish to
focus on solutions to (
8.3
) corresponding to the initial data given in the statement of the lemma and then to calculate the
limit (
8.11
), we should strictly speaking use
T(\xi)
as
the starting time. This causes complications when carrying out arguments as the one used above to prove that
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
is surjective.
On the other hand, the map we are interested in is such that a change in initial time is immaterial. In order to justify this statement,
consider two solutions to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) denoted by
X
and
\bar{X}
and with the property that

\bar{X}(\tau)=X(\tau+\tau_{1})
; i.e.,
\bar{N}_{i}(\tau)=N_{i}(\tau+\tau_{1})
etc. For
i
such that
\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
, it can then be verified that

\bar{\nu}_{i}(\tau)=\nu_{i}(\tau+\tau_{1})+6[\Sigma_{i}(\tau+\tau_{1})+1/3]\tau_{1}.

Similarly,

\bar{\varkappa}(\tau)=\varkappa(\tau+\tau_{1})-3\tau_{1},\ \ \ \bar{\psi}_{0}(\tau)=\psi_{0}(\tau+\tau_{1})+\phi_{1}(\tau+\tau_{1})\tau_{1}.

Finally,
\bar{\Sigma}_{i}(\tau)=\Sigma_{i}(\tau+\tau_{1})
and
\bar{\phi}_{1}(\tau)=\phi_{1}(\tau+\tau_{1})
. Using notation analogous to
(
8.11
), it is then clear that
\bar{\sigma}_{i,\infty}=\sigma_{i,\infty}
, that
\bar{\Phi}_{1}=\Phi_{1}
and that if
k
is such
that
N_{k}\neq 0
, then

\bar{\nu}_{k,\infty}=\nu_{k,\infty}+6(\sigma_{k,\infty}+1/3)\tau_{1},\ \ \ \bar{\varkappa}_{\infty}=\varkappa_{\infty}-3\tau_{1},\ \ \ \bar{\Phi}_{0}=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\tau_{1}.

Given these observations, it can be verified that
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is independent of initial translations in
\tau
. We can thus
assume the initial data
\zeta\in W_{2}
to be specified at
\tau=\tau_{0}
instead of at
\tau=T(\xi)
. In what follows, we ignore
\nu_{i}
in case

\epsilon_{i}=0
. Assume that

\zeta=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3},\varkappa_{0},\bar{\psi}_{0},\bar{\psi}_{1}).

If
n_{i}\neq 0
, define
\epsilon_{i}
and
\mu_{i}
by
n_{i}=\epsilon_{i}e^{\hat{\mu}_{i}}
and define

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(\tau_{0}):=

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{i}-6(\sigma_{i}+1/3)\tau_{0},\ \ \Sigma_{i}(\tau_{0}):=\sigma_{i},\ \ \varkappa(\tau_{0}):=\varkappa_{0}+3\tau_{0},

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(\tau_{0}):=

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}_{0}-\bar{\psi}_{1}\tau_{0},\ \ \phi_{1}(\tau_{0}):=\bar{\psi}_{1}.

Let
x
denote the solution to (
8.3
) corresponding to these initial data at
\tau=\tau_{0}
. Since the distance between

T(\xi)
and
\tau_{0}
can be assumed to be less than
1
, we can assume estimates such as (
8.17
) and
(
8.18
) to hold uniformly on
W_{2}
to the past of
\tau_{0}
. This means that we can carry out arguments similar to
those used to prove that
(\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}})_{*}
is surjective in order to prove that the pushforward of
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, when
restricted to
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
, is surjective. This map thus defines a local diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood
W_{1}
of

\xi\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
to
\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
∎

One consequence of the arguments given in the proof is that admissible convergent solutions to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
)
correspond to developments that induce data on the singularity.

Lemma 8.15
.

Assume that
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Consider a Bianchi class A non-linear scalar field development
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
as
obtained in Proposition
1.31
. Assume that there is a
t_{0}
in the associated existence interval such that
\theta(t)>0
for

t\leq t_{0}
and such that
\theta
is not integrable to the past. Assume, moreover, that introducing variables as in Section
3.3
,
the corresponding solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) is an admissible convergent solution. Then

\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces data on the singularity.

Proof.

Due to the arguments given at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
8.13
,
q-2
,
\Sigma_{+}-\sigma_{+}
and
\Sigma_{-}-\sigma_{-}
decay exponentially.
Moreover,
\Omega

decays exponentially, and by an argument similar to the proof of this fact,
V^{\prime}\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
decays exponentially. This means that

\phi_{1}^{\prime}
and
(\ln\theta+3\tau)^{\prime}
decay exponentially. In particular, in case condition (i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied,
the estimates (
4.23
) hold. In case condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied, it can be verified
that the conclusions of either Corollary
5.3
or Corollary
5.4
are satisfied. For these reasons,
the desired conclusion follows by arguments similar to the proofs of the results of Chapter
6
.
∎

Next, it is of interest to make the following observation concerning the regularity of the map from regular data to data on the singularity.

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{\infty}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}

satisfying one of the following conditions:
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
;
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{iso}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{IX}\}
;
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{LRS}
and

\mathfrak{T}\in\{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{II},\mathrm{VIII},\mathrm{IX}\}
; and
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{VI}_{0},\mathrm{per})
.
Let
\varkappa_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and assume that
e^{\varkappa_{0}}>[3\inf_{s}V(s)]^{1/2}
if
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
.
Let, moreover,

\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
and assume that the
q
corresponding to
\xi_{0}
is
>-1
. Then there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
in

\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
, say
U
, so that, considering
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)
as an open subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(e^{\varkappa_{0}})
, the map

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)\ni\eta\mapsto\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta)

(8.22)

is a diffeomorphism onto its image that decends to a map from
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)/\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
which is a local
representation of the restriction of the map from Lemma
1.48
to the corresponding open subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](e^{\varkappa_{0}})
.

Remark 8.17
.

When we speak of representations of the map from isometry classes of regular data to isometry classes of data on the singularity, we always have
in mind that there is a natural parametrisation of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
by
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
, see
Remark
2.20
; and that there is a natural parametrisation of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
by

\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, see Lemma
2.38
.

Remark 8.18
.

The maps
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}
are defined in (
8.6
) and (
8.9
) respectively.

Remark 8.19
.

In the case of isotropic Bianchi type I, the condition that
q>-1
corresponds to the condition that the initial datum for the normal
derivative of the scalar field is non-vanishing.

Proof.

Let
U
be the open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\varkappa_{0})
whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
8.13
. That the
map (
8.22
) is a diffeomorphism onto its image is an immediate consequence of Lemma
8.13
. That it descends
to a map from
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)/\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to
\mathsf{U}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is an immediate consequence of (
8.7
), (
8.10
) and
(
8.12
). What remains to be verified is that it is a local representation of the restriction of the map from
Lemma
1.48
to the corresponding open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](e^{\varkappa_{0}})
.
Let
\eta\in\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)\subset\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(e^{\varkappa_{0}})
. Then there are
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](e^{\varkappa_{0}})
such that
\eta

arises from
\mathfrak{I}
; see Remarks
2.17
and
2.5
. Let
\{e_{i}\}
be the corresponding basis of
\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\xi:=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta)
and let
x
be the solution to (
8.3
) corresponding to the initial data defined by
\xi
at
\tau=0
as
in the statement of Lemma
8.13
. By assumption, the limit on the right hand side of (
8.11
) exists, and we
use the notation introduced on the left hand side of (
8.11
). Note also that the development associated with
\mathfrak{I}
is such that
if we associate Wainwright-Hsu variables with this development as described in the proof of Proposition
1.31
and in
Section
3.3
(using the frame
\{e_{i}\}
) and if we associate variables
\nu_{k}
,
\Sigma_{k}
,
\varkappa
,
\psi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
with
the Wainwright-Hsu variables as described in connection with (
8.3
), then this also produces the solution to (
8.3
)
corresponding to the initial data
\xi
(at
\tau=0
). One particular consequence of the above observations is that the limit of
\mathcal{K}
, say
\mathscr{K}
,
exists, and that
\mathscr{K}e_{i}=p_{i}e_{i}
(no summation), where
p_{i}=\sigma_{i,\infty}+1/3
. Next, the limit of
\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}
is the same as the limit of

\phi_{1}/3
. If
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
denotes the limit of
\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}
, we conclude that
\bar{\Phi}_{1}=\Phi_{1}/3
. Next, we turn to the limit of

\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta
, say
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
. Since

\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta=\psi_{0}+\phi_{1}\varkappa/3\rightarrow\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\varkappa_{\infty}/3,

it follows that
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\varkappa_{\infty}/3
. Finally, we need to calculate the limit of the expansion normalised metric. Note, to
this end, that the coefficients
a_{i}
satisfy (
6.1
), where
\ell_{i}:=\Sigma_{i}+1/3
, and
a_{i}(0)=1
. This means that

\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}e_{i},\theta^{\mathcal{K}}e_{j})=a_{i}^{2}\exp\big{(}2(\varkappa-3\tau\big{)}\cdot(\Sigma_{i}+1/3))\delta_{ij}

(no summation). This means we have to prove that the following expressions have a limit:

\begin{split}&6\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{\tau}(\Sigma_{i}+1/3)ds+2\varkappa(\Sigma_{i}+1/3)-6\tau\Sigma_{i}-2\tau\\
=&6\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{\tau}(\Sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i,\infty})ds+2\varkappa(\Sigma_{i}+1/3)-6\tau(\Sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i,\infty}).\end{split}

However, since
\Sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i,\infty}
and
\varkappa-\varkappa_{\infty}
decay exponentially, it is clear that they do. In order to calculate
the
o
appearing in Definition
2.27
, let

e_{i}^{\prime}:=\theta^{-\ell_{i}}a_{i}^{-1}e_{i}

(no summation). If the components of the commutator matrix associated with
a_{i}^{-1}e_{i}
are
n_{i}(\tau)
, then the
components of the commutator matrix associated with
e_{i}^{\prime}
are

\hat{n}_{k}=\theta^{\ell_{k}-\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}n_{k}=\theta^{1+\ell_{k}-\ell_{i}-\ell_{j}}(n_{k}/\theta)=\theta^{2\ell_{k}}\epsilon_{k}e^{\mu_{k}},

assuming
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
. Note that
o_{k}
is the limit of
\hat{n}_{k}
. Compute

\hat{n}_{k}=\epsilon_{k}\exp\big{(}2\ell_{k}\cdot(\varkappa-3\tau)+\nu_{k}+6\ell_{k}\tau\big{)}\rightarrow\epsilon_{k}e^{\nu_{k,\infty}+2p_{k}\varkappa_{\infty}}.

This means that
o_{k}=\epsilon_{k}e^{\nu_{k,\infty}+2p_{k}\varkappa_{\infty}}
. This means that the data at the singularity are represented by

\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{Q}}\circ\Psi_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}\circ\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta),

as desired.
∎

8.2
Initial data with fixed mean curvature

Let
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\vartheta_{0}>\inf_{s\in\mathbb{R}}[3V(s)]^{1/2}
and
\vartheta_{1}>\vartheta_{0}
. Due to Lemma
3.4
, we
know that if
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
,
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and
\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
, then the development
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
, the mean
curvature of which is initially (say at
t=t_{0}
) equal to
\vartheta_{0}
, is such that there is a unique
t_{1}<t_{0}
in the existence interval with the
property that the mean curvature of the
t=t_{1}
hypersurface equals
\vartheta_{1}
. This defines a map

P_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{1}).

(8.23)

Next, let
\mathfrak{t}\in\{+,\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
. Due to the requirements of Definition
1.55
, it is possible to formulate a definition similar
to that of
P_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}
in order to obtain

P_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{1}).

(8.24)

Next, we analyse the regularity of the corresponding maps between isometry classes of initial data.

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-negative. First, fix
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\neq(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
such that
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and assume that

\vartheta_{0}>\inf_{s\in\mathbb{R}}[3V(s)]^{1/2}
and that
\vartheta_{1}>\vartheta_{0}
. Then
P_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}

induces a smooth map

\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{1})

(8.25)

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

Second, fix
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{gen}\}
and assume that
\vartheta_{0}>0
and that
\vartheta_{1}>\vartheta_{0}
. Then

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
is an
open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
and
P_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},+}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}
induces a smooth map

\Pi_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},+}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{1})

(8.26)

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Finally, if, in addition to the above assumptions in the case that
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
,
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
, where

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
, and

\mathfrak{t}\in\{\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
, then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{0})

and
P_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}
induces a smooth map

\Pi_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{1})

(8.27)

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

Proof.

Consider (
3.1
)–(
3.4
). Note that we can consider
\mathsf{B}
introduced in Definition
2.1

to be a set of initial data for these equations. Note also that, given initial data in
\mathsf{B}
, the corresponding solutions to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) have the property that
n_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
and
\sigma_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
; see the
beginning of the proof of Proposition
1.31
. Recall the notation
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
introduced in
Definition
2.18
.

Let
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
denote the set of
\xi\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
such that the solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data
\xi
at
t=t_{0}
has the following properties:
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all

t\leq t_{0}
; and there is a
t_{1}\leq t_{0}
such that
X(t_{1})>0
; see (
3.16
). If
\xi\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
, the
initial data are specified at
t_{0}
,
t_{1}\leq t_{0}
and
X(t_{1})>0
, then, due
to the fact that solutions depend continuously on initial data, it is clear that there is a neighbourhood of
\xi
in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})

such that the corresponding solutions have the property that
\theta_{t}<0
on
[t_{1},t_{0}]
and
X(t_{1})>0
. Due to the proof of
Lemma
3.4
), it then follows that
X(t)>0
and
\theta(t)>0
for all
t\leq t_{1}
. Combining this observation with
(
3.20
), this means that
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. To summarise:
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},+}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
is an open
subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
.

Next, assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
, where
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Then

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})={}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{pp}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta_{0})
; see Corollary
4.12
. For this
reason, it is sufficient to assume that
\mathfrak{t}=\mathrm{ap}
. Consider the set of
\xi\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
such that the solution
to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data
\xi
at
t=t_{0}
has the following properties:
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all

t\leq t_{0}
;
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
; and the negative part of
X(t)/\theta^{2}(t)
converges to zero as

t\downarrow t_{-}
. Denote this set by
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
. Note that there are two possibilities, given

\xi\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
: Either the solution
corresponding to
\xi
is such that there is a
t_{1}\leq t_{0}
with the property that
X(t_{1})>0
or there is no such
t_{1}
. If there is such a
t_{1}
,
then there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi
in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
contained in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathrm{ap}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta_{0})
; see the argument
in the case that
\mathfrak{t}=+
as well as Corollary
4.12
and its proof. We can therefore assume that
\xi
is such that the corresponding
solution has the property that

X(t)\leq 0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. This means that the conditions of Lemma
3.4
are not satisfied, but that the conditions
of Lemma
3.6
are. Thus the last statement of Theorem
4.13
applies. As noted in
Remark
8.2
, this means that the corresponding solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) is an admissible
convergent solution. Since the set of admissible solutions is open, see Lemma
8.13
(note that the proof of openness only relies on

V
belonging to
\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
), there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi
in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
such that the corresponding solutions
are admissible convergent solutions. Next, due to Lemma
8.13
and Remark
8.14
, the map from initial data to
asymptotic data is continuous. There is thus a neighbourhood of
\xi
such that the corresponding solutions have
\Phi_{1}\neq 0
. Combining this
observation with the definition of admissible convergent solutions, it follows that there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi
such that the
corresponding solutions have the property
that
V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
and the negative part of
X/\theta^{2}
converge to zero. What remains is to prove that the corresponding solutions also have
the property that
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. However, due to the proof of Lemma
8.13
, it is clear that
q
converges uniformly
to
2
(for initial data in a suitably small neighbourhood of
\xi
). From this observation and the continuity of the flow, it can be deduced that

\theta_{t}<0
, as desired. This finishes the proof of openness in the case that
\mathfrak{t}\in\{\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
.

Due to the observations made below (
2.4
), we know that
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
is a smooth submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{8}
.
Note also that an element
\xi\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
can be considered to be initial data for (
3.1
)–(
3.4
),
with the following caveat: the Weingarten matrix
K
appearing in
\xi
is divided into its trace
\theta
and trace free part
\sigma
in order
to obtain initial data for (
3.1
)–(
3.4
). Since this distinction corresponds to a trivial algebraic decomposition,
we, in what follows, abuse notation and say that
\xi
are initial data for (
3.1
)–(
3.4
). Next, assume that

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})\notin\{(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}),(\mathrm{VII}_{0},\mathrm{iso}),(\mathrm{VII}_{0},\mathrm{LRS})\}
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
. Then, due to Lemma
3.4
,
\theta_{t}<0

for
t\leq t_{0}
(assuming the initial data have been specified at
t_{0}
) and
\theta
diverges to
\infty
to the past. There is thus a map from

\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
to
\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{1})
, defined as follows: given
\xi\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
, let
x
be the
solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) with
x(t_{0})=\xi
. Then there is a unique
t_{1}<t_{0}
such that the mean curvature of the solution
equals
\vartheta_{1}
at
t=t_{1}
, so that
x(t_{1})\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{1})
(note that the flow associated with (
3.1
)–(
3.4
)
respects the symmetry type; see the proof of Proposition
1.31
). This yields a map

\Xi:\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{1})
(which is a representation of the map introduced in (
8.23
),
keeping Remark
2.17
in mind). Note that this map is injective due to the uniqueness of solutions to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) and the fact that
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for all

t\leq t_{0}
. Next, we prove that this map is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Let, to this end,

\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
and let
t_{a}<t_{0}
be such that the solution
x
to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) with
x(t_{0})=\xi_{0}

has the property that the mean curvature at
t_{a}
is
\vartheta_{1}
. There is an open neighbourhood
U\subset\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
of
\xi_{0}
and
an open interval
I
containing
t_{0}
and
t_{a}
such that the flow associated with the equations (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) is well defined
and smooth on
I\times U
. Let
\theta(t;\xi)
denote the mean curvature of the solution with initial data
\xi\in\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
at
t=t_{0}
,
evaluated at
t
. Then
\theta(t_{0};\xi)=\vartheta_{0}
and
\theta(t_{a};\xi_{0})=\vartheta_{1}
. We wish to find the time
t
such that
\theta(t;\xi)=\vartheta_{1}
.
By the implicit function theorem, it is, locally, sufficient that the derivative of
\theta
with respect to
t
is non-zero. However, we
know that
\theta_{t}(t;\xi)<0
. This means that there is an open neighbourhood
V
of
\xi_{0}
in

\mathsf{B}^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\vartheta_{0})
and a smooth function
T:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
T(\xi_{0})=t_{a}
such that
\theta(T(\xi);\xi)=\vartheta_{1}
. This means
that, locally,
\Xi
is represented by
\Xi(\xi)=x(T(\xi);\xi)
, so that
\Xi
is smooth. The proof that the local inverse is smooth is
similar. To conclude,
\Xi
is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Finally, due to the symmetries of the
equations, it is clear that
\Xi
descends to a map from
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{0})
to
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta_{1})
, which is also a diffeomorphism
onto its image; cf. Lemma
2.14
, Definition
2.18
and Lemma
2.19
. In order to see
that this map is a representation of (
8.25
), it is sufficient to appeal to Lemma
2.15
and its proof,
Remark
2.17
and Lemma
A.8
. The arguments in the remaining two cases are essentially
identical.
∎

In the case of isotropic Bianchi type I, we need to impose additional conditions on the potential. Assume that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\mathrm{par}}
and that

\vartheta_{-}:=[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Fix
\vartheta_{-}<\vartheta_{0}<\vartheta_{1}
. Let
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
with initial mean curvature

\vartheta_{0}
. Due to Lemma
3.8
, the range of the mean curvature in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
includes
(\vartheta_{-},\infty)
.
Moreover, due to the same lemma, if the initial data induced by the development at
t_{0}
is
\mathfrak{I}
, there is a unique
t_{1}<t_{0}
such that
the initial data induced at the
t=t_{1}
hypersurface has mean curvature
\vartheta_{1}
. This naturally leads to a map as in (
8.23
).
Moreover, this map induces a map

{}^{\mathrm{pre}}\Pi_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0})\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{1})

which is a bijection. Let, for
\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
be the subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
with
\bar{\phi}_{1}\neq 0
. The main advantage of this set is that if the isometry class of
\mathfrak{I}
belongs to
it, then the initial value of
\theta_{t}
is strictly negative; see (
3.1c
). Let

U_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:=({}^{\mathrm{pre}}\Pi_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}})^{-1}\big{(}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{1})\big{)}\cap{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{0}).

Then, by arguments similar to the proof of Lemma
8.20
,
U_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}
is an open set and

\Pi_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:={}^{\mathrm{pre}}\Pi_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}|_{U_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}}

defines a smooth map

\Pi_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}:U_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{1})

(8.28)

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Next, we prove Lemma
1.59
.

Proof of Lemma
1.59
.

That the map
\iota_{\vartheta}
is well defined is an immediate consequence of Proposition
1.35
. In order to prove that it is
injective, assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, and that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
are isometric.
Due to Lemma
3.4
, we know that the existence interval of both developments is
(0,\infty)
. Moreover, the time coordinate

t
of a point in one of the developments can invariantly be characterised as the maximal length of a past directed timelike curve. Since
this characterisation is preserved by isometries, it is clear that the constant-
t
hypersurfaces are preserved by the isometry. This means
that the initial data induced on the constant-
t
hypersurfaces of
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
are isometric. In particular, the
mean curvature of
G_{0}\times\{t\}
in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
equals the mean curvature of
G_{1}\times\{t\}
in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
. It is therefore
meaningful to speak of
\theta(t)
, the mean curvature at the constant-
t
hypersurface, irrespective of the development. Since

\theta_{t}<0
, there is a unique
t_{0}
such that
\theta(t_{0})=\vartheta
(the existence is guaranteed by construction). Since the isometry between
the developments preserves the hypersurfaces with
t=t_{0}
, it induces an isometry from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
. This proves injectivity of

\iota_{\vartheta}
. By the definition of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, it is also clear that it is the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
.

Next, we need to define a smooth structure on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Let
\vartheta_{-}:=\inf_{s\in\mathbb{R}}[3V(s)]^{1/2}
and define

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]:=\bigsqcup_{\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)/\sim,

(8.29)

where
x_{1}\sim x_{2}
for
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{i})
if
x_{2}=\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{1},\vartheta_{2}}(x_{1})
, in case
\vartheta_{2}>\vartheta_{1}
;

x_{1}=\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{2},\vartheta_{1}}(x_{2})
, in case
\vartheta_{1}>\vartheta_{2}
; and
x_{1}=x_{2}
, in case
\vartheta_{1}=\vartheta_{2}
. In what follows,
we denote the map from the disjoint union
to the quotient by
\pi
. In order to prove that there is a bijection from
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
,
note that by the above observations concerning
\iota_{\vartheta}
, there is a surjective map

\iota:\bigsqcup_{\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

defined by taking
x\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
to
\iota_{\vartheta}(x)
. Assume now that
\iota(x_{0})=\iota(x_{1})
for

x_{\alpha}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{\alpha})
, where
\vartheta_{-}<\vartheta_{0}\leq\vartheta_{1}
. If
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{1}
, we know
that
x_{0}=x_{1}
, since
\iota_{\vartheta}
is injective. We can therefore assume that
\vartheta_{0}<\vartheta_{1}
. Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
,
be such that
\iota(x_{\alpha})
is the isometry class of
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})
. Then, by assumption,
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
are
isometric. By an argument similar to the proof of the injectivity of
\iota_{\vartheta}
, it is clear that there is a
t_{1}>0
such that
the initial data induced on
G_{\alpha}\times\{t_{1}\}
by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})
are isometric; the mean curvature of the
t=t_{1}
hypersurface is

\vartheta_{1}
; and the isometry class of the initial data induced on
G_{1}\times\{t_{1}\}
by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})
is
x_{1}
. Moreover, there is
a
t_{0}>t_{1}
such that the mean curvature of the
t=t_{0}
hypersurface in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
is
\vartheta_{0}
and the isometry class of the
initial data induced on the
t=t_{0}
hypersurface in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
is
x_{0}
. This means that
x_{1}=\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}(x_{0})
.
This proves that
\iota
induces a bijection, say
\bar{\iota}:{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

To define a topology on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, we use the fact that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
are smooth manifolds;
see Lemma
2.19
and Remark
2.20
. This defines a topology on the disjoint union
of the
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
for
\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}
. We endow
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with the quotient
space topology. Assume now that
x_{\alpha}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, are such that
x_{0}\neq x_{1}
. We can, without
loss of generality, assume that
x_{\alpha}
is the projection of
y_{\alpha}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{\alpha})
, where

\vartheta_{-}<\vartheta_{0}\leq\vartheta_{1}
.
If
\vartheta_{0}=\vartheta_{1}
, then we can choose open neighbourhoods
U_{\alpha}
of
y_{\alpha}
in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{1})
which are
disjoint. If
\vartheta_{0}<\vartheta_{1}
, we can choose open neighbourhoods
U_{\alpha}
of
\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}(y_{0})
and

y_{1}
in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{1})
which are disjoint. Let
V_{\alpha}:=\pi(U_{\alpha})
. We need to prove that the
V_{\alpha}
are open and disjoint.
Note, to this end, that

\pi^{-1}(V_{\alpha})=\bigsqcup_{\vartheta_{-}<\vartheta<\vartheta_{1}}(\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta,\vartheta_{1}})^{-1}(U_{\alpha})\bigsqcup U_{\alpha}\bigsqcup_{\vartheta_{1}<\vartheta}\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{1},\vartheta}(U_{\alpha}),

(8.30)

which is clearly an open set. This means that
V_{\alpha}
is open. From this formula, it is also clear that since the
U_{\alpha}
are disjoint, the
V_{\alpha}

are disjoint. This proves that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is Hausdorff. Next, let
Q
be the set of rational
\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}
and
for for each
\vartheta\in Q
, let
B_{\vartheta}
be a countable basis for the topology of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
. Let
B
be the set
consisting of the projections of the sets in
B_{\vartheta}
for
\vartheta\in Q
to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. We leave it as an exercise
to the reader to verify that
B
is a basis for the topology.

Next, let
\pi_{\vartheta}
denote the restriction of
\pi
to
{{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)}
. In order to prove that this map is a homeomorphism
onto its image, note that it can be written as a composition of the inverse of
\bar{\iota}
with
\iota_{\vartheta}
. Since
\bar{\iota}
is a bijection
and
\iota_{\vartheta}
is injective, it is clear that
\pi_{\vartheta}
is a bijection onto its image. Due to (
8.30
), it is clear that

\pi_{\vartheta}
maps open sets to open sets. That the inverse image of an open set under
\pi_{\vartheta}
is an open set is an immediate consequence of
the definition of the quotient space topology. Thus
\pi_{\vartheta}
is a homeomophism onto its image, and the image is an open set.

Next, we need to define a smooth structure. Fix
\vartheta<\vartheta_{0}\leq\vartheta_{1}
and local charts
(U_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})
in

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta_{\alpha})
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
; here
\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
V_{\alpha}=\pi_{\vartheta_{\alpha}}(U_{\alpha})
and define

\phi_{\alpha}:=\varphi_{\alpha}\circ(\pi_{\vartheta_{\alpha}})^{-1}|_{V_{\alpha}}.

(8.31)

Note that
\phi_{\alpha}
is a homeomorphism from
V_{\alpha}
to
\varphi_{\alpha}(U_{\alpha})
. Note also that the domains of maps of this form cover

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Next, note that

\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{0}^{-1}=\varphi_{1}\circ\Pi_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{0},\vartheta_{1}}\circ\varphi_{0}^{-1}.

This means that
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{0}^{-1}
and
\phi_{0}\circ\phi_{1}^{-1}
are smooth. Thus coordinate charts of the form
(V_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})
define a
smooth atlas. This atlas can be extended to a maximal atlas, endowing
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with a smooth structure. Combining
this smooth structure with the bijection
\bar{\iota}
endows
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with a (topology and) smooth structure. Finally, we wish to
prove that the image of
\iota_{\vartheta}
is open and that
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a diffeomorphism onto its image (we have already noted that the union
of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
is all of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
). Since
\iota_{\vartheta}=\bar{\iota}\circ\pi_{\vartheta}
, it is clear that the
range of
\iota_{\vartheta}
is an open set and that
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a homeomorphism onto its image. Next, let

p\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
. Let
(U,\varphi)
be local coordinates on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
with
p\in U
.
Define
W:=\pi_{\vartheta}(U)
and
\phi:W\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
in analogy with (
8.31
). Let
\psi
be the composition of
\phi
with
the inverse of
\bar{\iota}
. Then
(\iota_{\vartheta}(U),\psi)
are local coordinates on
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Calculate

\psi\circ\iota_{\vartheta}\circ\varphi^{-1}=\varphi\circ\pi_{\vartheta}^{-1}\circ(\bar{\iota})^{-1}\circ\iota_{\vartheta}\circ\varphi^{-1}=\varphi\circ(\bar{\iota}\circ\pi_{\vartheta})^{-1}\circ\iota_{\vartheta}\circ\varphi^{-1}=\mathrm{Id}.

This proves that
\iota_{\vartheta}
is a local diffeomorphism. The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we prove Lemmas
1.60
and
1.65
.

Proof of Lemma
1.60
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
1.59
. The details are left to the reader.
∎

Proof of Lemma
1.65
.

Again, the proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
1.59
. However, there are some differences. First, in the
proof of Lemma
1.59
, we use the fact that the existence interval of all developments is
(0,\infty)
. In the
present setting, it could be
\mathbb{R}
. However, that the map
\iota_{\vartheta}
is well defined is an immediate consequence of
Proposition
1.35
and Proposition
3.8
. In order to prove that it is injective, assume that

\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, where
\vartheta\in I_{V}
, and that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
and
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{1})

are isometric. Denote the isometry by
\psi
. Let
G_{\alpha,t}:=G_{\alpha}\times\{t\}
denote the
constant-
t
hypersurfaces in
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha})=(M_{\alpha},g_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})
, where
M_{\alpha}=G_{\alpha}\times J_{\alpha}
. Due to the proof of
Lemma
3.8
, we know that the constant-
t
hypersurfaces on which the time derivative of the scalar field are zero are
isolated. Fix a
t_{0}\in J_{0}
such that
(\partial_{t}\phi_{0})(t_{0})\neq 0
. Let
p_{0}\in G_{0}
. Note that
\partial_{t}|_{(p_{0},t_{0})}
can be characterised as the
unique future directed unit vector parallel with the spacetime gradient of
\phi_{0}
at
(p_{0},t_{0})
. Since all these properties are preserved by

\psi
, it follows that
\psi_{*}\partial_{t}|_{(p_{0},t_{0})}=\partial_{t}|_{\psi(p_{0},t_{0})}
. Since this holds on an open and dense subset of
M_{0}
, it is clear that

\psi
maps
\partial_{t}
to
\partial_{t}
. Since
\psi
is an isometry, curves in
G_{0,t}
are mapped to curves perpendicular to
\partial_{t}
. This means that
their image is contained in a constant-
t
hypersurface. Due to the connectedness of
G_{0}
, this means that for each
t
,
G_{0,t}
is mapped
into a constant-
t
hypersurface. Since the same argument can be applied to
\psi^{-1}
, it follows that
\psi
preserves the constant-
t

hypersurfaces. Since
\psi
is an isometry and since the mean curvatures are strictly decreasing, see Lemma
3.8
, it
also follows that
\psi
maps constant-
t
hypersurfaces of a mean curvature belonging to
I_{V}
to a constant-
t
hypersurface with the same
mean curvature. This applies, in particular, to the case that the mean curvature is
\vartheta
, so that
\psi
induces an isometry between

\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
. This proves injectivity. That the union of the images of the
\iota_{\vartheta}
is

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
is an immediate consequence of the definition and Lemma
3.8
.

In order to define the smooth structure, define

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{Sol}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]:=\bigsqcup_{\vartheta>\vartheta_{-}}{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)/\sim,

where
\vartheta_{-}:=[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. The equivalence relation
\sim
is defined in analogy with the equivalence relation introduced in
connection with (
8.29
). The only difference is that we use the maps introduced in (
8.28
) instead of
the ones given in (
8.25
). We leave it to the reader to verify that the remaining steps of the proof of
Lemma
1.59
can be modified to apply in the present setting as well. The lemma follows.
∎

Under the assumptions of Lemma
1.64
, the set of isometry classes of initial data with one fixed mean
curvature parametrises the set of isometry classes of developments. The situation in case
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s})=(\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso})
is somewhat more complicated.
Nevertheless, Lemma
1.67
holds.

Proof of Lemma
1.67
.

Let
x\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
. Due to Lemma
3.8
, developments corresponding to initial data in
x
have
crushing singularities. Combining this observation with Proposition
1.35
, it follows that
x
gives rise to a unique element
of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
. That the corresponding map, say
\varphi
, is injective follows by an argument similar to the beginning of the
proof of Lemma
1.65
. Next, given an isotropic and simply connected Bianchi type I development with a crushing singularity,
it has to induce an element in
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
due to Lemma
3.8
. Thus
\varphi
is a bijection.
In order to prove that
\varphi
is a homeomorphism, let
x
be as above and
y=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})=(M,g,\phi)
, where
\mathfrak{I}\in x
and
M=G\times J
.
Assume
t_{0}\in J
to be such that the initial data induced by
y
on
G_{t_{0}}
are
\mathfrak{I}
. Let
t_{1}<t_{0}
with

t_{1}\in J
be such that the isometry class of initial data induced by
y
at
t_{1}
belongs to
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{1})
for
some
\vartheta_{1}>\vartheta
. That there is such a
t_{1}
follows from Lemma
3.8
.
By an argument similar to the proof of Lemma
8.20
, there is then an open neighbourhood, say

U
, of
x
such that the time at which the isometry class of solutions, corresponding to initial data
\xi\in U
at
t_{0}
, induces data at

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta_{1})
, say
T(\xi)
, depends smoothly on
\xi
. If
y(\cdot;\eta)
is a development corresponding to
initial data
\eta\in\xi
at
t_{0}
, it is then clear that the map taking
\xi\in U
to the isometry class of initial data induced by
y(\cdot;\eta)

at
T(\xi)
is smooth. For this reason, the map
\varphi
is smooth. The continuity of the inverse can be demonstrated by the strict monotonicity
of
\theta
along a solution curve and the smoothness of the flow associated with the equations. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Chapter 9
Geometric results in the direction of the singularity

9.1
Geometric data on the singularity

Our next goal is to prove Theorem
1.45
. However, it is convenient to first characterise the symmetry conditions
in Definition
1.21
in terms of a basis for the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Let
(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
; see
Definition
1.25
. Let
\{e_{i}\}
and
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
be two bases of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying

\mathscr{H}(e_{i},e_{j})=\delta_{ij},\ \ \ \mathscr{K}e_{i}=p_{i}e_{i},\ \ \ [e_{i},e_{j}]=\epsilon_{ijk}o_{k}e_{k}

(9.1)

(no summation on
i
in the second equality and no summation on
k
in the third equality)
and (
9.1
) with
e_{i}
,
p_{i}
and
o_{i}
replaced by
e_{i}^{\prime}
,
p_{i}^{\prime}
and
o_{i}^{\prime}
respectively; cf. Lemma
2.28
. Denote the
corresponding commutator matrices (see Definition
1.3
) by
o=\mathrm{diag}(o_{1},o_{2},o_{3})
and
o^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(o_{1}^{\prime},o_{2}^{\prime},o_{3}^{\prime})
respectively.
Then there is an orthogonal matrix
A
such that
e_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}e_{j}^{\prime}
. Moreover, due to
[
54
, Lemma 19.6, p. 207]
,

o^{\prime}=(\det A)^{-1}A^{t}oA.

(9.2)

In addition,
K^{\prime}=A^{t}KA
, where
K=\mathrm{diag}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})
and
K^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
is isotropic if and only if there is a basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that (
9.1
) holds with all the

p_{i}
equal to
1/3
and all the
o_{i}
equal and
\geq 0
.

Remark 9.2
.

In the case of isotropic Bianchi type I and IX data, applying an isometry
T:\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\mathscr{H}
to a frame
\{e_{i}\}
as in the
statement of the lemma yields a frame with the same properties, except that
o
is transformed to
-o
if
T
is orientation reversing.

Proof.

The statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.31
, up to the requirement that
o_{i}\geq 0
. However, interchanging

e_{1}
with
-e_{1}
, if necessary, this condition can also be ensured; see (
9.2
).
∎

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are LRS if and only if there is a frame
\{e_{i}\}
of

\mathfrak{g}
satisfying the conditions of (
9.1
) with
p_{2}=p_{3}
,
o_{2}=o_{3}
, signs as in Table
1.1

and either
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
or
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
.

Assume
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
to be LRS. Let
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
be a second basis of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying the conditions of
(
9.1
) with
p_{i}
and
o_{i}
replaced by
p_{i}^{\prime}
and
o_{i}^{\prime}
respectively,
p_{2}^{\prime}=p_{3}^{\prime}
,
o_{2}^{\prime}=o_{3}^{\prime}
and signs as in
Table
1.1
. Then, if
e_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}e_{j}^{\prime}
,
A=\mathrm{diag}(\pm 1,A_{2})
, where
A_{2}
is an orthogonal

2\times 2
-matrix. Moreover, for all Bianchi types except I,
\det A
has to equal
1
.

Proof.

The first statement of the lemma is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.33
(up to relabeling of the frame and
interchanging
e_{1}
with
-e_{1}
, if necessary).

Next, let
\{e_{i}\}
and
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
be frames as in the statement of the theorem. If
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
, then
p_{1}^{\prime}
has to differ from
p_{2}^{\prime}
, and
p_{1}=p_{1}^{\prime}
.
This means that
e_{1}
and
e_{1}^{\prime}
are unit eigenvectors corresponding to the same distinct eigenvalue, so that
e_{1}^{\prime}=\pm e_{1}
. The argument in case
o_{1}\neq o_{2}

is similar due to (
9.2
). Thus
A=\mathrm{diag}(\pm 1,A_{2})
, where
A_{2}
is an orthogonal
2\times 2
-matrix. In order for the
signs in Table
1.1
to be respected,
\det A
has to equal
1
, except for Bianchi type I.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}=(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathcal{S}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
are neither isotropic nor LRS. Then there is a frame
\{e_{i}\}
of

\mathfrak{g}
satisfying the conditions of (
9.1
), with signs as in Table
1.1
. Moreover, the following Bianchi
type specific conditions hold:

•

In Bianchi type I,
p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
.

•

In Bianchi type II,
p_{2}>p_{3}
.

•

In Bianchi type VI
0
,
p_{2}\geq p_{3}
and
o_{2}\geq-o_{3}
in case
p_{2}=p_{3}
.

•

In Bianchi type VII
0
and VIII,
p_{2}\geq p_{3}
and
o_{2}>o_{3}
in case
p_{2}=p_{3}
.

•

In Bianchi type IX,
p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
if the
p_{i}
are all distinct;
p_{1}\neq p_{2}=p_{3}
and
o_{2}>o_{3}
if two
p_{i}
’s coincide
and are different from the third; and
o_{1}>o_{2}>o_{3}
if
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}
.

If
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
is a frame of
\mathfrak{g}
satisfying the same conditions with
p_{i}
and
o_{i}
replaced by
p_{i}^{\prime}
and
o_{i}^{\prime}
respectively and

e_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}e_{j}^{\prime}
, then
A
is a diagonal element of
\mathrm{O}(3)
in the case of Bianchi type I and a diagonal element of

\mathrm{SO}(3)
for the other Bianchi types, with one exception: for Bianchi type VI
0
data such that
p_{2}=p_{3}
and
o_{2}=-o_{3}
,
replacing
e_{2}
with
e_{3}
is also allowed.

Proof.

We first verify that
\{e_{i}\}
can be chosen so that the Bianchi specific conditions are satisfied. In the case of Bianchi type I, we can
assume the
p_{i}
to all be distinct, since the data would otherwise be isotropic or LRS. Moreover, the condition
p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
can be achieved
by permuting
the basis vectors. In the case of Bianchi II,
o_{2}=o_{3}=0
. We can thus assume
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
to be distinct, since the data would otherwise
be LRS. If
p_{2}>p_{3}
, nothing needs to be done. If
p_{2}<p_{3}
, we permute
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
while, at the same time, mapping
e_{1}
to
-e_{1}
. This
yields the desired frame. The argument in the case of Bianchi types VI
0
, VII
0
and VIII is similar. Consider Bianchi type IX. If all
the
p_{i}
are distinct, first permuting the frame vectors appropriately and then flipping the sign of one vector, if necessary, yields

p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
. If two
p_{i}
are equal and different from the third, we can similarly achieve
p_{1}\neq p_{2}=p_{3}
. Ensuring
o_{2}>o_{3}
is then
achieved by an argument similar to the one in the case of Bianchi type II. If all the
p_{i}
coincide, then all the
o_{i}
are distinct (since
the data would otherwise be isotropic or LRS). We can then assume
o_{1}>o_{2}>o_{3}
by an argument similar to the case that all the
p_{i}
are distinct.

Assume now that we have a second frame
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
with the same properties. In the case of Bianchi type I, since the
p_{i}
and
p_{i}^{\prime}
are
distinct and ordered, we have to have
p_{i}=p_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
, so that
e_{i}=\pm e_{i}^{\prime}
. This means that
A
is a diagonal element of

\mathrm{O}(3)
. In the case of Bianchi type II, since
o_{1}>0
and
o_{2}=o_{3}=0
(and similarly for
o_{i}^{\prime}
),
A=\mathrm{diag}(\pm 1,A_{2})
,
where
A_{2}
is an orthogonal
2\times 2
-matrix. Since
p_{2}>p_{3}
(and similarly for the
p_{i}^{\prime}
), we, additionally have to have
p_{B}=p_{B}^{\prime}

for
B=2,3
,
e_{2}^{\prime}=\pm e_{2}
and
e_{3}^{\prime}=\pm e_{3}
. Thus
A
is a diagonal element of
\mathrm{O}(3)
. However, since
o_{1}
and
o_{1}^{\prime}
are both
strictly positive, we have to have
A\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
. The arguments in the remaining cases are similar. The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we prove Theorem
1.45
and Remark
1.47
.

Proof of Theorem
1.45
and Remark
1.47
.

Let
p_{i}
denote the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
and choose
\sigma_{+}
,
\sigma_{-}
and
\sigma_{i}
so that (
7.12
) holds. Then

\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}+\bar{\Phi}_{1}^{2}/6=1,

(9.3)

where the relation between the
\Phi_{1}
appearing in the statement of the theorem and
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
is
\bar{\Phi}_{1}:=3\Phi_{1}
; this relation
holds throughout the present proof. Next, let
\{e_{i}\}
be a frame satisfying the conditions of (
9.1
).
Assume the frame to be as described in one of Lemmas
9.1
;
9.3
; and
9.4

according to whether the data are isotropic; LRS; or neither isotropic nor LRS, respectively. Note, in particular,
that the
o_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
.

Uniqueness.
Assume that there is a development
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
as in the statement of the theorem. In particular
M:=G\times J
,
where
J
is an open interval, and
g
can be written

g=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{3}a_{i}^{2}(t)\eta^{i}\otimes\eta^{i},

(9.4)

where
a_{i}\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
,
i=1,2,3
,
\{\eta^{i}\}
is the dual basis of a basis
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
of
G
with associated commutator matrix
\bar{n}=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
, and the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as indicated in
Table
1.1
. This statement follows from the proof of Proposition
1.31
; see, in particular,
(
3.13
) and the adjacent text. We also assume that
\mathscr{H}(\bar{f}_{i},\bar{f}_{i})=1
(no summation) for all
i
.
Note that replacing
\bar{f}_{i}
with
-\bar{f}_{i}
leaves the metric invariant. However, with the exception of Bianchi types I and VI
0
, we have to
flip two basis vectors in this way in order for the sign convention in Table
1.1
to be respected; in the case of Bianchi type
VI
0
, interchanging
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{3}
respects the signs in Table
1.1
. By assumption,
(M,g,\phi)
has a
crushing singularity at the left endpoint of
J
, say
t_{-}
. Fix
t_{0}>t_{-}
such that
\theta(t)>0
on
(t_{-},t_{0})
. On this
interval, the expansion normalised Weingarten map
\mathcal{K}
is well defined, and the
\bar{f}_{k}
are eigenvectors.

Let
\theta_{i}:=\dot{a}_{i}/a_{i}
and
\ell_{i}:=\theta_{i}/\theta
, so that
\mathcal{K}\bar{f}_{i}=\ell_{i}\bar{f}_{i}
(no summation). Combining this observation with
the second equality in (
9.1
) and the fact that
\mathcal{K}
converges to
\mathscr{K}
yields the conclusion that
\ell_{i}
converges
to an element in
\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
, say
\bar{p}_{i}
. Moreover,
\bar{f}_{i}
is an eigenvector of
\mathscr{K}
corresponding to
\bar{p}_{i}
. Note
that
\{\bar{p}_{1},\bar{p}_{2},\bar{p}_{3}\}=\{p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\}
. Next, note that, by assumption,

\mathcal{H}:=\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}\cdot,\theta^{\mathcal{K}}\cdot)\rightarrow\mathscr{H}.

(9.5)

This means, in particular, that
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
converges to
1
(recall that the
\bar{f}_{i}
are unit vectors with respect to
\mathscr{H}
). Let

\hat{f}_{i}:=a_{i}^{-1}\theta^{-\ell_{i}}\bar{f}_{i}
(no summation). Then
\{\hat{f}_{i}\}
is an orthonormal frame with respect to
\mathcal{H}
introduced in
(
9.5
). Since
\mathcal{H}
converges to
\mathscr{H}
and
a_{i}\theta^{\ell_{i}}
converges to
1
, it follows that
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
is an
orthonormal frame with respect to
\mathscr{H}
. This means that
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
satisfies all the conditions of (
9.1
),
but with
o_{i}
and
p_{i}
replaced by
\bar{n}_{i}
and
\bar{p}_{i}
respectively. Moreover, the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
.
If we let
\bar{n}:=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{n}_{1},\bar{n}_{2},\bar{n}_{3})
, then there is an
A\in\mathrm{O}(3)
such that
e_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\bar{f}_{j}
and
(
9.2
) hold with
o^{\prime}
replaced by
\bar{n}
. This means, in particular, that unless
G
is of Bianchi type I or VI
0
,

A\in\mathrm{SO}(3)
, since the
o_{i}
and the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
.

If
G
is of Bianchi type I, the transformation law does not say anything, since
o=\bar{n}=0
in that case. However, we can
permute the frame so that
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
. In the case of Bianchi type IX, we can similarly permute the frame (and, in the end, replace
\bar{f}_{1}
by

-\bar{f}_{1}
, if necessary) in order to ensure
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
, while respecting
\bar{n}_{i}>0
. For the remaining Bianchi types, it is clear that

e_{1}
is distinguished by the requirement that the
\bar{n}_{i}
and
o_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
. This means that
\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}

and
\bar{p}_{1}=p_{1}
. Finally, interchanging
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{3}
, if necessary, and changing
\bar{f}_{1}
to
-\bar{f}_{1}
, if necessary, in the case of
Bianchi types II-VIII, it can be ensured that
\bar{p}_{B}=p_{B}
for
B=2,3
. Finally, if
p_{2}=p_{3}
, we can, additionally, ensure that
\bar{n}_{2}\geq\bar{n}_{3}

for Bianchi types II, VII
0
and VIII. To summarise, we can assume that
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
satisfies all the conditions
of (
9.1
), but with
o_{i}
and
p_{i}
replaced by
\bar{n}_{i}
and
\bar{p}_{i}
respectively. Moreover, we can assume that
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
.
Finally, we can assume the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
.

In the case of Bianchi type VI
0
, we have to have
\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
by the above argument. If
p_{2}\neq p_{3}
, then we have to have
\bar{f}_{2}=\pm e_{2}

and
\bar{f}_{3}=\pm e_{3}
. This means that
o_{i}=\bar{n}_{i}
for all
i
. If
p_{2}=p_{3}
, we can interchange
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{3}
, if necessary, to ensure that

\bar{n}_{2}\geq-\bar{n}_{3}
. If
\bar{n}_{2}>-\bar{n}_{3}
, we then have to have
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
for all
i
. Thus
o_{i}=\bar{n}_{i}
for all
i
. If
\bar{n}_{2}=-\bar{n}_{3}
, then we also
have
o_{i}=\bar{n}_{i}
for all
i
. Moreover, by interchanging
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{3}
, if necessary, we can assume that
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
. To summarise, in
the case of Bianchi types I and VI
0
, we can always assume
o_{i}=\bar{n}_{i}
for all
i
. Moreover, in the case of Bianchi type VI
0
, we can assume
that
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
for all
i
.

Isotropic setting.
In case the data on the singularity are isotropic, then
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
, so that
p_{i}=1/3
, irrespective of the
choice of frame. Moreover, the
o_{i}
are all equal; see Lemma
9.1
. Due to the transformation law
(
9.2
) and the requirement that the
o_{i}
and the
\bar{n}_{i}
both have signs as in Table
1.1
, it follows that

o_{i}=\bar{n}_{i}
for all
i
. To summarise,
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
for all
i
.

LRS setting.
Assume the data to be LRS. Then
\{e_{i}\}
is such that
o_{2}=o_{3}
and
p_{2}=p_{3}
, but either
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
or
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
; see
Lemma
9.3
. Since
\bar{f}_{i}
is an eigenvector of
\mathscr{K}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
p_{i}
, it is clear that if
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
,
then
\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
. Similarly, if
o_{1}\neq o_{2}
and
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
, then
\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
due to the
transformation law (
9.2
) and the fact that the
o_{i}
and the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as in Table
1.1
. Thus

\bar{n}_{1}=o_{1}
. Again, due to the transformation law (
9.2
) and the fact that the
o_{i}
and the
\bar{n}_{i}
have signs as in
Table
1.1
, we also conclude that
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}=o_{2}
. If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{IX}
and
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}
, then we can permute the frame (and replace

\bar{f}_{1}
by
-\bar{f}_{1}
, if necessary) in order to ensure that
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
. To summarise,
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
. Due to
Lemma
9.3
we conclude that
A
must take the form
A=\mathrm{diag}(\pm 1,A_{2})
, where
A_{2}
is an orthogonal matrix. Moreover,

\det A=1
for all Bianchi types except I.

Data that are neither isotropic nor LRS.
In the case of Bianchi types I and VI
0
, we already know that
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
and that

\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
. Moreover,
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
(in the case of Bianchi type VI
0
, we already know this to be true, and in the case of Bianchi
type I it follows from the fact that
p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
).
In the case of Bianchi types II, VII
0
and VIII, we know that
\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
. If
p_{2}\neq p_{3}
, we have to have
\bar{f}_{B}=\pm e_{B}

for
B\in\{2,3\}
. If
p_{2}=p_{3}
, we know that
o_{2}>o_{3}
and
\bar{n}_{2}>\bar{n}_{3}
, so that
\bar{f}_{B}=\pm e_{B}
for
B\in\{2,3\}
. We thus have
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
,

\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
for these Bianchi types. Consider Bianchi type IX. If all the
p_{i}
are distinct, then
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
for all
i
.
This means that
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
and
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
for all
i
. Assume that two of the
p_{i}
are equal and different from the third. Then
p_{1}\neq p_{2}=p_{3}
,

\bar{f}_{1}=\pm e_{1}
and
\bar{n}_{1}=o_{1}
. Since
o_{2}>o_{3}
, we can exchange
\bar{f}_{2}
and
\bar{f}_{3}
and replace
\bar{f}_{1}
with
-\bar{f}_{1}
(if necessary) so
that
\bar{n}_{2}>\bar{n}_{3}
. This means that
\bar{f}_{A}=\pm e_{A}
for
A\in\{2,3\}
. To summarise,
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
,
\bar{n}_{i}=n_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
. Finally, if
all the
p_{i}
are equal, permuting the elements of the frame
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
and interchanging
\bar{f}_{1}
with
-\bar{f}_{1}
, if necessary, it can be
ensured that
\bar{n}_{1}>\bar{n}_{2}>\bar{n}_{3}
. This means that
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
, so that
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
. To summarise the case that the data are neither isotropic
nor LRS, we can (by permuting the elements of
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
) ensure that
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
, that
p_{i}=\bar{p}_{i}
and that
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
.

To summarise: in what follows, we can always assume that
\bar{n}_{i}=o_{i}
and
\bar{p}_{i}=p_{i}
for all
i
.

Asymptotics.
Defining
n_{i}
by (
3.5
),

\frac{n_{1}}{\theta}=\frac{a_{1}\theta^{\ell_{1}}}{a_{2}\theta^{\ell_{2}}a_{3}\theta^{\ell_{3}}}\bar{n}_{1}\theta^{\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}-\ell_{1}-1},

and similarly for
n_{2}
and
n_{3}
. Since the
\ell_{i}
sum to one, the last factor equals
\theta^{-2\ell_{1}}
. In case
p_{1}>0
, it follows that

n_{1}/\theta
converges to zero; recall that
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
converges to
1
. In particular, if all the
p_{i}
are strictly positive, all
the
n_{i}/\theta
converge to zero. Assume that
p_{3}\leq 0
. Due to Definition
1.17
, there are two possibilities.

The first possibility
is that the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are all
<1
. Then, since the eigenvalues sum up to one, it is clear that

p_{1},p_{2}>0
. This means that
e_{3}
is uniquely determined (up to a constant multiple). Since

\mathscr{K}\bar{f}_{3}=\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\mathcal{K}\bar{f}_{3}=\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\ell_{3}\bar{f}_{3}=p_{3}\bar{f}_{3},

(9.6)

it is clear that
\bar{f}_{3}=\pm e_{3}
. Next, by assumption, the vector subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
is a subalgebra. This means that

e_{3}
is perpendicular to
[e_{1},e_{2}]
, so that
\bar{f}_{3}
is perpendicular to
[e_{1},e_{2}]
. Since
\bar{f}_{3}
is also perpendicular to
\bar{f}_{1}
and

\bar{f}_{2}
, it follows that there are constants
\kappa_{A}^{B}
,
A,B\in\{1,2\}
such that
\bar{f}_{A}=\kappa_{A}^{B}e_{B}
. This means that
[\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2}]

is a constant multiple of
[e_{1},e_{2}]
, so that
\bar{f}_{3}
is perpendicular to
[\bar{f}_{1},\bar{f}_{2}]=\bar{n}_{3}\bar{f}_{3}
. Thus
\bar{n}_{3}=0
and

\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}[n_{i}(t)/\theta(t)]=0

(9.7)

for all
i
, irrespective of whether
p_{3}\leq 0
or not. Combining this observation with (
3.1c
) and the assumptions, which imply

\frac{3}{2}\theta^{-2}\sigma^{ij}\sigma_{ij}+\frac{3}{2}\theta^{-2}\phi_{t}^{2}\rightarrow 1,

(9.8)

it follows that
\theta_{t}/\theta^{2}\rightarrow-1
. This means, in particular, that
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past, so that we can
assume
t_{-}=0
. Moreover, it means that
\theta
is not integrable to the past. Introducing a time coordinate
\tau
according to (
3.27
),
it is thus clear that the range of
\tau
is of the form
(-\infty,\tau_{0})
. From now on, a prime denotes differentiation with respect to
\tau
.

The second possibility
is that
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
. We then assume
\mathscr{K}e_{1}=e_{1}
, so that
n_{1}/\theta
converges to zero as
above. Moreover,
n_{1}n_{i}/\theta^{2}
converge to zero for
i=2,3
. Next, note that, by the assumptions, (
9.8
) holds.
Combining this observation with (
3.1d
) and the fact that
n_{1}n_{i}/\theta^{2}\rightarrow 0

for all
i
, it follows that
V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}\rightarrow 0
and
(n_{2}-n_{3})/\theta\rightarrow 0
(
note that this is the only point at
which we use the assumption that

V\geq 0
). Combining these observations with
(
3.1c
) and
(
9.8
), it is clear that
\theta_{t}/\theta^{2}\rightarrow-1
. Thus
t_{-}=0
and the range of
\tau
is of the form

(-\infty,\tau_{0})
, as before.

Next, define
\Sigma_{i}
,
\Sigma_{\pm}
and
N_{i}
by

\Sigma_{i}:=\theta_{i}/\theta-1/3,\ \ \ \Sigma_{+}:=\tfrac{3}{2}(\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3})=-\tfrac{3}{2}\Sigma_{1},\ \ \ \Sigma_{-}:=\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2}(\Sigma_{2}-\Sigma_{3}),\ \ \ N_{i}:=\tfrac{n_{i}}{\theta}.

(9.9)

Finally, define
\phi_{1}=\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi_{0}=\phi
. Then the arguments presented in Subsections
3.1
and

3.3
yield the conclusion that (
3.31
)–(
3.39
) hold. In case
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
,
we can assume that
(\Sigma_{+},\Sigma_{-})
converges to
(-1,0)
. Moreover, in this case, we assume
V\geq 0
. Note also that, due to
(
3.17
) and the above observations,
X
converges to
1
in this setting. Thus either the conditions of
Lemma
3.9
or the conditions of Lemma
3.10
are satisfied. Thus Proposition
4.4

applies, so that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and

N_{2}=N_{3}
. This means that
\sigma_{22}=\sigma_{33}
, so that
a_{2}/a_{3}
is constant due to the first equality in (
3.13
).
On the other hand, we also know that
\theta^{\ell_{A}}a_{A}\rightarrow 1
for
A\in\{2,3\}
and that
\ell_{2}=\ell_{3}
. This means that
a_{2}/a_{3}

converges to
1
. Thus
a_{2}=a_{3}
. Combining this observation with
(
3.5
) and the fact that
N_{2}=N_{3}
, it follows that
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
. This means that
o_{2}=o_{3}
and
p_{2}=p_{3}=0
, so that
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}

are LRS. Note that this proves the necessity of the last requirement in Definition
1.17
in case
1
is an eigenvalue of

\mathscr{K}
. In particular, the last statement of Theorem
1.45
follows.

If
1
is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
, then we can, by the above, assume that
p_{1}=1
,
p_{2}=p_{3}=0
,
a_{2}=a_{3}
,
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
, and we do so in
what follows. If the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are all
<1
, then, by the argument presented below (
9.6
), it follows that if

p_{i}\leq 0
, then
N_{i}=0
. Moreover, if
p_{3}\leq 0
, then
p_{1},p_{2}>0
. If the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
are
<1
and there is a
p_{i}\leq 0
, we, from
now on, assume that it is
p_{3}
. In this case, we can thus always assume
p_{1},p_{2}>0
, so that
f_{i}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})>0
for
i=1,2
; see
(
7.12
). If
p_{3}>0
, we can, additionally, assume that
f_{3}(2,\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-})>0
; see (
7.12
). Keeping
(
3.30b
) in mind, the Hamiltonian constraint (
3.39
) can be written

\Sigma_{+}^{2}+\Sigma_{-}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{1}^{2}+\tfrac{3V\circ\phi_{0}}{\theta^{2}}+\tfrac{3}{4}[N_{1}^{2}+N_{2}^{2}+N_{3}^{2}-2(N_{1}N_{2}+N_{2}N_{3}+N_{1}N_{3})]=1.

(9.10)

By assumption
\Sigma_{\pm}
converges to
\sigma_{\pm}
and
\phi_{1}
converges to
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
. Moreover, the sum of the first three terms on
the left hand side converges to
1
. Next, either all the
N_{i}
tend to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, or
N_{2}=N_{3}
,
\Sigma_{-}=0
,
N_{1}
tends
to zero and
N_{1}N_{2}
tends to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Combining these observations with (
9.10
), it follows that

\Omega
tends to zero. Moreover, keeping the definition of
q
, i.e. (
3.30c
