), in mind, it follows that

q
converges to
2
. Combining this fact with the above observations and (
3.35a
) yields the conclusion that if all the eigenvalues of

\mathscr{K}
are
<1
, then the
N_{i}
converge to zero exponentially. Moreover, if one of the eigenvalues of
\mathscr{K}
equals
1
, then
N_{1}
and

N_{1}N_{2}
converge to zero exponentially. Next, note that since

|\phi^{\prime}|\leq\sqrt{6}+\dots
(where the dots represent exponentially decaying terms), see (
9.10
), there exists, for every
\eta>0
, a
constant
C_{\eta}
such that

\displaystyle|\phi(\tau)|\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{6}|\tau|+C_{\eta},\ \ \ \ln\theta(\tau)\geq-(3-\eta)\tau-C_{\eta}

for all
\tau\leq 0
, where we appealed to (
3.31
).
Combining this observation with the assumption that
V\in\mathfrak{P}^{2}_{\alpha_{V}}
, it follows that
\Omega
decays exponentially. Note that
polynomials in the
N_{i}
appearing in (
3.37
) and (
3.40
) decay exponentially. Since
\Omega
converges to zero exponentially,
(
3.40
) implies that
q-2
converges to zero exponentially. Since
S_{\pm}
also converge to zero exponentially, we also conclude that

\Sigma_{\pm}-\sigma_{\pm}
decay exponentially. Moreover,

\theta(\tau)=\exp\left(\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{0}}[1+q(s)]ds\right)\theta(\tau_{0})=C_{0}e^{-3\tau}[1+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})]

(9.11)

for some constants
C_{0},\epsilon>0
, where
\tau_{0}
is an element of the existence interval of the solution. At this point, we can fix the translation
ambiguity in the definition of
\tau
in order to ensure that

\theta(\tau)=e^{-3\tau}[1+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})].

(9.12)

Since
t\downarrow 0
corresponds to
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, we conclude that

t(\tau)=\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}3e^{3s}[1+O(e^{\epsilon s})]ds=e^{3\tau}[1+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})].

(9.13)

In particular,

t\theta=1+O(e^{\epsilon\tau}).

(9.14)

Next, let
m_{i}=\ln|\bar{n}_{i}|
if
\bar{n}_{i}\neq 0
and
m_{i}=0
otherwise. Let
\epsilon_{i}=\mathrm{sign}\bar{n}_{i}
if
\bar{n}_{i}\neq 0
and
\epsilon_{i}=0
otherwise.
Note, for future reference, that
m_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}
are determined by the data on the singularity and the choice of frame
\{e_{i}\}
. In particular,
they do not depend on the development (
9.4
). Define
\mu_{i}
by

\mu_{i}:=\ln\frac{a_{i}}{\theta a_{j}a_{k}}+m_{i},

(9.15)

where
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
. Then
N_{i}=\epsilon_{i}e^{\mu_{i}}
. Moreover,

\mu_{i}^{\prime}=q+6\Sigma_{i}.

Next, compute

\mu_{i}=\ln\frac{\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}}{\theta^{\ell_{j}}a_{j}\theta^{\ell_{k}}a_{k}}-2\ell_{i}\ln\theta+m_{i}=-\frac{1}{3}(2+6\Sigma_{i})\ln\theta+\ln\frac{\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}}{\theta^{\ell_{j}}a_{j}\theta^{\ell_{k}}a_{k}}+m_{i};

(9.16)

note that
\ell_{i}=\Sigma_{i}+1/3
due to (
9.9
) and that
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}+\ell_{3}=1
. Due to (
9.12
),
\ln\theta+3\tau

decays exponentially. Finally,
\theta^{\ell_{i}}a_{i}
converges to
1
. Combining these observations with (
9.16
) yields

\mu_{i}(\tau)=6(\Sigma_{i}(\tau)+1/3)\tau+m_{i}+\dots,

(9.17)

where the dots signify terms that converge to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In particular, defining
\nu_{i}
by (
7.9a
), it follows that

\nu_{i}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Next,
s_{i}:=\Sigma_{i}-\sigma_{i}\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.
Defining
\varkappa
by (
7.9c
) with
\varkappa_{\infty}=0
, it is clear that
\varkappa(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.
Next, note that
\phi_{1}
is bounded due to (
9.10
) and the fact that the polynomial in the
N_{i}
converges to zero exponentially.
Since
2-q
converges
to zero exponentially, this means that the first term on the right hand side of (
3.38b
) converges to zero exponentially. By an
argument similar to the proof of the fact that
\Omega
converges to zero exponentially, it is clear that the second term on the right hand side
of (
3.38b
) converges to zero exponentially. This means that
\phi_{1}
converges to its limit
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
exponentially. In particular,

\psi_{1}\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, where
\psi_{1}
is defined by (
7.9e
) with
\Phi_{1}
replaced by
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
. Moreover,
integrating the exponential convergence of
\phi_{1}
to
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
yields the conclusion that
\phi_{0}-\bar{\Phi}_{1}\tau-\bar{\Phi}_{0}
converges to zero
exponentially for some
\bar{\Phi}_{0}
. However, since
\bar{\Phi}_{1}=3\Phi_{1}
and
\theta+3\tau
decays exponentially, it follows that

\phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\ln\theta-\bar{\Phi}_{0}
converges to zero. Since
\phi_{t}/\theta-\Phi_{1}
decays exponentially, this means that
\bar{\Phi}_{0}=\Phi_{0}
. Moreover,

\psi_{0}
defined by (
7.9d
) converges to zero. Thus Proposition
7.3
applies, so that
\nu_{i}
,
s_{\pm}
,

\varkappa
,
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
are thus uniquely determined. Note also, for future
reference, that
\Pi
appearing in (
7.7
) is determined by the data on the singularity and the choice of frame
\{e_{i}\}
. In particular,
it does not depend on the development converging to the data on the singularity. Next, note that since
\varkappa
is uniquely determined,
\theta

is uniquely determined as a function of
\tau
. Combining this observation with (
3.27
) and the fact that
\tau\rightarrow-\infty

corresponds to
t\rightarrow 0
, it is clear that
t
is uniquely determined as a function of
\tau
. Next, since the
s_{i}
(or, equivalently, the

s_{\pm}
) are uniquely determined, it follows that the
\Sigma_{i}
are uniquely determined. Combining this observation with the fact that the

\nu_{i}
are uniquely determined, it follows that the
\mu_{i}
are uniquely determined. Due to (
9.15
), it follows that the
a_{i}
are
uniquely determined. Thus

g=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i}a_{i}^{2}(t)\eta^{i}\otimes\eta^{i},

(9.18)

where
\{\eta^{i}\}
is the frame dual to
\{\bar{f}_{i}\}
and the
a_{i}
are uniquely determined by
\Pi
appearing in (
7.7
), which, in its turn,
is determined by the data on the singularity and the choice of frame
\{e_{i}\}
. Similarly, the scalar field is uniquely determined by
\Pi
.

Next, we wish to prove that if the data at the singularity are isotropic or LRS, then so is the development. Assume, to begin with, that the
data on the singularity are isotropic. Then
\sigma_{i}=0
, all the
\epsilon_{i}
are equal and all the
m_{i}
are equal. Thus all the
N_{i}
equal and
all the
\Sigma_{i}
equal (since
\eta_{\lambda}(\Pi)=\Pi
for any
\lambda\in S_{3}
, using the notation of Proposition
7.3
, the

N_{i}
and
\Sigma_{i}
are invariant under permutations). Since the
\Sigma_{i}
sum up to
0
, this means that all the
\Sigma_{i}
vanish. Next, due to
Proposition
7.3
, all the
\nu_{i}
equal. Thus, all the
\mu_{i}-m_{i}
are identically equal for the entire solution.
Recalling (
9.15
), this means that
a_{i}/(a_{j}a_{k})
, where
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
, is independent of
i
. This means that
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}
.
Letting
a(t):=a_{1}(t)
, it follows that

g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\mathscr{H}.

(9.19)

This proves uniqueness in the isotropic setting, since
a
is uniquely determined by the data on the singularity. In addition, the above observations
justify the first statement of Remark
1.47
.

In the LRS setting,
\bar{n}_{2}=\bar{n}_{3}
(so that
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{3}
,
m_{2}=m_{3}
) and
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{3}
. By the uniqueness statement of
Proposition
7.3
, it follows that
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}
and
\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{3}
(so that
\Sigma_{-}=0
). This means, in particular, that

\mu_{2}-m_{2}=\mu_{3}-m_{3}
, so that
a_{2}=a_{3}
. Thus the metric takes the form

g=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\eta^{1}\otimes\eta^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(t)[\eta^{2}\otimes\eta^{2}+\eta^{3}\otimes\eta^{3}].

(9.20)

Since
e_{i}=A_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}\bar{f}_{j}
, where
A=\mathrm{diag}(\pm 1,A_{2})
and
A_{2}
is an orthogonal
2\times 2
-matrix, it follows that

g=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\xi^{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(t)[\xi^{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+\xi^{3}\otimes\xi^{3}],

(9.21)

where
\{\xi^{i}\}
is the basis dual to
\{e_{i}\}
and the
a_{i}
are uniquely determined by the data on the singularity. This proves uniqueness.
In addition, the above observations justify the second statement of Remark
1.47
.

Next, consider the case that
G
is of Bianchi type VI
0
. Assume, moreover, that
\bar{n}_{2}=-\bar{n}_{3}
and
p_{2}=p_{3}
. Then, due to
Proposition
7.3
, we know that
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}
and that
\Sigma_{2}=\Sigma_{3}
; note that
\eta_{\lambda}^{-}(\Pi)=\Pi
, if

\lambda\in S_{3}
is such that
\lambda(1)=1
,
\lambda(2)=3
and
\lambda(3)=2
. This means that
\mu_{2}-m_{2}=\mu_{3}-m_{3}
. Due to (
9.15
),
this means that
a_{2}=a_{3}
. Since we, as noted above, can assume
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
, it follows that the metric takes the form
(
9.21
), where the
a_{i}
are uniquely determined by the data on the singularity. This proves uniqueness. In addition, the above
observations justify the third statement of Remark
1.47
. This proves uniqueness.

In the remaining cases, the
a_{i}
are uniquely determined by the data on the singularity. Moreover,
\bar{f}_{i}=\pm e_{i}
. This means that the
metric is uniquely determined.

Existence.

It remains to prove existence. Fix
\{e_{i}\}
,
p_{i}
,
o_{i}
,
\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{\pm}
as at the beginning of the proof. Then, with
\Phi_{0}
and

\Phi_{1}
as in the statement of the theorem and
\bar{\Phi}_{1}
defined as at the beginning of the proof,
\sigma_{+}^{2}+\sigma_{-}^{2}+\bar{\Phi}_{1}^{2}/6=1
.
Next, let
\epsilon_{i}
equal zero if
o_{i}=0
and

\epsilon_{i}=o_{i}/|o_{i}|
if
o_{i}\neq 0
. Let
\varkappa_{\infty}=0
. Finally, let
m_{k}=\ln|o_{k}|
if
o_{k}\neq 0
and
m_{k}=0
otherwise. Note
that there are two possibilities: either
p_{i}>0
whenever
\epsilon_{i}\neq 0
; or
\sigma_{+}=-1
,
\sigma_{-}=0
,
\Phi_{1}=0
,
m_{2}=m_{3}
,
\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon_{3}

and all the
\epsilon_{i}
are non-vanishing. The conditions of Proposition
7.3
are thus satisfied. Applying
this proposition yields a unique solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.38
) such that the statements of
Proposition
7.3
hold; note, in particular, that
N_{2}=N_{3}
and
\Sigma_{-}=0
in case
\sigma_{+}=-1
and all the
\epsilon_{i}
are
non-zero. Define
\theta_{i}:=\theta(\Sigma_{i}+1/3)
, define the time coordinate
t
by (
3.27
) and the requirement that

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
corresponds to
t\downarrow 0
. Define
a_{i}
, up to a constant, by
\partial_{t}\ln a_{i}=\theta_{i}
. Due to
Proposition
7.3
, we know that
\theta_{i}/\theta
converges to
p_{i}
. Due to the assumptions, the polynomial in the

N_{i}
appearing on the right hand side of (
3.40
) converges to zero exponentially. Moreover,
\Omega
also converges to zero
exponentially. Due to (
3.40
), this means that
q-2
converges
to zero exponentially. Combining this observation with (
3.31
) and the fact that
\varkappa_{\infty}=0
, it follows that
(
9.12
) holds. Since
t\downarrow 0
corresponds to
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, we conclude that (
9.13
) and
(
9.14
) hold. Next, since
q-2
and the
S_{\pm}
converge to zero exponentially, it follows that
\Sigma_{i}
converges exponentially
to its limit, so that
\theta_{i}/\theta
converges exponentially to
p_{i}
. Since
\partial_{\tau}\ln a_{i}=3\theta_{i}/\theta
, we conclude that

\ln a_{i}=3p_{i}\tau+\alpha_{i}+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})
for some constants
\alpha_{i}
and
\epsilon>0
. Fix the ambiguity in the definition of
a_{i}
so that

\ln a_{i}=3p_{i}\tau+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})
. This means that
\ln a_{i}=p_{i}\ln t+O(t^{\epsilon})
. At this stage, we can define
\phi
by
\phi=\phi_{0}
and

g
by (
9.18
) with
\eta^{i}
replaced by
\xi^{i}
, where
\{\xi^{i}\}
is the frame dual to
\{e_{i}\}
. By the assumed asymptotics, it
follows that
\theta\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
. The above observations also yield the conclusion that
(M,g,\phi)
induces the desired
data on the singularity. By appealing to the arguments appearing in Subsections
3.1
and
3.3
, it
can be verified that the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations are satisfied; the details are left to the reader. Finally, it remains to prove
that the development arises from regular initial data in the same symmetry class as the data on the singularity.

Assume the data on the singularity to be isotropic. Then they are of Bianchi type I or IX and the development is of the same type. By the argument
presented in the proof of uniqueness, the development is isotropic. Moreover, if the development is isotropic, then the data on the singularity have
to be isotropic. If
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
, the development arises from initial data in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}[V]
due to the argument presented
in the proof of uniqueness. Next, assume the data on the singularity to be LRS. Then the development is LRS or isotropic due to the argument presented
in the proof of uniqueness. It can only be isotropic in the case of Bianchi types I and IX (note that we have excluded isotropic and LRS initial data
on the singularity in the case of Bianchi type VII
0
). Moreover, if it is isotropic, then the initial data on induced on the singularity are
isotropic, which they are not. Finally, if
\mathfrak{s}=\mathrm{gen}
, then the development has to be of the same type, since it would otherwise induce data on
the singularity with
\mathfrak{s}\neq\mathrm{gen}
. The theorem follows.
∎

9.2
Regularity of the map from developments to data on the singularity

Next, we prove Proposition
1.70
.

Proof of Proposition
1.70
.

Fix
\vartheta\in I_{V}
, let
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and assume
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
to induce data on the singularity. Since
the conditions of Lemma
3.9
are satisfied, the development corresponds to a solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) (see the proof of Proposition
1.31
) which induces a global solution
S
to
(
3.31
)–(
3.39
). Moreover, due to the uniqueness part of the proof of Theorem
1.45
, it
follows that
S
is an admissible convergent solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.39
); see
Definition
8.1
. In order to justify this statement, note that
\phi_{1}
and the
\Sigma_{i}
converge due to the assumption that
the development induces data on the singularity. In addition, (
9.12
)
holds, so that
\theta(\tau)\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}
for
\tau\leq 0
. Next, assume that all the eigenvalues
of
\mathscr{K}
are
<1
. Then, due to the proof of Theorem
1.45
, all the
N_{i}
converge to zero exponentially.
In fact, if
\bar{\sigma}_{i}
is the limit of
\Sigma_{i}
, then
\bar{\sigma}_{i}+1/3>0
in case
N_{i}\neq 0
; see, again, the proof of
Theorem
1.45
. Finally, assume that there is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
which equals
1
. Then, due to the
proof of Theorem
1.45
, we can assume that
N_{2}=N_{3}
, that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and that
\Sigma_{+}\rightarrow-1
. Note
also that the limit of
\phi_{1}
has to equal zero, since
\mathscr{K}
could otherwise not have an eigenvalue equal to
1
. If
\mathfrak{T}=\mathrm{VIII}
,
condition (ii) of Definition
8.1
is thus satisfied. Since Bianchi type I
solutions clearly satisfy (i) of Definition
8.1
; since we have excluded isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
;
and since Bianchi type VI
0
is not consistent with local rotational symmetry, what remains to be considered is LRS Bianchi type
II with
N_{1}>0
. Since
\Sigma_{+}
converges to
-1
,
N_{1}
converges to zero exponentially and
\bar{\sigma}_{1}+1/3>0
. Thus condition
(i) of Definition
8.1
is satisfied.

Next, note that, due to Lemma
2.14
, Definition
2.18
and Remark
2.20
, we can identify

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
with the quotient
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)=\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)/\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
.
Moreover, due to Lemma
2.19
,
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
is a smooth manifold. Next,
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}
, introduced in
(
8.6
) defines a diffeomorphism from
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
to
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
;
cf. Lemma
8.5
; Definitions
2.12
,
2.18
and
8.7
; and the proof of Lemma
8.9
.
In addition, note that, given
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
, there is an

\eta\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
arising from
\mathfrak{I}
and that this correspondence gives rise to the identification between

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
; cf. the proof of Lemma
2.15
and
Remark
2.17
. Given that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\eta_{0}
are related in this way, let
\xi_{0}:=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta_{0})
. Then the first
part of the proof yields the conclusion that
\xi_{0}\in\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
in case
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
induces data on the singularity; cf.
Definitions
8.10
and
8.12
. Due to Lemma
8.13
, there is thus an open neighbourhood of

\xi_{0}
in
\mathsf{P}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
, say
U
, contained in
\mathsf{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
. Due to Lemma
8.15
, it follows
that if
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\eta\in\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)
arises from
\mathfrak{I}
, then
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})

induces data on the singularity. The subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
that gives rise to data on the singularity, say
W
, is
therefore open. Since the corresponding subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
is a quotient of
W
, we conclude that

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
.

That
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
8.16
; due to
Lemma
3.4
,
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t
in the developments of interest here, so that the condition
q>-1
is always
fulfilled. In order to prove that it is a diffeomorphism onto its image, it is sufficient to prove injectivity. Assume, to this end, that

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}(x_{2})
. This means
that if
[\mathfrak{I}_{i}]=x_{i}
,
i=1,2
, then the data induced on the singularity by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
are isometric. Denote the isometry between
the data on the singularity by
\chi
. Due to Proposition
1.50
,
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is then an isometry between
the developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
. In particular,
\chi
induces an isometry between
\mathfrak{I}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, since the mean curvatures of
\mathfrak{I}_{i}

coincide and
\theta_{t}<0
for all
t
. This means that
x_{1}=x_{2}
and the global injectivity of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta}
follows.

Next, assume that (
1.18
) holds with
\vartheta_{2}\geq\vartheta_{1}
. Then, since
\theta\rightarrow\infty
to
the past and
\theta_{t}<0
for the developments under consideration here, see Lemma
3.4
, a development corresponding
to the isometry class of developments induced by
x_{1}
has a hypersurface of the form
G_{1}\times\{t\}
with mean curvature
\vartheta_{2}
. Call the
corresponding isometry class of initial data
x_{3}
. Then
\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{3})=\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
, so that
x_{2}=x_{3}
due to
Lemma
1.59
. Thus
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. The surjectivity of

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
follows from Theorem
1.45
. That
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

and that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
1.59
. In order to prove injectivity, assume
that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}(y_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}(y_{2})
. We can assume that
y_{i}=\iota_{\vartheta_{i}}(x_{i})
and that
\vartheta_{2}\geq\vartheta_{1}
. Then

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. By arguments similar to the above, this equality can be used to conclude that

y_{1}=y_{2}
. The proposition follows.
∎

Next, we prove Proposition
1.74
.

Proof of Proposition
1.74
.

The proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
1.70
. However, there are a few slightly subtle differences. For that
reason, we provide a complete argument. Fix
\vartheta>0
. Due to Lemma
2.14
, Definition
2.18
and
Remark
2.20
, we can identify
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
with the quotient

\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)=\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)/\Gamma_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover, due to Lemma
2.19
,

\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
is a smooth manifold. Denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
which is projected to

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
via this identification by
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta)
. Next,
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}

induces a diffeomorphism from
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
to
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
; cf. Lemma
8.5
;
Definitions
2.12
,
2.18
and
8.7
; and the proof of Lemma
8.9
.
Let

\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\ln\vartheta):=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}[\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta)].

Due to Lemmas
3.4
and
3.6
and Definition
1.55
,
initial data in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta)
correspond to developments such that the left end point of the existence interval
is
0
;
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
; there is a
t_{0}>0
with the property that
\theta(t)>0
and
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for

t\leq t_{0}
; and
\theta
is not integrable on
(0,t_{0})
. Moreover,
t_{0}
can be assumed to be the initial time at which the initial data
are specified. Next, since
\theta
is not integrable on
(0,t_{0})
, it is possible to introduce
\tau
by the requirements that
\tau(t_{0})=0

and that (
3.27
) hold. The range of
\tau
then includes
(-\infty,0]
. Let

\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\eta_{0}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta)
be such that it arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
;
cf. Remark
2.17
. Note that this correspondence gives rise to the identification between

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
; cf. the proof of Lemma
2.15
. Next, let

\xi_{0}:=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta_{0})
. Assume
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to induce data on the singularity. Then, due to the uniqueness part of the proof of
Theorem
1.45
, it follows that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
corresponds to an admissible convergent solution to
(
3.31
)–(
3.39
); see Definition
8.1
. In order to justify this statement, note first that
the existence interval (in
\tau
-time) is unbounded to the past. Moreover,
\phi_{1}
and the
\Sigma_{i}

converge due to the assumption that the development induces data on the singularity. In addition, (
9.12
) holds,
so that
\theta(\tau)\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}
for
\tau\leq T
. Next, assume that all the eigenvalues
of
\mathscr{K}
are
<1
. Then, due to the proof of Theorem
1.45
, all the
N_{i}
converge to zero exponentially.
In fact, if
\bar{\sigma}_{i}
is the limit of
\Sigma_{i}
, then
\bar{\sigma}_{i}+1/3>0
in case
N_{i}\neq 0
; see, again, the proof of
Theorem
1.45
. Finally, assume that there is an eigenvalue of
\mathscr{K}
which equals
1
. Then, due to the
proof of Theorem
1.45
, we can assume that
N_{2}=N_{3}
, that
\Sigma_{-}=0
and that
\Sigma_{+}\rightarrow-1
. Note also
that the limit of
\phi_{1}
has to equal zero, since
\mathscr{K}
could otherwise not have an eigenvalue equal to
1
. This means that (ii) of
Definition
8.1
is satisfied.

Due to Lemma
8.13
, there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
in
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\ln\vartheta)
, say
U
, which leads to
admissible solutions. Since
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
,
see Lemma
8.20
, we can assume that
U
is contained in
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\ln\vartheta)
.
Due to Lemma
8.15
, it follows that if
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\eta\in\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)

arises from
\mathfrak{I}
, then
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces data on the singularity. The set of elements in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}(\vartheta)
that
give rise to data on the singularity, say
W
, is therefore an open subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}(\vartheta)
. Since the corresponding subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
is a quotient of
W
, we conclude that

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s},\mathrm{nd}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{IX}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V](\vartheta)
.

That
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
8.16
. In order to prove that
it is a diffeomorphism onto its image, it is sufficient to prove injectivity. Assume, to this end, that

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}(x_{2})
. This means
that if
[\mathfrak{I}_{i}]=x_{i}
,
i=1,2
, then the data induced on the singularity by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
are isometric. Denote the isometry between
the data on the singularity by
\chi
. Due to Proposition
1.50
,
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is then an isometry between
the developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
. In particular,
\chi
induces an isometry between
\mathfrak{I}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, since the mean curvatures of

\mathfrak{I}_{i}
coincide and
\theta_{t}<0
for
t\leq t_{0}
. This means that
x_{1}=x_{2}
and the global injectivity of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta}
follows.

Next, assume that (
1.20
) holds with
\vartheta_{2}\geq\vartheta_{1}
. Then, since
\theta\rightarrow\infty
to
the past and
\theta_{t}<0
for
t\leq t_{0}
for the developments under consideration here, see Definition
1.55
, a development
corresponding to the isometry class of developments induced by
x_{1}
has a hypersurface of the form
G_{1}\times\{t\}
with mean curvature
\vartheta_{2}

and
t\leq t_{0}
. Call the corresponding isometry class of initial data
x_{3}
. Then
\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{3})=\iota_{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
, so that
x_{2}=x_{3}

due to Lemma
1.60
. Thus
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. In order to prove surjectivity
in case
\mathfrak{t}\in\{\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
, note that, given data on the singularity, there is a corresponding development due to
Theorem
1.45
. Due to the proof of Theorem
1.45
, we know that
\theta
tends to infinity,
that
V/\theta^{2}
converges to zero and that
\bar{S}/\theta^{2}
converges to zero in the direction of the singularity. Moreover, we know that
q

converges to
2
, so that there is a
t_{1}
with the property that
\theta_{t}(t)<0
for
t\leq t_{1}
. Combining these observations with
Definitions
1.53
and
1.55
, it follows that the data are in the image of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}
for

\mathfrak{t}\in\{\mathrm{ap},\mathrm{pp}\}
. That
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is an open subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

and that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
1.60
. In order to prove injectivity, assume
that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}(y_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}(y_{2})
. We can assume that
y_{i}=\iota_{\vartheta_{i}}(x_{i})
and that
\vartheta_{2}\geq\vartheta_{1}
. Then

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{IX},\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{t}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. By arguments similar to the above, this equality can be used to conclude that

y_{1}=y_{2}
. The proposition follows.
∎

Finally, we prove Proposition
1.78
.

Proof of Proposition
1.78
.

The proof is again similar to the proof of Proposition
1.70
. However, there are, again, a few slightly subtle
differences. For that reason, we provide a complete argument here. Fix
\vartheta>[3v_{\max}(V)]^{1/2}
. Due to Lemma
2.14
,
Definition
2.18
and Remark
2.20
, we can identify
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
with
the trivial quotient
\mathsf{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)=:\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)
; recall that

\Gamma_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{iso}}=\{\mathrm{Id}\}
, see Definition
2.12
. Next note that the inverse of
\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
, introduced in
(
8.6
) defines a diffeomorphism from
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)
to

\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\ln\vartheta):=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}[\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)];

cf. Lemma
8.5
; Definitions
2.12
,
2.18
and
8.7
; and the proof of
Lemma
8.9
. Next, assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
are such that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
induces
data on the singularity. This means that the mean curvature
\theta
diverges to
\infty
to the past. Moreover, the expansion normalised Weingarten
map
\mathcal{K}
converges to
\mathrm{Id}/3
, so that, by the asymptotic constraint,
\phi_{t}^{2}/\theta^{2}
has to converge to
2/3
. Combining
this observation with (
3.1c
), it follows that

\theta_{t}/\theta^{2}\rightarrow-1
. From the latter observation, it follows that
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past and
that
\theta
is not integrable to the past. Combining these observations with Lemma
3.4
, it follows that

\theta
is neither integrable to the future nor to the past. It is therefore
possible to introduce
\tau
by the requirement that if
t_{0}
corresponds to the initial hypersurface, then
\tau(t_{0})=0
and
\tau
satisfies
(
3.27
). The range of
\tau
then includes
(-\infty,0]
. Let
\eta_{0}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)
be such that it arises from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
;
cf. Remark
2.17
. Finally, let
\xi_{0}:=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}^{-1}(\eta_{0})
. Due to the uniqueness part of the proof of
Theorem
1.45
, it follows that
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{0})
corresponds to an admissible convergent solution to
(
3.31
)–(
3.39
); see Definition
8.1
. In order to justify this statement, note first that
the existence interval (in
\tau
-time) is unbounded to the past. Moreover,
\phi_{1}
and the
\Sigma_{i}

converge due to the assumption that the development induces data on the singularity. In addition, (
9.12
) holds,
so that
\theta(\tau)\geq-3\tau-C_{\theta}
for
\tau\leq T
. Finally, since all the
N_{i}
vanish, it is clear that condition (i) of
Definition
8.1
is satisfied.

Due to Lemma
8.13
, there is an open neighbourhood of
\xi_{0}
in
\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\ln\vartheta)
, say
U
, which leads to
admissible solutions. Moreover, the solutions are such that
\phi_{t}^{2}/\theta^{2}
converges to
2/3
. As above, this means that
\theta

diverges to
\infty
to the past (in finite time) and that
\theta
is not integrable to the past. Combining these observations with
Lemma
8.15
, it follows that if
\mathfrak{I}\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
and
\eta\in\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{P}}(U)
arises from

\mathfrak{I}
, then
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
induces data on the singularity. The subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}(\vartheta)
that gives rise to data on the
singularity, say
W
, is therefore open, so that
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
is an open subset of

{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{nd}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V](\vartheta)
.

That
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
8.16
, keeping
Remark
8.19
in mind. In order to prove that
it is a diffeomorphism onto its image, it is sufficient to prove injectivity. Assume, to this end, that

\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}(x_{2})
. This means
that if
[\mathfrak{I}_{i}]=x_{i}
,
i=1,2
, then the data induced on the singularity by
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
are isometric. Denote the isometry between
the data on the singularity by
\chi
. Due to Proposition
1.50
,
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is then an isometry between
the developments
\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I}_{i})
. In particular,
\chi
induces an isometry between
\mathfrak{I}_{i}
,
i=1,2
(in order to obtain this conclusion,
we use the monotonicity of the mean curvature obtained in Lemma
3.8
). This means that
x_{1}=x_{2}
and
the global injectivity of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta}
follows.

Next, assume that (
1.22
) holds. Due to Lemma
3.8
, we know that

\theta_{t}\leq 0
and that the zeros of
\theta_{t}
are isolated. This means that there is a
\vartheta_{3}>\vartheta_{i}
,
i=1,2
, such
that developments corresponding to the isometry classes of developments induced by the
x_{i}
have hypersurfaces of the form
G_{i}\times\{t\}

with mean curvature
\vartheta_{3}
and
\theta_{t}<0
(which is equivalent to the normal derivative of the scalar field being non-zero). Call
the corresponding isometry classes of initial data
x_{3,i}
. Then
\iota_{\vartheta_{3}}(x_{3,1})=\iota_{\vartheta_{3}}(x_{3,2})
, so that

x_{3,2}=x_{3,1}
due to Lemma
1.65
. Thus
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
.
The surjectivity of
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
follows from Theorem
1.45
. That
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}_{\mathrm{qu}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
is an open subset
of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
and that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}
is a local diffeomorphism follows from Lemma
1.65
. In order
to prove injectivity, assume that
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}(y_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}(y_{2})
. We can assume that
y_{i}=\iota_{\vartheta_{i}}(x_{i})
and that

\vartheta_{2}\geq\vartheta_{1}
. Then
\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{1}}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{R}_{\mathrm{I},\mathrm{iso}}^{\vartheta_{2}}(x_{2})
. By arguments similar to the above, this equality
can be used to conclude that
y_{1}=y_{2}
. The proposition follows.
∎

Chapter 10
The
k=0
and
k=-1
FLRW solutions

In the present chapter, we discuss spatially homogeneous and isotropic solutions with vanishing or negative spatial curvature.

10.1
The isotropic Bianchi type I setting

As is clear from Remark
3.5
, isotropic Bianchi type I solutions need not have a crushing singularity. However, in the present section,
we provide conditions on
V
ensuring that this behaviour is non-generic. We also derive asymptotics that can be combined with
[
42
]
in order to
yield stability results. Due to Remark
3.5
, it is clear that
\theta^{2}(t)=3V\circ\phi(t)
is a problematic condition. The next
lemma illustrates that if
\theta
is slightly bigger than this, a crushing singularity occurs. In the following results, we use the terminology introduced in
Definition
1.94
.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-negative, and let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J
is the maximal existence interval.
Assume that there is a
t_{0}\in J
and a
\delta>0
such that
\theta^{2}(t)\geq 3(1+\delta)V\circ\phi(t)
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. Then, after a translation
in
t
, if necessary,

J=(0,\infty)
,
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow 0
,
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
and
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
. In particular, introducing

\tau
according to (
3.27
), the range of
\tau
is
\mathbb{R}
.

Assume, in addition to the above, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Let, moreover,
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}=:a
, where
a_{i}
is defined by
(
3.13
) and
\sigma_{ii}=0
in the present setting. Then, if
\alpha_{V}<\delta/(1+\delta)
, there are constants
\Phi_{0}
,

\Phi_{1}=\pm(2/3)^{1/2}
,
\alpha>0
and
C
such that

|\theta^{1/3}a-\alpha|+|\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta-\Phi_{0}|+|\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}-\Phi_{1}|\leq C\langle\ln\theta\rangle\theta^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}

(10.1)

for all
t\leq t_{0}
.

Remark 10.2
.

Given that all the assumptions of the lemma are satisfied, the spacetime metric on the universal covering space can be written

g=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\bar{g}_{0},

where
\bar{g}_{0}
is the standard flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Letting
\mathscr{H}:=\alpha^{2}\bar{g}_{0}
,
\mathscr{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
and
\bar{g}=a^{2}(t)\bar{g}_{0}
, it then follows from
the conclusions of the lemma that
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
satisfy the conditions of Definition
1.17
and that,
for all
v,w\in T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{3}
and all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}\bar{g}(\theta^{\mathcal{K}}v,\theta^{\mathcal{K}}w)=\mathscr{H}(v,w),\ \ \ \mathcal{K}=\mathscr{K},\ \ \ \lim_{t\downarrow 0}(\theta^{-1}\phi_{t})=\Phi_{1},\ \ \ \lim_{t\downarrow 0}(\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta)=\Phi_{0},\ \ \

where
\mathcal{K}=\mathrm{Id}/3
. In other words, the development induces data on the singularity.

Remark 10.3
.

In the isotropic setting, all the eigenvalues of the expansion normalised Weingarten map equal
1/3
. Imposing suitable conditions on the potential, the
estimate (
10.1
) can thus be used to prove that the development constructed in Proposition
1.31
(after taking a quotient by a
co-compact free and properly discontinuous subgroup of the isometry group of
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\bar{g}_{0})
) is a quiescent model solution in the sense of

[
42
, Definition 46]
. Moreover, for quiescent model solutions, stable big bang formation is demonstrated in
[
42
, Theorem 49]
.

Remark 10.4
.

Assuming
J=(t_{-},\infty)
, that
\theta>0
and that
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta(t)^{2}\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
, any
\alpha_{V}<1
is sufficient.
In fact, if
\alpha_{V}<1
, choose
\delta
large enough that
\alpha_{V}<\delta/(1+\delta)
. Choosing
t_{0}
close enough to
t_{-}
, we then have

\theta^{2}(t)\geq 3(1+\delta)V\circ\phi(t)
for all
t\leq t_{0}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
,
J=(t_{-},\infty)
. Moreover, combining (
3.1d
) with the assumptions yields

\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}+3V\circ\phi=\theta^{2}\geq 3(1+\delta)V\circ\phi

on
J_{-}:=(t_{-},t_{0}]
, so that
\phi_{t}^{2}\geq 2\delta V\circ\phi
on
J_{-}
. Combining this observation with (
3.1d
) again yields

\theta^{2}=\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}+3V\circ\phi\leq\tfrac{3}{2}(1+\delta^{-1})\phi_{t}^{2}.

Due to this estimate and (
3.1c
),

\theta_{t}\leq-\tfrac{\delta}{1+\delta}\theta^{2}

(10.2)

on
J_{-}
. Since
\theta>0
on
J
, this means that
\theta
explodes in finite time to the past. Since we already know that solutions exist globally
to the future, see Lemma
3.4
, we can assume that
J=(0,\infty)
. The statements concerning the lack of integrability
of
\theta
follow from Lemma
3.4
.

Next, note that
|\phi_{\tau}|\leq\sqrt{6}
due to (
3.1d
) and the non-negativity of
V
. Combining this estimate with the fact that

V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
yields
|V(\phi(\tau))|\leq Ce^{6\alpha_{V}|\tau|}
for all
\tau\leq 0
. On the other hand, due to (
10.2
), there
is a constant
c_{a}>0
such that

\theta(\tau)\geq c_{a}\exp\big{(}-\tfrac{3\delta}{1+\delta}\tau\big{)}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining the last two estimates yields

\tfrac{|V(\phi(\tau))|}{\theta(\tau)^{2}}\leq C\exp\big{[}6\big{(}\tfrac{\delta}{1+\delta}-\alpha_{V}\big{)}\tau\big{]}

(10.3)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. By assumption, the right hand side decays to zero exponentially as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. On the other hand, combining
(
3.1c
) and (
3.1d
) yields

\theta_{\tau}=-\left(3-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\right)\theta.

(10.4)

This means that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)
converges exponentially to a non-zero constant
\theta_{\infty}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Returning to the above arguments
with this information at hand yields the following improvement of (
10.3
):

\tfrac{|V(\phi(\tau))|}{\theta(\tau)^{2}}\leq C\exp\left[6\left(1-\alpha_{V}\right)\tau\right]

(10.5)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Returning to (
10.4
) then yields

|e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)-\theta_{\infty}|\leq C\exp\left[6\left(1-\alpha_{V}\right)\tau\right]

(10.6)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Next, note that
a_{\tau}/a=1
. Thus
a(\tau)=a(0)e^{\tau}
. Thus

|(\theta^{1/3}a)(\tau)-\theta_{\infty}^{1/3}a(0)|\leq C\exp\left[6\left(1-\alpha_{V}\right)\tau\right]\leq C[\theta(\tau)]^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Next, note that due to (
3.1d
),

|\phi_{\tau}^{2}(\tau)-6|\leq C\exp\left[6\left(1-\alpha_{V}\right)\tau\right]

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Thus there is a
\Phi_{1}=\pm\sqrt{2/3}
such that

|\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}-\Phi_{1}|\leq C\theta^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Integrating this estimate yields a real number
\Phi_{0}
such that

|\phi+\Phi_{1}\ln\theta-\Phi_{0}|\leq C\theta^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}

for all
\tau\leq 0
. The lemma follows.
∎

Next, we discuss the case of a bounded potential.

Corollary 10.5
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be non-negative and bounded, and let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J
is the maximal existence interval. Then
there are two possibilities. Either all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
hold; or
J=\mathbb{R}
,
\theta(t)\leq[3\sup_{s}V(s)]^{1/2}

for all
t
and there is a
\theta_{\infty}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\theta(t)\rightarrow\theta_{\infty}
as
t\rightarrow-\infty

Proof.

Assume that there is a
t_{0}\in J
such that
\theta(t_{0})>[3\sup_{s}V(s)]^{1/2}
. Since
\theta
is increasing to the past, see
(
3.1c
), this means that
\theta(t)\geq\theta(t_{0})>[3\sup_{s}V(s)]^{1/2}
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. Combining this observation with
(
3.1d
) yields

\tfrac{3}{2}\phi_{t}^{2}(t)=\theta^{2}(t)-3V\circ\phi(t)\geq\theta^{2}(t_{0})-3\sup_{s}V(s)=:\alpha

for all
t\leq t_{0}
, where the last equality defines the constant
\alpha>0
. Combining this observation with (
3.1c
) yields
the conclusion that
\theta_{t}(t)\leq-\alpha
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. Assuming that the solution exists for all
t\leq t_{0}
, we conclude that

\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\rightarrow-\infty
. Since
V
is bounded, this means that
V\circ\phi(t)/\theta^{2}(t)\rightarrow 0

as
t\rightarrow-\infty
. Combining this observation with Lemma
10.1
, it follows that all the conclusions
of Lemma
10.1
hold; in particular, the solution does not exist for all
t\leq t_{0}
. We can thus assume that
the solution does not exhibit past global existence. Thus
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past; cf.
Remark
1.32
. This means that all the conclusion of Lemma
10.1
hold.

Next, assume that
\theta(t)\leq[3\sup_{s}V(s)]^{1/2}
for all
t\in J
. Then
\theta
converges to a finite number
\theta_{\infty}
to the past
due to (
3.1c
).
∎

By Lemma
10.1
, the condition that
\theta^{2}(t)\geq 3(1+\delta)V\circ\phi(t)
for all
t\leq t_{0}
is sufficient
to guarantee the desired asymptotics, assuming a suitable bound on
V
. The advantage of this result is that it illustrates that while the equality

\theta^{2}(t)=3V\circ\phi(t)
is problematic (in that it is compatible with solutions that do not have a crushing singularity), a slightly bigger
mean curvature leads to a crushing singularity. The disadvantage is that the result is based on assumptions concerning the solution for all
t\leq t_{0}
.
For this reason, we next prove a result which is only based on assumptions concerning the initial data.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Fix a
\Phi_{1}=\pm\sqrt{2/3}
and a
\Phi_{0}
. Then there is a constant
c_{1}>1
depending
continuously and exclusively on the constant
c_{0}
(appearing in Definition
1.44
),
\alpha_{V}
and an upper bound on
|\Phi_{0}|
, such that
the following holds: If

\theta(t_{0})>c_{1}\ \ and\ \ \phi(t_{0})=-\Phi_{1}\ln\theta(t_{0})+\Phi_{0},

then
\theta^{2}(t_{0})-3V\circ\phi(t_{0})>0
, so that

\phi_{t}(t_{0}):=\pm\big{(}\tfrac{2}{3}\big{)}^{1/2}\left(\theta^{2}(t_{0})-3V\circ\phi(t_{0})\right)^{1/2}

(10.7)

is well defined, where the sign is chosen so that
\phi_{t}(t_{0})
and
\Phi_{1}
have the same sign. Moreover, if
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and

\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
constitute the maximal isotropic Bianchi type I solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to
the initial data
\theta(t_{0})
,
\phi(t_{0})
and
\phi_{t}(t_{0})
, then

\lim_{t\downarrow t_{-}}\frac{V\circ\phi(t)}{\theta^{2}(t)}=0,

(10.8)

where
t_{-}
is the left endpoint of
J
. Finally, all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
hold.

Remark 10.7
.

The set of initial data satisfying the conditions is an open subset of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

Proof.

Estimate, appealing to (
1.9
),

\tfrac{V\circ\phi(t_{0})}{\theta^{2}(t_{0})}\leq c_{0}\exp\big{(}-2(1-\alpha_{V})\ln\theta(t_{0})+\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\Phi_{0}|\big{)}.

Assuming
c_{1}
to be large enough, the bound depending continuously and exclusively on
c_{0}
,
\alpha_{V}
and an upper bound on
|\Phi_{0}|
, we can
assume the right hand side to be as small as we wish.

Next, we change time coordinate according to (
3.27
). Due to Lemma
3.4
, the existence time in
\tau
-time
is
\mathbb{R}
. Define
\tau
so that
\tau(t_{0})=0
. Expressing everything with respect to
\tau
-time from now on (so that we write
\theta(0)
instead
of
\theta(t_{0})
, for example), define

\mathcal{A}:=\{\tau\leq 0:\ln\theta(s)\geq-3s+\ln\theta(0)-1\ \forall s\in[\tau,0]\}.

Clearly,
0\in\mathcal{A}
. It is also clear that
\mathcal{A}
is closed and connected. It remains to prove that
\mathcal{A}
is open. Let
\tau_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Since
|\phi_{\tau}|\leq\sqrt{6}
,

|\phi(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}|\tau|+(2/3)^{1/2}\ln\theta(0)+|\Phi_{0}|

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this estimate with (
1.9
) and the assumption that
\tau_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
yields

\begin{split}\tfrac{V\circ\phi(\tau)}{\theta^{2}(\tau)}\leq&c_{0}\exp\left(6\tau-2\ln\theta(0)+2-6\alpha_{V}\tau+2\alpha_{V}\ln\theta(0)+\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\Phi_{0}|\right)\\
\leq&Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}[\theta(0)]^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}\end{split}

(10.9)

for all
\tau\in[\tau_{1},0]
, where
C
depends continuously and exclusively on
c_{0}
and an upper bound on
|\Phi_{0}|
. Next, note that, due to
(
3.1c
) and (
3.1d
),

\partial_{\tau}\ln\theta=-\tfrac{9}{2\theta^{2}}\phi_{t}^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\tau}^{2}=-3+\tfrac{9V\circ\phi(\tau)}{\theta^{2}(\tau)}.

Integrating this equality from
\tau_{1}
to
0
, keeping (
10.9
) in mind, yields

\ln\theta(0)-\ln\theta(\tau_{1})\leq 3\tau_{1}+C[\theta(0)]^{-2(1-\alpha_{V})}.

Assuming
c_{1}
to be large enough, the bound depending continuously and exclusively on
c_{0}
,
\alpha_{V}
and an upper bound on
|\Phi_{0}|
, it follows
that
\ln\theta(\tau_{1})\geq-3\tau_{1}+\ln\theta(0)-1/2
. This means that
\mathcal{A}
is open, so that
\mathcal{A}=(-\infty,0]
.
Next, fix
\delta>0
large enough that
\alpha_{V}<\delta/(1+\delta)
. Then, due to (
10.9
), there is a
t_{0}\in J

such that
\theta^{2}(t)\geq 3(1+\delta)V\circ\phi(t)
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. This means that all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1

hold. The lemma follows.
∎

Assume that
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Then there are open conditions on initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
ensuring that the
conclusions of Lemmas
10.1

and
10.6
hold. In particular, the corresponding solutions induce data on the singularity in the
sense of Definition
1.17
; see Remark
10.2
. However, in order to obtain a more complete picture
of the dynamics, we here impose stronger conditions on the potential. To begin with, we characterise the case that the mean curvature remains bounded
to the past.

Proposition 10.8
.

Assume
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to be non-negative and such that if
V^{\prime}(s)=0
, then
V^{\prime\prime}(s)\neq 0
. Assume, moreover, that

V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J
is the maximal existence
interval. If
\theta
is bounded, then
J=\mathbb{R}
and there are
\theta_{\infty},\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\theta(t)=\theta_{\infty},\ \ \ \lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\phi(t)=\phi_{\infty},\ \ \ \lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\phi_{t}(t)=0.

Moreover,
V^{\prime}(\phi_{\infty})=0
, so that
(\theta_{\infty},\phi_{\infty},0)
is a fixed point of the system. In addition,
V(\phi_{\infty})>0
,

V^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{\infty})<0
and there are two solutions (up to time translations) in the set
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
converging to the fixed point,
each of which is a submanifold of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

Remark 10.9
.

Since
V^{\prime}
has at most countably many zeros, the set of initial data corresponding to solutions with bounded mean curvature is contained in a
countable union of codimension one submanifolds. In other words, the developments that do not have a crushing singularity correspond to a
Baire and Lebesgue non-generic set of initial data.

Proof.

Since
\theta
is bounded to the past, the solution exists globally to the past; cf. Remark
1.32
. Since

V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
, the equality (
3.1d
) implies that
\phi
and
\phi_{t}
are bounded to the
past. Moreover,
\phi_{t}^{2}
is integrable to the past due to (
3.1c
) and the fact that
\theta
is bounded to the past. Due to the
boundedness of
\theta
,
\phi_{t}
and
\phi
, we conclude that
\phi_{tt}
is bounded to the past, see
(
3.4
). Combining this observation with the integrability of
\phi_{t}^{2}
to the past leads to the conclusion that
\phi_{t}

converges to zero. Since
\theta
converges to a limit, say
\theta_{\infty}>0
to the past, it follows that

\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}V[\phi(t)]=\theta_{\infty}^{2}/3.

(10.10)

Combining this observation with the fact that
\phi
is bounded and the fact that
V^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
when
V^{\prime}=0
, it follows that
\phi
converges
to a limit, say
\phi_{\infty}
. Thus, due to (
3.4
),
\phi_{tt}
converges to
V^{\prime}(\phi_{\infty})
. In order for this to be consistent,

V^{\prime}(\phi_{\infty})
has to vanish. In other words, the solution converges to a fixed point of the system. Note also that
V(\phi_{\infty})>0
due to
(
10.10
) and the fact that
\theta_{\infty}>0
. Rewriting (
3.1
)–(
3.4
), close to the fixed point, as an
unconstrained second order equation for only
\phi
, we conclude that if
V^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{\infty})>0
, then both eigenvalues of the linearisation of the
corresponding first order system have negative real parts. This means that no solutions can converge to this fixed point to the past, except for
the fixed point itself. However, this possibility has been excluded by the definition of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.
In case
V^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{\infty})<0
, the linearisation has one negative and one positive eigenvalue. This means that there is a
one dimensional stable manifold (going into the past; i.e., for the time reversed flow). In order for the solution to converge to the fixed point,
it therefore, for
t
close enough to
-\infty
, has to belong to the stable manifold. Since we have excluded the possibility that the solution
coincides with the fixed point, there are thus two possibilities: the solution converges to the fixed point along
\pm v_{+}
, where
v_{+}
is an
eigenvector corresponding to the positive eigenvalue. Each of these possibilities corresponds to a unique (up to time translation) solution to the
equations. Finally, we wish to prove that these solutions constitute submanifolds of the state space
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
. In order to prove this, note
first that if
\theta_{t}(t_{0})=0
, then

\phi_{t}(t_{0})=0
. If, in addition,
V^{\prime}(\phi(t_{0}))=0
, then
(\theta(t_{0}),\phi(t_{0}),\phi_{t}(t_{0}))
is a fixed point, contradicting the assumption that
the solution is non-constant. In other words, if
\theta_{t}(t_{0})=0
, then we have to have
\phi_{tt}(t_{0})\neq 0
, so that
\theta_{t}<0
in a punctured
neighbourhood of
t_{0}
. Next, we know that the solution curve is a smooth immersion. In order to prove that it is an embedding, note that the map taking

t\in\mathbb{R}
to
(\theta(t),\phi(t),\phi_{t}(t))
is injective, since
\theta
is strictly monotonically decreasing (it is of course also surjective onto its
image and continuous). In order to prove that the inverse is continuous, let
(\theta_{k},\phi_{0,k},\phi_{1,k})
be elements of the image convering
to
(\theta_{*},\phi_{0,*},\phi_{1,*})
in the image. Then, since
\theta
is strictly monotonic, the
t_{k}
corresponding to

(\theta_{k},\phi_{0,k},\phi_{1,k})
have to converge to a limit, say
t_{*}
. Moreover,
t_{*}
has to correspond to
(\theta_{*},\phi_{0,*},\phi_{1,*})
, again
due to the monotonicity of
\theta
. Thus the solution is a smooth embedding of
\mathbb{R}
into
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
. Thus the solution is a submanifold of

B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.
∎

Next, we consider the case that
\theta
is unbounded.

Proposition 10.10
.

Assume
0\leq V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a solution to
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J=(t_{-},\infty)
is the maximal existence interval.
If
\theta
is unbounded, then
|\phi(t)|\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
3.4
, we know that
\theta
is neither integrable to the future nor to the past. Thus, by introducing
\tau

according to (
3.27
), the range of
\tau
is
\mathbb{R}
.
In order to prove that
|\phi|
has to
converge to
\infty
, assume that it does not. This means that there is a sequence
\{\tau_{k}\}
with
\tau_{k}\rightarrow-\infty
such that

\phi(\tau_{k})
is bounded. In fact, we can assume
\phi(\tau_{k})
to converge to, say,
\phi_{\infty}
. Fix
\epsilon>0
. Given
k
, let

\mathcal{A}_{k}:=\{\tau\leq\tau_{k}:\phi_{\tau}^{2}(s)/2\geq 3-\epsilon\ \forall s\in[\tau,\tau_{k}]\}.

Note that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is closed, connected and, if
k
is large enough, non-empty. The last statement is due to the fact that

3=\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\tau}^{2}+\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}},

(10.11)

see (
3.1d
), the fact that
V\circ\phi(\tau_{k})\rightarrow V(\phi_{\infty})
and the fact that
\theta(\tau_{k})\rightarrow\infty
. Next, we
wish to prove that if
k
is large enough, then
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is an open subset of
(-\infty,\tau_{k}]
. Let, to this end,
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
. Then

\partial_{\tau}\theta(s)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\tau}^{2}(s)\theta(s)\leq-(3-\epsilon)\theta(s)

for all
s\in[\tau,\tau_{k}]
, so that
\theta(s)\geq\theta(\tau_{k})e^{(3-\epsilon)(\tau_{k}-s)}
for all
s\in[\tau,\tau_{k}]
. On the other hand,

|\phi(s)|\leq\sqrt{6}(\tau_{k}-s)+|\phi(\tau_{k})|

for all
s\in[\tau,\tau_{k}]
. Combining the above observations

\tfrac{9V\circ\phi(s)}{\theta^{2}(s)}\leq\tfrac{9c_{0}e^{\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}|\phi(\tau_{k})|}}{\theta(\tau_{k})^{2}}e^{-6(1-\epsilon/3-\alpha_{V})(\tau_{k}-s)}

(10.12)

for all
s\in[\tau,\tau_{k}]
. Assume
\epsilon>0
to be small enough that
1-\epsilon/3-\alpha_{V}>0
. Assume, moreover,
k
to be large enough that
the first factor on the left hand side of (
10.12
) is bounded from above by
\epsilon/2
. Then
s\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
implies that

\phi_{\tau}^{2}(s)/2\geq 3-\epsilon/2
and
\mathcal{A}_{k}
contains a neighbourhood of
s
. In other words,
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is open, so that
\mathcal{A}_{k}=(-\infty,\tau_{k}]
.
This means that
V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
decays exponentially. This means that Lemma
10.1
and
Remark
10.4
apply. In particular,
|\phi(t)|\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
.
∎

It is natural to conjecture that generic solutions have asymptotics corresponding to the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
.
In the case of an exponential potential, this follows from Proposition
10.15
below. Proving it for
general potentials is more difficult. However, we prove one result of this nature in Theorem
10.17
below. This theorem
rests on the following proposition.

Proposition 10.11
.

Assume
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and that there is an
M\geq 1
and an
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
such that
V(s)>0
for all
|s|\geq M
;

|(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)|\leq\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}

(10.13)

for all
|s|\geq M
; and

\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}(s)=0.

(10.14)

Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding
to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J
is the maximal existence interval. Assume that
\theta
is unbounded to the past. Then either
all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
hold, or

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow-\infty}\left[\phi_{\tau}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}[\phi(\tau)]\right]=0.

(10.15)

Remark 10.12
.

If
V
is asymptotically a polynomial or an exponential function, then (
10.14
) is satisfied.

Remark 10.13
.

It is reasonable to expect (
10.15
) to correspond to an exceptional situation.

Remark 10.14
.

If we replace the assumptions that
V(s)>0
and that (
10.13
) hold for
|s|\geq M
with the assumptions that these estimates hold only for

s\geq M
and the assumption that
V
is bounded for
s\leq 0
; and if we replace the assumption that (
10.14
) holds with the assumption
that it only holds as
s\rightarrow\infty
, then the same conclusions hold. Finally, if
\bar{V}(s)=V(-s)
satisfies these modified conditions, then the
same conclusions hold.

Proof.

Due to (
10.13
),
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{0}
. Due to Lemma
3.4
, we know that
\theta
is neither
integrable to the future nor to the past. Thus, introducing
\tau
according to (
3.27
), the range of
\tau
is
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,

|\phi(\tau)|\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
due to Proposition
10.10
. Note that

\phi_{\tau\tau}=-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\phi_{\tau}-\tfrac{9V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}=-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\big{(}\phi_{\tau}+\tfrac{V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{V\circ\phi}\big{)}=-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\big{(}\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi\big{)}.

(10.16)

This means that

\begin{split}\partial_{\tau}\big{(}\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi\big{)}=&-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\big{(}\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi\big{)}+(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi\cdot\phi_{\tau}\\
=&-\big{(}\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}-(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi\big{)}\big{(}\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi\big{)}\\
&-(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi.\end{split}

(10.17)

Let
\epsilon>0
and
\beta_{V}:=2(1-\alpha_{V}^{2})
. Assuming
\epsilon
to be small enough, the bound depending only on
\alpha_{V}
;
\tau
to be such that
|\phi(\tau)|\geq M
;
and the estimate
|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)|\leq\epsilon
to hold, it follows that
9V\circ\phi(\tau)/\theta^{2}(\tau)\geq\beta_{V}
; cf.
(
10.11
) and (
10.13
). Let
T
be close enough to
-\infty
that

\big{|}(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)\big{|}\leq\tfrac{\beta_{V}}{2},\ \ \ \big{|}(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)\big{|}\leq\tfrac{\epsilon\beta_{V}}{4}

and
|\phi(\tau)|\geq M

for all
\tau\leq T
. This means that if
|\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi|=\epsilon
, then the absolute value of the first term on the far
right hand side of (
10.17
) is bounded from below by
\epsilon\beta_{V}/2
. Moreover, the absolute value of the second
term on the far right hand side is bounded from above by
\epsilon\beta_{V}/4
. Since the first term has the opposite sign of

\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi
, it is clear that if
|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)|=\epsilon
for some
\tau\leq T
, then

|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)|\geq\epsilon
for all
\tau\leq T
. To conclude, either (
10.15
) holds or there is
an
\epsilon>0
and a
T
such that
|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)|\geq\epsilon
for all
\tau\leq T
. In the latter case, it follows from
(
10.16
) that
|\phi_{\tau\tau}|\geq 9\epsilon V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
for all
\tau\leq T
. Since we can assume
V(\phi(\tau))>0

for
\tau\leq T
, this means that
\phi_{\tau}
is strictly monotonic for
\tau\leq T
. This means that
\phi_{\tau}
converges to a limit. If this
limit is different from
\pm\sqrt{6}
, it follows that
9V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
converges to a non-zero limit; see (
10.11
). Since

|\phi_{\tau\tau}|\geq 9\epsilon V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
for all
\tau\leq T
, this means that
|\phi_{\tau}|
tends to infinity, a conclusion which is
inconsistent with (
10.11
). Thus
V\circ\phi(\tau)/\theta^{2}(\tau)
converges to zero as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
and all the
conclusions of Lemma
10.1
hold. The proposition follows.

In order to prove Remark
10.14
, note that we know that
|\phi(\tau)|\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.
If
\phi(\tau)\rightarrow-\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, then
V\circ\phi
is bounded to the past, so that

V\circ\phi(\tau)/\theta^{2}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Thus all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
hold.
If
\phi(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
, then the first part of the proof applies (alternately, one can modify the potential
V(s)

for
s\leq 0
so that the conditions of the proposition apply).
∎

As mentioned, we expect (
10.15
) to correspond to an exceptional situation. Next, we verify that this is the case when the
potential is of the form
V(s)=V_{0}e^{\lambda s}
.

Proposition 10.15
.

Let
0<V_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<\lambda<\sqrt{6}
. Define
V(s):=V_{0}e^{\lambda s}
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})

be a solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J
is the maximal existence
interval. Then
J
can be assumed to equal
(0,\infty)
and introducing
\tau
as in (
3.27
), the range of
\tau
is
\mathbb{R}
. Finally,
there are two possibilities concerning the asymptotics as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Either

\phi_{\tau}=-\lambda
for the entire solution or all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
apply. Moreover, there is a
unique solution satisfying
\phi_{\tau}=-\lambda
.

Remark 10.16
.

Since
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
is two dimensional, the condition
\phi_{\tau}=-\lambda
is non-generic.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
3.4
,
\theta(t)>0
for all
t
and
\theta
is neither integrable to the future nor to the past. By
introducing
\tau
according to (
3.27
), the range of
\tau
is thus
\mathbb{R}
. Next, combining (
3.1d
) and
(
10.16
) yields

\partial_{\tau}\phi_{\tau}=-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}(\phi_{\tau}+\lambda)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\phi_{\tau}-\sqrt{6})(\phi_{\tau}+\sqrt{6})(\phi_{\tau}+\lambda).

(10.18)

In particular,
\phi_{\tau}
converges to a limit belonging to
\{\pm\sqrt{6},-\lambda\}
. Due to (
3.1c
), there is thus a constant
\beta>0
and
a
T\in J
such that
\theta_{t}\leq-\beta\theta^{2}
for all
t\in J
with
t\leq T
. This means that
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past, so
that
J
can be assumed to equal

(0,\infty)
. Next, note that if
\phi_{\tau}
converges to
\pm\sqrt{6}
, then all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
apply. What
remains to be considered is thus that
\phi_{\tau}
asymptotes to
-\lambda
. However, if
\phi_{\tau}\in(-\sqrt{6},-\lambda)
, then
\phi_{\tau\tau}>0

(meaning that
\phi_{\tau}
decreases to the past), and if
\phi_{\tau}\in(-\lambda,\sqrt{6})
, then
\phi_{\tau\tau}<0
(meaning the
\phi_{\tau}
increases to
the past). In order for
\phi_{\tau}
to asymptote to
-\lambda
to the past, we thus need to have

\phi_{\tau}=-\lambda
for the entire solution. Then
\phi(\tau)=-\lambda\tau+\phi_{0}
for some constant
\phi_{0}
. However, by
a time translation, we can assume
\phi_{0}=0
. This means that
\ln\theta(\tau)=-\lambda^{2}\tau/2+\ln\theta_{0}
for some constant
\theta_{0}>0
.
Finally, in order for the constraint to be satisfied, we have to have
3=\lambda^{2}/2+9\theta_{0}^{-2}V_{0}
.
This means that there is, up to time translation, a unique solution with
\phi_{\tau}=-\lambda
. The proposition follows.
∎

Theorem 10.17
.

Assume
0\leq V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and that there are constants
C_{V}
and
M
such that
V(s)>0
and

\left|\left(\ln V\right)^{\prime\prime}(s)\right|\leq C_{V}\langle s\rangle^{-2}

(10.19)

for all
|s|\geq M
. This means that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)
converges to limits as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
. Call the limits
\lambda_{\pm}
and assume
that
-\sqrt{6}<\lambda_{-}<0
and that
0<\lambda_{+}<\sqrt{6}
. Let
\theta\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J,\mathbb{R})
be a
solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, where
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
is the maximal
existence interval. Assuming that
\theta
is unbounded, there are the following, mutually exclusive, cases:

(i)

The solution is such that (
10.15
) holds and
\phi(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\rightarrow t_{-}
. Up to
time translation, there is exactly one such solution, and its image is a smooth submanifold of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

(ii)

The solution is such that (
10.15
) holds and
\phi(t)\rightarrow-\infty
as
t\rightarrow t_{-}
. Up to
time translation, there is exactly one such solution, and its image is a smooth submanifold of
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
.

(iii)

All the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
apply.

In particular, excluding two exceptional solutions (which are also codimension one submanifolds), initial data in
B^{\mathrm{iso}}_{\mathrm{I},+}
, whose developments
have crushing singularities, give rise to solutions such that all the conclusions of Lemma
10.1
apply.

Remark 10.18
.

If we replace the assumptions that
V(s)>0
and that (
10.19
) hold for
|s|\geq M
with the assumption that these estimates hold only for

s\geq M
and the assumption that
V
is bounded for
s\leq 0
; and if we remove the assumption that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)\rightarrow\lambda_{-}
as

s\rightarrow-\infty
, then either
(i)
or
(iii)
have to hold. Finally, if
\bar{V}(s)=V(-s)
satisfies these modified conditions,
then either
(ii)
or
(iii)
have to hold.

Proof.

Increasing
M
appearing in the statement of the theorem, if necessary, there is an
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
such that (
10.13
) and
(
10.14
) hold. This means that the conditions of Propositions
10.10
and
10.11
are satisfied.
We can therefore assume that (
10.15
) holds and that
|\phi(\tau)|\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Due to
(
10.15
),
\phi_{\tau}(\tau)
converges
to either
-\lambda_{+}
or to
-\lambda_{-}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In the first case,
\phi(\tau)
tends to
\infty
(essentially linearly),
and in the second case, it tends to
-\infty
(essentially linearly) as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In particular, there is a time
T
and a constant

C\geq 1
such that
|\phi(\tau)|\geq M
and

C^{-1}\langle\tau\rangle\leq\langle\phi(\tau)\rangle\leq C\langle\tau\rangle

for all
\tau\leq T
. Combining this estimate with (
10.19
) yields

\left|(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi(\tau)\right|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}

for all
\tau\leq T
. Next, since
\phi_{\tau}(\tau)
converges to
-\lambda_{\pm}
, it follows that
9V\circ\phi(\tau)/\theta^{2}(\tau)
converges
to
3-\lambda_{\pm}^{2}/2
; see (
10.11
). Moreover, due to (
10.13
),
|\lambda_{\pm}|\leq\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}
. This means
that (
10.17
) can be written

\psi_{\tau}=-a\psi+b,

where
a
is a function converging to
3-\lambda_{\pm}^{2}/2>0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
and
|b(\tau)|\leq C_{b}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}
for all
\tau\leq T

and some constant
C_{b}
. Moreover,
\psi=\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi
. Since we know
\psi
to converge to zero and since we can assume

a(\tau)\geq\alpha
for all
\tau\leq T
, where
0<\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, this means that
|\psi(\tau)|\leq\alpha^{-1}C_{b}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}
for all
\tau\leq T
.

A model for the asymptotics.
Next, we introduce a model for the asymptotic behaviour:

\xi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\xi=0.

(10.20)

There is a unique (up to time translation) solution to this equation diverging to
-\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, and there is a unique
(up to time translation) solution diverging to
\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. In order to justify the first statement, let
\xi_{0}
be close
enough to
-\infty
that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)\leq\lambda_{-}/2
for all
s\leq\xi_{0}
. Consider the solution to (
10.20
) with
\xi(0)=\xi_{0}
.
Since
\xi_{\tau}(\tau)\geq-\lambda_{-}/2>0
if
\xi(\tau)\leq\xi_{0}
, it is clear that
\xi
decreases to the past, so that
\xi(\tau)\leq\xi_{0}
for all

\tau\leq 0
. Since
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)
converges to
\lambda_{-}
as
s\rightarrow-\infty
, it is clear that
\xi_{\tau}
is bounded for
\tau\leq 0
. This
means that
\xi
exists globally to the past. This yields existence of the desired solution. In order to prove uniqueness (up to time translation),
let
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
be two solutions. Then there are
M_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
i=1,2
, such that the range of
\xi_{i}
contains
(-\infty,M_{i}]
. Let

M_{3}\leq\min\{M_{1},M_{2}\}
. Then there are
\tau_{i}
,
i=1,2
, in the existence intervals of
\xi_{i}
such that
\xi_{i}(\tau_{i})=M_{3}
. By uniqueness of
solutions to (
10.20
), it follows that
\xi_{1}(\tau)=\xi_{2}(\tau+\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})
. In other words, we have uniqueness up to
time translation. The proof of the statement that there is a unique (up to time translation) solution diverging to
\infty
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
is similar.

Fix a solution
\xi
to (
10.20
) converging to
-\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Let
\max\{1,M\}\leq n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be such that

-n
belongs to the range of
\xi
for
n\geq n_{0}
. Assume, moreover, that
(\ln V)^{\prime}(s)\leq\lambda_{-}/2
for all
s\leq-n_{0}
and note that
V(s)>0
for

s\leq-n_{0}
. Next, let
\beta_{V}:=(\alpha_{V}+1)/2
and assume, in addition to the above,
n_{0}
to be such that

C_{V}\langle n_{0}\rangle^{-2}\leq 2(1-\beta_{V}^{2})(1-\beta_{V})<1-\beta_{V}^{2}.

(10.21)

Let
\tau_{n}
be such that
\xi(\tau_{n})=-n
. Then
\tau_{n}>\tau_{n+1}
and
\tau_{n}\rightarrow-\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. By a time translation,
it can be arranged that
\tau_{n_{0}}=2n_{0}/\lambda_{-}
. Then, for
\tau\leq\tau_{n_{0}}
,

\xi(\tau_{n_{0}})-\xi(\tau)=-\textstyle{\int}_{\tau}^{\tau_{n_{0}}}(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\xi(s)ds\geq-\frac{\lambda_{-}}{2}(\tau_{n_{0}}-\tau)=-n_{0}+\frac{\lambda_{-}}{2}\tau.

Since
\xi(\tau_{n_{0}})=-n_{0}
, this means that

\xi(\tau)\leq-\lambda_{-}\tau/2

(10.22)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{n_{0}}
. In what follows, we use
\xi
as a scaffold for constructing a solution as in
(ii)
.

Existence of solutions.
We construct a solution as in
(ii)
by taking a limit of a sequence of solutions. Moreover, the sequence is
constructed by specifying the initial data at times closer and closer to
-\infty
to equal those of the model solution (scaffold) constructed previously.
More specifically, for
n\geq n_{0}
, let
\phi_{n}(\tau_{n}):=-n
and
(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{n}):=\xi_{\tau}(\tau_{n})
. Finally, define
\theta_{n}(\tau_{n})>0
by the
requirement that (
10.11
) hold. That this is possible follows from the fact that
(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{n})^{2}\leq 6\alpha_{V}^{2}<6
. Let

(\phi_{n},\theta_{n})
be the solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
) corresponding to these data. Since
\theta
is neither integrable to the
future or to the past, see Lemma
3.4
, the existence interval of the solution in
\tau
-time is
\mathbb{R}
. Next, let

\mathcal{A}_{n}:=\{\tau\geq\tau_{n}:\phi_{n}(s)\leq-n_{0},\ |(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(s)|\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}\ \forall s\in[\tau_{n},\tau]\}.

Note that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is closed, connected and non-emtpy (
\tau_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
).
Assume now that
\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})<-n_{0}
and that
\tau_{*}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
. If
|(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{*})|<\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
, it is clear that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
contains an
open neighbourhood of
\tau_{*}
in
[\tau_{n},\infty)
. Let us therefore assume that
|(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{*})|=\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
. Let us first prove that

(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{*})>0
. If not,
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}
has to have passed through
0
in the interval
[\tau_{n},\tau_{*}]
, since

(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{n})=\xi_{\tau}(\tau_{n})=-(\ln V)^{\prime}(-n)>0
. Say that
(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{a})=0
for
\tau_{n}\leq\tau_{a}\leq\tau_{*}
(so that

\tau_{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
) and that
\tau_{a}
is the first element of
[\tau_{n},\tau_{*}]
with this property. Then (
10.11
) and
(
10.16
) yield

(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\phi_{n})(\tau_{a})=-3(\ln V)^{\prime}[\phi_{n}(\tau_{a})]>0.

On the other hand, since
(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{a})=0
and
(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(s)\geq 0
for
s\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{a}]
, we have to have

(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\phi_{n})(\tau_{a})\leq 0
. This yields a contradiction. To conclude,
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}>0
in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. This means that

(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{*})=\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
. In particular,

\tfrac{9V\circ\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})}{\theta^{2}_{n}(\tau_{*})}=3(1-\beta_{V}^{2}),

see (
10.11
), and, due to (
10.13
),

\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})+(\ln V)^{\prime}[\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})]\geq\sqrt{6}(\beta_{V}-\alpha_{V})=\sqrt{6}(1-\alpha_{V})/2.

Combining these observations with (
10.16
) yields the conclusion that
\partial_{\tau}^{2}\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})<0
. Since
\tau_{*}>\tau_{n}
,

\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}(\tau)\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
for
\tau\in[\tau_{n},\tau_{*}]
and
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})=\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
, we, on the other hand, have

\partial_{\tau}^{2}\phi_{n}(\tau_{*})\geq 0
. This contradiction proves that
|(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n})(\tau_{*})|<\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
, so that
\mathcal{A}_{n}
contains an open
neighbourhood of
\tau_{*}
. In particular,
\phi_{n}
has to reach
-n_{0}
before
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}|
reaches
\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
. To conclude,

\phi_{n}
is strictly increasing from
-n
to
-n_{0}
as
\tau
increases. Moreover,
0<\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}<\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
in the corresponding interval.
Next, consider

h_{n}:=\phi_{n}^{2}\left(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\right).

Due to (
10.17
),

\partial_{\tau}h_{n}=-\big{(}\tfrac{9V\circ\phi_{n}}{\theta_{n}^{2}}-2\tfrac{\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}}{\phi_{n}}-(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\big{)}h_{n}-\phi_{n}^{2}\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}.

Note that
\phi_{n}\leq-n_{0}
and that
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}>0
in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. Thus
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}/\phi_{n}<0
on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. Moreover, due to
(
10.11
),

\tfrac{9V\circ\phi_{n}(\tau)}{\theta^{2}_{n}(\tau)}-(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi_{n}(\tau)\geq 3(1-\beta_{V}^{2})-C_{V}\langle n_{0}\rangle^{-2}>2(1-\beta_{V}^{2})

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
, where we appealed to (
10.19
) and (
10.21
) in the last two steps. Next,

\left|\phi_{n}^{2}\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\cdot(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\right|\leq\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}C_{V}.

Moreover,
h_{n}(\tau_{n})=0
by definition. Combining these estimates, it is clear that

|\phi_{n}^{2}(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n})|=|h_{n}|\leq\tfrac{\sqrt{6}C_{V}}{2(1-\beta_{V}^{2})}

in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. Denoting the right hand side by
C_{\phi}/2
, we conclude that

\left|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}\right|\leq C_{\phi}\langle\phi_{n}\rangle^{-2}

(10.23)

in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
(since
n_{0}\geq 1
). Next, let
\zeta_{n}:=\phi_{n}-\xi
and compute

\partial_{\tau}\zeta_{n}=\partial_{\tau}\phi_{n}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\xi-(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}.

(10.24)

The sum of the first two terms on the right hand side can be estimated by appealing to (
10.23
). Consider

(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\xi-(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{n}=-\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}\left(\ln V\right)^{\prime\prime}(s\xi+(1-s)\phi_{n})ds\cdot\zeta_{n}.

(10.25)

Combining the above observations, there are functions
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
such that

\partial_{\tau}\zeta_{n}=f_{n}\zeta_{n}+g_{n}.

(10.26)

Let
\mathcal{B}_{n}
be the set of
\tau\in\mathcal{A}_{n}\cap(-\infty,\tau_{n_{0}}]
such that
|\zeta_{n}(s)|\leq 2
for all
s\in[\tau_{n},\tau]
. Then there
is a constant
C_{f}^{\prime}
, depending only on
C_{V}
and
\alpha_{V}
, and a constant
C_{f}
, depending only on
C_{V}
,
\alpha_{V}
and
\lambda_{-}
, such that

|f_{n}(\tau)|+|g_{n}(\tau)|\leq C_{f}^{\prime}\langle\xi(\tau)\rangle^{-2}\leq C_{f}^{\prime}\langle-\lambda_{-}\tau/2\rangle^{-2}\leq C_{f}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, where we appealed to (
10.22
) in the second to last step. Estimate

\partial_{\tau}(\zeta_{n}^{2}+1)=2\zeta_{n}\partial_{\tau}\zeta_{n}=2f_{n}\zeta_{n}^{2}+2g_{n}\zeta_{n}\leq 2C_{f}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}(\zeta_{n}^{2}+1)

(10.27)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
. Since
\zeta_{n}(\tau_{n})=0
, this means that

\zeta_{n}(\tau)^{2}+1\leq\exp\left(\textstyle{\int}_{\tau_{n}}^{\tau}2C_{f}\langle s\rangle^{-2}ds\right).

Assuming
n_{0}
to be large enough, the bound depending only on
C_{V}
,
\alpha_{V}
and
\lambda_{-}
, we can assume the right hand side to be bounded
from above by
2
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{n_{0}}
. By a bootstrap argument, it can therefore be verified that
\mathcal{B}_{n}=\mathcal{A}_{n}\cap(-\infty,\tau_{n_{0}}]
.
Moreover,
|\zeta_{n}(\tau)|\leq 1
for all
\tau\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
Recall that, by definition,
\xi(\tau_{n_{0}})=-n_{0}
and that if
\sigma_{n}
is the right end point of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, then
\phi_{n}(\sigma_{n})=-n_{0}
.
If
\tau_{n_{0}}\leq\sigma_{n}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{n}=[\tau_{n},\tau_{n_{0}}]
. If
\sigma_{n}\leq\tau_{n_{0}}
, then

-1-n_{0}=-1+\phi_{n}(\sigma_{n})\leq\xi(\sigma_{n})\leq\xi(\tau_{n_{0}})=-n_{0}.

Thus

-\tfrac{\lambda_{-}}{2}(\tau_{n_{0}}-\sigma_{n})\leq\textstyle{\int}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\tau_{n_{0}}}\xi_{\tau}(s)ds=\xi(\tau_{n_{0}})-\xi(\sigma_{n})\leq 1,

so that
u_{n_{0}}\leq\sigma_{n}
, where
u_{n_{0}}:=\tau_{n_{0}}+2/\lambda_{-}
. This means that
\mathcal{C}_{n}:=[\tau_{n},\tau_{n_{0}}+2/\lambda_{-}]\subset\mathcal{B}_{n}
. For

k\geq n
,
\phi_{k}
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{C}_{n}
;
|\phi_{k}(\tau)|\leq 1+|\xi(\tau)|
. Moreover, since
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{k}|\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}

on
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, it is clear that
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{k}|
,
\theta_{k}
and
1/\theta_{k}
are uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{C}_{n}
; see
(
10.11
). Taking a subsequence, say
k_{l}
, it is clear that
\theta_{k_{l}}(u_{n_{0}})
,
\phi_{k_{l}}(u_{n_{0}})
and

(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{k_{l}})(u_{n_{0}})
converge as
l\rightarrow\infty
. Denote the limits by
\theta_{0,*}
,
\phi_{0,*}
and
\phi_{1,*}
. The limits satisfy
the constraint equation (
10.11
). Let
