(\theta_{*},\phi_{*})
be the solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.4
)
corresponding to the initial data
\theta_{0,*}
,
\phi_{0,*}
and
\phi_{1,*}
at
u_{n_{0}}
. Due to continuous dependence on initial data,

(\theta_{k_{l}},\phi_{k_{l}},\partial_{\tau}\phi_{k_{l}})
converges uniformly to
(\theta_{*},\phi_{*},\partial_{\tau}\phi_{*})
on any compact subset of
(-\infty,u_{n_{0}}]
.
Combining this observation with (
10.23
), it is clear that

\left|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{*}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{*}(\tau)\right|\leq C_{\phi}\langle\phi_{*}(\tau)\rangle^{-2}

for all
\tau\leq u_{n_{0}}
. Note also that
|\phi_{*}(\tau)-\xi(\tau)|\leq 1
for all
\tau\leq u_{n_{0}}
.

Uniqueness of solutions.

Next, we wish to prove that
\phi_{*}
is the unique (up to time translation) solution such that
\phi_{*}(\tau)\rightarrow-\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty

and such that (
10.15
) holds. Assume, to this end, that there are two such solutions, say
(\theta_{a},\phi_{a})

and
(\theta_{b},\phi_{b})
. By the arguments presented at the beginning of the proof, there are constants
C_{a}
and
T_{a}
such that

\left|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{a}(\tau)\right|\leq C_{a}\langle\phi_{a}(\tau)\rangle^{-2}

(10.28)

for all
\tau\leq T_{a}
. An analogous statement holds for
\phi_{b}
. Since
\phi_{a}
converges to
-\infty
, we can, without loss of
generality, assume that
\phi_{a}(\tau)\leq-n_{0}
and
\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a}(\tau)\geq-\lambda_{-}/2
for all
\tau\leq T_{a}
, and similarly for
\phi_{b}
.
Thus,
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{b}
can be assumed to be strictly decreasing to the past. Let

\Phi_{0}\in\phi_{a}((-\infty,T_{a}])\cap\phi_{b}((-\infty,T_{b}])\cap\xi((-\infty,\tau_{n_{0}}]).

Let
\tau_{\Phi}\in(-\infty,\tau_{n_{0}}]
be such that
\xi(\tau_{\Phi})=\Phi_{0}
and modify
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{b}
by a time translation so that

\phi_{a}(\tau_{\Phi})=\phi_{b}(\tau_{\Phi})=\Phi_{0}
. Let
T:=\min\{T_{a},T_{b},\tau_{n_{0}}\}
. Introduce
\zeta_{a}:=\phi_{a}-\xi

and define
\zeta_{b}
analogously. Then
\zeta_{a}
and
\zeta_{b}
satisfy (
10.24
) with
n
replaced by
a
and
b
respectively.
This means that they also satisfy (
10.26
) with
n
replaced by
a
and
b
for suitable choices of
f_{a}
,
g_{a}
,
f_{b}

and
g_{b}
. Define
\mathcal{C}_{a}
to be the set of
\tau\leq T
such that for all
s
between
\tau
and
\tau_{\Phi}
,
|\zeta_{a}(s)|\leq 1
. Note that

\mathcal{C}_{a}
is closed, connected and non-empty. Due to (
10.19
), (
10.22
), (
10.28
),

f_{a}(\tau):=-\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\ln V)^{\prime\prime}(s\xi(\tau)+(1-s)\phi_{a}(\tau))ds,

and the fact that
|\zeta_{a}(\tau)|\leq 1
for
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
,

|f_{a}(\tau)|+|g_{a}(\tau)|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}

for
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
, where
C
only depends on
C_{V}
,
\lambda_{-}
and
C_{a}
. In analogy with (
10.27
),

|\partial_{\tau}(\zeta_{a}^{2}+1)(\tau)|\leq 2C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}(\zeta_{a}^{2}+1)(\tau)

for all
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
. Integrating this estimate yields

\zeta_{a}^{2}(\tau)+1\leq\exp\big{(}-\tfrac{2C}{\max\{\tau,\tau_{\Phi}\}}\big{)}

(10.29)

for
\tau\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
, where we used the fact that
\zeta_{a}(\tau_{\Phi})=0
. Assuming
T
to be close enough to
-\infty
that
-2C/T<\ln 2
,
a bootstrap argument can be used to conclude that
|\zeta_{a}(\tau)|\leq 1
for
\tau\leq T
. An identical argument yields the same
conclusion for
\zeta_{b}
. Moreover, (
10.29
) and a similar estimate for
\zeta_{b}
hold for
\tau\leq T
. This
means, in particular, that there is a constant depending only on
C_{V}
,
\lambda_{-}
,
C_{a}
and
C_{b}
such that

|\zeta_{a}(\tau)|+|\zeta_{b}(\tau)|\leq C\langle\tau_{\Phi}\rangle^{-1/2}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{\Phi}
, where we used the fact that
|e^{x}-1|\leq|x|e^{|x|}
. This means, in particular, that if
\varphi:=\phi_{a}-\phi_{b}
, then

|\varphi(\tau)|\leq 2
for all
\tau\leq T
and
|\varphi(\tau)|\leq C\langle\tau_{\Phi}\rangle^{-1/2}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{\Phi}
. Let
\{\Phi_{l}\}
be a
decreasing sequence
such that
\Phi_{1}\leq\Phi_{0}-1
and
\Phi_{l}\rightarrow-\infty
. Let
\tau_{l}
be such that
\xi(\tau_{l})=\Phi_{l}
. Since
\phi_{a}(\tau_{\Phi})=\xi(\tau_{\Phi})=\Phi_{0}
and

\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a}(\tau)\geq-\lambda_{-}/2
for
\tau\leq T
, there is a
\tau_{a,l}\leq\tau_{\Phi}
such that
\phi_{a}(\tau_{a,l})=\Phi_{l}
. Moreover,

-\frac{\lambda_{-}}{2}|\tau_{l}-\tau_{a,l}|\leq|\phi_{a}(\tau_{a,l})-\phi_{a}(\tau_{l})|=|\xi(\tau_{l})-\phi_{a}(\tau_{l})|\leq C\langle\tau_{\Phi}\rangle^{-1/2}.

Assuming
T
to be close enough to
-\infty
, the bound depending only on
C_{V}
,
\lambda_{-}
and
C_{a}
, we can assume
|\tau_{l}-\tau_{a,l}|\leq 1/2
.
We can define
\tau_{b,l}
similarly and prove that
|\tau_{l}-\tau_{b,l}|\leq 1/2
. Define

\phi_{a,l}(\tau):=\phi_{a}(\tau+\tau_{a,l}-\tau_{l}),\ \ \ \phi_{b,l}(\tau):=\phi_{b}(\tau+\tau_{b,l}-\tau_{l}).

By taking subsequences, if necessary, we can assume
\tau_{a,l}-\tau_{l}
and
\tau_{b,l}-\tau_{l}
to converge to limits, say
\delta_{a}
and
\delta_{b}

respectively. Define
\phi_{a,*}(\tau):=\phi_{a}(\tau+\delta_{a})
and
\phi_{b,*}(\tau):=\phi_{b}(\tau+\delta_{b})
. Note that

\begin{split}|\phi_{a,l}(\tau)-\phi_{b,l}(\tau)|\leq&|\phi_{a}(\tau+\tau_{a,l}-\tau_{l})-\xi(\tau+\tau_{a,l}-\tau_{l})|+|\xi(\tau+\tau_{a,l}-\tau_{l})-\xi(\tau+\tau_{b,l}-\tau_{l})|\\
&+|\xi(\tau+\tau_{b,l}-\tau_{l})-\phi_{b}(\tau+\tau_{b,l}-\tau_{l})|\leq 2+\sqrt{6}\alpha_{V}\end{split}

for all
\tau\leq T-1
. In particular, there is a numerical bound on the difference. On the other hand,
\phi_{a,l}(\tau_{l})=\phi_{b,l}(\tau_{l})
.
Note also that (
10.22
), (
10.28
),
|\zeta_{a}(\tau)|\leq 1
for
\tau\leq T
and
|\tau_{l}-\tau_{a,l}|\leq 1/2
yield the
conclusion that

\left|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l}(\tau)+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{a,l}(\tau)\right|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}

(10.30)

for all
\tau\leq T-1
, where
C
only depends on
C_{a}
and
\lambda_{-}
. There is a similar estimate for
\phi_{b,l}
.
Since
\phi_{a,l}(\tau_{l})=\phi_{b,l}(\tau_{l})
, this means that

|(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l})(\tau_{l})-(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l})(\tau_{l})|\leq C\langle\tau_{l}\rangle^{-2},

(10.31)

where
C
only depends on
C_{a}
,
C_{b}
and
\lambda_{-}
. Next, we wish to compare
\phi_{a,l}
and
\phi_{b,l}
on
[\tau_{l},T-1]
.
Note, to this end, that combining (
10.11
) and (
10.16
) yields

\phi_{\tau\tau}=-\left(3-\phi_{\tau}^{2}/2\right)\left(\phi_{\tau}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi\right).

(10.32)

Since
\phi_{a,l}
and
\phi_{b,l}
both satisfy this equation, it can be deduced that
\varphi_{l}:=\phi_{a,l}-\phi_{b,l}
satisfies

\begin{split}\partial_{\tau}^{2}\varphi_{l}=&(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l}/2+\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l}/2)\left(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{a,l}\right)\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l}\\
&-\left(3-(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l})^{2}/2\right)\left[\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l}+(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{a,l}-(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{b,l}\right].\end{split}

(10.33)

We can assume
T
to be such that
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
for
\tau\leq T
, and similarly for
\phi_{b}
.
Finally, due to (
10.19
), (
10.22
),
|\zeta_{a}(\tau)|\leq 1
for
\tau\leq T
,

|\zeta_{b}(\tau)|\leq 1
for
\tau\leq T
,
|\tau_{l}-\tau_{a,l}|\leq 1/2
and
|\tau_{l}-\tau_{b,l}|\leq 1/2
,

\left|(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{a,l}(\tau)-(\ln V)^{\prime}\circ\phi_{b,l}(\tau)\right|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}|\varphi_{l}(\tau)|

(10.34)

for all
\tau\leq T-1
, where
C
only depends on
C_{V}
and
\lambda_{-}
and we rewrite the difference in analogy with
(
10.25
). Introduce

\mathfrak{E}_{l}:=\frac{1}{2}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha}(\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\beta}\varphi_{l}^{2},

where
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
are constants that remain to be determined. Note that since
\tau\leq 0
,
\partial_{\tau}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha}\leq 0
for
\alpha\geq 0
.
Due to (
10.30
);
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}
and
|\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l}(\tau)|\leq\sqrt{6}\beta_{V}

for
\tau\leq T-1
(so that
3-(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l})^{2}/2>0
); (
10.33
); and (
10.34
)
it can be deduced that

\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)\leq-\gamma_{V}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha}(\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l})^{2}+C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)+C\langle\tau\rangle^{\beta-\alpha}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)+C\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha-2}|\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l}(\tau)||\varphi_{l}(\tau)|

(10.35)

for
\tau\leq T-1
, where
C
only depends on
C_{a}
,
C_{b}
,
C_{V}
and
\lambda_{-}
. Here
\gamma_{V}:=3(1-\beta_{V}^{2})>0
.
In order to estimate the last term on the right hand side of (
10.35
), note that

\begin{split}&\sqrt{\gamma_{V}}\langle\tau\rangle^{\alpha}|\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l}(\tau)|\cdot\gamma_{V}^{-1/2}C\langle\tau\rangle^{\alpha-\beta-2}\langle\tau\rangle^{\beta}|\varphi_{l}(\tau)|\\
\leq&\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{V}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha}(\partial_{\tau}\varphi_{l})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}C^{2}\gamma_{V}^{-1}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha-2\beta-4}\langle\tau\rangle^{2\beta}\varphi_{l}(\tau)^{2}.\end{split}

Combining the last two estimates yields

\partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-2}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)+C\langle\tau\rangle^{\beta-\alpha}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)+C\langle\tau\rangle^{2\alpha-2\beta-4}\mathfrak{E}_{l}

for
\tau\leq T-1
, where
C
only depends on
C_{a}
,
C_{b}
,
C_{V}
,
\alpha_{V}
and
\lambda_{-}
.
In order for
\mathfrak{E}_{l}
not to change too much, we want
to have
\beta-\alpha<-1
and
2\alpha-2\beta-4<-1
. Since
\varphi_{l}(\tau_{l})=0
and (
10.31
) holds, we also know that

\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau_{l})\leq C\langle\tau_{l}\rangle^{2\alpha-4}
, where
C
only depends on
C_{a}
,
C_{b}
and
\lambda_{-}
. In order for this to tend to zero as

l\rightarrow\infty
, we need to have
\alpha<2
. However,
\beta=3/4
and
\alpha=15/8
satisfy all the above requirements. With these choices,

\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau_{l})\leq C\langle\tau_{l}\rangle^{-1/4},\ \ \ \partial_{\tau}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)\leq C\langle\tau\rangle^{-9/8}\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)

for all
\tau\leq T-1
. This means that
\mathfrak{E}_{l}(\tau)\leq C\langle\tau_{l}\rangle^{-1/4}
for all
\tau\in[\tau_{l},T-1]
. Thus

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}[\phi_{a,l}(T-1),(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{a,l})(T-1)]=\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}[\phi_{b,l}(T-1),(\partial_{\tau}\phi_{b,l})(T-1)].

This means that
\phi_{a,*}(T-1)=\phi_{b,*}(T-1)
and similarly for the first derivative. Since
\phi_{a,*}
and
\phi_{b,*}
both satisfy
(
10.32
), it follows that they are equal. Thus
\phi_{a}
is a time translation of
\phi_{b}
. That the
image of the corresponding solution is a smooth submanifold follows by an argument identical to the one presented in the proof
of Proposition
10.8
. This finishes the proof of
(ii)
. In order to prove
(i)
, it is sufficient to
note that introducing
\bar{\phi}:=-\phi
and
\bar{V}(s):=V(-s)
, case
(i)
can be reduced to case
(ii)
.

Next, let us prove the statements in Remark
10.18
. Proposition
10.10
still applies
and leads to the conclusion that
|\phi(\tau)|\rightarrow\infty
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. If
\phi(\tau)\rightarrow-\infty
, then, since

V(s)
is bounded for
s\leq 0
, we conclude that
V\circ\phi(\tau)/\theta^{2}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
, so that all the conclusions of
Lemma
10.1
apply. If
\phi(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
, then we can reduce the argument to the above (alternately, we
can modify the potential in regimes that are irrelevant for the dynamics in such a way that the exact statement of the theorem can be applied).
∎

10.2
The spatially homogeneous and isotropic setting; the case of negative spatial curvature

Let
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
be a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold with constant scalar curvature
-6
. This means that
\mathrm{Ric}[\bar{g}_{-}]=-2\bar{g}_{-}
.
Let
J
be an open interval,
a\in C^{\infty}(J,(0,\infty))
,
\phi\in C^{\infty}(J)
and consider a metric of the form

g:=-dt\otimes dt+a^{2}(t)\bar{g}_{-}

(10.36)

on
M:=\bar{M}\times J
. Finally, let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. The Einstein non-linear scalar field equations for
(M,g,\phi)
read

\displaystyle\tfrac{a_{tt}}{a}=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{3}\phi_{t}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}V\circ\phi,

(10.37a)

\displaystyle\phi_{tt}=

\displaystyle-\theta\phi_{t}-V^{\prime}\circ\phi,

(10.37b)

\displaystyle\tfrac{2}{3}\theta^{2}=

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{2}+\tfrac{6}{a^{2}}+2V\circ\phi,

(10.37c)

where
\theta:=3a_{t}/a
denotes the mean curvature; see
[
53
, (220), p. 214]
. Given initial data
as in Definition
1.107
, there is a unique corresponding maximal development in the sense of Definition
1.111
. In
fact, the following holds.

Lemma 10.19
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathcal{N}[V]
. Then there is a unique (up to time translation) spatially locally homogeneous
and isotropic non-linear scalar field development of
\mathfrak{I}
, say
(M,g,\phi)
with
M=\bar{M}\times J
and
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
, which is maximal in the sense that
either
t_{\pm}=\pm\infty
or
|\theta|+|\phi|
is unbounded as
t\rightarrow t_{\pm}
, where
\theta
denotes the mean curvature of the constant-
t

hypersurfaces. Moreover, this development is globally hyperbolic.

If, in addition to the above,
V\geq 0
,
J
can be assumed to equal
(0,\infty)
. Moreover, there is a unique
t_{0}\in J
such that if
g
is given by
(
10.36
), where
\bar{g}_{-}
is the constant multiple of
\bar{g}
with scalar curvature
-6
, then the initial data induced on

\bar{M}_{t_{0}}:=\bar{M}\times\{t_{0}\}
by
(M,g,\phi)
and pulled back by
\iota_{t_{0}}
equal
\mathfrak{I}
, where
\iota_{t_{0}}(p):=(p,t_{0})
. Moreover, this solution
has a crushing singularity at
t=0
and is such that
\theta\notin L^{1}(0,1)
and
\theta\notin L^{1}(1,\infty)
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\geq 0
be defined by the conditions that
\bar{g}=\alpha^{2}\bar{g}_{-}
and
\bar{k}=\alpha\beta\bar{g}_{-}
, where
\bar{g}_{-}
is a metric satisfying

\mathrm{Ric}[\bar{g}_{-}]=-2\bar{g}_{-}
. Let
(a,\phi)
be the solution to (
10.37a
) and (
10.37b
) with initial data

a(0)=\alpha
,
a_{t}(0)=\beta
,
\phi(0)=\bar{\phi}_{0}
and
\phi_{t}(0)=\bar{\phi}_{1}
. Let
J=(t_{-},t_{+})
be the maximal existence interval on which
a>0
. Define

H:=\tfrac{2}{3}\theta^{2}-\phi_{t}^{2}-\tfrac{6}{a^{2}}-2V\circ\phi,

(10.38)

where
\theta=3a_{t}/a
. It can then be verified that
H_{t}=-2\theta H/3
. Moreover, since
\mathfrak{I}
satisfies (
1.25
), we know that
(
10.37c
) holds with
\theta
,
a
,
\phi
and
\phi_{t}
replaced by
3\beta/\alpha
,
\alpha
,
\bar{\phi}_{0}
and
\bar{\phi}_{1}
respectively, so that

H(0)=0
.
Combining these two observations yields the conclusion that
H
vanishes identically. Thus (
10.37c
) holds for all
t\in J
. To
conclude,
(M,g,\phi)
, where
g
is given by (
1.28
), is a solution to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations. The uniqueness
statement follows from the uniqueness (up to time translation) of solutions to (
10.37a
) and (
10.37b
), given
initial data, and the fact that the initial data for these equations are determined by
\mathfrak{I}
. The fact that
(M,g)
is a globally hyperbolic manifold
follows from the fact that
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{-})
is a complete manifold and an argument similar to the one presented in the proof of

[
54
, Proposition 20.3, p. 215]
. In order to prove the characterisation of
t_{\pm}
, assume that
t_{+}<\infty
and that
\phi
and
\theta
are
bounded to the future. Since
a_{t}/a=\theta/3
, it follows that
\ln a
remains bounded to the future. Moreover,
\phi_{t}
remains bounded to the future due
to (
10.37b
). To conclude,
\phi
,
\phi_{t}
,
\ln a
and
\theta
remain bounded to the future. This means that the solution can
be extended to the future, contradicting the assumption that
J
is the maximal existence interval. The argument in the opposite time direction is the same.

Assume now that, in addition to the above,
V\geq 0
. Then, by an argument similar to, but simpler than, the proof of
Lemma
3.4
, the existence interval of solutions can be assumed to be of the form
J=(t_{-},\infty)
where
t_{-}>-\infty
. Moreover,

\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
,
\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{-},t_{-}+1)
and
\theta\notin L^{1}(t_{-}+1,\infty)
. By a translation in time, there is
a unique
t_{0}>0
such that the existence interval is
(0,\infty)
and
the original initial data are induced at
t_{0}
. This demonstrates existence. In order to prove uniqueness,
assume that
a_{i}
,
\phi_{i}
and
t_{0,i}
,
i=1,2
, satisfy the conditions of the lemma. Then, due to the arguments provided in connection with
(
10.37
), we know that
a_{i}
and
\phi_{i}
both solve (
10.37
). Due to the requirement that
the solutions should induce the correct initial data at
\bar{M}_{t_{0,i}}
, we conclude that
a_{1}(t_{0,1})=a_{2}(t_{0,2})
and

\phi_{1}(t_{0,1})=\phi_{2}(t_{0,2})
, and similarly for the time derivatives. Due to uniqueness of solutions of systems of ODE’s, we conclude that
the solution
(a_{1},\phi_{1})
is simply a time translation of
(a_{2},\phi_{2})
. However, since both solutions have a crushing singularity at

t=0
, the time translation has to be trivial. The lemma follows.
∎

Assume that all the conditions of Lemma
10.19
are fulfilled. In the special case of trivial initial data as in
Definition
1.107
, we conclude from (
10.37b
) that
\phi\equiv\bar{\phi}_{0}
. Let

\Lambda:=V(\bar{\phi}_{0})
and
H:=(\Lambda/3)^{1/2}
. Then (
10.37a
) implies that

a(t)=\alpha\sinh(Ht)
for some constant
\alpha>0
in case
\Lambda>0
and that
a(t)=\alpha t
for some constant
\alpha>0
in case
\Lambda=0
. Combining this observation
with the requirement that (
10.37c
) hold yields the conclusion that
a(t)=a_{\Lambda}(t)
, where
a_{\Lambda}
is defined by
(
1.27
). The statements of Remark
1.108
follow.

Given that all the assumptions of Lemma
10.19
are fulfilled, let
\tau
be a time coordinate satisfying (
3.27
).
Then the range of
\tau
equals
\mathbb{R}
; cf. the last conclusions of Lemma
10.19
. Moreover,

\theta^{\prime}=-(1+q)\theta,\ \ \ a(\tau)=a(0)e^{\tau},

(10.39)

where
q:=\phi_{\tau}^{2}/3-3V\circ\phi/\theta^{2}
and a prime denotes differentiation with respect to
\tau
. Next, we derive the asymptotics of solutions.

Proposition 10.20
.

Assume that all the conditions of Lemma
10.19
are fulfilled. Assume, in addition, that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some

\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
. Let
(a,\phi)
be the solution obtained in Lemma
10.19
, defined on
J=(0,\infty)
. Then there are
two possibilities. The first is that there is a
\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
and a constant
C
such that

|\phi_{t}(t)/\theta(t)|+|\phi(t)-\phi_{\infty}|\leq Ct^{2}

(10.40)

holds for all
t\leq 1
. Then the metric asymptotes to a solution to the Einstein vacuum equations with a cosmological constant
\Lambda:=V(\phi_{\infty})

in the direction of the singularity, cf. Remark
1.108
, in the sense that there is a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle|a(t)-a_{\Lambda}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{5},

(10.41a)

\displaystyle\left|\theta(t)-3\tfrac{\dot{a}_{\Lambda}(t)}{a_{\Lambda}(t)}\right|\leq

\displaystyle Ct^{3}

(10.41b)

for all
t\leq 1
.

The second possibility is that there is a
\Phi_{1}=\pm\sqrt{2/3}
, a
\Phi_{0}
, an
a_{0}>0
and a constant
C
such that

|\theta^{p}a-a_{0}|+|\phi_{t}/\theta-\Phi_{1}|+|\phi+\Phi_{1}\ln\theta-\Phi_{0}|\leq C\theta^{-4/3}

(10.42)

for all
t\leq 1
, where
p=1/3
. In this case, there is also a constant
C
such that

|t\theta(t)-1|\leq Ct^{4/3}

(10.43)

for all
t\leq 1
.

Remark 10.21
.

The estimates (
10.41
) can also be written

\left|a(t)-t-\tfrac{\Lambda}{18}t^{3}\right|\leq Ct^{4},\ \ \ \left|\theta(t)-\tfrac{3}{t}-\tfrac{\Lambda}{3}t\right|\leq Ct^{2}

for all
t\leq 1
.

Remark 10.22
.

When (
10.42
) holds, the development induces data on the singularity in the sense of
Definition
1.113
. Moreover, due to (
10.43
), the
\theta^{-4/3}
appearing on
the right hand side of (
10.42
) can be replaced by
t^{4/3}
.

Remark 10.23
.

In the case of the Einstein scalar field equations, the only way for
\phi_{t}/\theta
to converge to zero to the past is if
\phi_{t}
is
identically zero; due to (
10.37
),
\partial_{t}(\phi_{t}/\theta)=-6\theta^{-1}a^{-2}\cdot(\phi_{t}/\theta)
. This means that
the scalar field is constant and the relevant solution is the Milne model.

Proof.

Introduce a time coordinate
\tau
according to (
3.27
) and note that (
10.37
) and (
10.39
) yield

\theta(\tau)\geq c_{\theta}e^{-\tau}
for some constant
c_{\theta}>0
.
Define
\Psi
and
\Omega
as in (
3.30
). Then
\Psi
satisfies (
3.42
). At this stage, we can revisit the arguments
presented in the proof of Theorem
4.13
. This means that we have two possibilities. The first one is that
(
4.22
) holds. Thus
q
decays exponentially, so that there is a
\theta_{\infty}>0
such that

e^{\tau}\theta(\tau)\rightarrow\theta_{\infty}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. This means that
|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\tau}
, so that there is a

\phi_{\infty}
such that

|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)|+|\phi(\tau)-\phi_{\infty}|\leq Ce^{2\tau}

(10.44)

holds for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this information with (
10.37
) and (
10.39
) yields the
conclusion that

\left|\theta^{2}(\tau)-9/a^{2}(\tau)-3V(\phi_{\infty})\right|\leq Ce^{2\tau}

(10.45)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. This yields

g=-9[\theta(\tau)]^{-2}d\tau\otimes d\tau+a^{2}(\tau)\bar{g}_{-}=-3\alpha e^{2\tau}[1+\alpha\Lambda e^{2\tau}+\psi(\tau)]^{-1}d\tau\otimes d\tau+3\alpha e^{2\tau}\bar{g}_{-},

(10.46)

where
\alpha=a^{2}(0)/3
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
|\psi(\tau)|\leq Ce^{4\tau}
for some constant
C
and all
\tau\leq 0
.
Considering proper time as a function of
\tau
, it follows that

\tfrac{dt}{d\tau}=\sqrt{3\alpha}e^{\tau}[1+\alpha\Lambda e^{2\tau}+\psi(\tau)]^{-1/2}=\sqrt{3\alpha}e^{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{3}\alpha^{3/2}\Lambda e^{3\tau}+O(e^{5\tau}).

Since
t(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, this means that

t(\tau)=\sqrt{3\alpha}e^{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{6}\sqrt{3}\alpha^{3/2}\Lambda e^{3\tau}+O(e^{5\tau}).

(10.47)

This relation implies that

a(t)=\sqrt{3\alpha}e^{\tau(t)}=t+\tfrac{\Lambda}{18}t^{3}+O(t^{5})

so that (
10.41a
) holds. Combining this with (
10.45
) yields

\left|\theta(t)-\tfrac{3}{t}-\tfrac{\Lambda}{3}t\right|\leq Ct^{3}

for all
t\leq 1
. Thus (
10.41b
) holds. Finally, combining (
10.44
) and
(
10.47
) yields (
10.40
).

The second possibility is that
F_{0}
, introduced in the proof of Theorem
4.13
, is non-zero. This means that
2-q
is integrable to
the past; see (
4.32
). There is thus a
\theta_{\infty}>0
such that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)\rightarrow\theta_{\infty}
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, so that

\Omega(\tau)\leq Ce^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(10.48)

for all
\tau\leq 0
. Combining this with (
10.37
) yields, recalling
6(1-\alpha_{V})>4
(since
\alpha_{V}<1/3
),

\big{|}|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)|-\sqrt{6}\big{|}\leq Ce^{4\tau}

(10.49)

for all
\tau\leq 0
, so that

|\phi_{\tau}(\tau)-\Phi_{1}|+|\phi(\tau)-\Phi_{1}\tau-\Phi_{0}|\leq Ce^{4\tau}

(10.50)

holds for all
\tau\leq 0
. Due to the definition of
q
, (
10.48
) and
(
10.49
) imply that
|q(\tau)-2|
is bounded by the right hand side of (
10.49
).
Combining this estimate with
\theta^{\prime}=-(1+q)\theta
yields a
\theta_{\infty}>0
and a constant
C
such that

|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|+|q(\tau)-2|\leq Ce^{4\tau}

(10.51)

holds for all
\tau\leq 0
, where
q
is defined in connection with (
10.39
). Since
a(\tau)=a(0)e^{\tau}
, the estimates
(
10.50
) and (
10.51
) yield (
10.42
). Due to (
3.27
) and
(
10.51
), one can finally verify that (
10.43
) holds.
∎

10.2.1
Specifying the asymptotics

In Subsection
10.2.3
below, we prove that the first outcome in Proposition
10.20

is non-generic, and in Remark
1.108
, we prove that it is possible. Our next goal is to prove that we can parametrise
solutions with the property that
\phi(t)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}
as
t\downarrow 0
by the limit. We begin by reformulating the relevant equations.

Equations.
Assume that all the conditions of Lemma
10.19
are satisfied. Then the equations for
\phi
and
\theta
,
i.e. (
10.37b
) and the first equation in (
10.39
), can be written

\displaystyle\phi_{\tau\tau}=

\displaystyle(q-2)\phi_{\tau}-9\vartheta^{2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi,

(10.52a)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{\prime}=

\displaystyle(1+q)\vartheta,

(10.52b)

where
\vartheta=1/\theta
and
q:=\phi_{\tau}^{2}/3-3\vartheta^{2}V\circ\phi
. Here the initial data have to be such that

\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\tau}^{2}+9\vartheta^{2}V\circ\phi<3;

(10.53)

this condition is required to ensure that there is an
a>0
such that (
10.37c
) holds. Given
\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
,
(
10.52
) can be written

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle e^{-2\tau}\varphi_{1},

(10.54a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle q\varphi_{1}-9e^{2\tau}\vartheta^{2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0},

(10.54b)

\displaystyle r^{\prime}=

\displaystyle q,

(10.54c)

where

\phi_{0}:=\phi,\ \ \ \varphi_{0}:=\phi_{0}-\phi_{\infty},\ \ \ \varphi_{1}:=e^{2\tau}\phi_{\tau},\ \ \ r:=\ln\vartheta-\tau.

In particular, in (
10.54
), it is understood that

\phi_{0}=\varphi_{0}+\phi_{\infty},\ \ \ \vartheta=e^{r+\tau},\ \ \ q=e^{-4\tau}\varphi_{1}^{2}/3-3\vartheta^{2}V\circ\phi_{0}.

(10.55)

Next, we prove Proposition
1.109
.

Proof of Proposition
1.109
.

We begin by proving existence.

Existence.
Fix
\tau_{0}\leq 0
and let
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
denote the set of
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},r\in C^{0}(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
,
i=1,2
, such that

|\varphi_{0}(\tau)|\leq e^{\tau/2},\ \ \ |\varphi_{1}(\tau)|\leq e^{3\tau},\ \ \ |r(\tau)|\leq e^{\tau}

(10.56)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Given
x_{i}=(\varphi_{0,i},\varphi_{1,i},r_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
,
i=1,2
, define

\begin{split}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{1},x_{2}):=&\textstyle{\sup}_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}[e^{-\tau/2}|\varphi_{0,1}(\tau)-\varphi_{0,2}(\tau)|]+\sup_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}[e^{-3\tau}|\varphi_{1,1}(\tau)-\varphi_{1,2}(\tau)|]\\
&+\textstyle{\sup}_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}[e^{-\tau}|r_{1}(\tau)-r_{2}(\tau)|].\end{split}

Note that
(\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}},d_{\tau_{0}})
is a complete metric space. Given
x_{a}=(\varphi_{0,a},\varphi_{1,a},r_{a})\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
, define

\varphi_{0,b}
,
\varphi_{1,b}
,
r_{b}\in C^{0}(-\infty,\tau_{0}]
by

\displaystyle\varphi_{0,b}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{-2s}\varphi_{1,a}(s)ds,

(10.57a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1,b}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}[q_{a}(s)\varphi_{1,a}(s)-9e^{2s}\vartheta_{a}^{2}(s)V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0,a}(s)]ds,

(10.57b)

\displaystyle r_{b}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}q_{a}(s)ds.

(10.57c)

Here
\phi_{0,a}
,
\vartheta_{a}
and
q_{a}
are defined by (
10.55
) in which
\varphi_{0}
,
r
and
\varphi_{1}
are replaced by

\varphi_{0,a}
,
r_{a}
and
\varphi_{1,a}
respectively. In what follows, we denote
(\varphi_{0,b},\varphi_{1,b},r_{b})
by
x_{b}
, and we denote the
map taking
x_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
to
x_{b}
by
F
; i.e.,
x_{b}=F(x_{a})
. Since
|\varphi_{1,a}(\tau)|\leq e^{3\tau}
, (
10.57a
) implies that

|\varphi_{0,b}(\tau)|\leq e^{\tau}

(10.58)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Let

M_{V}:=\textstyle{\sup}_{s\in[-1,1]}|V(\phi_{\infty}+s)|+\sup_{s\in[-1,1]}|V^{\prime}(\phi_{\infty}+s)|+\sup_{s\in[-1,1]}|V^{\prime\prime}(\phi_{\infty}+s)|.

We can then estimate that the integrand in (
10.57b
) is bounded in absolute value by
4C_{\varphi}e^{4s}
, where
C_{\varphi}
only depends
on
M_{V}
. Similarly,
|q_{a}(\tau)|\leq 2C_{q}e^{2\tau}
, where
C_{q}
only depends on
M_{V}
. This means that

|\varphi_{1,b}(\tau)|\leq C_{\varphi}e^{4\tau},\ \ \ |r_{b}(\tau)|\leq C_{q}e^{2\tau}

(10.59)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. From (
10.58
) and (
10.59
), it follows that there is a
T_{0}\leq 0
, depending only on
M_{V}
, such
that if
\tau_{0}\leq T_{0}
, then
x_{b}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
. For
\tau_{0}\leq T_{0}
, we thus conclude that
F
is a map from
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
to itself. We wish to prove
that this map is a contraction for a suitable choice of
\tau_{0}
.

Let
x_{a},x_{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
,
x_{b}:=F(x_{a})
and
x_{d}:=F(x_{c})
. Then, using notation analogous to the above,

|\varphi_{0,b}(\tau)-\varphi_{0,d}(\tau)|\leq\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{-2s}|\varphi_{1,a}(s)-\varphi_{1,c}(s)|ds\leq e^{\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})

(10.60)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Before proceeding to the other components, estimate, for
k\in\{0,1\}
,

\begin{split}|V^{(k)}[\phi_{0,a}(\tau)]-V^{(k)}[\phi_{0,c}(\tau)]|=&\left|\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}V^{(k+1)}[s\phi_{0,a}(\tau)+(1-s)\phi_{0,c}(\tau)]ds\cdot[\varphi_{0,a}(\tau)-\varphi_{0,c}(\tau)]\right|\\
\leq&M_{V}|\varphi_{0,a}(\tau)-\varphi_{0,c}(\tau)|\leq M_{V}e^{\tau/2}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})\end{split}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. We also estimate

\begin{split}|\vartheta_{a}^{2}(\tau)-\vartheta_{c}^{2}(\tau)|\leq&[\vartheta_{a}(\tau)+\vartheta_{c}(\tau)]\vartheta_{c}(\tau)|e^{r_{a}(\tau)-r_{c}(\tau)}-1|\\
\leq&2e^{4}e^{2\tau}|r_{a}(\tau)-r_{c}(\tau)|\leq 2e^{4}e^{3\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})\end{split}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Due to estimates of this type, it follows that

|q_{a}(\tau)-q_{c}(\tau)|\leq 2K_{q}e^{2\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})

(10.61)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
K_{q}
only depends on
M_{V}
. Similarly

\begin{split}&\left|[q_{a}(\tau)\varphi_{1,a}(\tau)-9e^{2\tau}\vartheta_{a}^{2}(\tau)V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0,a}(\tau)]-[q_{c}(\tau)\varphi_{1,c}(\tau)-9e^{2\tau}\vartheta_{c}^{2}(\tau)V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0,c}(\tau)]\right|\\
\leq&4K_{\varphi}e^{4\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})\end{split}

(10.62)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
K_{\varphi}
only depends on
M_{V}
. Combining (
10.57c
) with (
10.61
) yields

|r_{b}(\tau)-r_{d}(\tau)|\leq\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}|q_{a}(s)-q_{c}(s)|ds\leq K_{q}e^{2\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})

(10.63)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Similarly, combining (
10.57b
) and (
10.62
) yields

|\varphi_{1,b}(\tau)-\varphi_{1,d}(\tau)|\leq K_{\varphi}e^{4\tau}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c})

(10.64)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Combining (
10.60
), (
10.63
) and (
10.64
) yields

d_{\tau_{0}}[F(x_{a}),F(x_{c})]=d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{b},x_{d})\leq Ke^{\tau_{0}/2}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c}).

There is thus a
T_{0}\leq 0
such that for
\tau_{0}\leq T_{0}
,
F
maps
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
and, for
x_{a},x_{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
,

d_{\tau_{0}}[F(x_{a}),F(x_{c})]\leq\tfrac{1}{2}d_{\tau_{0}}(x_{a},x_{c}).

By the Banach fixed point theorem, we conclude that
F
has a unique fixed point in
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
. This means that there are unique continuous
functions
\varphi_{0}
,
\varphi_{1}
and
r
for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, satisfying the bounds (
10.56
) and the equations

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{-2s}\varphi_{1}(s)ds,

(10.65a)

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}[q(s)\varphi_{1}(s)-9e^{2s}\vartheta^{2}(s)V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}(s)]ds,

(10.65b)

\displaystyle r(\tau)=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}q(s)ds.

(10.65c)

From these equalities, it follows that the components of the fixed point are smooth for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Define
\theta(\tau)=e^{-r(\tau)-\tau}
and

\phi(\tau)=\varphi_{0}(\tau)+\phi_{\infty}
. Then, due to (
10.56
) and (
10.65a
),

|\phi(\tau)-\phi_{\infty}|\leq e^{\tau/2},\ \ \ |e^{\tau}\theta(\tau)-1|\leq Ce^{\tau}

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Due to (
10.65
), it can also be verified that

\phi_{\tau\tau}=(q-2)\phi_{\tau}-9\tfrac{V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}},\ \ \ \theta_{\tau}=-(1+q)\theta.

(10.66)

Moreover,

q=\tfrac{1}{3}\phi_{\tau}^{2}-\tfrac{3V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}.

(10.67)

Next, introduce a time coordinate
t
by

t(\tau)=\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}\tfrac{3}{\theta(s)}ds.

(10.68)

Combining (
10.66
), (
10.67
) and (
10.68
) yields

\theta_{t}=-\tfrac{1}{3}\theta^{2}-\phi_{t}^{2}+V\circ\phi,\ \ \ \phi_{tt}=-\theta\phi_{t}-V^{\prime}\circ\phi.

(10.69)

Next, define
a
by the condition that

a_{t}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta a,

(10.70)

the condition that
a
be strictly positive and the condition that

\tfrac{2}{3}\theta^{2}=\phi_{t}^{2}+\tfrac{6}{a^{2}}+2V\circ\phi

(10.71)

hold at some
t=t_{0}
. Given this definition of
a
, define
H
by (
10.38
). It can then be verified that
H_{t}=-2\theta H/3
.
Since
H(t_{0})=0
, we conclude that
H
vanishes identically, so that (
10.71
) holds for all
t
. Finally, combining
(
10.69
), (
10.70
) and (
10.71
), it follows that the metric
g
defined by
(
10.36
) and the scalar field
\phi
satisfy the Einstein scalar field equations; see (
10.37
).

Uniqueness.
In order to prove uniqueness, assume
(a,\phi)
to satisfy the conditions of the proposition. In particular,

\phi(t)\rightarrow\phi_{\infty}
as
t\downarrow 0
. Let
S
be the image of
(0,1)
under
\phi
. Then
S
is bounded and its closure is compact.
We can modify
V
, if necessary, outside an open neighbourhood containing the closure of
S
so that
V\in\mathfrak{P}_{\alpha_{V}}^{1}
for some
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1/3)
.
As far as the asymptotics in the direction of the singularity are concerned, we can therefore, without loss of generality, assume that the conditions
of Proposition
10.20
are satisfied. This means that the conclusions of this proposition concerning the first
possibility in Proposition
10.20
(and the corresponding conclusions in the proof) hold. In particular, we can
assume (
10.44
) to hold. This means that
|\varphi_{0}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\tau}
and
|\varphi_{1}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{4\tau}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
and some suitably chosen
\tau_{0}
. By replacing the time coordinate
\tau
by
\tau+\tau_{1}
for some
\tau_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
if necessary, the estimate (
10.45
) implies that
e^{\tau}\theta(\tau)=1+O(e^{2\tau})
. As a consequence,

|r(\tau)|\leq Ce^{2\tau}
. In particular, for
\tau_{0}
close enough to
-\infty
,
x:=(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},r)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\tau_{0}}
. Since

\varphi_{0}
,
\varphi_{1}
and
r
satisfy (
10.54
), it is clear that
x
is a fixed point of the map
F
.
By uniqueness of fixed points to
F
, we conclude that
x
is the unique fixed point we already constructed. Since the original solution can
be uniquely recovered from
x
by the argument described at the end of the proof of existence, we conclude that the
(a,\phi)
constructed
in the existence part are unique.

Asymptotics.
The statements concerning the asymptotics follow from Proposition
10.20
.
∎

10.2.2
Initial data on the singularity

Next, we prove Proposition
1.115
.

Proof of Proposition
1.115
.

Let
(\bar{M},\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\bar{\Phi}_{1},\bar{\Phi}_{0})=\mathfrak{I}_{\infty}
. By definition,
(\bar{M},\mathscr{H})
is a complete hyperbolic
3
-manifold. In particular, there is a
unique hyperbolic metric, say
\bar{g}_{-}
, with scalar curvature
-6
on
\bar{M}
such that
\mathscr{H}=a_{\infty}^{2}\bar{g}_{-}
for some
a_{\infty}\in(0,\infty)
.
In what follows, it is convenient to use the notation
\nu_{0}:=\ln a_{\infty}
. We also use the notation
\Phi_{1}:=3\bar{\Phi}_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}:=\bar{\Phi}_{0}
.

Existence.
We begin by proving existence of the desired development. Moreover, we do so by appealing to the abstract framework developed
in Section
7.1
. However, this necessitates reformulating the equations. Note, to this end, that the Hamiltonian
constraint (
10.37c
) can be reformulated to

1=\tfrac{1}{6}\phi_{\tau}^{2}+A^{2}+\tfrac{3V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}},

where
A:=3/(\theta a)
. This means that we can rewrite

q=\tfrac{1}{3}\phi_{\tau}^{2}-\tfrac{3V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}=2-2A^{2}-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}.

(10.72)

Variables.
The basic variables in the existence proof are
\nu
,
\varkappa
,
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
. Given these variables and constants

\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Phi_{1}\in\{-\sqrt{6},\sqrt{6}\}
as above, we
define
the following quantities:

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}:=

\displaystyle e^{-3\tau+\varkappa},

(10.73a)

\displaystyle\bar{A}:=

\displaystyle 3e^{2\tau-\nu_{0}-\nu},

(10.73b)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}:=

\displaystyle\psi_{1}+\Phi_{1},

(10.73c)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}:=

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1}+\psi_{1})\tau+\Phi_{0}+\psi_{0},

(10.73d)

\displaystyle\bar{q}:=

\displaystyle 2-2\bar{A}^{2}-\tfrac{9V\circ\phi_{0}}{\bar{\theta}^{2}}.

(10.73e)

Given these definitions, consider the following system of equations

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2-\bar{q},

(10.74a)

\displaystyle\varkappa^{\prime}=

\displaystyle 2-\bar{q},

(10.74b)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle[(2-\bar{q})\phi_{1}+9\bar{\theta}^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}]\tau,

(10.74c)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-(2-\bar{q})\phi_{1}-9\bar{\theta}^{-2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0}.

(10.74d)

Our goal is to prove that there is a unique solution to (
10.74
) such that
x:=(\nu,\varkappa,\psi_{0},\psi_{1})

converges to zero exponentially. Given
\Pi:=(\nu_{0},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
, let, to this end,
\mathcal{X}[x(\tau),\tau;\Pi]
be defined by the right hand side of
(
10.74
). Note also that, comparing with the framework developed Section
7.1
,

k=4
in our case. For a given
\epsilon>0
and a
\tau_{0}\leq 0
we define
X_{\tau_{0}}
and
d_{\tau_{0}}
as at the beginning of Section
7.1
.
We wish to prove that there is a choice of
\epsilon>0
such that (
7.3
) is satisfied.

Existence, abstract setting.

Let
x_{a},x_{b}\in X_{\tau_{0}}
,
(\nu_{a},\varkappa_{a},\psi_{0,a},\psi_{1,a})=x_{a}
and similarly for
x_{b}
. We also assume
that
\bar{q}_{a}
,
\bar{A}_{a}
,
\phi_{1,a}
etc. are obtained by inserting
\nu_{a}
etc. in (
10.73
) and similarly for
\bar{q}_{b}
etc. In order to
estimate
\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]
, note that

|\bar{q}_{a}-2|+\tfrac{9|V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0,a}|}{\bar{\theta}_{a}^{2}}\leq 2\bar{A}_{a}^{2}+\tfrac{9|V\circ\phi_{0,a}|}{\bar{\theta}_{a}^{2}}+\tfrac{9|V^{\prime}\circ\phi_{0,a}|}{\bar{\theta}_{a}^{2}}\leq C_{A}e^{4\tau}+C_{\Omega}e^{6(1-\alpha_{V})\tau}

(10.75)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C_{A}
only depends on
\nu_{0}
and
C_{\Omega}
only depends on
c_{1}
,
\epsilon
and
\Phi_{0}
; here
c_{1}
is the
constant appearing in (
1.9
). From (
10.75
), we deduce that

|\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]|\leq C_{\mathcal{X},0}\langle\tau\rangle e^{\beta_{V}\tau}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
\beta_{V}:=\min\{4,6(1-\alpha_{V})\}
and
C_{\mathcal{X},0}
only depends on
\Pi
,
\epsilon
and
c_{1}
. This means that the first estimate in
(
7.3
) is satisfied for any
\epsilon<2\beta_{V}/3
. Next, note that

|\bar{A}_{a}^{2}-\bar{A}_{b}^{2}|\leq\bar{A}_{b}(\bar{A}_{a}+\bar{A}_{b})\left|\tfrac{\bar{A}_{a}}{\bar{A}_{b}}-1\right|\leq Ce^{4\tau}|e^{\nu_{b}-\nu_{a}}-1|\leq Ce^{4\tau}|\nu_{b}-\nu_{a}|,

for
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C
changes from line to line and only depends on
\nu_{0}
. Similarly,

|\bar{\theta}_{a}^{-2}-\bar{\theta}_{b}^{-2}|\leq Ce^{6\tau}|\varkappa_{a}-\varkappa_{b}|

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C
is a numerical constant. Finally, by equalities such as (
7.20
), if
l\in\{0,1\}
, then

|V^{(l)}\circ\phi_{0,a}-V^{(l)}\circ\phi_{0,b}|\leq Ce^{-6\alpha_{V}\tau}[|\psi_{0,a}-\psi_{0,b}|+\langle\tau\rangle|\psi_{1,a}-\psi_{1,b}|]

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C
only depends on
c_{2}
,
\epsilon
and
\Phi_{0}
. Combining these observations with estimates such as
(
7.19
) yields the conclusion that

|\mathcal{X}[x_{a}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]-\mathcal{X}[x_{b}(\tau),\tau;\Pi]|\leq C_{\mathcal{X},0}\langle\tau\rangle^{2}e^{\beta_{V}\tau}|x_{a}-x_{b}|

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
C
only depends on
c_{2}
,
\epsilon
and
\Pi
. This means the second estimate in (
7.3
) is satisfied for
any
\epsilon<\beta_{V}
. To conclude, letting, e.g.,
\epsilon:=\beta_{V}/2
, Lemma
7.1
applies and demonstrates that there is a unique solution to
(
10.74
) such that
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
.

Existence, geometric setting.

Given the unique solution to (
10.74
) constructed above, define
\bar{\theta}
by (
10.73a
) and a new time
coordinate
t
by

t(\tau):=\textstyle{\int}_{-\infty}^{\tau}3e^{3s-\varkappa(s)}ds=e^{3\tau}+O(e^{(3+\epsilon)\tau}).

(10.76)

Next, define
a
by
a:=e^{\tau+\nu_{0}}e^{-\varkappa+\nu}
. It can then be verified that
\bar{\theta}=3a_{t}/a
, so that we can interpret
\bar{\theta}
as the mean curvature
of a metric of the form (
1.32
). For this reason, we, from now on, write
\theta=\bar{\theta}
. These definitions yield

\tfrac{3}{\theta a}=\tfrac{3}{\bar{\theta}a}=3e^{3\tau-\varkappa}e^{-\tau-\nu_{0}}e^{\varkappa-\nu}=3e^{2\tau-\nu-\nu_{0}}=\bar{A}.

For this reason, we, from now on, write
\bar{A}=A=3/(\theta a)
. Defining
\phi:=\phi_{0}
, see (
10.73d
), we conclude that

\bar{q}
introduced in (
10.73e
) coincides with the
q
defined by the far right hand side of (
10.72
).
For this reason, we, from now on, equate
\bar{q}
and
q
. Next, compute

\theta_{t}=\theta^{\prime}\tfrac{\theta}{3}=(-3+\varkappa^{\prime})\theta\tfrac{\theta}{3}=-(1+q)\tfrac{\theta^{2}}{3}.

(10.77)

Since
a_{t}=\theta a/3
, this equality implies that

a_{tt}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta_{t}a+\tfrac{1}{3}\theta a_{t}=-\tfrac{1}{9}q\theta^{2}a.

(10.78)

Next, note that
\phi^{\prime}=\phi_{1}
, so that
\phi_{t}=\theta\phi_{1}/3
. Compute

\partial_{t}\phi_{1}=\tfrac{\theta}{3}\psi_{1}^{\prime}=-(2-q)\phi_{t}-3\theta^{-1}V^{\prime}\circ\phi.

Combining this computation with (
10.77
) yields

\phi_{tt}=\tfrac{1}{3}\theta_{t}\phi_{1}+\tfrac{1}{3}\theta\partial_{t}\phi_{1}=-\theta\phi_{t}-V^{\prime}\circ\phi.

(10.79)

Next, consider
H
defined by (
10.38
). Given the equations that are satisfied here, it can be computed that

H_{t}=-2\theta H
. Introducing
\mathcal{H}:=H/\theta^{2}
, it can then be calculated that

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=2(q-2)\mathcal{H}.

Since
q-2
is integrable to the past, this means that if
\mathcal{H}
is ever non-zero,
\mathcal{H}(\tau)
converges to a non-zero number as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. On the other hand, we know that
\mathcal{H}
converges to
2/3-\Phi_{1}^{2}/9=0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. This
means that
\mathcal{H}\equiv 0
, so that
H\equiv 0
and (
10.37c
) holds. Due to (
10.79
),
we also know that (
10.37b
) holds. Finally, combining (
10.37c
) and
(
10.78
) yields (
10.37a
). To conclude
a
and
\phi
satisfy (
10.37
).
This means that we have a solution to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations. It remains to be verified that this solution has
the desired asymptotics. However, to begin with,

\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}=\tfrac{1}{3}\phi_{1}=\tfrac{1}{3}\Phi_{1}+O(t^{\epsilon/3}),

where we used that
\phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}+\psi_{1}
, that
\psi_{1}=O(e^{\epsilon\tau})
and that (
10.76
) holds. Next, note
that
e^{3\tau}\theta(\tau)=e^{\varkappa}=1+O(e^{\epsilon\tau})
, so that
t\theta=1+O(t^{\epsilon/3})
. By the definition of
a
, it follows that

\theta^{1/3}a=e^{\nu_{0}}[1+O(t^{\epsilon/3})].

Finally, similar arguments yield

\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta=\Phi_{0}+O(t^{\epsilon/3}).

This concludes the proof of existence.

Uniqueness.

Next, let us assume that
(a,\phi)
is a solution inducing the given data on the singularity. By assumption, the corresponding spacetime has
a crushing singularity, so that
\theta(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. From now on, we fix a
t_{0}
in the existence interval of the
solution such that
\theta(t)\geq 1
for all
t\leq t_{0}
. Moreover, for
l\in\{0,1,2\}
, we estimate

\tfrac{|V^{(l)}\circ\phi(t)|}{\theta^{2}(t)}\leq C\exp\left[-2(1-\alpha_{V})\ln\theta\right]

(10.80)

for all
t\leq t_{0}
and some constant
C
, depending only on
c_{2}
and
\Phi_{0}
. In order to obtain this estimate, we used the fact that

\theta^{-1}|\phi_{t}|\leq(2/3)^{1/2}
; cf. (
10.37c
). Next, note that
a=\theta^{1/3}a\cdot\theta^{-1/3}
,
so that
a(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. Since
a_{t}=\theta a/3
, this means that
\theta
is not integrable to the past. Next,

\theta_{t}=-\theta^{2}+\tfrac{6}{a^{2}}+3V\circ\phi.

(10.81)

Due to this equality and previous observations, it is clear that
\theta
blows up in finite time to the past. This means that we can assume

t_{-}=0
. Introducing a time coordinate satisfying (
3.27
), it is clear that
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
to the past. Combining this change
of time coordinate with (
10.81
) yields

(\ln\theta)^{\prime}=-3+\tfrac{18}{a^{2}\theta^{2}}+\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}.

(10.82)

Since the last two terms on the right hand side converge to zero, it is clear from this equality that
\theta
tends to infinity exponentially in

\tau
. Combining this observation with (
10.80
) and the fact that
\theta^{1/3}a
converges to a strictly positive limit,
it follows that the last two terms on the right hand side of (
10.82
) converge to zero exponentially. This means that

\ln\theta+3\tau
converges. This information can be used to deduce the existence of
\theta_{\infty}\in(0,\infty)
and a constant
C
such that

|\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau-\ln\theta_{\infty}|\leq Ce^{\beta_{V}\tau}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
\beta_{V}:=\min\{4,6(1-\alpha_{V})\}
. By a translation in
\tau
, we can assume
\theta_{\infty}=1
. This means that

|\varkappa(\tau)|\leq Ce^{\beta_{V}\tau}

(10.83)

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
, where
\varkappa(\tau):=\ln\theta(\tau)+3\tau
. Next, let
\nu:=\ln(\theta a)+2\tau-\nu_{0}
. Since we assume that

\theta^{1/3}a\rightarrow a_{\infty}=e^{\nu_{0}}
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
, and since (
10.83
) holds,
we conclude that
\nu(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. Since
\nu^{\prime}=(\ln\theta)^{\prime}+3
, (
10.82
) yields

|\nu(\tau)|\leq Ce^{\beta_{V}\tau}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Next, define
\psi_{1}:=\phi^{\prime}-\Phi_{1}
. Then
\psi_{1}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as

\tau\rightarrow-\infty
by assumption. On the other hand, it can be computed that

\psi_{1}^{\prime}=-\left(\tfrac{18}{\theta^{2}a^{2}}+\tfrac{9V\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}\right)\phi^{\prime}-\tfrac{9V^{\prime}\circ\phi}{\theta^{2}}.

By arguments similar to the above, we conclude that
|\psi_{1}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{\beta_{V}\tau}

for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. Next, we know that
\phi+\theta^{-1}\phi_{t}\ln\theta
converges to
\Phi_{0}
in the direction of the singularity.
Due to (
10.83
), we conclude that
\psi_{0}:=\phi-\phi^{\prime}\tau-\Phi_{0}
converges to zero. On the other hand,

\psi_{0}^{\prime}=-\phi^{\prime\prime}\tau=-\psi_{1}^{\prime}\tau
. Thus
|\psi_{0}(\tau)|\leq C\langle\tau\rangle e^{\beta_{V}\tau}
for all
\tau\leq\tau_{0}
. To summarise,
\nu
,
\varkappa
,

\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
satisfy (
10.74
). Moreover, for any
\epsilon<\beta_{V}/2
, it is clear that

x:=(\nu,\varkappa,\psi_{0},\psi_{1})
satisfies
e^{-\epsilon\tau}|x(\tau)|\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow-\infty
. This means that
x
is the unique
solution we already constructed. The estimates recorded in Remark
1.116
follow by arguments similar to the above.
∎

10.2.3
Generic behaviour

Finally, we are in a position to prove Proposition
1.118
.

Proof of Proposition
1.118
.

In order to prove the statement concerning non-genericity, recall (
10.52
).
Note that the maximal existence interval of solutions to (
10.52
) whose initial data satisfy
(
10.53
) is always
\mathbb{R}
(assuming
V
to be non-negative). This is also true if
\vartheta
vanishes.
Moreover, for any
\phi_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{\tau}=0
,
\phi=\phi_{\infty}
and
\vartheta=0
is a fixed point of
(
10.52
). Moreover, the corresponding fixed points satisfy (
10.53
).
Rewriting (
10.52
) as a first order system with variables
\phi_{0}:=\phi
,
\phi_{1}:=\phi_{\tau}

and
\vartheta
, each fixed point has one negative, one positive and one vanishing eigenvalue. At this stage, we can apply

[
43
, Theorem B.3]
(which is a special case of
[
20
, Theorem 9.1 (i) and (iv)]
). In the application
of this theorem
M
is the open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by
(
10.53
);
Y
is the smooth vector field determined by rewriting (
10.52
) as
a first order system for
\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}
and
\vartheta
;
\mathcal{E}
is the subset of
M
defined by
\phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{1}=0

and
\vartheta=0
; and
K=K_{n}:=[-n,n]\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
K
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{E}
. Let

r\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, due to
[
43
, Theorem B.3]
, there is a
C^{r}
center unstable manifold
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
near
K
and a
neighbourhood
U_{n}
of
K
such that
A^{-}(U_{n})\subset\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
. This means the following. First,
K\subset\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
;
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
is locally
invariant under the flow of
Y
; and for each
m\in K
,
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
is tangent to the direct sum of the eigenspaces of the linearization
of the equation at
m
corresponding to the positive and the vanishing eigenvalue. Note, in particular, that
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
is two
dimensional. Second

m\in A^{-}(U_{n})\Longleftrightarrow\overline{\{\Phi_{\tau}(m):\tau\in(-\infty,0]\}}\subset U_{n},

where
\Phi
is the flow associated with
Y
. Note, in particular, that any solution converging to a point in
K_{n}
sooner or later
has to belong to
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
in the past direction. In other words, the solutions that converge to a point in
K_{n}
have to, in finite
time, flow backwards to a point in
\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n}
. This means that taking the union of
\Phi_{\tau}(\mathcal{C}^{u}_{n})
for
\tau\in\mathbb{Q}
and

n\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain a set that contains all the solutions converging to a point in
\mathcal{E}
in the past. Moreover, this set is a
countable union of codimension one submanifolds.
∎

Chapter 11
Future asymptotics

The future asymptotics of spatially homogeneous solutions to the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations have been studied in many
articles; see, e.g.,
[
40
,
46
,
55
]
. Here we state results in the Bianchi class A and
k=-1
FLRW settings.

11.1
The Bianchi class A setting

We begin by using modifications of the arguments of
[
55
]
to derive the following conclusions.

Proposition 11.1
.

Assume
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
to have a strictly positive lower bound;
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
; and
V^{\prime}
to only
have simple zeroes. Fix a Bianchi class A type
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{IX}
and an
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{iso},\mathrm{LRS},\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
. Let
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]

be the subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
with the following property: If
\xi\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
is represented by an

\eta\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\sigma_{ij}
,
n_{ij}
,
\theta
and
\phi
denote the solution to (
3.1b
)–(
3.4
)
with initial data determined by
\eta
, then the
n_{ij}
converge to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
; the
\sigma_{ij}
converge
to zero;
\phi_{t}
converges to zero;
\phi
converges to a limit, say
s_{0}
;
\theta
converges to
[3V(s_{0})]^{1/2}
;
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
;
and
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
. Then
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, considered as a subset of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]\cap\{\theta>0\}
, is open, dense
and has full measure.

Remark 11.2
.

Due to Lemmas
2.14
and
2.15
,
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
can be represented by a
quotient of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
by a finite group
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. In this sense, elements of the former set are represented by a finite number
of elements of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover, given an element
(\bar{n},K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, the corresponding initial data to
(
3.1b
)–(
3.4
) are obtained as follows:
\sigma(0)
is the trace free part of
K
;
\theta(0)=\mathrm{tr}K
;

n(0)=\bar{n}
;
\phi(0)=\bar{\phi}_{0}
and
\phi_{t}(0)=\bar{\phi}_{1}
. Finally, since the elements of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
map solutions to solutions and since they
only permute the diagonal elements of
n
and
\sigma
and, possibly, change the signs of all the elements of
n
at the same time, it
is clear that the elements of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
map solutions to (
3.1b
)–(
3.4
) with the properties stated in the proposition
to solutions with the same properties. For this reason, the choice of the representative
\eta\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
for an element

\xi\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
does not matter.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
be the subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
defined in analogy with
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
. Then, since
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is a finite
covering space of
{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, it is sufficient to prove that
\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is open, dense and of full measure.

Consider a solution corresponding to initial data in
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Due to
[
55
, Corollary 26.4, p. 450]
, we then know that there is a

0\leq V_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\theta(t)\rightarrow\sqrt{3V_{1}}
and

\phi_{t}^{2}(t)+2V\circ\phi(t)\rightarrow 2V_{1}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
\sigma_{ij}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.
Since
V
has a strictly positive lower bound,
V_{1}>0
and (
3.3
) yields the conclusion that
n_{ij}(t)
converges to zero
exponentially. Due to the arguments presented in the proof of
[
55
, Corollary 26.7, p. 452]
, we also know that
\phi_{t}(t)\rightarrow 0

as
t\rightarrow\infty
and that
\phi(t)
converges to a limit. Since
V(s)\rightarrow\infty
as
|s|\rightarrow\infty
, we know that this
limit cannot be infinite. Thus there is an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi(t)\rightarrow s_{0}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover,
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
.
Note that, by assumption,
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})\neq 0
and that

\sigma_{ij}=0,\ \ \ n_{ij}=0,\ \ \ \phi_{t}=0,\ \ \ \phi=s_{0},\ \ \ \theta=\sqrt{3V(s_{0})}

is a fixed point of (
3.1
)–(
3.4
). In order to find out what type of fixed point this is, it is useful to reformulate
the equations as an unconstrained system. Recall, to this end, that
n
and
\sigma
are diagonal. The two constraints to be eliminated are then
(
3.1d
) and the fact that the trace of
\sigma
vanishes. To eliminate the
second one, we remove the equation for
\sigma_{11}
and define
\sigma_{11}:=-\sigma_{22}-\sigma_{33}
. In order to remove the first one, we
define
\theta
to be the positive square root of the right hand side of (
3.1d
) (note that the right hand side is strictly positive
due to the fact that
V
has a strictly positive lower bound and due to the fact that we have excluded Bianchi type IX). If the Bianchi type is such
that a certain
n_{ii}
(no summation) vanishes, we also remove the equation for that variable. However, we restrict the present discussion to the case
that all the
n_{ii}
(no summation) are non-vanishing; the modifications necessary to cover the other cases simply consist of omitting the corresponding

n_{ii}
’s from the variables. The remaining variables are then

x=(\sigma_{22},\sigma_{33},n_{11},n_{22},n_{33},\phi_{0},\phi_{1}),

where
\phi_{0}:=\phi
and
\phi_{1}:=\phi_{t}
, and the relevant equations are (
3.1b
) and (
3.2
) for
\{l,m\}=\{2\}
and

\{l,m\}=\{3\}
; the diagonal components of (
3.3
); and (
3.4
). Assume that we have a solution to this unconstrained system of
equations. We then wish to prove that it corresponds to a solution to the original system. Note, to this end, that

\dot{\sigma}_{11}=-\dot{\sigma}_{22}-\dot{\sigma}_{33}=\theta\sigma_{22}+\theta\sigma_{33}+s_{22}+s_{33}=-\theta\sigma_{11}-s_{11},

where we used the fact that the trace of the matrix with components
s_{ij}
vanishes. This means that all the equations in
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) are satisfied, except possibly (
3.1c
). However, at this stage, (
3.1c
) can be
verified by differentiating (
3.1d
) and appealing to the equations we know to hold. In short, we can reformulate
(
3.1
)–(
3.4
) to an unconstrained system, say
\dot{x}=F\circ x
. We wish to linearise this system around

x_{0}=(0,0,0,0,0,s_{0},0)
. However, denoting the Jacobi matrix of
F
at
x_{0}
by
F^{\prime}(x_{0})
, then

F^{\prime}(x_{0})=\mathrm{diag}(D_{1},D_{2},R),

where
D_{1}:=-3H\mathrm{Id}_{2}
,
D_{2}:=-H\mathrm{Id}_{3}
and

R:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-9H^{2}\zeta/4&-3H\end{array}\right),

H:=\sqrt{V(s_{0})/3}
,
\zeta=4V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})/(3H)^{2}
and
\mathrm{Id}_{n}
denotes the
n\times n
dimensional identity matrix. Clearly, the eigenvalues
of
F^{\prime}(x_{0})
are
-3H
,
-H
and

\lambda_{\pm}:=\tfrac{3H}{2}[-1\pm(1-\zeta)^{1/2}].

In particular, if
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
, all the eigenvalues have strictly negative real parts, and the fixed point is stable. If
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})<0
, then all the
eigenvalues but one are strictly negative. However, the remaining eigenvalue is strictly positive, and the fixed point is unstable. Let

\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
denote the subset of
\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
such that the corresponding solutions converge to one of the fixed points
with
V^{\prime\prime}>0
. Then, by the above,
\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
is open. We wish to prove that it also has full measure in the state space (and therefore is also
dense). Since the
\phi
-coordinates of the fixed points are isolated, there are at most countably many zeroes of
V^{\prime}
such that
V^{\prime\prime}<0
. Denote
the set of such zeroes by
\mathcal{Z}_{-}
. Each
s\in\mathcal{Z}_{-}
corresponds to an unstable hyperbolic fixed point of the above system. There is thus an
open neighbourhood
U_{s}
of the fixed point and
a
C^{1}
-submanifold of
U_{s}
, say
M_{s}
, such that solutions that do not leave
U_{s}
to the future have to belong to
M_{s}
. Let
\{W_{i}\}
,

i\in\mathbb{N}
, be a countable basis of the topology of
\mathbb{R}^{7}
and
V_{i}:=W_{i}\cap M_{s}
. For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Q_{i}
be the set of

r\in\mathbb{Q}
such that the time
r
flow of the vector field of the unconstrained system is defined on all of
W_{i}
. Finally, define

\mathcal{V}_{s}:=\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\cup_{r\in Q_{i}}\Phi_{r}(V_{i}),\ \ \ \mathcal{V}:=\cup_{s\in\mathcal{Z}_{-}}\mathcal{V}_{s},

where
\Phi_{T}(\xi)
denotes the solution of the unconstrained system with initial data
\xi
at
t=0
, evaluated at
t=T
. Since
\Phi_{T}
is a
diffeomorphism when it is defined,
\mathcal{V}_{s}
and
\mathcal{V}
are countable unions of codimension
1
submanifolds. Next, assume
\xi
to be initial data
such that if
x
is the solution determined by
x(0)=\xi
, then
x
converges to the fixed point corresponding to
s\in\mathcal{Z}_{-}
to the future. Then
there is a
T
such that

x(t)\in U_{s}
for all
t\geq T-1
. In particular, this means that
x(t)\in M_{s}
for all
t\geq T-1
. In particular, for
0<\epsilon<1
,
x(t)\in M_{s}

for
t\in[T-\epsilon,T+\epsilon]
. Due to the fact that the domain of the flow associated with the unconstrained system is defined on an open subset of

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{7}
, it follows that there is an
\epsilon>0
and an open neighbourhood
X_{T}
of
x(T)
in
\mathbb{R}^{7}
such that the flow is defined
on
I_{\epsilon}\times X_{T}
, where
I_{\epsilon}:=[-T-\epsilon,-T+\epsilon]
. Let
W_{i}
be such that
x(T)\in W_{i}
and
W_{i}\subseteq X_{T}
. Next, let

0<\delta<\epsilon
be such that the image of
[T-\delta,T+\delta]
under
x
belongs to
W_{i}
and let
u\in\mathbb{Q}\cap I_{\delta}
. Then the time
-u
flow
of
W_{i}
is defined on all of
W_{i}
. This means that the time
-u
flow of
x(u)
belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{s}
; i.e.,
\xi\in\mathcal{V}_{s}
.
To conclude:
\mathcal{V}_{s}
consists of all the elements of the phase space such that the corresponding solutions converge to the fixed point corresponding
to
s\in\mathcal{Z}_{-}
to the future. Moreover,
\mathcal{V}
consists of all the elements of the phase space such that the corresponding solutions converge to an
unstable fixed point to the future. Since
\mathcal{V}
has zero measure, the complement, i.e.
\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, is dense. Since we know
\mathsf{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
to be
open, we conclude that it is open, dense and has full measure. The proposition follows.
∎

Proposition 11.3
.

Assume the conditions of Proposition
11.1

to be satisfied. Let, using the corresponding notation,
\xi\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathfrak{G}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
;

\mathfrak{I}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
be such that
[\mathfrak{I}]=\xi
; and
(M,g,\phi)=\mathcal{D}[V](\mathfrak{I})
. Then
M
is of the form
M=G\times J
, where

J=(t_{-},\infty)
and
g
is of the form (
1.7
). Moreover, there is a left invariant Riemannian metric
\bar{g}_{\infty}
on
G
; an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}

with the property that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
and
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
; and constants
C>0
and
b>0
such that

\displaystyle|\phi(t)-s_{0}|+|\phi_{t}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{-bt},

(11.1a)

\displaystyle|\bar{K}(t)-H\mathrm{Id}|_{\bar{g}_{\infty}}+|e^{-2Ht}\bar{g}(t)-\bar{g}_{\infty}|_{\bar{g}_{\infty}}\leq

\displaystyle Ce^{-bt}

(11.1b)

for all
t\geq t_{0}
, where
H:=\sqrt{V(s_{0})/3}
,
t_{0}\in J
and
\bar{g}(t)
and
\bar{K}(t)
are the metric and Weingarten map induced on

M_{t}=G\times\{t\}
respectively.

Remark 11.4
.

There is a basis
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that the metric
g
of the proposition can be written

g=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i}a_{i}^{2}(t)\xi^{i}\otimes\xi^{i},

(11.2)

where
\{\xi^{i}\}
is the dual basis to
\{e_{i}^{\prime}\}
. Using this form of the metric, (
11.1b
) can be written

|\partial_{t}a_{i}(t)/a_{i}(t)-H|+|e^{-Ht}a_{i}(t)-\alpha_{i}|\leq Ce^{-bt}

for all
t\geq t_{0}
and some constants
\alpha_{i}>0
.

Proof.

That the metric
g
takes the form (
11.2
) follows from the proof of Proposition
1.31
, see
Section
3.2
. Moreover, the diagonal components of
\sigma
are given by
\partial_{t}a_{i}/a_{i}-\theta
.
Combining the knowledge obtained from Proposition
11.1
with the arguments presented at the beginning of

[
55
, Proof of Lemma 27.1, pp. 462–464]
, it follows that
\phi_{t}
and
\phi-s_{0}
converge to zero exponentially. This means that

V\circ\phi-V(s_{0})
converges exponentially to zero, so that the last two terms on the right hand side of (
3.16
) converge exponentially
to their limits. Since the left hand side converges exponentially to zero, see (
3.18
), it follows that
\theta
converges exponentially to

3H
, where
H=\sqrt{V(s_{0})/3}
. Next, combining (
3.17
) with the fact that
X
converges to zero exponentially, it is clear that

\sigma_{ij}
converges to zero exponentially. This means that

\dot{a}_{i}/a_{i}-H=\sigma_{i}+(\theta-3H)/3

converges to zero exponentially. This means that there are constants
\alpha_{i}>0
such that
e^{-Ht}a_{i}(t)-\alpha_{i}
decays exponentially.
The proposition follows.
∎

11.2
The
k=-1
FLRW setting

Next, we prove Proposition
1.124
.

Proof of Proposition
1.124
.

Note that
[
55
, Corollary 26.4, p. 450]
and
[
55
, Corollary 26.7, p. 452]
apply in this case as well. We can thus argue as at the
beginning of the proof of Proposition
11.1
in order to conclude that
\phi_{t}(t)\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
,
that there is an
s_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\phi(t)\rightarrow s_{0}
and that
V^{\prime}(s_{0})=0
. Moreover, we know that
a
tends to infinity exponentially.
Next, consider the unconstrained system (
10.52
), where the initial data are required to satisfy
(
10.53
). Note that

\phi_{\tau}=0,\ \ \ \phi=s_{0},\ \ \ \vartheta=[3V(s_{0})]^{-1/2}

(11.3)

constitutes a fixed point for (
10.52
). Moreover, if we formulate (
10.52
) as a first
order system, the linearised system around the fixed point (
11.3
) has three eigenvalues with negative real part
if
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
and two negative eigenvalues and one positive eigenvalue in case
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})>0
. By an argument similar to the proof of
Proposition
11.1
, the set of initial data corresponding to solutions converging to a fixed point with
V^{\prime\prime}(s_{0})<0

is contained in a countable union of one dimensional submanifolds. The proof of exponential convergence is quite similar to the
argument presented in the proof of Proposition
11.3
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Appendix A
Parametrising solutions, regular data and data on the singularity

The purpose of this appendix is to prove the statements concerning the parametrisation of solutions, regular data and data on the singularity we need in the
main body of the text. As noted in Lemma
2.4
, regular initial data give rise to an element of
\mathsf{B}
. We begin
by demonstrating that this element determines the data up to a Lie group isomorphism, assuming the Lie group to be simply connected.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},\bar{g}_{\alpha},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{B}[V]
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
; see Definition
1.12
. Let

\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
(the Lie algebra of
G_{\alpha}
) with
respect to
\bar{g}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, such that if
\nu_{\alpha}
and
K_{\alpha}
are the corresponding commutator and Weingarten matrices respectively, then

x_{\alpha}:=(\nu_{\alpha},K_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})\in\mathsf{B}
, where
\mathsf{B}
is introduced in Definition
2.1
. Then, if

x_{0}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{1})
for some
\sigma\in S_{3}
, see (
2.1
), there is a Lie group isomorphism
\psi:G_{0}\rightarrow G_{1}
such
that
\psi^{*}\bar{g}_{1}=\bar{g}_{0}
,
\psi^{*}\bar{k}_{1}=\bar{k}_{0}
,
\psi^{*}\bar{\phi}_{1,0}=\bar{\phi}_{0,0}
and
\psi^{*}\bar{\phi}_{1,1}=\bar{\phi}_{0,1}
.

Remark A.2
.

That bases
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
with the properties stated in the lemma exist follows fromLemma
2.4
.

Proof.

Let
\nu_{\alpha,i}
denote the diagonal components of
\nu_{\alpha}
.
Define
\Psi:\mathfrak{g}_{0}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{1}
to be the linear map such that
\Psi(e_{0,i})=\pm\mathrm{sgn}\sigma\cdot e_{1,\sigma(i)}
.
Then, if
\{i,j,k\}
is an even permutation of
\{1,2,3\}
,

\begin{split}[\Psi(e_{0,i}),\Psi(e_{0,j})]=&[e_{1,\sigma(i)},e_{1,\sigma(j)}]=\epsilon_{\sigma(i)\sigma(j)\sigma(k)}\nu_{1,\sigma(k)}e_{1,\sigma(k)}\\
=&\pm\mathrm{sgn}\sigma\cdot\nu_{0,k}e_{1,\sigma(k)}=\epsilon_{ijk}\nu_{0,k}\Psi(e_{0,k})=\Psi([e_{0,i},e_{0,j}]).\end{split}

Appealing to, e.g.,
[
36
, Theorem 20.19, p. 530]
, it follows that
\Psi
arises from a Lie group isomorphism
\psi
. In particular
\psi_{*}=\Psi
.
By the definition of
\Psi
and the conditions of the lemma, it is clear that
\psi
is an isometry of initial data.
∎

A similar argument yields a similar conclusion in the case of initial data on the singularity.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(G_{\alpha},\mathscr{H}_{\alpha},\mathscr{K}_{\alpha},\Phi_{\alpha,0},\Phi_{\alpha,1})\in{}^{\mathrm{sc}}\mathcal{S}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
; see Definition
1.25
. Let

\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
(the Lie algebra of
G_{\alpha}
), with respect to
\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, such that if

o_{\alpha}
and
P_{\alpha}
are defined as in Lemma
2.28
, then

x_{\alpha}:=(o_{\alpha},P_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})\in\mathsf{S}
, where
\mathsf{S}
is introduced in Definition
2.27
. Then, if

x_{0}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{1})
for some
\sigma\in S_{3}
, there is a Lie group isomorphism
\psi:G_{0}\rightarrow G_{1}
such that

\psi^{*}\mathscr{H}_{1}=\mathscr{H}_{0}
,
\mathscr{K}_{1}\psi_{*}=\psi_{*}\mathscr{K}_{0}
,
\psi^{*}\Phi_{1,0}=\Phi_{0,0}
and
\psi^{*}\Phi_{1,1}=\Phi_{0,1}
.

We also need to prove that all the cases admitted by Definition
2.1
are realised by initial data.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
(\nu,K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}
; see Definition
2.1
. Then there are non-trivial simply
connected Bianchi class A initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations, say
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
, and an orthonormal
frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such that if
\bar{\nu}
and
\bar{K}
are the commutator and Weingarten matrices associated with
\{e_{i}\}
,
then
\bar{\nu}=\nu
and
\bar{K}=K
.

Proof.

To begin with, let us give examples of Lie groups that belong to the different Bianchi class A types. The Lie group
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, with group operation
given by addition, is of Bianchi type I. The Heisenberg group is of Bianchi type II; see, e.g.,
[
54
, Section 23.4, p. 247]
. The
Lie group Sol is of Bianchi type VI
0
; see, e.g.,
[
54
, Section 23.3, p. 246]
. An example of a Lie group of Bianchi type VII
0

is given in the proof of
[
54
, Lemma 23.3, p. 245]
. The groups
\mathrm{Sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathrm{SU}(2)
are of Bianchi type VIII and
IX respectively. Let
x:=(\nu,K,\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})\in\mathsf{B}
. Fix a Lie group
G
, with Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
, of the appropriate type. Then, due to

[
54
, Lemma 19.8, p. 208]
and the arguments used to prove it, it follows that there is a basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
the associated commutator matrix is
\nu
. At this point, we can define a metric
\bar{g}
by declaring
\{e_{i}\}
to be orthonormal. Moreover, we
can define
\bar{k}
by requiring that the Weingarten matrix associated with
\{e_{i}\}
equals
K
. Since (
1.3
) is satisfied,
we conclude that (
1.2
) holds. Thus
(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
are Bianchi class A initial data for the
Einstein non-linear scalar field equations and
\{e_{i}\}
is a frame with the desired properties.
∎

There is a similar result in the case of data on the singularity.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
(o,P,\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}
; see Definition
2.27
. Then there are simply connected quiescent Bianchi class A initial data on the
singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
(G,\mathscr{H},\mathscr{K},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
, and an orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with
respect to
\mathscr{H}
such that if
\bar{o}
is the commutator matrix associated with
\{e_{i}\}
and
\bar{P}
is the matrix with components

\bar{P}_{ij}=\mathscr{H}(\mathscr{K}e_{i},e_{j})
, then
\bar{o}=o
and
\bar{P}=P
.

Before we proceed, it is useful to note that simply connected isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
initial data are of Bianchi type I.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}=(G,\bar{g},\bar{k},\bar{\phi}_{0},\bar{\phi}_{1})
be simply connected Bianchi type VII
0
initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar
field equations which are either isotropic or LRS. Then
\mathfrak{I}
are isometric to initial data of Bianchi type I.

Proof.

Assume first
\mathfrak{I}
to be isotropic. Then, due to the arguments given in the proof of Lemma
2.8
, there is an orthonormal
frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
with respect to
\bar{g}
such that the corresponding commutator and Weingarten matrices are diagonal and such that if
\nu_{i}
are
the diagonal components of the commutator matrix and
k_{i}
are the diagonal components of the Weingarten matrix, then
\nu_{1}=0
;
\nu_{2}=\nu_{3}
; and all the

k_{i}
are equal. If
\mathfrak{I}
are LRS, the arguments given in the proof of Lemma
2.10
yield the same conclusion, with one
modification; we still have
k_{2}=k_{3}
, but
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
.

Next, due to
[
54
, Section 23.2, pp. 245–246]
, the set
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with multiplication given by
[
54
, (23.11), p. 245]
is a simply
connected Lie group of Bianchi type VII
0
, say
H
. Moreover, given
\nu_{2}\neq 0
, the frame

e_{1}^{\prime}=\nu_{2}\partial_{x},\ \ \ e_{2}^{\prime}=-\cos(x)\partial_{y}+\sin(x)\partial_{z},\ \ \ e_{3}^{\prime}=\sin(x)\partial_{y}+\cos(x)\partial_{z}

(A.1)

is left invariant and such that the associated commutator matrix satisfies
\nu^{\prime}=\mathrm{diag}(0,\nu_{2},\nu_{2})
. Defining
\psi:\mathfrak{h}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
to be the
linear map satisfying
\psi(e_{i}^{\prime})=e_{i}
, it follows that
\psi
is a Lie algebra isomorphism. Combining this observation with
[
36
, Theorem 20.19, p. 530]

and the fact that
G
and
H
are simply connected, it follows that
\psi=\Psi_{*}
for some Lie group isomorphism
\Psi
. Pulling back the initial data using
\Psi
,
we can assume
G=H
and that
e_{i}=e_{i}^{\prime}
. The dual frame to (
A.1
) is given by

\xi^{1}=\nu_{2}^{-1}dx,\ \ \ \xi^{2}=-\cos(x)dy+\sin(x)dz,\ \ \ \xi^{3}=\sin(x)dy+\cos(x)dz.

This means that

\bar{g}=\xi^{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+\xi^{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+\xi^{3}\otimes\xi^{3}=\nu_{2}^{-2}dx\otimes dx+dy\otimes dy+dz\otimes dz;

i.e., that the metric is Euclidean. Next, note that since
k_{2}=k_{3}
, then

\bar{k}=k_{1}\xi^{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+k_{2}\xi^{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+k_{2}\xi^{3}\otimes\xi^{3}=k_{1}\nu_{2}^{-2}dx\otimes dx+k_{2}dy\otimes dy+k_{2}dz\otimes dz.

Due to the above, it is clear that the initial data are in fact of Bianchi type I.
∎

There is a similar result for data on the singularity.

Lemma A.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}
be simply connected quiescent Bianchi type VII
0
initial data on the singularity for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
which are either isotropic or LRS. Then
\mathfrak{I}
are isometric to initial data on the singularity of Bianchi type I.

Proof.

The argument concerning the metric is identical to the argument in the proof of Lemma
A.6
. Turning to the
(1,1)
-tensor field,
say
\mathscr{K}
:

\mathscr{K}=P_{1}e_{1}\otimes\xi^{1}+P_{2}e_{2}\otimes\xi^{2}+P_{2}e_{3}\otimes\xi^{3}=P_{1}\partial_{x}\otimes dx+P_{2}\partial_{y}\otimes dy+P_{2}\partial_{z}\otimes dz.

The lemma follows.
∎

Due to the last two lemmas, we exclude isotropic and LRS Bianchi type VII
0
data in what follows. Next, we need to prove that isometric data,
when represented by elements of
\mathsf{B}
, see Definition
2.1
and Lemma
2.4
, are related by
a map of the form
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}:=(G_{\alpha},\bar{g}_{\alpha},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})\in\mathcal{B}[V]
,
\alpha=0,1
. Let
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
(the Lie
algebra of
G_{\alpha}
) with respect to
\bar{g}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=0,1
, such that if
\nu_{\alpha}
and
K_{\alpha}
are the corresponding commutator and Weingarten matrices
respectively, then
x_{\alpha}:=(\nu_{\alpha},K_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})\in\mathsf{B}
. Assume that there is an isometry
\psi
between
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
. Then, if

x_{0}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, it follows that
x_{1}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover, there is a
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, see Definition
2.12
,
such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
.

Remark A.9
.

One particular consequence of the proof is that if
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}[V]
,
\alpha=0,1
, are isometric and
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, then

\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
.

Remark A.10
.

In case
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
is neither isotropic nor LRS, there is a
\sigma\in S_{3}
and
\epsilon_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
,
i=1,2,3
, such that

\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,i}=\epsilon_{i}e_{1,\sigma(i)}
; see the proof of the lemma. Here
(\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}X)_{x}=\psi_{*}X|_{\psi^{-1}(x)}
.

Proof.

That there are frames as in the statement of the lemma follows from Lemma
2.4
. From now on, we assume such frames to have
been chosen, and we consistently use the following notation:

\nu_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}(\nu_{\alpha,1},\nu_{\alpha,2},\nu_{\alpha,3});\ \ K_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}(K_{\alpha,1},K_{\alpha,2},K_{\alpha,3});\ \ R_{\alpha}=\mathrm{diag}(R_{\alpha,1},R_{\alpha,2},R_{\alpha,3}).

(A.2)

Here
R_{\alpha}
collects the diagonal components of the Ricci tensor of
\bar{g}_{\alpha}
with respect to

\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
(the off diagonal components vanish) and is called the
Ricci matrix
associated with
\nu_{\alpha}
. Due to

[
54
, Lemma 19.11, p. 209]
, if
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
,

R_{\alpha,i}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}(\nu_{\alpha,j}-\nu_{\alpha,k})^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\nu_{\alpha,i}^{2}

(A.3)

(no summation on any index). Note also that, due to the existence of the isometry,
\bar{\phi}_{0,0}=\bar{\phi}_{1,0}
and
\bar{\phi}_{0,1}=\bar{\phi}_{1,1}
. Consequently, we
only need to focus on
\nu_{\alpha}
and
K_{\alpha}
. It is of interest to sort out the consequences of the components of the Ricci tensor being equal. We have

R_{\alpha,2}=R_{\alpha,3}\ \text{iff}\ \nu_{\alpha,2}=\nu_{\alpha,3}\ \text{or}\ \nu_{\alpha,1}=\nu_{\alpha,2}+\nu_{\alpha,3}.

(A.4)

In particular, if, for a fixed
\alpha
, all the
R_{\alpha,i}
are equal, there are two possibilities. Either all the
\nu_{\alpha,i}
are equal or one vanishes and the
remaining two are equal.

Assume
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to be isotropic.
Then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
are also isotropic. Since we have excluded isotropic Bianchi type VII
0
initial data, this is
only possible in the case of Bianchi types I and IX. For topological reasons, we conclude that
G_{\alpha}
are either both of type I or both of type IX. Since
the eigenvalues of the Weingarten maps have to conicide, it follows that
K_{0}=K_{1}
. Moreover, since all the
\nu_{0,i}
have to equal, all the
\nu_{1,i}

have to equal and the scalar curvatures of
\bar{g}_{\alpha}
are
3\nu_{\alpha,1}^{2}/2
, it is clear that
\nu_{0}=\pm\nu_{1}
. There is thus a

\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
; cf. Definition
2.12
. One immediate consequence of the above
observations is that if
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
; cf. Remark
A.9
.
Moreover, if
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}[V]
, then
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{iso}}
.

Assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
are LRS.
Assume, to begin with, that
\{e_{0,i}\}
is an orthonormal frame of the type assumed to exist in
Definition
1.8
. It can then be deduced that
\nu_{0,2}=\nu_{0,3}
and that
K_{0,2}=K_{0,3}
. On the other hand, either
\nu_{0,1}\neq\nu_{0,2}
or

K_{0,1}\neq K_{0,2}
(since the data are not isotropic). Then
R_{0,2}=R_{0,3}
. Note that we can assume that either
R_{0,1}\neq R_{0,2}
or
K_{0,1}\neq K_{0,2}

(since
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
would otherwise be isotropic). Next, we can assume that
K_{1,2}=K_{1,3}=K_{0,2}
and that
R_{1,2}=R_{1,3}=R_{0,2}
by permuting the basis

\{e_{1,i}\}
. In fact, we can assume the permutation to be even, since we only need to align the basis vector corresponding to the element of
K_{1}
or

R_{1}
which is distinct. Thus the change of basis corresponds to replacing
x_{1}
by
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x_{1})
, where
\sigma
is even. Combining these
observations implies that
\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,1}=\xi e_{1,1}
, where
|\xi|=1
and
(\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}X)_{x}=\psi_{*}X|_{\psi^{-1}(x)}
. Thus

\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,2}=\alpha e_{1,2}+\beta e_{1,3},\ \ \ \psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,3}=\gamma e_{1,2}+\delta e_{1,3}.

(A.5)

Moreover, since
\psi
is an isometry,

\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}=1,\ \ \ \gamma^{2}+\delta^{2}=1,\ \ \ \alpha\gamma+\beta\delta=0.

(A.6)

Due to the fact that the Levi-Civita connection is torsion free and the fact that isometries preserve the Levi-Civita connection (see, e.g.,

[
41
, Proposition 59, p. 90]
), we conclude that

\begin{split}\nu_{0,3}(\gamma e_{1,2}+\delta e_{1,3})=&\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}([e_{0,1},e_{0,2}])=[\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,1},\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,2}]=[\xi e_{1,1},\alpha e_{1,2}+\beta e_{1,3}]\\
=&\xi(e_{1,1}(\alpha)-\nu_{1,2}\beta)e_{1,2}+\xi(e_{1,1}(\beta)+\nu_{1,3}\alpha)e_{1,3}.\end{split}

(A.7)

This means that

\displaystyle e_{1,1}(\alpha)=

\displaystyle\nu_{1,2}\beta+\xi\nu_{0,3}\gamma,

(A.8a)

\displaystyle e_{1,1}(\beta)=

\displaystyle-\nu_{1,3}\alpha+\xi\nu_{0,3}\delta.

(A.8b)

Applying
\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}
to
[e_{0,1},e_{0,3}]
similarly yields

\displaystyle e_{1,1}(\gamma)=

\displaystyle\nu_{1,2}\delta-\xi\nu_{0,2}\alpha,

(A.9a)

\displaystyle e_{1,1}(\delta)=

\displaystyle-\nu_{1,3}\gamma-\xi\nu_{0,2}\beta.

(A.9b)

Applying
e_{1,1}
to the first equality in (
A.6
) and appealing to (
A.6
) and (
A.8
) yields

0=\alpha e_{1,1}(\alpha)+\beta e_{1,1}(\beta)=\alpha\beta(\nu_{1,2}-\nu_{1,3}).

(A.10)
