There are thus two possibilities. Either
\alpha\beta=0
or
\nu_{1,2}=\nu_{1,3}
. Assume, for the moment, that
\alpha\beta=0
. Combining this equality with
(
A.6
) yields two possibilities. Either
|\alpha|=1
,
\beta=0
,
\gamma=0
and
|\delta|=1
; or
\alpha=0
,
|\beta|=1
,
|\gamma|=1
and
\delta=0
.
In the first case, (
A.8
) and (
A.9
) imply that
\nu_{1,i}=\alpha\delta\xi\nu_{0,i}
for
i=\{2,3\}
. In particular, this means
that
\nu_{1,2}=\nu_{1,3}
. Assume now that
\alpha=0
,
|\beta|=1
,
|\gamma|=1
and
\delta=0
. In this case, a similar argument yields

\nu_{1,2}=-\beta\gamma\xi\nu_{0,3}
and
\nu_{1,3}=-\beta\gamma\xi\nu_{0,2}
. To summarise, we can always assume that
\nu_{1,2}=\nu_{1,3}
. Thus
\mathfrak{I}_{1}

are also LRS. Since we have excluded LRS Bianchi type VII
0
, we know that if
\nu_{0,2}\neq 0
, then
\nu_{0,1}\neq 0
. Similarly, if
\nu_{1,2}\neq 0
,
then
\nu_{1,1}\neq 0
. Assume now that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
is of Bianchi type I. Then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
is either of Bianchi type I or Bianchi type VII
0
(since these
are the only Bianchi types compatible with vanishing Ricci curvature). Since we know
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
to be LRS,
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
can, on the other hand, not be of
Bianchi type VII
0
. This means that
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
is of Bianchi type I. Since the eigenvalues of the Weingarten map have to coincide, there is a

\psi_{\sigma}^{+}
such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x_{0})
. Moreover, since we can always interchange
K_{0,2}
and
K_{0,3}
, we can assume
\sigma
to be even. This
means that we can assume
\psi_{\sigma}^{+}\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{I}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
. Assume now that
\nu_{0,1}\neq 0
. Since
R_{0,1}=R_{1,1}
, (
A.3
) and
previous deductions imply that
\nu_{0,1}=\eta\nu_{1,1}
, where
|\eta|=1
. Combining this observation with
R_{0,2}=R_{1,2}
and previous deductions imply
that
\nu_{0,1}\nu_{0,3}=\nu_{1,1}\nu_{1,3}
. Since
\nu_{0,1}\neq 0
, it follows that
\nu_{0,3}=\eta\nu_{1,3}
. Thus
\nu_{1}=\eta\nu_{0}
. Since
K_{0,i}=K_{1,i}

for
i=2,3
and the eigenvalues of the Weingarten matrices have to coincide, we also have
K_{0}=K_{1}
. This means that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
have the same
Bianchi type, say
\mathfrak{T}\neq\mathrm{I}
, and that there is a
\sigma\in S_{3}
such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
. Due to the fact that the data are LRS, we can,
if necessary, add a transposition to
\sigma
in order to ensure that
\sigma
is even. This means that
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
. If

\{e_{0,i}\}
is not an orthonormal frame of the type assumed to exist in Definition
1.8
, we can apply the above argument twice; from an
orthonormal frame of the type assumed to exist in Definition
1.8
, say
\{f_{0,i}\}
, to
\{e_{0,i}\}
(with
\psi
replaced by
\mathrm{Id}
) and from

\{f_{0,i}\}
to
\{e_{1,i}\}
. Combining the corresponding maps then yields the desired conclusion. One immediate consequence of the above observations is
that if
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
; cf. Remark
A.9
. Moreover, if

\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}[V]
, then
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathrm{LRS}}
.

In what follows, we assume that neither
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
nor
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
are LRS (or isotropic).

Remaining cases.
If
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
is of Bianchi type I but neither isotropic nor LRS, then the
K_{0,i}
are all distinct. Thus the conclusion of
Remark
A.10
holds. Since
\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}
preserves the Lie bracket, this means that
\nu_{1}=0
, so that
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
is of Bianchi type
I. Moreover, it is clear that there is a
\sigma\in S_{3}
such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{+}(x_{0})
. In what follows, we therefore assume that
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
is not of
Bianchi type I. If all the
K_{0,i}
are equal and all the
R_{0,i}
are equal, we are in the isotropic setting, which has already been handled. By
permuting the elements of the frame
\{e_{0,i}\}
, we can therefore assume that either
K_{0,1}\notin\{K_{0,2},K_{0,3}\}
or

R_{0,1}\notin\{R_{0,2},R_{0,3}\}
. Similarly, permuting the frame
\{e_{1,i}\}
, we can assume that
\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,1}=\epsilon_{1}e_{1,1}
, where
|\epsilon_{1}|=1
. This
means that
K_{0,1}=K_{1,1}
. If
K_{0,2}\neq K_{0,3}
, we can assume that
\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,A}=\epsilon_{A}e_{1,A}
for
A\in\{2,3\}
, where
|\epsilon_{A}|=1
; i.e. that the
conclusion of Remark
A.10
holds. This means that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
for some
\sigma\in S_{3}
. Assume now that

K_{0,2}=K_{0,3}
. Then
K_{1,2}=K_{1,3}
, so that
\nu_{1,2}\neq\nu_{1,3}
, since
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
would otherwise be LRS. The argument leading up to
(
A.10
) still applies
and implies that
\alpha\beta=0
, since
\nu_{1,2}\neq\nu_{1,3}
. We conclude that the conclusion of Remark
A.10
holds. Thus

x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
for some
\sigma\in S_{3}
. This observation covers all the remaining cases except for permutation symmetric Bianchi type VI
0

initial data. However, just as in the LRS setting, that case can be handled by adding a transposition, if necessary. One immediate consequence of the
above observations is that if
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathrm{per},\mathrm{gen}\}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, then
\mathfrak{I}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
; cf.
Remark
A.9
. Moreover, if
\mathfrak{I}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}[V]
, then
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathsf{B}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. The lemma follows.
∎

With this information at hand, we are in a position to prove Proposition
1.35
.

Proof of Proposition
1.35
.

Assume that the initial data are
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},\bar{h}_{\alpha},\bar{k}_{\alpha},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,0},\bar{\phi}_{\alpha,1})
,
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
. Fix frames
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
as in the
statement of Lemma
A.8
. Due to Lemma
A.8
, we know that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
,
using the terminology of Lemma
A.8
. Relabelling the frame
\{e_{1,i}\}
and replacing
e_{1,1}

by
-e_{1,1}
, if necessary, we can ensure that
x_{1}=x_{0}
. Fix
t_{0}
as in the statement of the proposition and compute

a_{\alpha,i}
as described in the proof of Proposition
1.31
; see (
3.13
). Then, since the initial data and the
initial time are the same, it follows that the existence intervals of the two developments coincide, that the scalar fields of the two developments
are the same and that
a_{0,i}(t)=a_{1,i}(t)
for all
t
. Moreover, the metrics take the form

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i}a_{i}^{2}(t)\xi_{\alpha}^{i}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{i},

(A.11)

where
a_{i}:=a_{0,i}=a_{1,i}
and
\{\xi_{\alpha}^{i}\}
is the frame dual to
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
.

If both of the data sets are isotropic, the
a_{i}
coincide, see (
3.13
), so that the developments take the form

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\bar{h}_{\alpha}.

Since
\chi
is an isometry from
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
, it follows that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry between the developments.

Next, assume that the data are LRS but not isotropic. Then, using the notation introduced in (
A.2
), we can assume that

\nu_{0,2}=\nu_{0,3}=\nu_{1,2}=\nu_{1,3},\ \ \ K_{0,2}=K_{0,3}=K_{1,2}=K_{1,3}.

Note that this implies that
a_{2}=a_{3}
; cf. (
3.14
) and the adjacent text. This means that the metrics take the form

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}\xi_{\alpha}^{1}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(\xi_{\alpha}^{2}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{2}+\xi_{\alpha}^{3}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{3}).

In order for the data not to be isotropic, we have to have either
K_{\alpha,1}\neq K_{\alpha,2}
for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
or
R_{\alpha,1}\neq R_{\alpha,2}
for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
.
This means that
\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,1}=\pm e_{1,1}
, where
(\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}X)_{x}=\chi_{*}X|_{\chi^{-1}(x)}
. Moreover, (
A.5
) and
(
A.6
) have to hold with
\psi
replaced by
\chi
. Summarising the above observations, it follows that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an
isometry between the developments.

Finally, assume that the data are neither isotropic nor LRS. Then, due to Remark
A.10
,
we can choose frames as in the statement of Lemma
A.8
such that
\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,i}=e_{1,i}
(just replace

\{e_{1,i}\}
with
\{\epsilon_{i}e_{1,\sigma(i)}\}
, if necessary). Combining this observation with the fact that the metrics take the form
(
A.11
), it is clear that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry between the developments.
∎

Next, we note that there is a result analogous to Lemma
A.8
concerning data on the singularity.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{I}_{\alpha}:=(G_{\alpha},\mathscr{H}_{\alpha},\mathscr{K}_{\alpha},\Phi_{\alpha,0},\Phi_{\alpha,1})\in\mathcal{S}
,
\alpha=0,1
. Let
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
be an orthonormal basis of

\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
(the Lie algebra of
G_{\alpha}
) with respect to
\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}
,
\alpha=0,1
, such that if
o_{\alpha}
and
P_{\alpha}
are the corresponding commutator
and Weingarten matrices respectively, then
x_{\alpha}:=(o_{\alpha},P_{\alpha},\Phi_{\alpha,0},\Phi_{\alpha,1})\in\mathsf{S}
. Assume that there is an isometry
\psi
between

\mathfrak{I}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
. Then, if
x_{0}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
, it follows that
x_{1}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
. Moreover, there is a
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\mathfrak{s}}
,
see Definition
2.12
, such that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
.

Remark A.12
.

In case
\mathfrak{I}_{0}
is neither isotropic nor LRS, there is a
\sigma\in S_{3}
and
\epsilon_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
,
i=1,2,3
, such that

\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,i}=\epsilon_{i}e_{1,\sigma(i)}
. Here
(\psi_{*\mathrm{d}}X)_{x}=\psi_{*}X|_{\psi^{-1}(x)}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
A.8
.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to prove Proposition
1.50
.

Proof of Proposition
1.50
.

Define
G_{\alpha}
,
\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}
,
\mathscr{K}_{\alpha}
,
\Phi_{\alpha,0}
and
\Phi_{\alpha,1}
by

(G_{\alpha},\mathscr{H}_{\alpha},\mathscr{K}_{\alpha},\Phi_{\alpha,0},\Phi_{\alpha,1}):=\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,\alpha}.

Due to the existence of the isometry,
\Phi_{0,\beta}=\Phi_{1,\beta}
for
\beta\in\{0,1\}
. For that reason, we define
\Phi_{\beta}:=\Phi_{0,\beta}
,
\beta\in\{0,1\}
.
For each
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, let
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
be a frame satisfying the conditions of (
9.1
) with
\mathscr{H}
replaced by
\mathscr{H}_{\alpha}
;

\mathscr{K}
replaced by
\mathscr{K}_{\alpha}
;
e_{i}
replaced by
e_{\alpha,i}
;
p_{i}
replaced by
p_{\alpha,i}
; and
o_{i}
replaced by
o_{\alpha,i}
. Assume, moreover, the frames
to be as described in one of Lemmas
9.1
;
9.3
; and
9.4
according
to whether the data are isotropic; LRS; or neither isotropic nor LRS, respectively. Note, in particular, that the
o_{\alpha,i}
have signs as in
Table
1.1
. Due to the existence part of the proof of Theorem
1.45
, we conclude that there are solutions

(M_{\alpha},g_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})
inducing the data
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,\alpha}
on the singularity. Moreover,
M_{\alpha}=G_{\alpha}\times(0,t_{\alpha,+})
and

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i}a_{\alpha,i}^{2}(t)\xi_{\alpha}^{i}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{i},

(A.12)

where
\{\xi_{\alpha}^{i}\}
is the frame dual to
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
.

Next, combining Lemmas
9.1
;
9.3
; and
9.4
with the proof of
Lemma
2.28
, it is clear that if
o_{\alpha}
and
P_{\alpha}
are the matrices with diagonal components
o_{\alpha,i}
and
p_{\alpha,i}

respectively, then
x_{\alpha}:=(o_{\alpha},P_{\alpha},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})\in\mathsf{S}
. Thus the frames
\{e_{\alpha,i}\}
have the properties required in the statement of
Lemma
A.11
, so that
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}(x_{0})
, using the terminology of Lemma
A.11
.
In the isotropic setting, the
o_{\alpha}
and
P_{\alpha}
are multiples of the identity. Moreover, due to the requirements of
Lemma
9.1
, the
o_{\alpha,i}
have to be non-negative. Combining these observations, it follows that
x_{0}=x_{1}
in the
isotropic setting. In the LRS setting, Lemma
9.3
ensures that
p_{\alpha,2}=p_{\alpha,3}
and that
o_{\alpha,2}=o_{\alpha,3}
, but
that, for each
\alpha
, either
p_{\alpha,1}\notin\{p_{\alpha,2},p_{\alpha,3}\}
or
o_{\alpha,1}\notin\{o_{\alpha,2},o_{\alpha,3}\}
. As a consequence,
\sigma(1)=1
, so that

\sigma
is either the identity or a transposition interchanging
2
and
3
. Since the elements of
o_{\alpha}
have signs as in
Table
1.1
, it follows that
x_{0}=x_{1}
. Finally, assume that the data are neither isotropic nor LRS. In the case of Bianchi type I,
the requirements of Lemma
9.4
ensure that
x_{0}=x_{1}
. In the case of Bianchi type II,
o_{\alpha,1}>0
and
o_{\alpha,2}=o_{\alpha,3}=0
,
so that
\sigma(1)=1
. Since, in addition,
p_{\alpha,2}>p_{\alpha,3}
, we conclude that
\sigma=\mathrm{Id}
. Finally, since
o_{\alpha,1}>0
, we conclude that

x_{0}=x_{1}
. In the case of Bianchi type VI
0
, the argument is similar, except if
p_{0,2}=p_{0,3}
and
o_{0,2}=-o_{0,3}
. In that case
\sigma

could be the transposition interchanging
2
and
3
, and we could have
x_{1}=\psi_{\sigma}^{-}(x_{0})
. However, we then still have
x_{1}=x_{0}
. In the
remaining cases, arguments similar to the above yield the conclusion that the map
\psi_{\sigma}^{\pm}
relating
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
has to be the identity.
To conclude,
x_{0}=x_{1}
. For this reason, we below use the notation
o_{i}:=o_{0,i}
and
p_{i}:=p_{0,i}
.

Next, since
x_{1}=x_{0}
, the data on the singularity used when appealing to Proposition
7.3
are the same for the two
initial data sets
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,\alpha}
; cf. the existence part of the proof of Theorem
1.45
. More specifically, if
o_{i}=0
,
then
\epsilon_{i}=0
and
m_{i}=0
; if
o_{i}\neq 0
, then
\epsilon_{i}:=o_{i}/|o_{i}|
and
m_{i}:=\ln|o_{i}|
;
\varkappa_{\infty}=0
; and
\sigma_{i}:=p_{i}-1/3
. The corresponding
solution to (
3.31
)–(
3.38
) such that the statements of Proposition
7.3
hold is thus the same
for the two initial data sets. Since the scalar fields
\phi_{\alpha}
and the
a_{\alpha,i}
are constructed solely on the basis of this solution, we conclude that

\phi_{0}=\phi_{1}
and that
a_{0,i}=a_{1,i}
; cf. (
A.12
). Moreover, the relevant existence intervals are the same, so
that
t_{0,+}=t_{1,+}
. In particular,

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{i}a_{i}^{2}(t)\xi_{\alpha}^{i}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{i},

(A.13)

where
a_{i}:=a_{0,i}=a_{1,i}
; cf. (
A.12
).

If both of the data sets are isotropic, the developments take the form

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}(t)\mathscr{H}_{\alpha};

cf. (
1.10
) and (
A.13
). Since
\chi
is an isometry from
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,0}
to
\mathfrak{I}_{\infty,1}
, it follows
that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry between the developments.

Next, assume that the data are LRS. Then
o_{2}=o_{3}
and
p_{2}=p_{3}
, so that
a_{2}=a_{3}
; cf. (
9.20
) and the adjacent
text. This means that the metrics take the form

g_{\alpha}=-dt\otimes dt+a_{1}^{2}\xi_{\alpha}^{1}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{1}+a_{2}^{2}(\xi_{\alpha}^{2}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{2}+\xi_{\alpha}^{3}\otimes\xi_{\alpha}^{3}).

In order for the data not to be isotropic, either
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
or the diagonal Ricci tensor component corresponding to
e_{0,1}
is
different from the other two diagonal Ricci tensor components.
This means that
\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,1}=\pm e_{1,1}
, where
(\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}X)_{x}=\chi_{*}X|_{\chi^{-1}(x)}
. Moreover, (
A.5
) and
(
A.6
) have to hold with
\psi
replaced by
\chi
. Summarising the above observations, it follows that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an
isometry between the developments.

Finally, assume that the data are neither isotropic nor LRS. Then, due to Remark
A.12
, there is a
\sigma\in S_{3}

and
\epsilon_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
,
i=1,2,3
, such that
\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}e_{0,i}=\epsilon_{i}e_{1,\sigma(i)}
. Since
\chi_{*\mathrm{d}}
preserves the Lie bracket, this means that

x_{1}=\psi_{\lambda}^{\pm}(x_{0})
, where
\lambda=\sigma^{-1}
. By the arguments given above,
\lambda
has to equal the identity, except if
the data belong to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VI}_{0}}^{\mathrm{per}}
, in which case
\lambda
is allowed to permute
2
and
3
and
x_{1}=\psi_{\lambda}^{-}(x_{0})
. However, in
this case
a_{2}=a_{3}
. Due to these observations, it is clear that
\chi\times\mathrm{Id}
is an isometry between the developments.
∎
